2.2.1
Spheres in the cubic surface

Abbildung 2.9:
Nine of the twenty-seven lines on a (deformation of) a cubic
surface, in the homology classes
[D_{1}]+\sum_{i=1,2,5,6}n_{i}[E_{i}]
.

In the following result, we show that a holomorphic sphere in

X:=X_{\epsilon}
with Chern number one consists of one long divisor and a
non-self crossing path of short divisors. There is one configuration
corresponding to each of the pictures in Figure
2.9
.
By symmetry there are twenty-seven such configurations in total.

Proposition 2.7
.

(Twenty seven lines in the cubic)
Any sphere of index zero in the manifold
X:=X_{\epsilon}
is of
the form shown in Figure
2.9
, and each such broken
sphere has multiplicity one. Therefore, a cubic has
27
lines.

Remark 2.8
.

(Disks versus spheres)
We point out that the results in this book are about Fukaya
algebras (defined using counts of disks), whereas Proposition

2.7
uses analogous results in Gromov-Witten
theory (defined using counts of spheres). Broken Gromov-Witten
invariants are well-defined in cases when markings are constrained
to lie on homology classes that are represented by cycles that are
contained in a single top-dimensional cut space. In the case of
lines in the cubic surface there are no markings. Whenever the
broken Gromov-Witten invariants are well-defined, they are equal to
the ordinary GW invariants on the unbroken manifold. The proofs are
easier than the
A_{\infty}
counterpart because the coherence condition
on the perturbation datum is weaker. We elaborate on this difference
in Remark
6.7
.

Abbildung 2.10:
Top left: Broken versions of some divisors in the cubic surface. Other figures: Broken maps
u^{1}
,
u^{2}
,
u^{3}
whose gluing is homologous to
[D_{1}]+k_{1}[E_{1}]+k_{2}[E_{2}]
for some
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
.

We now prove the result on spheres on the cubic surface.

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.

We recall from the discussion at the beginning of the section that
the homology class of a sphere of index zero in
X
is the sum of a
long divisor class
[D_{i}]
and an arbitrary number of short divisor
classes. Consider a broken sphere
u
such that its gluing has
homology class

[u_{{\operatorname{glue}}}]=[D_{1}]+n_{1}[E_{1}]+n_{2}[E_{2}]+n_{5}[E_{5}]+n_{6}[E_{6}],

where
D_{1}
,
E_{i}
are defined in Figure
2.9
. We
aim to prove that
u
is of the form shown in Figure

2.9
. We first assume that that
n_{5}=n_{6}=0
, and
so,

[u_{{\operatorname{glue}}}]=[D_{1}]+n_{1}[E_{1}]+n_{2}[E_{2}].

(2.11)

We will show that a regular configuration is one of the types
(a)-(c) in Figure
2.9
. The conclusion for the
general case (
n_{5},n_{6}\neq 0
) follows in a similar way. Indeed,
the arguments given below for the “
E_{1}
-end” of the long divisor

D_{1}
can be applied to the “
E_{6}
-end” also.

Given a broken map
u
with homology class as in (
2.11
)
and a tropical graph
\Gamma
, we will show that
n_{1},n_{2}\leq 1
.
The proof is carried out in steps, where in every step, we uncover
the possible maps
u_{v}
corresponding to a vertex
v
of the
tropical graph. In the following analysis we refer to Figure

2.10
for notation.

Step 1
(
v_{0}
): Since the long divisor is a
summand in the homology class represented by the map
u
, there is a
component of
u_{v_{0}}
of the broken map that maps to
D_{1}^{\prime}
(see
Figure
2.10
for notations). By the assumption that

n_{5}=n_{6}=0
,
v_{0}
has

—

an edge to
v_{-1}
with
P(v_{-1})=P_{9}
, and the homology class of the map
u_{v_{-1}}
is
[D_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}]
; and

—

an edge to
v_{1}
with
P(v_{1})=P_{1}
;

and no other edges.

Step 2
(
v_{1}
): So far, we have shown that the
broken map has vertices
v_{0}
,
v_{-1}
and further,
v_{0}
has an
edge of multiplicity one to a vertex
v_{1}
with
P(v_{1})=P_{1}
. Given
that the homology class of the gluing of
u
is given by
(
2.11
), the homology class of
u_{v_{1}}
is

[D_{1}^{\prime\prime}]+k[E_{1}^{\prime}]\quad\text{for some $k\geq 0$.}

(2.12)

We proceed to show that
k=0
or
1
: Recall that
E^{\prime}_{1,J_{v_{1}}}

is a
J_{v_{1}}
-holomorphic sphere in
\overline{X}_{P_{1}}
that is
homologous to the
(-1)
-sphere
E_{1}^{\prime}\subset\overline{X}_{P_{1}}
, using
the notation in (
2.4
). Since the map
u_{v_{1}}

intersects the relative divisor
\overline{X}_{P_{01}}
, the homology class

[u_{v_{1}}]
can not be a multiple of the class
[E_{1}^{\prime}]
. The
property

(Constant on univalent components)

then implies that the
domain-dependent almost complex structure for
u_{v_{1}}
is equal to
the constant
J_{v_{1}}
. In (
2.12
) if
k>1
, then

[u_{v_{1}}].[E^{\prime}_{1,J_{v_{1}}}]<0
. This implies that the image of

u_{v_{1}}
is contained in
E^{\prime}_{1,J_{v_{1}}}
, contradicting the fact
that
u_{v_{1}}
intersects the relative divisor
\overline{X}_{P_{01}}
. In case

k=0
, the broken map
u
is
u^{1}
represented in Figure

2.9
(a) and Figure
2.10
(a),

[u_{\operatorname{glue}}^{1}]=[D_{1}]
and there are no more vertices in the tropical
graph. If
k=1
,
v_{1}
has an edge to a vertex
v_{2}
with

P(v_{2})=P_{2}
.

Step 3
(
v_{2}
): So far, we have shown that for a
broken map
u
whose gluing has homology class (
2.11
),
either
[u_{\operatorname{glue}}]=[D_{1}]
or the tropical graph of
u
has vertices

v_{-1}
,
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
and
v_{1}
has an edge of multiplicity
1
to
a vertex
v_{2}
with
P(v_{2})=P_{2}
. In the latter case, the homology
class (
2.11
) of the glued map
[u_{\operatorname{glue}}]
implies that
the homology of
u_{v_{2}}
is

[u_{v_{2}}]=k_{1}[E_{1}^{\prime\prime}]+k_{2}[E_{2}^{\prime}],

and since
v_{2}
has an edge to
v_{1}
,
k_{1}\geq 1
.

First, consider the case
k_{2}=0
. If
k_{1}=1
, then there are no more
vertices,
u=u^{2}
, and the map is represented in Figure

2.9
(b) and Figure
2.10
(b). Now
suppose
k_{2}=0
and
k_{1}>1
. Then
u_{v_{2}}
is a multiple cover of
the sphere
E^{\prime\prime}_{1,J_{v_{2}}}
(using notation from
(
2.4
)). Besides the edge
e
to
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
has at
least one more edge to a vertex, say
\overline{v}_{3}
, with

P(\overline{v}_{3})=P_{1}
, see Figure
2.11
. Indeed, at the node

w_{e^{\prime}}
corresponding to the edge
e^{\prime}:=(v_{1},v_{2})
, the map

u_{v_{2}}
has an intersection of order
1
with the relative divisor

\overline{X}_{P_{12}}
. More edges are required to account for the
k

intersections that
u_{v_{2}}
has with
\overline{X}_{P_{12}}
. The
homology class of
u_{\operatorname{glue}}
in (
2.11
) implies that the
homology class of the map
u_{\overline{v}_{3}}
is a multiple of

[E_{1}^{\prime}]
. For generic background almost complex structures

J_{v_{2}}^{b}
,
J_{\overline{v}_{3}}^{b}
and small enough domain-dependent
perturbations, the evaluations

\operatorname{ev}_{w_{e}}(u_{v_{2}}),\quad\operatorname{ev}_{w_{e}}(u_{\overline{v}_{3}})\in\overline{X}_{P_{12}}

will not agree; see Remark
2.4
which explains a similar
result. Therefore, matching conditions can not be simultaneously
satisfied at the node
(v_{1},v_{2})
and
(v_{1},\overline{v}_{3})
.
This argument rules out the possibility that
k_{1}>1
when
k_{2}=0
.

Abbildung 2.11:
A hypothetical broken map (ruled out in proof of Proposition
2.7
).

Next, assuming
k_{2}>0
, we show that
k_{1}=k_{2}
. The argument is
similar to the one used in Step 2. For example, if
k_{2}>k_{1}
, then

[u_{v_{2}}].[E_{2}^{\prime}]<0
. It follows that the image of
u_{v_{2}}
lies in
the
J_{v_{2}}
-holomorphic sphere homologous to
E_{2}^{\prime}
, contradicting
the fact that
k_{1}\geq 1
. The case
k_{1}>k_{2}
is ruled out by a
similar argument.

Abbildung 2.12:
The space
\overline{X}_{P_{2}}\backslash(\overline{X}_{P_{02}}\cup E^{\prime\prime}_{1,J_{v_{2}}}\cup E^{\prime}_{2,J_{v_{2}}})
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration. The map
f
maps an end-point of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fiber to its other end-point.

Next, we rule out the case
k_{1}=k_{2}>1
using the genericity of
J
.
Gluing the
(-1)
-spheres
E^{\prime\prime}_{1,J_{v_{2}}}
and
E^{\prime}_{2,J_{v_{2}}}

produces a
J_{v_{2}}
-holomorphic sphere with a trivial normal bundle
in
\overline{X}_{P_{2}}
. In this case, there is a fibration

\pi:\overline{X}_{P_{2}}\backslash(\overline{X}_{P_{02}}\cup E^{\prime\prime}_{1,J_{v_{2}}}\cup E^{\prime}_{2,J_{v_{2}}})\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}

where the fibers are
J_{v_{2}}
-holomorphic spheres. When
k_{1}=k_{2}
,
the map
u_{v_{2}}
is a cover of one of the fibers of
\pi
. We
define a diffeomorphism

f:\overline{X}_{P_{12}}\backslash\overline{X}_{P_{012}}\to\overline{X}_{P_{23}}\backslash\overline{X}_{P_{023}}

by the condition that
x
and
f(x)
lie on the same fiber of
\pi
,
see Figure
2.12
. As we argued in the previous paragraph,
the inequality
k_{1}>1
implies that
v_{2}
has an edge to a vertex,
say,
\overline{v}_{3}
with
P(\overline{v}_{3})=P_{1}
and
u_{\overline{v}_{3}}
is a cover of
the sphere
E^{\prime}_{1,J_{\overline{v}_{3}}}
(see Figure
2.13
). Since

k_{2}>0
,
v_{2}
also has an edge to a vertex, say,
\overline{v}_{4}
with

P(\overline{v}_{4})=P_{3}
and
u_{\overline{v}_{4}}
is a cover of the sphere

E^{\prime\prime}_{2,J_{\overline{v}_{4}}}
. For generic background almost complex
structures
J_{\overline{v}_{3}}
,
J_{\overline{v}_{4}}
,

f(E^{\prime}_{1,J_{\overline{v}_{3}}}\cap X_{P_{12}})\neq E^{\prime\prime}_{2,J_{\overline{v}_{4}}}\cap X_{P_{23}}.

The domain-dependent perturbations are small enough that the
evaluations
\operatorname{ev}_{w_{e^{\prime\prime}}}(u_{v_{2}})
,

\operatorname{ev}_{w_{e^{\prime\prime}}}(u_{\overline{v}_{4}})\in\overline{X}_{P_{23}}
lie in disjoint
neighborhoods of
f(E^{\prime}_{1,J_{\overline{v}_{3}}}\cap\overline{X}_{P_{12}})
,

E^{\prime\prime}_{2,J_{\overline{v}_{4}}}\cap\overline{X}_{P_{23}}
. As a consequence, there is no
broken map
u
for which
[u_{\operatorname{glue}}]=D_{1}+k(E_{1}+E_{2})
,
k>1
.

Abbildung 2.13:
A hypothetical broken map (ruled out in proof of Proposition
2.7
).

Finally, we are left with the case
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
. In this case
u_{v_{2}}

is a simple map to a fiber of
\pi
, and
v_{2}
has an edge to a vertex

v_{3}
with
P(v_{3})=0
.

Step 4
(
v_{3}
) : So far, we have shown that if a
broken map
u
whose gluing has homology class (
2.11
),
then either
[u_{\operatorname{glue}}]=[D_{1}]
or
[u_{\operatorname{glue}}]=[D_{1}]+[E_{1}]
or the
tropical graph of
u
has vertices
v_{-1}
,
v_{0}
,
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
, and

v_{2}
has an edge of multiplicity
1
to a vertex
v_{3}
with

P(v_{3})=P_{3}
. In this last case, the only possibility is that

u_{v_{3}}
is a simple map of class
[E_{2}^{\prime\prime}]
. The resulting broken
map, of class
[D_{1}]+[E_{1}]+[E_{2}]
is shown in Figure

2.9
(c) and Figure
2.10
(c). Multiple
covers of
[E_{2}^{\prime\prime}]
are ruled out using arguments similar to those in
Step 2: If
u_{v_{3}}
is a multiple cover of
E^{\prime\prime}_{2}
then there is a
path
P
in
\Gamma
emanating out of
v_{3}
(which does not contain

v_{2}
) whose end-point is a
(-1)
-curve. Let
e\ni v_{3}
be the first
edge in the path
P
. Since the path ends in a
(-1)
-curve, there is
a point constraint on
u_{v_{3}}
at the node
w_{e}
corresponding to

e
. For a generic background
J
and a small enough perturbation, the
point constraint will be distinct from

E^{\prime\prime}_{2,J_{v_{3}}}\cap\overline{X}_{P_{23}}
, contradicting the existence of
such a multiple cover
m[E_{2}^{\prime\prime}]
in the broken map.

Finally, we show that each tropical graph in Figure

2.9
contributes
+1
to the curve count. Since the
sphere
u_{v}
corresponding to each vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
is
pseudoholomorphic with respect to an integrable almost complex
structure
J_{v}^{b}
(see (
2.3
)), and matching conditions are
cut out by diagonals on the relative divisors, each of the broken maps
has positive orientation in the moduli space. In each of these cases,
there is no automorphism of the tropical graph. Therefore, a broken
map with
n
interior markings contributes
\frac{1}{n!}
to the curve
count (see (
10.18
)). Assuming that the components of the
broken map have
n_{1},\dots,n_{m}
markings (that add up to
n
),
permuting the labellings of marked points within each component gives

(n_{1}!\dots n_{m}!)
curves. Finally, there are

\frac{n!}{n_{1}!\dots n_{m}!}
ways of assigning interior markings to
components – each of these would give rise to a different almost
complex structure. Our proof shows that in all the cases, the number
of broken maps corresponding to each tropical graph is
unaffected. This finishes the proof of the curve count in broken
maps. Since Gromov-Witten invariants are not altered by
neck-stretching and deformation of the symplectic form, we have shown
that there are
27
lines on a cubic surface.
∎

2.2.2
Disks in the cubic surface

In the next result, we compute the disk potential of the monotone
Lagrangian torus
L_{0}
(see (
2.6
)) in the cubic surface.
The monotone Lagrangian, with its trivial local system split-generates
the sub-category of the Fukaya category corresponding to the “small
eigenvalue” in Sheridan’s language in
[
78
]
. To compute
the potential of the Lagrangian
L_{0}\subset X_{0}
, we work in

(X_{\epsilon},\omega_{\epsilon})
which is symplectic deformation equivalent to

(X_{0},\omega_{0})
, and compute the potential of the Lagrangian torus

L_{\epsilon}\subset X_{\epsilon}
(see Proposition
2.6
). We show
that a disk in the deformed cubic surface
X_{\epsilon}
with boundary in

L_{\epsilon}
and of Maslov index
I(u)=2
either

(a)

intersects a long divisor
D_{i}
(there are 3 such disks), or

(b)

is a nodal disk consisting of a disk
u_{0}:S\to X

intersecting a short divisor
E_{i}
and a path of spheres

u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to X
, each mapping to a short divisor

E_{j}
, and thus having Maslov index zero. The maps
u_{i}
,

u_{i+1}
intersect at a nodal point; and the maps
u_{i}
,
u_{j}
do
not intersect if
j-i\geq 2
. There are
18
such nodal
disks. See Figure
2.14
.

Abbildung 2.14:
There are twenty-one Maslov-index-two-disks
on a cubic surface: 3 of type (a), and 6 each of type (b), (c) and (d).

Proposition 2.9
.

(Twenty one disks in the cubic surface) Any holomorphic disk in

X:=X_{\epsilon}
bounding the Lagrangian
L_{\epsilon}
with Maslov index two
is of the form shown in Figure
2.14
; there are twenty
one such disks and each disk has a multiplicity of one.

The cubic surface
(X_{0},\omega_{0})
has twenty one disks of Maslov index
two that bound the monotone Lagrangian
L_{0}\subset X_{0}
(see
(
2.6
)). The disk potential of
(X_{0},L_{0})
is

W(y_{1},y_{2})=y_{1}y_{2}+y_{1}/y_{2}^{2}+y_{2}/y_{1}^{2}+3(y_{1}+y_{1}/y_{2}+y_{2}+y_{2}/y_{1}+1/y_{1}+1/y_{2})

(2.13)

Remark 2.10
.

The disk potential of
L_{0}
in the cubic
surface was originally obtained by Pascaleff-Tonkonog

[
72
]
using mutations. This formula is equivalent to the
one in
[
72
, Table 1]
by the change of variables

z_{1}=y_{1}^{-1},z_{2}=y_{2}^{-1}

after which the formula becomes

W(z_{1},z_{2})=(1+z_{1}+z_{2})^{3}/z_{1}z_{2}-6.

The formula is also obtained in Galkin-Usnich
[
59
]
;
c.f.
[
7
, Table 5.1]
.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

As in the proof of the sphere count, we first count the number of
broken disks of Maslov index
2
in the broken manifold, and then
argue that the disk count is the same for the cubic surface. Let

\mathfrak{X}_{\epsilon}
be the broken manifold obtained by first deforming the
symplectic form on the cubic surface to produce a toric manifold

X^{\epsilon}
, and then by multiple cutting.

The homology classes of the disks of Maslov index two in
X

(equipped with a torus-invariant almost complex structure) must
consist of a single Blaschke product (i.e. a disk having exactly one
intersection with one of the toric divisors) and a collection of
short divisors, each of which have Chern number zero:

H_{2}(X_{\epsilon},L)\ni[u]=\begin{cases}\delta_{\operatorname{long}}\quad\text{or}\\
\delta_{\operatorname{short}}+\sum_{i}a_{i}[E_{i}],\end{cases}

(2.14)

where
\delta_{\operatorname{long}}
resp.
\delta_{\operatorname{short}}\in H_{2}(X,L)

is the class of a disk which is a single Blaschke product
intersecting a long resp. short divisor,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and any

E_{i}\in H_{2}(X)
is the homology class of a short divisor.

A semi-Fano toric surface has a well-defined disk potential in the
following sense: The standard torus-invariant complex structure

J_{\epsilon}
on
X_{\epsilon}
is not regular, but for any (domain-dependent)
almost complex structure
J_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
that is sufficiently close to

J_{\epsilon}
, the disk count is independent of
J_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
, see
Proposition
11.11
. Furthermore, the potential is
unchanged under neck-stretching if the broken manifold, with the
standard almost complex structure (componentwise torus-invariant)

\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}
, does not have disks of Maslov index zero, as explained
in Proposition
11.13
. This condition is indeed
satisfied in the multiple cut we consider on the cubic surface, as
explained in Proposition
11.15
. Therefore, for any
regular domain-dependent almost complex structure
J_{\epsilon}^{\prime}

resp.
\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
close enough to
J_{\epsilon}
resp.
\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}
, the
potential on
(X_{\epsilon},J_{\epsilon}^{\prime})
and
(\mathfrak{X}_{\epsilon},\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}^{\prime})
is the
same. In particular, the contribution to the potential from any disk
homology class
\beta\in H_{2}(X_{\epsilon},L)
is the same for

(X_{\epsilon},J_{\epsilon}^{\prime})
and
(\mathfrak{X}_{\epsilon},\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}^{\prime})
. Thus we may assume
that the homology class of any broken disk in
\mathfrak{X}_{\epsilon}
is as in
(
2.14
).

Next, we show that out of all the homology classes, only twenty one
classes, corresponding to
a_{i}=0,1
are represented by

\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
-holomorphic broken disks, with a single disk in each
class. Let
u
be a broken disk in
\mathfrak{X}_{\epsilon}
whose gluing has
relative homology class

[u_{{\operatorname{glue}}}]=[\delta_{E_{1}}]+\sum_{i}n_{i}[E_{i}]\in H_{2}(X_{\epsilon},L),

where
[\delta_{E_{1}}]
is the class of a Maslov index two disk in

(X_{\epsilon},L_{\epsilon})
that intersects the short divisor
E_{1}
(using
notation in Figure
2.14
). The other cases where

\delta_{E_{1}}
is replaced by
\delta_{E_{i}}
,
i=2,\dots,6
, can be
analyzed similarly. Now we need to show that the gluing of
u
is
either (b), (c) or (d) in Figure
2.14
. Since the
class
[\partial u]\in H_{1}(L_{\epsilon})
is preserved by neck
stretching, we conclude that the disk component of
u
is a Maslov
index two disk in
\overline{X}_{P_{0}}
that intersects the relative divisor

\overline{X}_{P_{01}}
. Indeed
\overline{X}_{P_{0}}
is a toric manifold; and no
other positive integral combination of outward normal vectors of
facets adds up to the outward normal
\nu(\overline{X}_{P_{01}})=(-1,0)
,
besides
(-1,0)
itself. The rest of the proof that
u
is of the
expected form is exactly same as Steps 2, 3, 4 in the proof of the
sphere count (Proposition
2.7
). Each of the
tropical graphs contributes
+1
to the curve
count: Using the standard spin
structure, isolated Blaschke disks have a positive orientation sign
(see
[
22
, p22]
), the maps
u_{v}
are pseudoholomorphic
with respect to an integrable almost complex structure
J_{v}^{b}
(see
(
2.3
)), matching conditions are cut out by diagonals on
the relative divisors, and each of the broken maps has positive
orientation in the moduli space.
The combinatorics involving marked points carries over
from the sphere case.

So far, we have proved that for any regular perturbation
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}

of the standard almost complex structure
J_{0}
on the toric manifold

X
, the potential of
CF(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}_{0})
has twenty one terms. It remains
to show that the potential is the same for the cubic surface

(X_{0},\omega_{0})
. We take an indirect route to construct the bijection
of disks in
X_{\epsilon}
and
X_{0}
, namely, we use a different multiple
cut
\mathcal{P}_{1}
(Figure
2.15
) than the one above, and show
that there is a bijection between the disks contributing to the
potential in the broken manifolds
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{0,\mathcal{P}_{1}}
.

Abbildung 2.15:
The cut
\mathcal{P}_{1}
.

Thus we need to prove
the three bijections of disks,
corresponding to the degenerations

X\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{0,\mathcal{P}_{1}}\longleftrightarrow X_{0}.

(2.15)

The first of these bijections between the set of disks in
X
and

\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
is a consequence of Proposition

11.13
. To prove the second bijection, denote by

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\mathfrak{J}}(\mathfrak{X})

the zero-dimensional component of the moduli space of broken disks
with symplectic area
\leq E_{0}
and
[\partial u]=\beta
with a
point constraint on the boundary, assuming that the broken disks are
defined with respect to a perturbation
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathfrak{J}
. Let

\{\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\epsilon]}
be a generic path of almost complex
structures with end-points
\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\epsilon}:=\mathfrak{J}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}

compatible with the monotone form
\omega_{0}
. Also, let

\{\mathfrak{p}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\epsilon]}
be a family of perturbations of

\{\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{t}\}_{t}
. Finally, the moduli space

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}}(\mathfrak{X}):=\cup_{t}\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{t}}(\mathfrak{X})

is one-dimensional whose end-points are

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{X})
and

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\epsilon}}(\mathfrak{X})
. Indeed,

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}}(\mathfrak{X})
there is no disk bubbling, because
each
t
, the torus-invariant divisors of
\overline{X}_{P-0}
are

\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{t}
-holomorphic; and for any broken map in the moduli space,
the disk component has Maslov index two and intersects the
divisor
\overline{X}_{P_{01}}
. Therefore, the boundary strata of

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}}(\mathfrak{X})
consist only of

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{0}}(\mathfrak{X})
and

\mathcal{M}^{<E_{0}}_{\beta,\tilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\epsilon}}(\mathfrak{X})
. It follows that these last two
moduli spaces are bijective.
This proves the second bijection in (
2.15
). The last
bijection in (
2.15
) follows from the fact that
neck-stretching does not alter the potential of a monotone
symplectic manifold, since there are no disks of Maslov index zero
in the family of neck-stretched manifolds.
This finishes the proof of the Proposition.
∎

2.3
Counting disks in flag varieties

In this Section, we use our results to compute the disk potential of
partial flag varieties. The Gelfand-Cetlin system
[
42
]
is a
collection of functions on a partial flag variety that forms a
completely integrable system. The image of these functions is the

Gelfand-Cetlin
polytope
\Delta
, and the toric variety
corresponding to this polytope is called the
Gelfand-Cetlin
toric
variety. In the complement of faces of
\Delta
of codimension

\geq 2
, the flag variety and Gelfand-Cetlin toric variety are
symplectomorphic. We use this observation to compute the disk
potential of the monotone Lagrangian torus in Theorem

2.18
; we use a multiple cut consisting of a collection of
intersecting single cuts (Figure
2.19
) each of which is
parallel to, and very close to a facet of the polytope
\Delta
. The
original approach of computing disk potentials in flag varieties by
Nishinou-Nohara-Ueda
[
61
]
and Nohara-Ueda
[
62
]

uses small resolutions.

We start by defining partial flag varieties.
Given a tuple
\underline{n}:=(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})
with
\sum_{i}n_{i}=n
, a

flag variety

\operatorname{Fl}(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})
is the set of flags

\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}):=\operatorname{Fl}(n_{1},\dots,n_{r}):=\{\{0\}\subset V_{n_{1}}\subset V_{n_{1}+n_{2}}\subset\ldots V_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{r}}=\mathbb{C}^{n}\\
:\dim(V_{i})=i\kern 5.0pt\forall i\}.

(2.16)

A flag variety may be identified with a coadjoint orbit of the action
of
U(n)
on
u(n)^{\vee}
. In particular,

\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathfrak{u}(n)^{\vee}

is the
coadjoint orbit of an element
\Lambda
defined as

\Lambda:=\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{-1}(\underbrace{\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{1}}_{n_{1}\text{ times}},\underbrace{\Lambda_{2},\dots,\Lambda_{2}}_{n_{2}\text{ times}},\dots,\underbrace{\Lambda_{r},\dots,\Lambda_{r}}_{n_{r}\text{ times}}))\in\mathfrak{u}(n)^{\vee},

(2.17)

where

\Lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\Lambda_{1}>\dots>\Lambda_{r},

and
\mathfrak{u}(n)
is identified to its dual via the
\operatorname{Ad}_{U(n)}
-invariant
inner product

(\cdot,\cdot):\mathfrak{u}(n)\times\mathfrak{u}(n)\to\mathbb{R},\quad(A,B)\mapsto\operatorname{trace}(A^{*}B).

Indeed, an element of (
2.16
) corresponds to an orthogonal decomposition

u(n)^{\vee}=W_{n_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus W_{n_{r}}

into subspaces
W_{n_{i}}
of dimension
n_{i}
for all
i=1,\dots,r
; and
such a decomposition gives a unique element in the coadjoint orbit of (
2.17
) for which the
\sqrt{-1}\Lambda_{i}
-eigenspace is
W_{n_{i}}
for each
i
.

The symplectic form
\omega_{\Lambda}
on
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
is given by the Kostant-Kirillov form: A tangent vector at a point

x\in\mathfrak{u}(n)
is of the form
\operatorname{ad}_{\xi}(x)
for some
\xi\in\mathfrak{u}(n)
and the symplectic form at
x
is given by
(\omega_{\Lambda})_{x}(\operatorname{ad}_{\xi_{1}}(x),\operatorname{ad}_{\xi_{2}}(x)):=\langle{x,[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]}\rangle
.

We recall the construction of the
Gelfand-Cetlin system on a flag
variety from Guillemin-Sternberg
[
42
]
. The
Gelfand-Cetlin system
is a
collection of functions that forms a completely integrable system on an
open set of
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
. For any
m=1,\ldots,n
, let

\pi_{m}:\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})\to\mathfrak{u}(m)^{\vee}

(2.18)

be a map sending a matrix
A
to its
upper-left
m\times m

submatrix
\pi_{m}(A)
.
Let

\Phi:\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})\to\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}:=(\sqrt{-1}\mathbb{R})^{n(n-1)/2}

(2.19)

be a collection of maps
\Phi=\{\sqrt{-1}\Phi_{i,k}\}_{1\leq k\leq i\leq n}
where for any
m\leq n

and
A\in\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
,

\Phi_{m,1}(A)\geq\Phi_{m,2}(A)\geq\ldots\geq\Phi_{m,m}(A),

and
\sqrt{-1}\Phi_{m,k}
,
1\leq k\leq m
are the eigenvalues of
\pi_{m}(A)
.

For any
i
, the eigenvalue functions
\Phi_{i,j}
are smooth
functions whenever they are distinct and are continuous everywhere.
By the min-max theorem for Hermitian matrices
[
85
, Theorem
8.10]
, the eigenvalues satisfy the interlacing inequalities

\Phi_{i+1,k}\leq\Phi_{i,k}\leq\Phi_{i,k+1},\quad\forall i,k<n

and they fit into the following diagram:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{3}

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n-1}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{n-1,1}$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{n-1,2}$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{n-1,n-1}$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{n-2,1}$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{n-2,n-2}$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\cdots$}}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\cdots$}}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{1,1}$}

We give a proof of the min-max theorem for completeness.

Lemma 2.11
.

(Interlacing inequality)
Let
A
be an
(n+1)\times(n+1)
Hermitian matrix with
eigenvalues

a_{1}\geq\dots\geq a_{n+1}
.
Suppose the upper left
n\times n
submatrix
\pi_{n}(A)

has eigenvalues
b_{1}\geq\dots\geq b_{n}
. Then,

a_{1}\geq b_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\dots\geq a_{n-1}\geq b_{n}\geq a_{n+1}.

Beweis.

It is enough to prove the result assuming that
b_{1}>\dots>b_{n}
,
since the general result follows by continuity. Since
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
are the eigenvalues of
\pi_{n}(A)
, the
\operatorname{Ad}_{U(n)}
-orbit of
A
contains a matrix

A^{\prime}:=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}&&0&\bar{z}_{1}\\
&\ddots&&\vdots\\
0&&b_{n}&\bar{z}_{n}\\
z_{1}&\ldots&z_{n}&r_{n+1},\end{pmatrix}.

where
z_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
r_{n+1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

\det(A-t\operatorname{Id})=\det(A^{\prime}-t\operatorname{Id})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(b_{i}-t)f(t),\quad f(t)=\left(r_{n+1}-t-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|z_{j}|^{2}}{b_{j}-t}\right).

The function

f(t)
is decreasing in
t
and discontinuous at

b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
. Since the real numbers

b_{j}
’s are distinct, the function
f(t)
has exactly
one root each in the intervals
(-\infty,b_{n})
,
(b_{i+1},b_{i})
,

i=1,\dots,n-1
,
(b_{1},\infty)
, and the roots are
a_{n+1},\dots,a_{1}
.
∎

For a partial flag manifold, certain values in the Gelfand-Cetlin
system are fixed by the interlacing inequalities. In particular, for
k\leq i\leq n-1
,

\Phi_{k}=\Phi_{n+k-i}=\Lambda_{j}\implies\Phi_{i,k}=\Lambda_{j}.

The Gelfand-Cetlin system thus consists of exactly

N:=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}n_{i}n_{j}

variables, which is half the dimension of
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
. See Figure

2.16
. We denote the set of variables in the
Gelfand-Cetlin system of the partial flag variety
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
by

\operatorname{Free}(\underline{n}):=\{(i,j):\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,j}$}\text{ is not a
constant on }\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})\}.

The image
\Phi(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}))
is contained in a polytope

\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}\subset(\sqrt{-1}\mathbb{R})^{N}

cut out by the Gelfand-Cetlin inequalities involving free variables

\{\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,j}$}:(i,j)\in\operatorname{Free}(\underline{n})\}
, which we call the
Gelfand-Cetlin polytope of
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
.

Abbildung 2.16:
For a partial flag, the variables of the Gelfand-Cetlin system lie in the shaded region. Each of the unshaded triangles is a block of non-free variables. In any block, all the entries are equal to
\Lambda_{i}
for some
i
.

Remark 2.12
.

In the Gelfand-Cetlin system corresponding to the flag variety

\operatorname{Fl}(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})
, the non-free variables can be partitioned into

r
disjoint sets

\{(i,k):1\leq k\leq i\leq n-1\}\backslash\operatorname{Free}(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}))=N_{1}\cup\dots N_{r}

where each
N_{j}
consists of the collection of indices for maps
\Phi_{i,k}
which are fixed to be equal to
\Lambda_{j}
by the interlacing inequalities. We call
N_{j}
a
block
of non-free variables. A block
N_{j}
is non-empty if
n_{j}>1
. Each non-empty block is triangular and has a
lowest
element whose location in the Gelfand-Cetlin diagram is the lowest. The lowest element in the block
N_{j}
is

\Phi_{\mathfrak{i}_{j},\mathfrak{k}_{j}},\quad\text{ where }(\mathfrak{i}_{j},\mathfrak{k}_{j})=\left(n-n_{j}+1,\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}n_{\ell}+1\right).

(2.21)

In Figure
2.16
the lowest position in each block is in red.

The face structure of the Gelfand-Cetlin polytope can be read off from
ladder diagrams
[
3
]
. For our purposes, we explicitly
describe the faces of codimension zero and one.
Let
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
be the Gelfand-Cetlin polytope of the flag
variety
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
.

(a)

(Interior) A point
\Phi\in\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
is an
interior point exactly if every Gelfand-Cetlin inequality involving at
least one free variable
\Phi_{i,j}
is strict. We denote the inverse image of the interior by

F^{(0)}:=\Phi^{-1}(\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}^{\circ})\subset\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}).

(b)

(Facet) A subset of the polytope
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}

\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{ldiag.pdf}}}:=\{\Phi_{i,k}=\Phi_{i+1,k}\}\quad\text{resp.}\quad\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{rdiag.pdf}}}:=\{\Phi_{i,k}=\Phi_{i+1,k+1}\}

(2.22)

is a facet of
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
if either both variables in the
equality are free or the non-free variable is the lowest variable in
a block of non-free variables (as defined in Remark

2.12
). We remark that if the non-free variable in
(
2.22
) were not the lowest in the block, then the
codimension of the subset would be more than
1
, because the
interlacing inequalities would fix the values of some free variables
not occurring in (
2.22
). We denote the set of indices of facets by

\operatorname{Facets}(\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})})\subset\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}\times\{{\includegraphics[width]{ldiag.pdf}},{\includegraphics[width]{rdiag.pdf}}\}.

A point
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{ldiag.pdf}}}
resp.
\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{rdiag.pdf}}}
is in the interior of the facet if all the other Gelfand-Cetlin inequalities are strict. The set of points in
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
that map to the interiors of facets is denoted by

F^{(1)}:=\cup_{I\in\operatorname{Facets}(\Delta(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})))}\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{F}_{I}).

(c)

(Regular points)
We denote by

F^{\operatorname{reg}}\subset\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})

(2.23)

the set of points
x
for which for every
m<n
the set of
eigenvalues of
\pi_{m}(x)
has the maximum cardinality. In other words,

x\in F^{\operatorname{reg}}
exactly when the following holds: For all positive
integers
i\leq n-1
,
k<i
,

\Phi_{i,k}(x)=\Phi_{i,k+1}(x)\Leftrightarrow(i,k),(i,k+1)\notin\operatorname{Free}(\underline{n}).

The Gelfand-Cetlin map is smooth on
F^{{\operatorname{reg}}}
(Proposition
2.16
).

Remark 2.13
.

(Non-simplicial points of the Gelfand-Cetlin polytope)
The Gelfand-Cetlin polytope contains non-simplicial points. A face
\mathcal{F}
of a polytope
\Delta
is
non-simplicial
if it is the intersection of
n_{\mathcal{F}}
facets where
n_{\mathcal{F}}>\operatorname{codim}(\mathcal{F})
.
A point
A\in\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
is mapped to a non-simplicial point the polytope
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
if it satisfies a loop of equalities (see
[
61
, Example 3.8]
),

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i+1,k+1}$}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k+1}$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i-1,k}$}

and each inequality in the loop corresponds to a facet.
Indeed, the loop of equalities in (
2.24
) gives a subset

S
of codimension three, whereas
S
is the intersection of four
facets corresponding to each of the equalities in the loop.
Observe that
S
is the intersection of four faces of
codimension two, each given by a pair of equalities as in the diagram of inequalities below:

\begin{aligned} &&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i+1,k+1}$}&&\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&\\
\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}&&&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k+1}$}\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}&\\
&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i-1,k}$}&&\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} &&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i+1,k+1}$}&&\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}&\\
\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}&&&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k+1}$}\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&\\
&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i-1,k}$}&&\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} &&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i+1,k+1}$}&&\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&\\
\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}&&&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k+1}$}\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&\\
&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i-1,k}$}&&\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} &&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i+1,k+1}$}&&\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}&\\
\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}&&&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k+1}$}\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}&\\
&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i-1,k}$}&&\end{aligned}

Example 2.14
.

The full flag
F^{(3)}=\operatorname{Fl}(1,1,1)
resp. the Grassmannian

\operatorname{Gr}(2,4)=\operatorname{Fl}(2,2)
has a singular point resp. a singular line given
by a loop of equalities (see Figure
2.17
)

\begin{aligned} &&\Lambda_{2}&&\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&\\
\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{2,1}$}&&&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{2,2}$}\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&\\
&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{1,1}$}&&\end{aligned}\quad\text{resp.}\quad\begin{aligned} &&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{3,2}$}&&\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&\\
\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{2,1}$}&&&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{2,2}$}\\
&\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$=$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$=$}&\\
&&\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{1,1}$}&&\end{aligned}.

(2.25)

Abbildung 2.17:
There is a non-simplicial point in
\Phi(\operatorname{Fl}(1,1,1))

corresponding to a loop of equalities.

Remark 2.15
.

(Gelfand-Cetlin toric variety)
For any tuple
\underline{n}
, the toric variety
M_{\underline{n}}
corresponding
to the polytope
\Phi(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}))
, called the
Gelfand-Cetlin
toric variety
, is a toric degeneration of the flag manifold

\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})

[
8
]
. The Gelfand-Cetlin toric variety
has conifold singularities of codimension three (see Batyrev et
al.
[
8
]
) corresponding to the non-simplicial points
in the polytope
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
.
The fibers of
\Phi|\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
over non-simplicial points has larger dimension
than the corresponding fibers in the toric variety. For example, the inverse image of the
singular point in
\Phi(\operatorname{Fl}(1,1,1))
in Figure
2.17
is a
Lagrangian
S^{3}

[
61
, Example 3.8]
. A detailed
description of the singular fibers in partial flag varieties, and
their comparison with the Gelfand-Cetlin toric variety is carried
out in
[
24
]
.

The following result shows that on the regular locus

F^{\operatorname{reg}}\subset\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
(defined in (
2.23
)), the
Gelfand-Cetlin system is smooth and is a moment map for a torus
action. As a symplectic toric manifold, this set is isomorphic to the
corresponding subset in the Gelfand-Cetlin toric variety. The regular
locus
F^{\operatorname{reg}}
includes
F^{(0)}\cup F^{(1)}
, that is, the inverse
image of the interior and the facets of the Gelfand-Cetlin polytope.

Proposition 2.16
.

(
[
42
]
)

Let
\Phi:\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
be the Gelfand-Cetlin system on the
flag variety.

(a)

The restriction
\Phi|F^{\operatorname{reg}}
is smooth and is a moment map for the action of the torus
\mathbb{T}^{N}
, where
F^{\operatorname{reg}}\subset\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
is defined in (
2.23
).

(b)

The map
\Phi
surjects onto the polytope

\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
.

(c)

As a symplectic toric manifold,

F^{\operatorname{reg}}\subset\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
is isomorphic to the corresponding
subset in the Gelfand-Cetlin toric variety. Furthermore, the
inverse image
F^{(0)}\cup F^{(1)}
of the interior and the
facets of
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
is contained in
F^{\operatorname{reg}}
.

(d)

Consequently, for any point
\lambda
in the interior of

\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
,
\Phi^{-1}(\lambda)
is a Lagrangian torus.

Beweis.

The map
\Phi
is smooth on the set of regular points, namely

F^{\operatorname{reg}}
. Indeed, the map
A\mapsto\lambda_{i}(A)
that sends a
symmetric
m\times m
matrix to its
i
-th eigenvalue in
non-increasing order is smooth at a point if

\lambda_{i}(A)\neq\lambda_{i+1}(A)
,
\lambda_{i-1}(A)
.
Any pair of functions in the Gelfand-Cetlin system Poisson commute :

\{\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,j}$},\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{k,l}$}\}=0

by
[
42
, Section 5]
,
[
61
, Lemma 3.3]
. So, the
system
\{\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}\}_{i,k}
generates a Hamiltonian
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-action
on
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
.
Any non-constant Hamiltonian trajectory generated by
\Phi_{i,k}
has
a period of
2\pi
by
[
42
, Lemma 3.4]
. Consequently, the
system
\{\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{i,k}$}\}_{i,k}
generates an action of the torus

(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{N}
on
F^{\operatorname{reg}}
.

The map
\Phi
surjects onto
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
by
[
42
, Section
5]
. The proof is by an inductive application of Lemma

2.17
, which constructs a matrix
A\in\mathfrak{u}(n)
for a
prescribed collection of eigenvalues
\{\Phi_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
. The
inductive construction gives a map
\{\Phi_{i,j}\}_{i,j}\mapsto A

that is smooth strata-wise. Since this map is a right inverse of

\Phi
, we have shown that
{\operatorname{d}}\Phi
has the maximum rank on every
stratum
S
of
F^{\operatorname{reg}}
,
2
2

2

The partial flag manifold

\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
is stratified on the basis of which of the
Gelfand-Cetlin inequalities are equalities.
that is,

\operatorname{rank}({\operatorname{d}}\Phi)={\tfrac{1}{2}}\dim(S)
. Therefore, on each stratum

S\subset F^{\operatorname{reg}}
,
\{\Phi_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
is a complete integrable
system; proving the first statement in (
c
). The
definition of
F^{\operatorname{reg}}
implies that

F^{(0)}\cup F^{(1)}\subset F^{\operatorname{reg}}
. By the Arnol’d-Liouville
theorem (proved in, for example, Duistermaat
[
30
]
),

\Phi|F^{(0)}
is a Lagrangian torus fibration. This finishes the
proof. As an aside, we remark that the rank of
{\operatorname{d}}\Phi
is smaller
than half the dimension of the stratum, in case of a stratum mapping
to a non-simplicial point of
\Delta_{\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})}
; see Example

2.14
.
∎

Lemma 2.17
.

Let

\Delta:=\{(a,b)=((a_{1},\dots,a_{n}),(b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1}))\in\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}:a_{1}\geq b_{1}\geq\dots\geq b_{n-1}\geq a_{n}\}.

For any
(a,b)\in\Delta
, there is a unique tuple

r=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n+1})\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n}\times\mathbb{R}

such that any matrix
A\in\sqrt{-1}\mathfrak{u}(n)
with eigenvalues

(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
, and for whom
\pi_{n-1}(A)
(defined in (
2.18
))
is the diagonal matrix with entries
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
is of the form

A:=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}&&0&\bar{z}_{1}\\
&\ddots&&\vdots\\
0&&b_{n}&\bar{z}_{n}\\
z_{1}&\ldots&z_{n}&r_{k+1}\end{pmatrix}

with
|z_{i}|^{2}=r_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
.

(a)

The map

r:\Delta\to(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R},\quad(a,b)\mapsto(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})

is continuous.

(b)

For any
i
,
r_{i}=0
exactly when either
b_{i}=a_{i}
or

b_{i}=a_{i+1}
.

(c)

For any subset
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,2n\}
, let

\Delta_{I}\subset\Delta
be the set of tuples

(a_{1},b_{1},\dots,b_{n-1},a_{n})
with equalities at the positions in

I
, and strict inequalities everywhere else. Then,
r|\Delta_{I}

is smooth.

Beweis.

We first consider the open stratum of
\Delta
, that is,

I=\emptyset
and

a_{1}>b_{1}>a_{2}>\dots>a_{n-1}>b_{n-1}>a_{n}.

Observe that

\det(A-t\operatorname{Id})=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(b_{i}-t)\right)\left(r_{n}-t-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{|z_{j}|^{2}}{b_{j}-t}\right).

(2.26)

We set

r_{n}:=\sum_{i}a_{i}-\sum_{i}b_{i},

and solve the system of equations

\det(A-a_{i}\operatorname{Id})=0\quad\text{for}\kern 5.0pti=1,\dots,n-1

(2.27)

for
z_{1},\dots,z_{n-1}
. Substituting
|z_{i}|^{2}=r_{i}
, the system
(
2.26
) is linear in
r_{1},\dots,r_{n-1}
, and the
coefficient matrix for the variables
r_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n-1
is

\left(\prod_{1\leq k\leq n:k\neq j}(b_{k}-a_{i})\right)_{i,j}.

(2.28)

The determinant of (
2.28
)
is

\det(\eqref{eq:cmat})=\pm\prod_{i<j}(a_{i}-a_{j})\prod_{i<j}(b_{i}-b_{j})\neq 0.

Therefore,
we can uniquely solve (
2.26
) for
(r_{1},\dots,r_{n-1})
.

It remains to show that
r_{1},\dots,r_{n-1}
are non-negative. Firstly,
we observe that

r^{-1}(\partial((\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}))\subset\partial\Delta_{b}
. Indeed, in the matrix
A
, if
z_{i}=0
then
b_{i}
is an
eigenvalue, and so
b_{i}
is equal to either
a_{i}
or

a_{i+1}
. Secondly, by the interlacing property the image
r(\Delta_{b}^{\circ})
of the interior

\Delta_{b}^{\circ}
of
\Delta_{b}
intersects the interior of

(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. We may then conclude that

r(\Delta_{b}^{\circ})
is in fact contained in
(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Finally, we define the map
r
on lower dimensional strata of

\Delta
. If
b_{i}=a_{i}
resp.
b_{i}=a_{i+1}
, then we set
r_{i}=0
.
Define
\hat{a}:=(a_{1},\dots\hat{a}_{i},\dots,a_{n})
resp.

(a_{1},\dots\hat{a}_{i+1},\dots,a_{n})
, and

\hat{b}:=(b_{1},\dots\hat{b}_{i},\dots,b_{n})
to be the tuples deleted
in each. The above construction applied to
(\hat{a},\hat{b})

produces
\hat{r}:=(r_{1},\dots,\hat{r}_{i},\dots,r_{n+1})
. The map

(\hat{a},\hat{b})\mapsto\hat{r}
is smooth, which shows that the
map
(a,b)\mapsto r
is smooth strata-wise.
∎

In the rest of the Section, we prove the following result, which was originally proved by Nishinou-Nohara-Ueda
[
61
]
.

Theorem 2.18
.

Let
X:=\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
be a flag variety. There is a symplectic form on

X
for which the Gelfand-Cetlin system of
X
has a unique monotone
Lagrangian fiber
L_{0}
. The
A_{\infty}
algebra
CF(L_{0})
is
weakly unobstructed and the naive potential of
CF(L_{0})
consists of one term
for every facet of the Gelfand-Cetlin polytope of
X
.

As part of the proof of Theorem
2.18
, we compute the disk
potential (defined in (
1.10
)) of
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n})
as

W(y_{i,k})_{i,k}=\left(\sum_{k\leq i:(i+1,k)\in\operatorname{Free}(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}))}\frac{y_{i+1,k}}{y_{i,k}}+\sum_{k\leq i:(i+1,k+1)\in\operatorname{Free}(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}))}\frac{y_{i,k}}{y_{i+1,k+1}}\right.\\
\left.+\sum_{1\leq j\leq r-1}y_{\mathfrak{i}_{j},\mathfrak{k}_{j}}+\sum_{2\leq j\leq r}\frac{1}{y_{\mathfrak{i}_{j},\mathfrak{k}_{j}}}\right)q.

(2.29)

Here the index
(\mathfrak{i}_{j},\mathfrak{k}_{j})
is the position of the lowest element in
the
j
-th block, see (
2.21
). In the above expression
(
2.29
) for the potential, each term corresponds to a facet
(
2.22
) of the Gelfand-Cetlin polytope – the first
resp. second summation corresponds to facets
\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{ldiag.pdf}}}

resp.
\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{rdiag.pdf}}}
where both variables in the equality defining
the facet are free, the third resp. fourth summation corresponds to
facets
\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{ldiag.pdf}}}
resp.
\mathcal{F}_{i,k,{\includegraphics[width]{rdiag.pdf}}}
where one of the
variables in the defining equality is non-free and is the lowest
element of the
j
-th block. The exponent of the Novikov formal
variable
q
is
1
, because the symplectic form (given by a choice of

(\Lambda_{i})_{i}
) and Lagrangian torus
L_{0}
are chosen so that the pair

(\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{n}),L_{0})
is monotone and the area of any disk of Maslov
index
2
is
1
. We will show for every facet, there is a single
holomorphic disk of Maslov index two intersecting the corresponding
divisor in
\operatorname{Fl}(\underline{(}n))
.

Example 2.19
.

The Gelfand-Cetlin inequalities of the Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(2,4)=\operatorname{Fl}(2,2)
are given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

(2.30)

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{3,2}$}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{315.0}{$\geq$}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\geq$}}

\displaystyle\hbox to16.49995pt{$\Phi_{2,1}$}
