continuous.

Remark 3.45
.

By Definition
3.42
, the
neck-stretched manifold
X^{\nu}
is equipped with

(a)

for each
P
, a
P
-cylindrical structure on the subset

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee}\subset X^{\nu}
; that is,
there is a projection

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee}\to\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})/T_{P}

(3.32)

whose fibers are
T_{P}\times\nu P^{\vee}\subset T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
; and

(b)

a symplectic form on the base manifold

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})/T_{P}
that is a
T_{P}
-reduction of the
symplectic form
\omega_{X}
on
X
.

Abbildung 3.10:
Stretching

Remark 3.46
.

(A warning about Figure
3.10
) Figure
3.10
is a
schematic representation of neck-stretched manifolds. We recall
symplectic broken manifolds are represented by the images of their
tropical moment map, for example in Figure
3.5
. In contrast, we do not
have a moment map on neck-stretched manifolds. A larger polytope is
just used to indicate a larger cylinder.

Remark 3.47
.

The neck-stretched manifold
X^{\nu}
is diffeomorphic to
X
, but the
diffeomorphism is not canonical. As a result, there is no canonical
symplectic form on
X^{\nu}
.

Example 3.48
.

(Neck-stretched manifolds for a single cut) We describe
neck-stretched manifolds in case of a single cut. Let
(X,\Phi,c)

be a tropical Hamiltonian action with a single cut along
\Phi^{-1}(c)
, and
whose polytopes are as in Example
3.20
. The
symplectic cylindrical structure consists of an
S^{1}
-equivariant
symplectomorphism defined on a neighborhood

\Phi^{-1}([c-\epsilon,c+\epsilon])\xrightarrow{\phi_{P_{0}}}(\Phi^{-1}(c)\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon],\omega_{/\kern-2.1097pt/}+{\operatorname{d}}(t\alpha)),

(3.33)

where
\omega_{/\kern-2.1097pt/}
is the reduced symplectic form on the quotient

\Phi^{-1}(c)/S^{1}
,
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
is the coordinate function,
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\Phi^{-1}(c))
is a connection one-form on the

S^{1}
-bundle
\Phi^{-1}(c)\to\Phi^{-1}(c)/S^{1}
. The other symplectic
cylindrical structure maps
\phi_{P_{-}}
,
\phi_{P_{+}}
are trivial
since
P_{+}
,
P_{-}
are top-dimensional. The neck-stretched manifold
is then

X^{\nu}:=\{\Phi\leq c-\epsilon\}\cup_{\Phi^{-1}(c)}\times[-\epsilon\nu,\epsilon\nu])\cup_{\Phi^{-1}(c)}\{\Phi\geq c+\epsilon\},

(3.34)

where the attachment maps are given by identifying

\{\Phi=c\pm\epsilon\}
to
\{\Phi=c\}
via the symplectic cylindrical
structure
\phi_{P_{\pm}}
, and in the middle piece,
\{\Phi=c\}
is
identified to the ends
\{\Phi=\pm\epsilon\nu\}
.

2
2

2

The interval
[-\epsilon\nu,\epsilon\nu]
in (
3.34
)
is chosen to be consistent with definitions, there is no canonical
choice of end-points, as long as the length of the segment is

\nu
times the neck length in
X
.

Definition 3.49
.

(Cylindrical almost complex structures on neck-stretched manifolds)

Let
(X,\mathcal{P})
be a tropical Hamiltonian action and let

\{X^{\nu}\}_{\nu}
be a sequence of neck-stretched manifolds. Recall
from (
3.30
) that a neck-stretched manifold has a
decomposition

X^{\nu}=\left(\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee}\right)/\sim,

where
\sim
identifies boundaries of the different components.

(a)

An almost complex structure
J^{\nu}
on the neck-stretched
manifold
X^{\nu}
is
cylindrical
if
J^{\nu}
is

P
-cylindrical in the sense of Definition
3.36
in the
subset

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee,\circ}\subset X^{\nu}.

The space of cylindrical almost complex structures on
X^{\nu}
is
denoted by

\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(X^{\nu}).

We say that
(X^{\nu},J^{\nu})

is a
family of neck-stretched almost complex structures

if
there are
\nu
-independent cylindrical almost complex structures

J_{P}
on
\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
for all
P
,
such that on the
P
-cylindrical subset

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee,\circ}
of
X^{\nu}
,

J^{\nu}
is the restriction of
J_{P}
.

(b)

(Local tamedness and compatibility)
Let
(X^{\nu},J^{\nu})
be a
family of neck-stretched almost complex structures. We say that
each element
J^{\nu}
is
locally tamed
resp.
locally
compatible
if
J^{1}
is
\omega_{X}
-tamed
resp.
\omega_{X}
-compatible. (The definition is based on the
observation that the neck-stretched manifold
X^{1}
has a canonical
diffeomorphism to
(X,\omega_{X})
, and this is not so for
X^{\nu}
,

\nu\neq 1
.)

(c)

(Local strong tamedness)
A cylindrical locally tamed almost
complex structure
J
on
X^{\nu}
is
locally strongly tamed

if for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
the connection one-form
\alpha_{P,J}

underlying the
P
-cylindrical almost complex structure on

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee,\circ}
in Definition

3.36
is equal to
the connection one-form underlying the symplectic cylindrical
structure in Definition

3.37
.

Remark 3.50
.

The following are some remarks on cylindrical almost complex structures on neck-stretched manifolds.

(a)

The cylindrical almost complex structures defined above in Definition
3.49

is sometimes referred to as
X
-cylindrical

to emphasize its dependence on the
X
-inner product (
3.10
) on
\{\mathfrak{t}_{P}\}_{P}
. Recall that the dependence on the inner product arises because of the identification (
3.20
) between the symplectic cylinder
\Phi^{-1}(P)\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
and the almost complex cylinder
\Phi^{-1}(P)\times\sqrt{-1}\mathfrak{t}_{P}

(b)

The definition of local (strong) tamedness depends not just on the symplectic form
\omega_{X}
,
but also on the symplectic cylindrical structure in Definition
3.37
.

(c)

In the space of cylindrical almost complex structures, the
cylindrical coordinate maps, taking values in

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee}
, are held fixed, but the
connection one-forms
\alpha_{P,J}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
underlying a
cylindrical almost complex structure
J
are allowed to vary.

(d)

We give an alternate definition of local
tamedness. A cylindrical almost complex structure
J^{\nu}
is

locally tamed
on
X^{\nu}
if for each
P
, the base almost complex
structure (see Definition
3.36
) on
X_{P}
induced by

J^{\nu}
on the
P
-cylinder
\Phi^{-1}(P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\nu P^{\vee}

is tamed by

\omega_{X_{P}}+\langle{{\operatorname{d}}\alpha_{P},c}\rangle
for all

c\in P^{\vee}-c_{P^{\vee}}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}
. Here

c_{P^{\vee}}:=\pi_{P^{\vee}}(P)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
is a constant and

\alpha_{P}
is the
T_{P}
-connection one-form underlying the
symplectic cylindrical structure in Definition
3.37
.
Note that the definition of local tamedness does not involve
\nu
, since
the “same” almost complex structure can be defined for any
\nu
.

The following Lemma is a quantitative version of the statement that
local tamedness is a
C^{0}
-open property in the space of cylindrical
almost complex structures. We use the alternate definition of local
tamedness from Remark
3.50
(
d
).

Lemma 3.51
.

Let
J^{0}_{P}
be an almost complex structure on
\Phi^{-1}(P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P}
that
is tamed by the form
\omega_{X_{P}}+\langle{{\operatorname{d}}\alpha_{P},\tau}\rangle
for all

\tau\in P^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}
. Then there exist constants

\epsilon
,
c
such that if
J_{P}\in\mathcal{J}(\Phi^{-1}(P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P})
is such that

\lVert J_{P}-J_{P}^{0}\rVert_{C^{0}}<\epsilon
then

c^{-1}\omega_{X_{P}}(v,J_{P}v)\leq(\omega_{X_{P}}+\langle{{\operatorname{d}}\alpha_{P},c}\rangle)(v,J_{P}v)\leq c\omega_{X_{P}}(v,J_{P}v)\\
\forall v\in T(\Phi^{-1}(P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P}).

(3.35)

Beweis.

By the compactness of the spaces
P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}/T_{P}
and
P^{\vee}
, and the
tamedness of
J_{P}^{0}
for
\omega_{X_{P}}+\langle{{\operatorname{d}}\alpha_{P},\tau}\rangle
for all

\tau\in P^{\vee}
, there are constants
c_{0},c_{1}>0
such that

c_{0}|v|^{2}\leq(\omega_{X_{P}}+\langle{{\operatorname{d}}\alpha_{P},\tau}\rangle)(v,J_{P}^{0}v)\leq c_{1}|v|^{2}

for all
v\in T(\Phi^{-1}(P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P}
,
\tau\in P^{\vee}
.
For any
\epsilon>0
and
J_{P}\in B_{\epsilon}(J^{0}_{P})
, there are constants
c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime}>0
such that

(c_{0}-c_{0}^{\prime}\epsilon)|v|^{2}\leq(\omega_{X_{P}}+\langle{{\operatorname{d}}\alpha_{P},\tau}\rangle)(v,J_{P}^{0}v)\leq(c_{1}+c_{1}^{\prime}\epsilon)|v|^{2}.

The Lemma follows by
choosing
\epsilon>0
such that
c_{0}-c_{0}^{\prime}\epsilon>0
and
c:=\frac{c_{1}+c_{1}^{\prime}\epsilon}{c_{0}-c_{0}^{\prime}\epsilon}
.
∎

3.5
Broken manifold as a degenerate limit

In this Section, we define cut spaces and broken manifolds as almost
complex manifolds with cylindrical ends. For any
P
, the cut space

X_{P}
is defined as the direct limit of the
P
-cylindrical subsets of
the neck-stretched manifolds quotiented by the torus
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
. The
broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is a
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
-fibration over
X_{P}
. For any

P
, the almost complex cut space
X_{P}
resp. broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{P}

is diffeomorphic to the corresponding symplectic object
X_{P}^{\omega}

resp.
\mathfrak{X}_{P}^{\omega}
, but there is no canonical diffeomorphism. See Lemma

3.54
and Remark
3.57
for related
discussion.

3.5.1
Defining almost complex broken manifolds

The broken manifold is the degenerate limit of neck-stretched almost
complex manifolds as we now explain. For a polytope
P
and

\nu\geq 1
, let

X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}:=\left(\bigsqcup_{Q\in\mathcal{P}:Q\subseteq P}(\Phi^{-1}(Q^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu Q^{\vee})/\sim\right)\subset X^{\nu}

(3.36)

be the subset of
X^{\nu}
that has a
P
-cylindrical structure. Thus

X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
has a free partial
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
-action (see
(
3.21
)). (Here the equivalence relation
\sim
is the same
as the one in the definition
3.30
of
X^{\nu}
.) Let

X^{\nu}_{P}:=X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}/T_{P,\mathbb{C}}

(3.37)

be the quotient of
X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
under the partial

T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
-action. Consequently, there are projections

X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{P}^{\prime}}Z^{\nu}_{P}\xrightarrow{\pi_{P}^{\prime\prime}}X^{\nu}_{P},

(3.38)

where
Z^{\nu}_{P}\xrightarrow{\pi_{P}^{\prime\prime}}X^{\nu}_{P}
is a
T_{P}
-bundle, and the fibers of
\pi_{P}^{\prime}
are
P^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
.

Lemma 3.52
.

Let
(X^{\nu},J^{\nu})
be a family of neck-stretched almost complex structures (as in Definition
3.49
). For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
\nu_{0}<\nu_{1}
there is a natural embedding

i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:(X^{\nu_{0}}_{P},J^{\nu_{0}})\to(X^{\nu_{1}}_{P},J^{\nu_{1}}),\quad i_{Z_{P},\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:Z^{\nu_{0}}_{P}\to Z^{\nu_{1}}_{P},

and for any
\nu_{0}<\nu_{1}<\nu_{2}
,

i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{2}}:=i_{P,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\circ i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}},\quad i_{Z_{P},\nu_{0},\nu_{2}}:=i_{Z_{P},\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\circ i_{Z_{P},\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}

Beweis.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be a top-dimensional polytope. Then,

X^{\nu}_{P}=X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
has a decomposition as in (
3.36
).
Define embeddings

i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:=(\operatorname{Id},\tau):\Phi^{-1}(Q^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}\to\Phi^{-1}(Q^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu_{1}Q^{\vee}

(3.39)

where
\tau
is the restriction of a translation on
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}

(recall that
\nu Q^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{Q}
for all
\nu
) that
maps the point
\nu_{0}P^{\vee}\in\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}
to the point

\nu_{1}P^{\vee}\in\nu_{1}Q^{\vee}
. The maps (
3.39
)
glue to yield an embedding

i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:X^{\nu_{0}}_{P}\to X^{\nu_{1}}_{P}
.

Next, consider a face
Q\in\mathcal{P}
of the top-dimensional polytope

P
. The embedding

i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:X^{\nu_{0}}_{{\tilde{Q}}}\to X^{\nu_{1}}_{{\tilde{Q}}}
descends
to
i_{Z_{Q},\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:Z^{\nu_{0}}_{Q}\subset Z^{\nu_{1}}_{Q}
and

i_{Q,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:X^{\nu_{0}}_{Q}\subset X^{\nu_{1}}_{Q}
. We leave it
to the reader to check that the embedding preserves the almost
complex structure.
∎

Next, we define cut spaces and the broken manifold as manifolds with
cylindrical structures. Cut spaces resp. broken manifolds were
already defined as symplectic manifolds in
Definition

3.8

resp.
3.31
. We now define these spaces as almost
complex manifolds. See Lemma
3.54
and Remark

3.57
for a reconciliation of the two viewpoints. The
symplectic spaces have a superscript
\omega
to distinguish them from
the corresponding almost complex spaces,
see Notation
3.9
.

Definition 3.53
.

(Almost complex cut spaces and broken manifold)

Let
(X,\mathcal{P},\Phi)
be a tropical Hamiltonian action and let
\{X^{\nu}\}_{\nu}
be
the corresponding family of neck-stretched
manifolds.

(a)

(Cut space)

For a polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
, the
cut space

X_{P}
is defined
as the direct limit

3
3

3

We do not just define the cut space as
\Phi^{-1}(P)/T_{P}

as in done in the case of a symplectic cut space. The definition
by direct limits allows us to realize the cut space as the
direct limit of almost complex manifolds
X^{\nu}_{P}
.

X_{P}:=(\cup_{\nu}X_{P}^{\nu})/\sim,

(3.40)

where
X_{P}^{\nu}
is the
T_{P}
-quotient of the
P
-cylindrical
subset of
X^{\nu}
(see (
3.37
)), and
\sim
is given by
the embeddings
i_{P,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:X^{\nu_{0}}_{P}\to X^{\nu_{1}}_{P}

from Lemma
3.52
. For any
\nu
, there is a natural
embedding

i_{P,\nu}:X_{P}^{\nu}\to X_{P}.

(b)

(
T_{P}
-bundle on the cut space
X_{P}
) Define the

T_{P}
-bundle
Z_{P}\to X_{P}
as the direct limit

Z_{P}:=(\cup_{\nu}Z_{P}^{\nu})/\sim

(3.41)

of
T_{P}
-bundles
Z_{P}^{\nu}
(defined in (
3.38
)), where

\sim
is given by the embeddings

i_{Z_{P},\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:Z^{\nu_{0}}_{P}\to Z^{\nu_{1}}_{P}
from Lemma

3.52
. There is a natural embedding

i_{Z_{P},\nu}:Z_{P}^{\nu}\to Z_{P}.

(c)

(Broken manifold) The broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
is the
disjoint union

\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathfrak{X}:=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathfrak{X}_{P},

(3.42)

where we use the notation
\mathfrak{X}
when the polyhedral decomposition

\mathcal{P}
is clear from the context.
Here,

\mathfrak{X}_{P}:=Z_{P}\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}

and is a
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
-bundle over
X_{P}
with projection

T_{P,\mathbb{C}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}\xrightarrow{\pi_{X_{P}}}X_{P}.

(3.43)

(d)

(Cylindrical almost complex
structures)

An almost complex
structure
\mathfrak{J}=(\mathfrak{J}_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathfrak{X}
consists of an
almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}_{P}
on each component

\mathfrak{X}_{P}\subset\mathfrak{X}
. Such a
\mathfrak{J}
is
cylindrical

(or
X
-cylindrical
to emphasize the dependence on the
X
-inner product,

see Remark
3.50
(
a
))
if it is
the limit of a family
(J^{\nu})_{\nu}
of neck-stretched cylindrical
almost complex structures on
X^{\nu}
. The space of cylindrical
almost complex structures on
\mathfrak{X}
is denoted by

\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})=\{\mathfrak{J}\ \text{cylindrical}\}.

The almost complex structure

\mathfrak{J}
is
locally tame
or
locally compatible
or

locally strongly tamed

if the corresponding property holds for
J^{\nu}
(see Definition

3.49
).

(e)

(Cylindrical ends)
For a pair of polytopes
Q\subset P
, the

Q
-cylindrical end of
X_{P}
is a subset

U_{Q}(X_{P})\subset X_{P}
defined as the exhaustion

U_{Q}(X_{P}):=\cup_{\nu}U_{Q}(X_{P}^{\nu})

(3.44)

of
Q
-cylindrical subsets
U_{Q}(X_{P}^{\nu})\subset X_{P}^{\nu}
given by

U_{Q}(X_{P}^{\nu}):=\cup_{R\subseteq Q}\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu R^{\vee}\subset X_{P}^{\nu}.

The
Q
-cylindrical end in
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
resp.
Z_{P}
is the lift of

U_{Q}(X_{P})
by the projection map (
3.38
)

\pi^{\prime\prime}_{P}:Z_{P}\to X_{P}
resp.
\pi_{X_{P}}:\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to X_{P}
,
and is denoted by

U_{Q}(\mathfrak{X}_{P})\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P}\quad\text{resp.}\quad U_{Q}(Z_{P})\subset Z_{P}.

The following is immediate from the definitions:

Lemma 3.54
.

Given a broken almost complex manifold
\mathfrak{X}
, any piece
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of
the broken manifold is diffeomorphic to the symplectic manifold
\mathfrak{X}^{\omega}_{P}
.

The cut space
X_{P}
has an orbifold compactification

\overline{X}_{P}

(3.45)

that is diffeomorphic to the compactification
\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
of the
symplectic cut space. The boundary
\overline{X}_{P}\backslash X_{P}
is a
collection of sub-orbifolds of codimension at least
2
, each
corresponding to either a relative divisor, or the intersection of a
collection of relative divisors in
\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
from Definition

3.31
(
b
). The compactification may
be constructed formally (without reference to
X_{P}^{\omega}
) by adding to

X_{P}
limit points of torus orbits; for example, a divisor

X_{Q}\subset\overline{X}_{P}
corresponding to a facet
Q\subset P
,

Q\in\mathcal{P}
with outward normal
\nu_{Q}\in\mathfrak{t}_{Q}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}
is the set of
orbits

\{[x]=T_{\nu_{Q},\mathbb{C}}x\kern 5.0pt:\kern 5.0ptx\in X_{P}\},

where

T_{\nu_{Q},\mathbb{C}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is the torus generated by
\nu_{Q}
, and
each
[x]
is the limit point of a
T_{\nu_{Q},\mathbb{C}}
-orbit, that
is,
\lim_{s\to\infty}e^{(s+it)\nu_{Q}}x=[x]
.
The cylindrical almost complex structure on
X_{P}
does not extend to
\overline{X}_{P}
.
Similarly, a component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of the broken manifold has a compactification
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
if
the
X
-inner product on
\{\mathfrak{t}_{P}\}_{P}

(from (
3.10
)) is rational.

Remark 3.55
.

The neck-stretched manifolds
X^{\nu}
can be recovered from the
broken almost complex manifold equipped with cylindrical coordinates
on its ends as

X^{\nu}=\left(\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P},\operatorname{codim}(P)=0}X_{P}\right)/\sim_{\nu}

where the equivalence relation
\sim_{\nu}
is as follows. For
top-dimensional polytopes
P_{0}
,
P_{1}
, and
Q:=P_{0}\cap P_{1}
,

X_{P_{0}}\ni x_{0}\sim_{\nu}x_{1}\in X_{P_{1}}\Longleftrightarrow\\
x_{0}\in U_{Q}(X_{P_{0}}),\kern 5.0ptx_{1}\in U_{Q}(X_{P_{1}}),\kern 5.0pti_{Q}^{P_{0}}(x_{0})=e^{\nu(P_{0}^{\vee}-P_{1}^{\vee})}i_{Q}^{P_{1}}(x_{1}).

(3.46)

Here,
P_{0}^{\vee}-P_{1}^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{t}_{Q}
, and the maps
i_{Q}^{P_{0}}
,
i_{Q}^{P_{1}}
are
Q
-cylindrical coordinate maps defined in Lemma
3.59
.
This ends the
Remark.

Example 3.56
.

(Cylindrical ends in a single
cut)
We continue Example
3.48
where we described
neck-stretched manifolds in case of a single cut. Let
(X,\Phi,c)

be a tropical Hamiltonian action with a single cut. First, we describe the

P
-cylindrical part in the neck-stretched manifold
X^{\nu}
. We
have,

X^{\nu}_{P_{+}}=\{\Phi\geq c+\epsilon\}\cup_{\Phi^{-1}(c)}(\Phi^{-1}(c)\times[-\epsilon\nu,\epsilon\nu]),

with
X^{\nu}_{P_{-}}
defined analogously, and

X^{\nu}_{P_{0}}=(\Phi^{-1}(c)\times[-\epsilon\nu,\epsilon\nu]).

Next, we describe the
P_{0}
-cylindrical end in
X_{P_{+}}
. Since

\operatorname{codim}(P_{+})=0
, the spaces
X_{P_{+}}
,
Z_{P_{+}}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{P_{+}}

are all the same. Furthermore,

X_{P_{+}}=\{\Phi\geq c+\epsilon\}\cup_{\Phi^{-1}(c)}\left((-\infty,0]\times\Phi^{-1}(c)\right),

(3.47)

where
\Phi^{-1}(c+\epsilon)
is mapped to
\Phi^{-1}(c)
via the symplectic
cylindrical structure map, see (
3.33
). The latter may be
identified with
\{0\}\times\Phi^{-1}(c)
.
Note that
Z_{P_{0}}=\Phi^{-1}(c)
, the normal cone

\operatorname{NCone}_{P_{+}^{\vee}}P_{0}^{\vee}
is
(-\infty,0]
. The subset

(-\infty,0]\times Z
in
X_{P_{+}}
is the
P_{0}
-cylindrical end.

Remark 3.57
.

(Symplectic versus almost complex broken manifolds) We have
defined the broken manifold as a collection of almost complex
manifolds
(\mathfrak{X}_{P},J_{P})
in (
3.42
), and as symplectic
manifolds
(\mathfrak{X}_{P}^{\omega},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}})
in (
3.14
). In this
Remark, we see how to relate the two spaces, and also why they can
not be viewed as the same space. Since a component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of a
broken manifold is a
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
-bundle over a cut space
X_{P}
, we
focus on cut spaces in this discussion.

(a)

(Maps respecting cylindrical
structures) A surjective map
\phi:X_{P}\to X_{P}^{\omega}

respects the cylindrical structure
if

—

\phi
is the natural embedding on the complement of
cylindrical ends, that is,

\phi(X_{P}\backslash(\cup_{Q\subset P}U_{Q}(X_{P})))=\Phi^{-1}(P^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P}\subset(\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega},\omega_{X_{P}}),

(3.48)

arising from the definition (
3.36
), (
3.37
),
(
3.40
) of
X_{P}
, and the definition (
3.6
)
of the symplectic cut space
X_{P}^{\omega}
;

—

\phi
is
T_{Q}
-equivariant on the
Q
-cylindrical end; and

—

\phi
commutes with the projection to
X_{Q}
on the

Q
-cylindrical end, that is,

\pi_{Q}^{P}=\pi_{Q}^{P,\omega}\circ\phi\quad\text{on }U_{Q}(X_{P})\backslash\cup_{R\subset Q}U_{R}(X_{P}),

where the projection
\pi_{Q}^{P}
to
X_{Q}
on the
Q
-cylindrical
end is defined in (
3.56
) and the corresponding
projection
\pi_{Q}^{P,\omega}
to
X_{Q}^{\omega}
for symplectic manifolds
is defined in (
5.4
). In the above equation, both
sides map to the complement of the cylindrical ends in
X_{Q}
:
The left hand side maps to

X_{Q}\backslash(\cup_{R\subset Q}U_{R}(X_{Q}))
, and the right hand side
maps to
\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{Q}
, and the two spaces are canonically
identified as in (
3.48
).

For a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
, there is a large collection of maps
that respect the cylindrical structure. In Chapter
7
,
we construct squashing maps that are continuous and piecewise
smooth. Those maps can be approximated by smooth maps that satisfy
the above properties. Furthermore, any two maps respecting the
cylindrical structure are homotopic via a family of maps
respecting the cylindrical structure. These maps are used to
define the symplectic area of pseudoholomorphic maps to the broken
manifold (Definition
4.18
), and to define Donaldson
divisors on broken manifolds (Definition
5.12
).

(b)

(No canonical diffeomorphisms) The manifolds
X_{P}
and

X_{P}^{\omega}
are diffeomorphic, but there is no canonical
diffeomorphism
\phi:X_{P}\to X_{P}^{\omega}
that respects the
cylindrical structure. Assuming
X_{P}
has a cylindrical locally
tamed almost complex structure
J_{P}
, we do not have a method of
constructing diffeomorphisms
\phi:X_{P}\to X_{P}^{\omega}
that respect
the cylindrical structure and for which
J_{P}
is tamed by

\phi^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}
.

(c)

(Canonical diffeomorphisms without gluability) The lack
of taming embeddings of broken almost complex manifolds into
compact symplectic broken manifolds can be remedied by introducing
a slight weakening in the definition of cylindrical almost complex
structures on broken manifolds. In particular, if one allows the
connection one-forms
(\alpha_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
and the inner
products
\{g_{P}\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
to differ across the set of cut
spaces
\{X_{P}\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}}
, one can construct taming
diffeomorphisms of
X_{P}
into
X_{P}^{\omega}
, see Lemma

5.11
. The cost of this choice is that the new
kind of almost complex structures on pieces of
\mathfrak{X}
do not glue
to give a neck-stretched almost complex structure in
X
. That
is, the almost complex structures are not

gluable
. Gluability of almost complex
structures on broken manifolds is necessary for the proof of
homotopy equivalence of the Fukaya algebras defined on the
unbroken manifold
(X,L)
and the broken manifold
(\mathfrak{X},L)
. Once
this result is proved, one has greater flexibility in choosing
almost complex structures on broken manifolds while keeping the
compactness and regularity results, as we point out in Section

10.8
. In particular, one can work with

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-tamed almost complex structures on
\mathfrak{X}
(Definition
5.9
)
– these are tamed
almost complex structures defined on compact symplectic manifolds
\overline{X}_{P}
,
and the distinct notations
X_{P}
,
X_{P}^{\omega}
for almost complex and
symplectic manifolds can be dropped. This finishes the Remark.

3.5.2
Coordinates on cylindrical ends

Our next task is to produce identifications between
Q
-cylindrical
ends of different components of a broken manifold. These
identifications are used in writing down the matching conditions at
nodes of a broken map: The matching condition compares the evaluation
of maps lying in different manifolds (say
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
,
\mathfrak{X}_{P^{\prime}}
),
although neighborhoods of both lifts of the node map to the

Q
-cylindrical end. The identifications between the cylindrical ends
are via certain natural cylindrical “coordinates” that take values
in cones of torus Lie algebras such as
\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
.

Remark 3.58
.

We give some relations between dual polytopes, cones and normal
cones (Definition
3.3
). Later in Remark

3.60
, these relations will be shown to be the
polytope analogues of the fact that cut spaces are degenerate limits
of neck-stretched manifolds. For a pair
Q\subset P
of polytopes
with
\operatorname{codim}(P)=0
, there are inclusions

\tilde{i}_{P,Q,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}\to\nu_{1}Q^{\vee},\quad\forall\nu_{0}<\nu_{1}

that are translations and which map the vertex

\nu_{0}P^{\vee}\in\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}
to

\nu_{1}P^{\vee}\in\nu_{1}Q^{\vee}
. Then
\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
is
the exhaustion

\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}=\sqcup_{\nu}\nu Q^{\vee}/\sim,

(3.49)

where, for any
\nu_{0}<\nu_{1}
,
\sim
identifies
\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}
to
its image under
\tilde{i}_{P,Q,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}
. In general for any
pair
Q\subset P
(with
\operatorname{codim}(P)>0
possibly) there are
inclusions

i_{P,Q,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}:\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}\to\nu_{1}Q^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P},\quad\forall\nu_{0}<\nu_{1}

which take the point
\nu_{0}P^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}
in the domain to the
point
\nu_{1}P^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}
in the target space. The resulting
exhaustion is the normal cone

\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}=(\sqcup_{\nu}\nu Q^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P})/\sim,

(3.50)

where, for any
\nu_{0}<\nu_{1}
,
\sim
identifies

\nu_{0}Q^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}
to its image under
i_{P,Q,\nu_{0},\nu_{1}}
.

Abbildung 3.11:
Some components of the broken manifold in the limit
\nu\to\infty
of the neck-stretching in Figure
3.10
.

The following lemma gives an identification between the

Q
-cylindrical end in a component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of the broken manifold

\mathfrak{X}
to the “standard”
Q
-cylinder
Z_{Q}\times\mathfrak{t}_{Q}

(which is equal to
\mathfrak{X}_{Q}
by definition), see Figure

3.11
.

Lemma 3.59
.

(Cylindrical ends on broken manifolds)

For any pair of polytopes
Q\subset P
in
\mathcal{P}
, there are natural embeddings

\begin{split}i_{Q}^{P}:U_{Q}(X_{P})&\to(Z_{Q}/T_{P})\times\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee},\\
i_{Q}^{Z,P}:U_{Q}(Z_{P})&\to Z_{Q}\times\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee},\\
i_{Q}^{{\tilde{P}}}:U_{Q}(\mathfrak{X}_{P})&\to Z_{Q}\times(\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{t}_{P})\subset Z_{Q}\times\mathfrak{t}_{Q}\simeq\mathfrak{X}_{Q}.\end{split}

(3.51)

Beweis.

We prove the Lemma for the case
\operatorname{codim}(P)=0
. In this case, the
torus
T_{P}
is trivial,
X_{P}=Z_{P}=\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, and

\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}=\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
. The domain of

i_{Q}^{P}
has a decomposition (into sets whose interiors are disjoint)

U_{Q}(X_{P})=\cup_{R\subseteq Q}(\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}).

(3.52)

Here, we use the fact that
\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
is the limit of
the polytopes
\nu R^{\vee}
, see (
3.49
).
The target space of
i_{Q}^{P}
has a decomposition (into sets whose
interiors are disjoint)

Z_{Q}=\Phi^{-1}(Q^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\cup(\cup_{R\subset Q}\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\operatorname{NCone}_{Q^{\vee}}R^{\vee})

(3.53)

which is a limit of the decompositions

Z_{Q}^{\nu}=\Phi^{-1}(Q^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\cup(\cup_{R\subset Q}\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu R^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{Q}),

since
\operatorname{NCone}_{Q^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
is the limit of

\nu R^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{Q}
as
\nu\to\infty
(see
(
3.50
)). The embedding
i_{Q}^{P}
maps a subset in the
domain decomposition (
3.52
) to the corresponding
subset in the target decomposition (
3.53
). Here

Z_{Q}^{\nu}
is a
T_{Q}
-bundle defined in (
3.38
). For any

R\subseteq Q
, the map

{\operatorname{i}}_{Q}^{P}:\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\to(\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\operatorname{NCone}_{Q^{\vee}}R^{\vee})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}

is defined in an following way: It is the
identity on
\Phi^{-1}(R^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})
and

\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\to\operatorname{NCone}_{Q^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}

is an orthogonal splitting. We leave to the
reader the construction of the maps
i_{Q}^{P}
,
i_{Q}^{Z,P}
when

\operatorname{codim}(P)>0
. Finally, we point out that the domain resp. target
space of the map
i_{Q}^{{\tilde{P}}}
is the product of
\mathfrak{t}_{P}
and the
domain resp. target space of
i_{Q}^{Z,P}
. Therefore the map

i_{Q}^{{\tilde{P}}}
is defined as such a product once
i_{Q}^{Z,P}
is known.
∎

Remark 3.60
.

We can now relate the direct limit definition of cut spaces to the
corresponding relations between cones from Remark

3.58
. For any pair
Q\subset P
, Lemma

3.59
gives a coordinate map

\pi_{\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}}:U_{Q}(X_{P})\to\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}

on the
Q
-cylindrical end
U_{Q}(X_{P})
of
X_{P}
. The maps

\pi_{\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}}
, for all
Q\subset P
, assemble to
give a map

\pi_{\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}:X_{P}\to\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee},

(3.54)

where the complement
X_{P}\backslash(\cup_{Q\subset P}U_{Q}(X_{P}))
is
mapped to the point
\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}P^{\vee}
. The continuity of
the map is a consequence of the construction in Lemma

3.59
. There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{P}^{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu B^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{P,\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\nu P^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}}

(3.55)

where the left downward arrow is the map (
3.31
)
composed with the
\mathfrak{t}_{P}
-quotient map, the top arrow is from
Definition
3.53
(
a
), the bottom arrow
is from (
3.50
), and the right downward arrow is from
(
3.54
).

Remark 3.61
.

(Projections on cylindrical ends) The coordinates on the
cylindrical ends of broken manifolds naturally yield projection
maps. For any pair of polytopes
Q\subset P
, the projection on the

Q
-cylindrical end of
X_{P}

\pi_{Q}^{P}:U_{Q}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}

(3.56)

is defined by the first component of
{\operatorname{i}}_{Q}^{P}
from (
3.51
) composed with the
T_{Q}
-quotient.

Neck-stretched manifolds and components of a broken manifold are
equipped with a cylindrical metric. We recall that the
X
-inner
product (
3.10
) defines a metric
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}}

resp.
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}}
on
\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
resp.
\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
for
all
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

Remark 3.62
.

(Compactifications of cut spaces)

Since the cylindrical ends of
a cut space have a standard form as in (
3.51
), any cut space
X_{P}
with
a cylindrical almost complex structure
J_{P}
has a compactification

\overline{X}_{P}

that is an almost complex orbifold, with
\overline{X}_{P}\backslash X_{P}
being a
union of divisors, each corresponding to a facet
Q\subset P
,

Q\in\mathcal{P}
. In a similar way, if the
\mathfrak{t}
-inner product from
(
3.10
) is rational, any component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of the broken
manifold
\mathfrak{X}
has a compactification

\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}

that is an almost complex orbifold, and
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}\backslash\mathfrak{X}_{P}

consists of divisors corresponding to the facets of
{\tilde{P}}
, see
(
3.12
).
The same construction defines a
T_{P}
orbifold bundle

\overline{Z}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}

(3.57)

that is an extension of the
T_{P}
-bundle
Z_{P}\to X_{P}
, and
is such that
\overline{Z}_{P}\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
is the complement of
the vertical divisors (Definition
3.31
(
b
)) of
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
.
Note that the
spaces
Z_{P}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
depend on the
\mathfrak{t}
-inner product.

Broken manifolds are equipped with a cylindrical metric.

Definition 3.63
.

(Cylindrical metric)

A metric
g_{P}
on
\mathfrak{X}_{P}\simeq Z_{P}\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
is

P
-cylindrical
if
g_{P}
is a product metric, that is, the
product of the linear metric
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}}
on
\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}

and a
T_{P}
-invariant metric
g_{Z_{P}}
on
Z_{P}
that satisfies

\lvert\xi_{Z_{P}}\rvert_{g_{Z_{P}}}=|\xi|_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}}\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}

where
\xi_{Z_{P}}
is the generating vector field as in
(
3.1
). On the multiply-stretched manifolds

X^{\nu}
, a metric
g_{\nu}
is
cylindrical
if for any

P\in\mathcal{P}
,
g_{\nu}
is
P
-cylindrical in the region

\Phi^{-1}(P^{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}})\times\nu P^{\vee}
.

3.6
Translations: Relating neck-stretched and broken manifolds

To examine the convergence behavior of maps in
neck-stretched manifolds to a limit map in the broken manifold, we
need to embed
P
-cylindrical regions of the neck-stretched manifold
into the
P
-cylindrical component of the broken manifold. The
embedding maps, called
translations
are parametrized by elements
in the scaled dual complex
\nu B^{\vee}
. Given an element

t\in\nu B^{\vee}
lying in the dual polytope
\nu P^{\vee,\circ}
,
the translation
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}
is a map from the
P
-cylindrical subset

X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\subset X^{\nu}
to the component of the broken manifold

\mathfrak{X}_{P}
.

We start by recalling some facts about neck-stretched and broken
manifolds:

(a)

For any polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
and any
\nu
, the

P
-cylindrical subset of
X^{\nu}
is
X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
which has a
projection

X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{P,\nu}^{\prime}}Z^{\nu}_{P}

where
Z^{\nu}_{P}
is a
T_{P}
-bundle over
X^{\nu}_{P}
and the fibers of

\pi_{P,\nu}^{\prime}
are subsets of
\nu P^{\vee}
, see
(
3.38
). In particular, there is a map

\pi_{\nu P^{\vee}}:X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\to\nu P^{\vee},

(3.58)

which is
\pi_{\nu B^{\vee}}:X^{\nu}\to\nu B^{\vee}
composed with
the orthogonal projection
Q^{\vee}\to P^{\vee}
(from Definition

3.19
(
c
)).

(b)

The
P
-cylindrical component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}\subset\mathfrak{X}
is a product

\mathfrak{X}_{P}=Z_{P}\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}

where
Z_{P}\to X_{P}
is a
T_{P}
-bundle over
X_{P}
.

(c)

There is a natural embedding
Z_{P}^{\nu}\to Z_{P}
,

X_{\tilde{P}}^{\nu}\to X_{P}
from Definition
3.53
.

Definition 3.64
.

(
P
-translation)
For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
,

t\in\nu P^{\vee}
, denote by

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}:X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}.

(3.59)

the lift of the inclusion
Z_{P}^{\nu}\to Z_{P}
that maps a level set

\{\pi_{\nu P^{\vee}}=c\}\subset X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
to

Z_{P}\times\{c-t\}\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, where
\pi_{\nu P^{\vee}}
is
from (
3.58
),
c\in\nu P^{\vee}
, and so

c-t\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
.

The notion of
P
-translation may be expressed via the following
commutative diagram for any
t\in\nu P^{\vee}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\tilde{P}}^{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\vee}\supseteq\cup_{Q\subseteq P}\nu Q^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\nu B^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}}

(3.60)

Here, the left downward arrow
\pi_{\nu B^{\vee}}
is from
(
3.31
), and for any
Q\subseteq P
, the arrow

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}:\nu Q^{\vee}\to\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
is a
translation by
t\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
.

For any
t\in\nu P^{\vee}
, the translation
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{Q}
is the
‘same’ for all
Q\subseteq P
. Indeed,

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}|X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{Q}}}={\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{Q}

since the restriction of
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}
to

X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{Q}}}\subset X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
maps to the
Q
-cylindrical end

U_{Q}(\mathfrak{X}_{P})\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P}
; the latter may be viewed as a subset of

\mathfrak{X}_{Q}
. This leads us to view the parameter
t
in the translation

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}
as an element in the dual complex
B^{\vee}
as in the
following definition:

Definition 3.65
.

(Generalized translation)
For any
t\in\nu B^{\vee}
,

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}:={\mathbb{e}}^{-t}_{P}\quad\text{if $t\in P^{\vee,\circ}$}.

For an element
t\in\nu P^{\vee,\circ}
, the inverse of the
translation
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}
is well-defined on a subset

\mathfrak{X}_{P,\nu}\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P}
:

{\mathbb{e}}^{t}:=({\mathbb{e}}^{-t})^{-1}:\mathfrak{X}_{P}\supset\mathfrak{X}_{P,\nu}\to X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}.

(3.61)

The sequence of subsets
\mathfrak{X}_{P,\nu}
exhaust
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
as
\nu\to\infty
.

Example 3.66
.

We illustrate translations in a multiple cut using an example with
two non-intersecting single cuts. Consider the tropical Hamiltonian action

(X,\Phi,\mathcal{P})
where the torus is
T=S^{1}
, and the polytopes in

\mathcal{P}
are
P_{0}
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
,
P_{01}
,
P_{12}\subset\mathbb{R}
shown
in Figure
3.12
.

Abbildung 3.12:
A polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
and its dual complex
B^{\vee}
.

The dual complex
B^{\vee}
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Let the point

P^{\vee}_{i}
be
g_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=0,1,2
. For
\nu>0
, the
neck-stretched manifold is

X^{\nu}:=(X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{0}}\cup([\nu g_{0},\nu g_{1}]\times Z_{0})\cup X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{1}}\cup([\nu g_{1},\nu g_{2}]\times Z_{1})\cup X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{2}})/\sim,

where
Z_{i}:=\Phi^{-1}(c_{i})
,
X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{i}}
is
\overline{X}_{P_{i}}
minus a
tubular neighbourhood of relative divisors, and
\sim
identifies
the copies of
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
on the boundaries.
The translations are described as follows.

—

Suppose
t\in\nu P_{1}^{\vee}
. Then
t=\nu g_{1}
and
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}

is the embedding

([\nu g_{0},\nu g_{1}]\times Z_{0})\cup_{Z_{0}}X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{1}}\cup_{Z_{1}}([\nu g_{1},\nu g_{2}]\times Z_{1})\\
\to X_{P_{1}}\simeq((-\infty,0]\times Z_{0})\cup_{Z_{0}}X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{1}}\cup_{Z_{1}}([0,\infty)\times Z_{1}),

that is identity on
X^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}}_{P_{1}}
, and on the cylindrical ends it is
a translation by
\nu g_{1}
, namely

([\nu g_{0},\nu g_{1}]\times Z_{0})\xrightarrow{((\cdot-\nu g_{1}),\operatorname{Id})}(-\infty,0]\times Z_{0},\quad([\nu g_{1},\nu g_{2}]\times Z_{1})\xrightarrow{((\cdot-\nu g_{1}),\operatorname{Id})}([0,\infty)\times Z_{1}).

The translation
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}
is defined similarly when

t\in\nu P_{0}^{\vee}
or
\nu P_{2}^{\vee}
.

—

Suppose
t\in P^{\vee}_{i(i+1)}
. Then

t\in[\nu g_{i},\nu g_{i+1}]
and

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}:[\nu g_{i},\nu g_{i+1}]\times Z_{i}\to\mathbb{R}\times Z_{i}

maps
\{c\}\times Z_{i}
to
\{c-t\}\times Z_{i}
.

This ends the Example.

3.7
Existence of symplectic cylindrical structures

We prove that manifolds with tropical Hamiltonian actions possess
symplectic cylindrical structures, which were used in the definition
of neck-stretched almost complex manifolds. We recall from Definition

3.37
that in a tropical Hamiltonian manifold

(X,\mathcal{P},\Phi)
, for a polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
, a
symplectic
cylindrical structure
in a neighborhood of
\Phi^{-1}(P)\subset X
is
a diffeomorphism

\phi_{P}:\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}})\to\Phi^{-1}(P)\times P^{\vee},

where the polytope
{\tilde{P}}
is a neighborhood of
P
, and

(\phi_{P}^{-1})^{*}\omega_{X}
has a certain standard cylindrical form on

\Phi^{-1}(P)\times P^{\vee}
. The space
\Phi^{-1}(P)\times P^{\vee}
is a
fibration over
\Phi^{-1}(P)/T_{P}
whose fibers are subsets of
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
;
via the identification by
\phi_{P}
, neck-stretched
T_{P}
-cylindrical
almost complex structures were defined on
\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}})
in Section

3.4
.

However, for the proof of the main result of the book, namely the homotopy equivalence between unbroken and broken Fukaya algebras, we do not use the neck-stretched manifolds constructed via the symplectic cylindrical structures from Proposition
3.67
below.
For that purpose, neck-stretched manifolds are constructed by gluing a broken manifold equipped with a stabilizing divisor as in Section
5.4
.

To construct the symplectic cylindrical structures, we may need to
modify the tropical moment map
\Phi
in the following sense: Given a
tropical Hamiltonian manifold
(X,\mathcal{P},\Phi)
, a tropical moment map

\Phi^{\prime}
is a
modification
of
\Phi
if for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
there is a sufficiently small
T_{P}
-invariant neighborhood
U_{P}\subset X
of
\Phi^{-1}(P)
,
and for which

\pi_{P^{\vee}}\circ\Phi=\pi_{P^{\vee}}\circ\Phi^{\prime}:U_{P}\to\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}.

(3.62)

In other words, the
\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
-component of
\Phi
agrees with that
of
\Phi^{\prime}
, wherever it is an honest
T_{P}
-moment map.

Proposition 3.67
.

(Existence of symplectic cylindrical
structures)
There exists a symplectic cylindrical structure (see Definition

3.37
) for a tropical Hamiltonian manifold
(X,\mathcal{P},\Phi)
, after
replacing
\Phi
by a modification of
\Phi
(see previous paragraph).

Proof of Proposition
3.67
.

We construct the maps
\{\phi_{P}\}_{P}
and connection one-forms

\{\alpha_{P}\}_{P}
by induction on the dimension of
P
. Consider a
polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
. We assume that
\phi_{Q}
and
\alpha_{Q}
are
determined for every proper face
Q\subset P
that is an element of

\mathcal{P}
. We first construct a
T_{P}
-equivariant diffeomorphism

\psi_{P}:\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}})\to\Phi^{-1}(P)\times P^{\vee}

that satisfies the properties expected of
\phi_{P}
in

\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}}\cap{\tilde{Q}})
for all proper faces
Q\subset P
as
follows: The inner product on
\mathfrak{t}
in (
3.10
) gives the
following orthogonal projection

Q^{\vee}\to P^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee},

(3.63)

and the image lies in
P^{\vee}\times\operatorname{NCone}_{Q}P
if we restrict the
map to an appropriate neighborhood in
Q^{\vee}
. Viewing

\operatorname{NCone}_{Q}P
as a subset of
Q^{\vee}
via the cylindrical structure
map
\phi_{Q}
, we obtain an embedding

\Phi^{-1}(Q)\times\operatorname{NCone}_{Q}P\to\Phi^{-1}(P)

(3.64)

defined on a neighborhood of the origin in
\operatorname{NCone}_{Q}P
. The map

\psi_{P}
is defined on
\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{Q}})
by (
3.63
) and
(
3.64
). Extend
\psi_{P}
to a
T_{P}
-equivariant
diffeomorphism on
\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}})
that respects the
T_{P}
-moment map,
that is, satisfies

\Pi_{P^{\vee}}\circ\psi_{P}=\pi_{P^{\vee}}\circ\Phi,

where

\Pi_{P^{\vee}}
is from Definition
3.37

(
b
) and
\pi_{P^{\vee}}
is from Definition

3.24
. Next, we define a symplectic form on

\Phi^{-1}(P)\times P^{\vee}
by choosing a connection one-form

\alpha_{P}
: For any proper face
Q\subset P
, on

\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{Q}}\cap P)/T_{P}
, the connection one-form
\alpha_{P}
is
given by the consistency condition (
3.23
). We may choose
any extension of
\alpha_{P}
in the rest of
\Phi^{-1}(P)/T_{P}
. By our
choice of connection one-form
\alpha_{P}
on

\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{Q}}\cap P)/T_{P}
,
\psi_{P}
is a symplectomorphism on

\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}}\cap{\tilde{Q}})
for all
Q\subset P
. Next, we apply Lemma

3.68
to isotope
\psi_{P}
to a sympectomorphism
\phi_{P}
,
so that
\phi_{P}\equiv\psi_{P}
on
\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{P}}\cap{\tilde{Q}})
for all

Q\subset P
. As a last step, we replace
\Phi
by a tropical
moment map
\Phi^{\prime}
, such that
\Phi\equiv\Phi^{\prime}
in
\Phi^{-1}({\tilde{Q}})
,

Q\subset P
,
\Phi^{\prime}
satisfies (
3.62
), and

\Phi^{\prime}\circ\phi_{P}=\pi_{P}\circ\Phi^{\prime}
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
3.67
uses the following
result, which is a version of the coisotropic neighborhood theorem.

Lemma 3.68
.

Let
Z\subset(X,\omega)
be a submanifold with boundary. Suppose a
neighborhood
U_{Z}\subset X
of
Z
has moment map

\Phi_{P}:U_{Z}\to\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
that is smooth and generates
a free Hamiltonian
T_{P}
-action, for which
Z=\Phi_{P}^{-1}(0)
. Then, for any connection one-form
\alpha_{P}\in\Omega^{1}(Z,\mathfrak{t}_{P})
on the bundle
Z\to Z/T_{P}
, there is a neighborhood
\Delta\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
of the origin, and a

T_{P}
-equivariant symplectomorphism

\phi_{P}:(U_{Z},\omega)\to(Z\times\Delta,\omega_{Z/T_{P}}+{\operatorname{d}}\langle{\alpha_{P},i}\rangle)

on
U_{Z}:=\Phi_{P}^{-1}(\Delta)
, where
i:\Delta\to\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
is
the inclusion map. Furthermore, if

\psi_{P}:U_{Z}\to Z\times\Delta
is a diffeomorphism that preserves
the symplectic form on
N:=U(\partial Z)\times\Delta
, where

U(\partial Z)\subset Z
is a neighborhood of
\partial Z
, then

\psi_{P}
can be isotoped to a symplectomorphism
\phi_{P}
relative to

N
.

The proof of the ordinary symplectic neighborhood (
[
56
, Lemma
3.14]
) can be used to prove the slightly stronger statement
of Lemma
3.68
.

Kapitel 4
Broken disks

The
goal of this chapter is to define
broken treed holomorphic
disks
. These are analogues of what Parker
[
67
]
calls
exploded
holomorphic maps. These structures combine the features of
treed holomorphic disks and tropical (or broken) maps.

—

Treed holomorphic disks consist of surface components that are
nodal holomorphic disks or spheres in a symplectic manifold whose
boundary lies in a Lagrangian submanifold, with the additional
feature that disk nodes may be replaced by tree segments mapping to
the Lagrangian submanifold.
Treed disks are generalizations of pearly trajectories in
Biran-Cornea
[
9
]
.

—

On the other hand, a broken map is defined on the normalization
of a nodal curve, and each of the domain components maps to a
different component of the target broken manifold, and the maps
satisfy a matching condition at the nodal lifts. The broken map is
equipped with the additional data of a tropical graph in the dual
complex associated with the degeneration of the symplectic manifold.

In a broken treed holomorphic disk, certain nodes in the domain are

tropical nodes
which means that the curve components incident on the
node map to different target components; and the other nodes, called

internal nodes
, are sphere nodes as seen in Gromov-Witten theory or
disk nodes with treed segments as in treed holomorphic disks. Since we
assume that the Lagrangian submanifold is disjoint from the relative
divisors of the broken manifold, all disk nodes are internal nodes.

Abbildung 4.1:
A broken treed holomorphic disk. The disk node
w_{3}
has been replaced by a treed segment in the Lagrangian
L
, and
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
are tropical nodes.

4.1
Treed disks

Treed disks
are the domains of broken treed holomorphic disks, which are the main objects defined in this Chapter.
Treed disks
are analogues of pearly trajectories of Biran-Cornea
[
9
]
,
Cornea-Lalonde
[
27
]
and Seidel
[
77
]
, and
they are combinations of trees, nodal disks and nodal spheres.

Definition 4.1
.

(Treed disks)

(a)

(Nodal disks) A nodal disk
S
is a union

S=\left(\bigcup_{\alpha=1,\ldots,d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}S_{\alpha,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{\beta=1,\ldots,d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}S_{\beta,{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}\right)/\sim

(4.1)

where the index
\alpha
, ranging over
\{1,\dots,d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\}
,
indexes disk components, that is, for each
\alpha
,
S_{\alpha}
is
biholomorphic to a unit disk
\mathbb{D}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
, and the
index
\beta
, ranging over
\{1,\dots,d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\}
, indexes
sphere components, that is, for each
\beta
,
S_{\beta}
is
biholomorphic to the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, glued together by an
equivalence relation
\sim
. The equivalence relation
\sim
is
generated by pairs of interior or boundary
nodal points

w_{e}=(w^{+}_{e},w^{-}_{e})\in(\cup_{\alpha}\partial S_{\alpha})^{2}\cup(\cup_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha}^{\circ})\cup\cup_{\beta}S_{\beta})^{2}

(4.2)

of pairs of boundary points of disks, interior points of disks or
spheres, with the property that the boundary
\partial S
is
connected and there are no cycles of components

S_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,S_{\alpha_{k}}=S_{\alpha_{1}}
connected by
nodes (so that in particular, each node connects two different
disk or sphere components of
S
). A

marking
of a nodal disk is a collection of boundary and
interior points

\underline{z}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}=(z_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},i}\in\partial S,i=0,\ldots,d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})),\quad\underline{z}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}=(z_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},i}\in S\backslash\partial S,i=1,\ldots,d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}))

(4.3)

distinct from the nodes. A marked nodal disk is
stable
if
it admits no automorphisms, or equivalently, if for each disk
component
S_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},i}
the sum of the number of special (nodal
or marked) boundary points and twice the number of interior
special points is at least three, and each sphere component

S_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},i}
has at least three special points.

(b)

(Combinatorial type of a nodal disk)
The
combinatorial type
of a
nodal disk
S
is the tree
\Gamma
whose vertices
\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)

correspond to disk or sphere components of
S
, and whose edges

\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)
correspond to markings or nodes, together with

(i)

(Sphere and disk vertices)

a partition of the vertex
set

\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Vert}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)

into disk vertices
\operatorname{Vert}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
and sphere vertices

\operatorname{Vert}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
;

(ii)

(Interior and boundary edges) a partition of the edge
set

\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)

into edges
\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
of boundary type and edges

\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
of interior type;

(iii)

(Leaf and non-leaf edges)
a partition of the edge set

\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)

\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Edge}_{\rightarrow}(\Gamma)

into leaf edges
\operatorname{Edge}_{\rightarrow}(\Gamma)
which correspond
to markings,
and non-leaf edges
\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)

which correspond to nodes (and so, leaf edges are incident on a
single vertex and non-leaf edges are each incident on two
vertices);

(iv)

(Ordering of leaves) an ordering of the boundary leaf
edges
\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\to}(\Gamma)
and the interior leaf edges

\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},\to}(\Gamma)
given by the corresponding ordering
of markings (see (
4.3
));

(v)

(Ribbon structure) a
ribbon structure
on

\Gamma
, which is a cyclic ordering
<_{v}
on the set of
boundary edges (both leaf and non-leaf)

\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}^{v}(\Gamma):=\{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma):v\in e\}

incident on each boundary vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)

such that the induced cyclic ordering on the set

\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\to}(\Gamma)
of boundary leaves corresponds to
the cyclic ordering

z_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},0},\dots,z_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})},z_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},0}
of
boundary markings.

(vi)

(Root edge and edge orientations) The edge

e_{0}\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
corresponding to the first
boundary marking
z_{0}
is an outgoing edge and
is called the
root edge
, all the other boundary markings
are incoming edges, and all edges corresponding to nodes are
oriented to point towards the root.

(c)

(Treed segments) A
treed segment
consists of a
collection of closed intervals
I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}
,

I_{j}\cong[0,\ell(I_{j})],\quad(-\infty,0],\quad\text{or}\quad I_{j}\cong[a_{j},\infty)\quad\text{or}\kern 5.0pt(-\infty,\infty)

glued along infinite end-points, to produce a topological space
isomorphic to some subset of
\mathbb{R}
. For example,

[0,\infty)\cup_{\infty}(-\infty,\infty)\cup_{\infty}(-\infty,0]

(4.4)

is a treed segment with three components and finite end-points.
Each treed segment
T
has a length
\ell(T)\in[0,\infty]
and a
number of breakings
b(T)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. A treed segment with

b(T)>0
is called a
broken segment
and has

\ell(T)=\infty
. We also consider treed segments with one
infinite end such as

[0,\infty)\cup_{\infty}(-\infty,\infty)

or both infinite ends such as
(-\infty,\infty)\cup_{\infty}(-\infty,\infty)
.

(d)

(Treed disk) A
treed nodal disk

C=S\cup T
is

(i)

either obtained from a nodal disk
S_{0}

—

by assigning a length
\ell(e)\in[0,\infty]

to each boundary node

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
, and replacing any boundary
node
w_{e}
,
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
with
\ell(e)>0

by a treed segment
T_{e}
with finite end-points, and the treed
segment
T_{e}
has length
\ell(e)
if
\ell(e)<\infty
or it
is a broken segment as in (
4.4
) if

\ell(e)=\infty
; and

—

each boundary marking
w_{e}
,

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\to}(\Gamma)
is replaced by a (possibly
broken) treed segment, one of whose end-points is infinite,
and thus
\ell(e)=\infty
for all boundary markings; or

(ii)

C
has no surface component and consists only of a treed
segment with two infinite ends. The
-\infty
resp.
\infty

end of the segment is regarded as the input resp. output, and
therefore there is one output and one input, so

d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})=1
.

A treed segment
T_{e}
,
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
, containing a
breaking is called a
broken edge
.
A non-leaf edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)
is broken if and only if

\ell(e)=\infty
.

(e)

(Isomorphism of treed disks)

An isomorphism of treed disks is a homeomorphism
\phi:C\to C^{\prime}

that is a biholomorphism on each sphere or disk component,
length-preserving on edges, and preserves the labelling of
leaves.

(f)

(Combinatorial type of a treed
disk) The
combinatorial type of a treed disk consists of the combinatorial
type
\Gamma
of the underlying nodal disk which includes the
vertex and edge partitions, ordering of markings, ribbon structure
and edge orientations; and in addition a partition

\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}^{0}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}^{(0,\infty)}\cup\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}^{\infty}(\Gamma)

of boundary edges corresponding to boundary nodes with zero,
finite, and infinite length edges.
Note that for a boundary edge
e
with edge length

\ell(e)\in(0,\infty)
, the length of the treed segment
T_{e}

is not part of the combinatorial type.

(g)

(Stable treed disk) A treed nodal disk
C
is

stable
if the underlying disk
S
is stable, the treed
segment at any node
T_{e},e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
has at
most one breaking, and treed segments at markings

T_{e},e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\rightarrow,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
are unbroken. A
treed disk with no surface component (that is, consisting of a
single possibly broken sequence of segments) is not stable.

(h)

(Disconnected types) Let
\Gamma
be a
disjoint union of treed disk types
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{k}
. A
treed curve of type
\Gamma
is a collection
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}

of treed disks of types
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{k}
.

For integers
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\geq 0
, denote by

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
the (possibly empty) moduli space of
isomorphism classes of stable treed disks with
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
incoming
boundary markings, one outgoing boundary marking and
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})

interior markings. For each combinatorial type
\Gamma
, denote by

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
the set of isomorphism classes of stable
treed disks of type
\Gamma
. The moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
then decomposes as

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}=\bigcup_{\Gamma}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}.

For any type
\Gamma
,
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
has a compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
where the boundary is

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}\backslash\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}=\bigcup_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma^{\prime}},

and
\Gamma^{\prime}
ranges over all strata such that
\Gamma
is obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by either collapsing an interior edge or a zero length boundary edge, or making a zero length boundary edge non-zero, or making an infinite boundary edge finite, or performing a combination of these operations.

Abbildung 4.2:
The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{1,1}
has three top-dimensional strata corresponding to the types
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
,
\Gamma_{3}
.

The top-dimensional cells in
\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
correspond to
strata where all boundary edges
e
have finite non-zero
length, that is,
\ell(e)\in(0,\infty)
. The
dimension of each of the top-dimensional cells is

d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+2d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})-2
. For the stratum with no finite edges, so
containing a single disk, this follows immediately from the fact that
each boundary leaf edge resp. interior leaf edge contributes
1

resp.
2
to the dimension, and the automorphism group of the disk is

PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
which has dimension
3
. The dimensions of the other
top-dimensional cells can be computed by first computing the
corresponding stratum of the moduli spaces of disks without trees, and
then adding one for each boundary edge with finite non-zero length.
The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
is a
manifold with corners where the codimension
k
corner stratum
consists of curves containing
k
broken treed edges. We do not give a
proof, since for our purposes it is enough to view

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
as a cell-complex, with a manifold
structure on each of the strata
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
.

Definition 4.2
.

(Orientation of moduli of treed disks) We fix an orientation
for moduli spaces of treed disks of type
\Gamma
, where
\Gamma

does not contain boundary edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
of
length
0
or
\infty
.

(a)

(Single disk) Let
\Gamma
be a treed disk type with a
single disk component with
\geq 2
incoming boundary leaves and
no other surface component. The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is the
quotient

\operatorname{Conf}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}/PSL(2,\mathbb{R}),

where

\operatorname{Conf}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}\subset\{\underline{z}=(\underline{z}^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\underline{z}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\in(\partial D)^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+1}\times(D^{\circ})^{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}\}

is the subset of configurations where
\underline{z}
consists of distinct
points and the boundary markings are ordered counter-clockwise.
Fixing
z_{0}^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}=-1
,
z_{1}^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}=1
,
z_{2}^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}=i
gives a
global slice
\Sigma

of the
PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action. Furthermore,

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is oriented via its identification to the slice

\Sigma

which is an open subset of

\mathbb{R}^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})-2}\times\mathbb{C}^{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}
. In the case that
\Gamma
has less than

2
incoming boundary leaves, we fix the global slice

\Sigma

by setting

z_{0}^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}=-1
,
z_{0}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}=0
.

(b)

(Multiple disks) Let
\Gamma
be a treed disk type with
no sphere components and all
of
whose boundary edges have finite
non-zero length. The orientation on
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is chosen by
inducting on the number of boundary edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
.
Suppose the treed disk types
\Gamma
,
\Gamma_{0}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
are related by the following morphisms:

\Gamma\xleftarrow{\text{Make the edge length $\ell(e)$ non-zero}}\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{\text{Collapse $e$}}\Gamma^{\prime}.

Here,
we point out that the edge length
\ell(e)
in
\Gamma_{0}
is zero.
The moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}
is a codimension one boundary stratum of both

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
via inclusions

i_{\Gamma,\Gamma_{0}}:\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma},\quad i_{\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma_{0}}:\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}.

Assuming an orientation on
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
,
the stratum
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is oriented so that
the orientations on

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}
induced by
i_{\Gamma,\Gamma_{0}}
,

i_{\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma_{0}}
are the opposite of each other.

(c)

(Disks and spheres) Let
\Gamma
be a treed disk type,
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the type obtained by collapsing interior
edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
. Then the orientation on

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

and the standard orientation on the moduli space of spheres
induces an orientation on
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

which is equal to the product orientation

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}\times\prod_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{v}}
. Here,
\Gamma_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}\subset\Gamma
is the
subgraph consisting of all the disk vertices and
\Gamma_{v}
is a
graph with a single vertex
\{v\}
and markings corresponding to
all edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)
incident on
v
.

Remark 4.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be a treed disk type containing a single edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
of infinite length, and all of
whose other boundary edges

e^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
have
\ell(e^{\prime})\in(0,\infty)
.
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a treed disk type obtained from
\Gamma
by making the edge length of
e
finite. Then,

i_{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}:\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

is a boundary stratum of codimension one. Suppose
\Gamma_{1}
,

\Gamma_{2}
are treed disk types obtained by cutting the edge
e
in

\Gamma
(see Definition
6.2
), and suppose the root of

\Gamma
is contained in
\Gamma_{1}
. Then the boundary orientation
on
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
induced by
i_{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}
differs from the
product orientation
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{1}}\times\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{2}}
by

\epsilon(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}):=(-1)^{\circ}

(4.5)

where
\circ
depends only on the combinatorial types
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
, see
[
76
, (12.22)]
.

The moduli spaces admit universal curves, which admit partitions into
one and two-dimensional parts. For any combinatorial type
\Gamma
,
let
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
denote the universal treed disk consisting of
isomorphism classes of pairs
(C,z)
where
C
is a treed disk of
type
\Gamma
and
z
is a point in
C
, possibly on a disk component,
sphere component, or one of the edges of the tree. The
map

\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma},\quad[C,z]\mapsto[C]

(4.6)

is the universal projection, whose fiber over
[C]
is a copy of
C
.
The union over types
\Gamma^{\prime}
with

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
is denoted
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
.
Denote by

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma},\quad\text{resp.}\quad\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}

the locus of points
[C,z]\in\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
where
z
lies on a
disk or sphere component resp. an edge of
C
.
Hence
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\cap\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}

is the set of points on the boundary of the disks meeting the edges of
the tree.
Denote by

\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma},\quad\text{resp.}\quad\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{\Gamma}

the compactification of
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
in
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
.

On each stratum, the universal curve admits local
trivializations. For a stratum
\Gamma
, the spaces

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma},\quad\text{resp.}\quad\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}

are smooth fibrations with
2
resp.
1
-dimensional fibers and
markings are sections

\underline{z}=(z_{i,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}},z_{j,{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}):\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma},\quad 0\leq i\leq d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}),1\leq j\leq d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}).

We view breaking points on broken treed segments as sections

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
. On a type
\Gamma
, the treed segment

T_{e}
corresponding to any edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
has a
fixed number of breakings, each of which gives rise to a section. The
union
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
has local trivializations:
For a curve
[C]\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
,
C=S\cup T
, and a small enough
neighborhood
U_{C}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
of
[C]
, there is a
homeomorphism

\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}|U_{C}\simeq U_{C}\times C.

(4.7)

When restricted to each stratum of

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}|U_{C}
resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}|U_{C}
, the homeomorphism above is a
diffeomorphism onto its image. The markings

z_{i,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}},z_{j,{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}|U_{C}:U_{C}\to S

are constant functions, whose values we denote by

\underline{z_{U_{C}}}=(z_{i,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},U(C)},z_{j,{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},U(C)})
, where

z_{i,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},U(C)},z_{j,{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},U(C)}\in S
. The fibers of

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\to U_{C}
possess a conformal structure, and the
trivialization in (
4.7
) induces a map

j:U_{C}\to\mathcal{J}(S)

(4.8)

to the space
\mathcal{J}(S)
of conformal structures on
S
such that for

[C_{1}]\in U_{C}
,
(C,j([C_{1}],\underline{z_{U_{C}}})
is biholomorphic to

(\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma},\underline{z})|_{[C_{1}]}
.

Remark 4.4
.

The structure of a “smooth fibration with sections” on

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
breaks down on
the extension to the compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
. For example,
a finite treed segment
T_{e}
in the fibers of
\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}
may be
transformed to a segment of “zero length” in the compactification

\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{\Gamma}
; or two disconnected components in the fibers of

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}
may connect at a disk node in the compactification

\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}
.

4.2
Treed pseudoholomorphic disks

Treed pseudoholomorphic disks are maps from treed disks to a
symplectic manifold equipped with a Lagrangian submanifold. The
symplectic manifold has a tamed almost complex structure and the
Lagrangian submanifold has a Morse function on it. On the
two-dimensional part of the treed disk the map is pseudoholomorphic,
and the boundaries of the disks map to the Lagrangian submanifold. On
the one-dimensional part of the domain, the map is a gradient flow
line of the Morse function on the Lagrangian submanifold, whose length
is the same as the length of the tree edge. Later in this chapter,
we will
adapt the definition of treed holomorphic disks in a symplectic
manifold to define broken treed holomorphic disks in a broken
manifold. Even later in the text, the almost complex structure, the Morse
function, and the metric on the Lagrangian will be given
domain-dependent perturbations in order to regularize the moduli
spaces of treed holomorphic (broken) disks.

We introduce the necessary notation for defining treed holomorphic
disks. Let
(X,\omega_{X})
be a symplectic manifold and
L\subset X
be a
Lagrangian submanifold. Let
J
be an
\omega_{X}
-tame almost complex
structure. Let
G_{L}
be a Riemannian metric on
L
and let

F\in C^{\infty}(L,\mathbb{R})
be a Morse function such that the pair

(F,G_{L})
is Morse-Smale.
The
gradient vector field
is defined
by the condition

\operatorname{grad}_{F}\in\operatorname{Vect}(L),\quad{\operatorname{d}}F(\cdot)=G_{L}(\operatorname{grad}_{F},\cdot).

Definition 4.5
.

A
treed holomorphic disk
with boundary in

L\subset X
consists of a treed disk
C=S\cup T
and a
continuous map

u:C\to X

satisfying the following conditions:

(a)

The tree components
T
and the boundary
\partial S
of the
surface components are mapped to the Lagrangian submanifold
L
;
that is,
u(T\cup\partial S)\subset L
;

(b)

the map
\left.u\right|_{S}
is a pseudoholomorphic map on the
surface part:

Jd(\left.u\right|_{S})=d(\left.u\right|_{S})\circ j
; and

(c)

the map
\left.u\right|_{T}
is a union of gradient
trajectories of
F
:

\frac{d}{ds}\left.u\right|_{T}=-\operatorname{grad}_{F}(\left.u\right|_{T})

where
s
is a unit velocity coordinate on
T
.

A treed holomorphic disk
u:C=S\cup T\to X
is
stable

if it has finitely many automorphisms,

\#\operatorname{Aut}(u)<\infty
, or equivalently

(a)

each surface component
S_{v}\subset S
on which the map
u
is
constant is stable as a component of a nodal disk
S
(see
Definition
4.1
); and

(b)

each treed segment
T_{e}
on which the map
u
is
constant has at most one infinite end, that is, one of the ends of

T_{e}
is an attaching point to a sphere or disk
S_{v}\subset S
.

Note that the case
C\cong\mathbb{R}
equipped with a non-constant Morse
trajectory
u:C\to L
is allowed under this stability condition and
corresponds to the case of a single incoming edge, that is,

d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})=1
.
The area of a sphere or disk
u:S\to X
is the symplectic area

{\operatorname{Area}}(u)=\int_{S}(u|_{S})^{*}\omega_{X}.

4.3
Multiply-broken disks

A broken map is a map from a nodal curve to a multiply cut manifold
that is discontinuous at tropical nodes. Different components of the
nodal curve map to different pieces of the multiply cut manifold, and
the lifts of the tropical nodal points satisfy an edge-matching
condition. The nodal points carry an additional data of intersection
multiplicity with relative divisors. This data is packaged into a
tropical structure
, which is part of the combinatorial type of the
broken map.

Definition 4.6
.

(Tropical graph)

Let
B^{\vee}
be the dual complex for a set of polytopes

\mathcal{P}=\{P\subset\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\}
as in Definition
3.19
.
A
pre-tropical graph
is a triple

(\Gamma,P,\mathcal{T})
consisting of

(a)

a graph
\Gamma
with vertex set
\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
and edge set

\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)\subset\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\times\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
,

(b)

polytope assignments

P:\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)\to\mathcal{P}

(4.9)

such that for any edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
,
P(e)=P(v_{+})\cap P(v_{-})
, and

(c)

edge
directions

\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\},\quad\forall\kern 5.0pte\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma).

A
tropical graph
is a pre-tropical graph
(\Gamma,P,\mathcal{T})
that
is
realizable

in the sense that there is a collection of
tropical vertex positions

\mathcal{T}(v)\in P(v)^{\vee,\circ}\subset B^{\vee},\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)

(4.10)

satisfying the following
direction condition

{\text{(Direction condition)}}\quad\mathcal{T}(v_{+})-\mathcal{T}(v_{-})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\mathcal{T}(e).

(4.11)

The set of
tropical vertex position maps on a tropical graph
\Gamma
is
denoted

\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)=\{(\mathcal{T}(v))_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}:\mathcal{T}\text{ is a tropical vertex position map on $\Gamma$}\}.

(4.12)

A tropical graph
\Gamma
is
rigid
if
\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)
has exactly
one element.

Remark 4.7
.

A deformation of vertex positions for a tropical graph
\Gamma
is shown
in Figure
4.3
in the dual complex
B^{\vee}

corresponding to two orthogonal cuts (see Figure
1.2
and
Figure
1.6
). By the definition of tropical graphs,
moving the vertices
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
to the dotted positions produces
a one-parameter space of tropical vertex positions. Thus,
the space of vertex positions is

\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)\simeq(0,1).

This remark is continued in Remark
4.19
.

Abbildung 4.3:
Moving the vertices
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
to the dotted positions
gives a different vertex position map for the same tropical graph
\Gamma
.

Remark 4.8
.

(The set of vertex positions is the interior of a polytope)
For a tropical graph
\Gamma
, the closure
\overline{\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
of the space
\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)
of vertex positions is given by

\overline{\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma):=\{(\mathcal{T}(v)\in P(v)^{\vee})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}:\\
\mathcal{T}(v_{+})-\mathcal{T}(v_{-})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{T}(e),\kern 5.0pt\forall e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)\}.

Thus
\overline{\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
is obtained by weakening the conditions (
4.10
) and (
4.11
) in the definition of
\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)
. The closure
\overline{\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
is a compact polytope in the affine space (of the same dimension)

A_{\Gamma}:=\{(\mathcal{T}(v)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}:\\
\mathcal{T}(v_{+})-\mathcal{T}(v_{-})\in\mathbb{R}\mathcal{T}(e),\kern 5.0pt\forall e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)\}.

The facets of the polytope
\overline{\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
are given by the conditions

\mathcal{T}(v)\in P(v)^{\vee},\quad\mathcal{T}(v_{+})-\mathcal{T}(v_{-})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mathcal{T}(e),

for each vertex
v
and each edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
of
\Gamma
.

Definition 4.9
.

(Tropical structure on a treed disk type)

Let
\Gamma
be the
combinatorial type of a treed disk (see Definition
4.1
(
f
)). Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a
broken manifold with an underlying polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
,
and a Lagrangian submanifold
L
contained in the component

X_{P_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{X}
,
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
.

A
tropical structure
on
\Gamma
consists of a tropical graph

(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}},P,\mathcal{T})
and an edge collapse morphism

{\operatorname{tr}}:\Gamma\to\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}}

(4.13)

called the
tropicalization
. Here

P:\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}})\cup\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}})\to\mathcal{P}
is the polytope
assignment on the tropical graph (see (
4.9
)), and

\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}})_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}})}
is the
collection of edge directions. The edges collapsed by
{\operatorname{tr}}
are called

internal edges
and uncollapsed edges are called
tropical
edges
. The subset of internal resp. tropical edges of
\Gamma
is
denoted by

\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{int}}(\Gamma)\quad{\text{resp.}}\quad\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\subset\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma).

All boundary edges are collapsed
by assumption.
Therefore,

\operatorname{Edge}_{-,{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)\subset\operatorname{Edge}_{{\operatorname{int}}}(\Gamma),\quad\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\subset\operatorname{Edge}_{-,{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma).

The map
{\operatorname{tr}}
is often suppressed in the notation. The polygon
assignment
P\circ{\operatorname{tr}}
and edge direction
\mathcal{T}\circ{\operatorname{tr}}
maps on

\Gamma
are often denoted by
P
,
\mathcal{T}
.

Remark 4.10
.

In a treed disk type
\Gamma

equipped with a tropical structure, since the tropicalization map
collapses all the boundary edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
, all
the boundary vertices
v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
are mapped to a
single vertex in
\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}}
.

We recall some notation required to define broken maps. The domain of
a broken map is a treed disk
C=T\cup S
(Definition

4.1
) where
S
is the surface part and
T
is the tree
part. For a treed disk
C
of type
\Gamma
equipped with a tropical
structure, for every vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
we denote by

S_{v}^{\circ}=S_{v}\backslash\{w_{e}:e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}

(4.14)

the surface part with cylindrical ends obtained by deleting nodal
points corresponding to tropical edges. The target space of a broken
map is a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
. We recall from
(
3.42
) that a broken manifold with an underlying
polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
is a disjoint union

\mathfrak{X}=\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathfrak{X}_{P},

and there is a top-dimensional polytope
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
such that

X_{P_{0}}
contains a Lagrangian submanifold
L
. Note that

X_{P_{0}}=\mathfrak{X}_{P_{0}}
since the torus
T_{P_{0}}
is trivial.

Definition 4.11
.

(A surface component of a broken map) Let
\Gamma
be the
combinatorial type of a treed disk equipped with a
tropical
structure
and let
v
be a vertex of
\Gamma
. Define the domain

S_{v}
to be a disk if
v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
and a sphere if

v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)
. The
surface component of a broken
map
corresponding to
v
consists of a punctured domain curve

S_{v}^{\circ}:=S_{v}\backslash\{w_{e}:v\in e,e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}
and a
holomorphic map

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to X_{P(v)}

with the following behavior at punctures: For any tropical edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
,
e\ni v
, and any holomorphic
coordinate in a punctured neighborhood
U_{w_{e}}
of

w_{e}
,

z_{e}:(U_{w_{e}}\backslash\{w_{e}\})\to(\mathbb{C},0),

the map
z_{e}^{-\mathcal{T}(e)}u_{v}
has a removable singularity at
w_{e}
.
The quantity
