\operatorname{ev}_{w_{e}}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}u_{v}:=\lim_{z_{e}\to 0}z_{e}^{-\mathcal{T}(e)}u_{v}\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}.

(4.15)

is called the
tropical evaluation
at the puncture
w_{e}
for the
choice of holomorphic coordinate
z_{e}
. Here,

\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}}
is the direction of the edge
e
,

1
1

1

It is enough to just consider broken maps whose edges have integral directions
\mathcal{T}(e)
,
and not rational directions,
because the free torus actions in the neighborhood of cut loci implies that in the neighborhood of a puncture, a map is asymptotic to a torus orbit generated by an integral element. Also, see the related Remark
7.31
following the proof of the removal of singularities result.

and for any

z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
z^{\mathcal{T}(e)}
is an element of the one-dimensional
torus
T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}\subset T_{P(e),\mathbb{C}}
. Via the cylindrical
coordinate map (
3.51
) which identifies the

P(e)
-cylindrical end to
\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
, we may assume that

u_{v}(U_{w_{e}}\backslash\{w_{e}\})\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}.

Recall that
\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
has a holomorphic
T_{P(e),\mathbb{C}}
-action.

It is often useful to have a projected version of the tropical evaluation map that does not involve the choice of a holomorphic coordinate in the neighborhood of the puncture.

Definition 4.12
.

(Projected tropical
evaluation map) In the
notation of Definition
4.11
of a component of a broken
map, for a puncture
w_{e}
and coordinates
z_{e}
in the neighborhood
of
w_{e}
, the
projected tropical evaluation
at a puncture

w_{e}
corresponding to an edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
is

\pi_{\mathcal{T}(e)}^{\perp}(\operatorname{ev}_{w_{e}}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}u_{v})\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}},

(4.16)

which is equal to
\lim_{w_{e}}(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ u_{v})
, and
therefore, is independent of the coordinate
z_{e}
. Here,

\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}:\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
is
the quotient map by the complex subtorus

T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}\subset T_{P(e),\mathbb{C}}
.

An unframed broken map consists of surface components as in Definition
4.11
, for which projected tropical evaluations on any pair of nodal lifts match; and treed segments that are mapped to gradient flow lines on the Lagrangian submanifold.

Definition 4.13
.

(Unframed broken map) Let

\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
be a broken manifold and
L\subset X_{P_{0}}
be a
Lagrangian submanifold as above. An
unframed broken map

u

to
\mathfrak{X}
is a datum consisting of

(a)

(Domain type and tropical structure) a
domain type

\Gamma
, which is the combinatorial type of a treed disk equipped
with a
tropical structure
(Definition

4.9
) for which disk vertices map to
P_{0}
:

v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)\implies P(v)=P_{0};

(b)

(Domain curve) a treed nodal disk
C=S\cup T
of type

\Gamma
consisting of a surface component
S_{v}
(sphere or disk) for every vertex
v
of
\Gamma
, and a treed segment
T_{e}
for every boundary edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
;

(c)

(Map) a collection of holomorphic maps on punctured
surface components

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)},\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)

as in Definition
4.11
, and continuous maps on the treed
segments

u_{e}:T_{e}\to L,\quad e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)

collectively denoted
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
, such that

—

(Behavior on the boundary) the restriction to the
components with boundary, namely

u|(\cup_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)}S_{v}^{\circ}\cup\cup_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)}T_{e}),

of
C
is a treed holomorphic map (in the sense of Definition

4.5
) to the target space
(X_{P_{0}},L)
;

—

(Matching at internal nodes) for an interior internal
edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)\cap\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{int}}(\Gamma),

the corresponding nodal points
w_{e}^{\pm}\in S_{v_{\pm}}
map to

\mathfrak{X}_{P(v_{\pm})}
and the map
u
is continuous at the node, that is,

u(w^{+}_{e})=u(w^{-}_{e})\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(v_{\pm})},

(4.17)

—

(Matching at tropical nodes) for a tropical edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
,
the projected tropical
evaluations (
4.16
) on the nodal lifts are equal, that is,

(\pi_{\mathcal{T}(e)}^{\perp}\circ u_{v_{+}})(w^{+}_{e})=(\pi_{\mathcal{T}(e)}^{\perp}\circ u_{v_{-}})(w^{-}_{e}),

(4.18)

that is stable (as in Definition
4.15
(
b
)). This
ends the Definition.

Definition 4.14
.

(Broken map) A broken map
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
is an
unframed broken map (Definition
4.13
) with the additional
data of a
framing
at tropical nodes
w_{e}
,

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. A framing is a linear isomorphism

{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}:T_{w^{+}_{e}}S_{v_{+}}\otimes T_{w^{-}_{e}}S_{v_{-}}\to\mathbb{C},

(4.19)

satisfying the following:
For any holomorphic
coordinate

z_{\pm}:(U_{w_{e}^{\pm}},w_{e}^{\pm})\to(\mathbb{C},0)

(4.20)

in the neighborhood of nodal lifts
w_{e}^{\pm}
that respect the framing, that is,

{\operatorname{d}}z_{+}(w^{+}_{e})\otimes{\operatorname{d}}z_{-}(w^{-}_{e})={\operatorname{fr}}_{e},

(4.21)

the following matching condition is satisfied at tropical nodes:

\lim_{z_{-}\to 0}z_{-}^{-\mathcal{T}(e)}u_{v_{-}}(z_{-})=\lim_{z_{+}\to 0}z_{+}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}u_{v_{+}}(z_{+}).{\@index{\@indexphrase{Matching condition at nodes}}}

(4.22)

The coordinates
z_{+}
,
z_{-}
are called
matching coordinates

at the node
w_{e}
.

We consider isomorphism classes of stable broken maps.

Definition 4.15
.

(Stability and isomorphism)

(a)

In a broken map
u
,
a surface component
\left.u\right|_{S_{v}^{\circ}}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
is

horizontally constant
if its projection to
X_{P(v)}
is constant.

(b)

(Stability)
A broken map
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
is required to be
stable
in the sense that
any surface component
S_{v}\subset C
on which the map

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to X_{P(v)}
is horizontally constant is stable
as a marked curve, and any tree component
T_{e}\subset C
on
which
\left.u\right|_{T_{e}}
is constant does not contain an infinite segment
\mathbb{R}\subset T_{e}
.

(c)

(Isomorphisms) An
isomorphism
between two unframed broken
maps
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
,
u^{\prime}:C^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{X}
is an isomorphism

\phi:C\to C^{\prime}
of treed disks (see

4.1
(
e
)) such that

u=u^{\prime}\circ\phi
. Two framed broken maps
(u,{\operatorname{fr}})
,
(u^{\prime},{\operatorname{fr}}^{\prime})
are
isomorphic if the preceding relation holds, and in addition for any
node
w
in
C
corresponding to an edge
e
in the underlying
graph, and lifts
w^{\pm}
,

{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}\circ({\operatorname{d}}\phi(w^{+})\otimes{\operatorname{d}}\phi(w^{-}))={\operatorname{fr}}_{e}^{\prime}
.

The stability condition on broken maps implies that the
automorphism group of any broken map is finite.

Remark 4.16
.

(Trivial cylinder components)
The stability condition implies
that a broken map can not have components without markings on which
the map is a trivial cylinder, that is, the map is of the form

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\times}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)},\quad z\mapsto z^{\mu}x

for some
\mu\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(v),\mathbb{Z}}
and
x\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
. Indeed, such
a component has two nodes and is horizontally constant. Trivial
cylinders with markings do not occur in zero and one-dimensional
moduli spaces of broken maps, as in Example
6.31
.

Remark 4.17
.

In this book, we only use the zero-dimensional
components of the moduli space, and the above notion of isomorphism
is sufficient. To construct higher-dimensional moduli spaces of isomorphism classes of broken maps, one also needs to take into account the symmetries of the target manifolds. In this case, one would define an isomorphism between two broken maps
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
,
u^{\prime}:C^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{X}

to be an isomorphism of domains
\phi:C\to C^{\prime}

and a tropical symmetry

(g_{v}\in T_{P(v),\mathbb{C}},v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma),z_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times},e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma))

intertwining the maps
u,u^{\prime}
in the sense that

u_{v}^{\prime}=g_{v}u_{v}\circ(\left.\phi\right|_{C_{v}}),\quad\forall v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma).

(4.23)

In fact, Theorem
8.2
shows that if a sequence of
maps in neck-stretched manifolds converges to a limit map whose tropical graph
\Gamma
is not rigid (and so, the group
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
of tropical symmetries is positive dimensional), the limit map
is determined only up to the action of the identity component
of the tropical symmetry group, denoted by

T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)\subset T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
.

Definition 4.18
.

(Area of a broken map)

The
area
of a surface component

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}

of a broken map is the symplectic area of its horizontal projection
\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to X_{P(v)}
. That is,

{\operatorname{Area}}(u_{v})=\langle{(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v})_{*}[S_{v}],\omega_{X_{P(v)}}}\rangle.

(4.24)

Note that the pairing is defined via a map

\phi:X_{P(v)}\to X_{P(v)}^{\omega}
that respects the cylindrical
structure (as made precise in Remark
3.57

(
a
)). Since at any puncture in
S_{v}^{\circ}
,

\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v}
is asymptotic to a trivial cylinder (see
(
4.15
)),
\phi(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v})
extends to a
continuous map
\overline{u}_{v}:S_{v}\to\overline{X}_{P(v)}^{\omega}
. A different
choice of
\phi
changes
\overline{u}_{v}
by a homotopy.

Remark 4.19
.

(Tropical graph as a combinatorial invariant) The tropical
graph of a broken map is a combinatorial invariant consisting of the
data of edge directions
\mathcal{T}(e)
,
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
and vertex
polytopes
P(v)
,
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
, since varying the tropical
positions of vertices does not produce a new broken map, see Remark
4.7
.

The following remarks are easy conclusions of the definition of broken maps, and are intended to help the reader process the definition.

Remark 4.20
.

(Continuity away from tropical nodes)
Suppose the tropical structure on a broken map is given by

\Gamma\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{tr}}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}}
where
{\operatorname{tr}}
is the
tropicalization map and
\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}}
is a tropical graph. The
domain of a broken map breaks up into connected components

C\backslash\{w_{e}:e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}=\cup_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}})}C_{v}^{\circ}.

For a vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}})
in the tropical graph, if

{\operatorname{tr}}^{-1}(v)
does not have disk vertices, then
C_{v}
is a nodal
sphere with punctures, with nodes corresponding to internal edges in

{\operatorname{tr}}^{-1}(v)
, and punctures corresponding to tropical edges
incident on
{\operatorname{tr}}^{-1}(v)
. If
{\operatorname{tr}}^{-1}(v)
has disk components then

C_{v}^{\circ}
is a treed disk with punctures. For any vertex

v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{tr}})
in the tropical graph, the restriction

u|C_{v}^{\circ}:C_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}

is continuous.

Remark 4.21
.

(Node matching for a single cut) In the case of a single cut, the
familiar form of the node matching condition from symplectic field
theory corresponds to the matching condition (
4.18
)
for unframed maps.
Indeed, for any edge
e
in a tropical graph,
\operatorname{codim}(P(e))=\dim T_{P(e)}=1
, and so,
T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}=T_{P(e),\mathbb{C}}
; the unframed matching condition (
4.18
) then translates to the matching of evaluations on
X_{P(e)}
, namely,

(\pi_{P(e)}\circ u_{v_{+}})(w^{+}_{e})=(\pi_{P(e)}\circ u_{v_{-}})(w^{-}_{e})
.

Definition 4.22
.

(Primitive direction, multiplicity of a tropical edge)
In a tropical graph
\Gamma
, the direction
\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}}\backslash\{0\}
of a tropical edge
e
can be written as a product

\mathcal{T}(e)=\mu_{e}\mathcal{T}(e)_{{\operatorname{prim}}}

of a primitive integer vector
\mathcal{T}(e)_{\operatorname{prim}}\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}}
,
called the
primitive direction
, and a positive integer

\mu_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
which is called the
multiplicity

of the edge
e
.

Remark 4.23
.

(Number of framings) Any unframed
broken map possesses framings. For an unframed broken map
u
of
type
\Gamma
, the number of framings is equal to

\Pi_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)}\mu_{e}
, where

\mu_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is the multiplicity of the edge
e

(Definition
4.22
). Indeed, the trivial cylinder

\mathbb{C}^{\times}\to T_{P(e)},\quad z\mapsto z^{\mathcal{T}(e)}

which is the one-parameter subgroup induced by the Lie algebra element
\mathcal{T}(e)
, is a
\mu_{e}
-cover.
As a consequence, if

{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}
is a framing for the node
w_{e}
, then

e^{2\pi ik/\mu_{e}}{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}
is also a framing for

k=0,1,\dots,\mu_{e}-1
.

Remark 4.24
.

(Extending broken map components over punctures) In the special
case that the compactifications of the components of the broken
manifold are manifolds, a broken map extends over lifts of nodal
points to yield
a map

u_{v}:S_{v}\to\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)},\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma).

(4.25)

Indeed, the existence of the tropical evaluation (
4.15
)
implies that, in
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
,
u_{v}
is asymptotically close to
trivial cylinders at punctures. If
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)}
has orbifold
singularities, that is, if the polytope
P(v)
is simple and not
Delzant, the extension (
4.25
) is defined on a domain curve
with orbifold singularities.
Since compactifications of cut spaces (
3.45
) are orbifolds, the projections extend over
punctures to yield
\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v}:S_{v}\to\overline{X}_{P(v)}
.
For any
nodal lift
w\in S_{v}
corresponding to an edge
e=(v,v^{\prime})
, we have

(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u)(w)\in X_{P(e)}\subset\overline{X}_{P(v)}.

(4.26)

This can be seen as follows: For each relative divisor

X_{Q}\subset\overline{X}_{P(v)}
, the normal vector
\mu_{Q}
in the moment
polytope lies in
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}/\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)}
, and a punctured neighborhood of

X_{Q}
in
X_{P(v)}
is a subset of a
T_{\mu_{Q},\mathbb{C}}
-fibration. If
the edge direction
\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e)}
is spanned by normal vectors

\mu_{Q_{1}},\dots,\mu_{Q_{k}}
, where each
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
is a facet of

P(v)
, then
X_{P(e)}=\cap_{i}X_{Q_{i}}
, and

(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u)(w)\in X_{P(e)}
since the map is
asymptotically close to a
T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
-orbit near the puncture

w
.

Remark 4.25
.

(Relationship of edge direction with intersection multiplicities)
Consider the special case when the compactifications
of the broken manifold are smooth manifolds.
Consider a component
u_{v}:S_{v}\to\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)}
of a broken map.
Suppose for a nodal lift
w\in S_{v}
corresponding to an edge

e=(v,v^{\prime})
,
u_{v}(w)
lies on the intersection of relative divisors

Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}\subset\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)}
. Furthermore, suppose

\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{k}\in\mathfrak{t}
are the primitive outward pointing normal
vectors to the facets of
{\tilde{P}}(v)
corresponding to the divisors

Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}
, and that the map
u_{v}
intersects the divisor

Y_{i}
with multiplicity
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Then we have the following
relation to the direction of the edge:

\sum\mu_{i}\nu_{i}=\mathcal{T}(e).

(4.27)

In the case when
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)}
is an orbifold, the intersection
multiplicities
\mu_{i}
may be fractional, though the relation
(
4.27
) still holds.
However, we do not use orbifold
intersection numbers in any of the proofs. This ends the Remark.

Remark 4.26
.

(Exponential convergence to a trivial cylinder at tropical
nodes) The removal of singularity of punctures from Remark

4.25
can alternately be stated as that a broken map is
asymptotically close to a trivial cylinder at the punctured
neighborhood of any nodal lift. For a tropical node
w_{e}

corresponding to
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
and holomorphic
coordinates

(s,t):U(w_{e}^{\pm})\backslash\{w_{e}^{\pm}\}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times S^{1}

(4.28)

in punctured neighborhoods of the nodal lifts, the limit

x_{e}^{\pm}:=\lim_{s\to\infty}e^{\pm\mathcal{T}(e)(s+it)}u_{v_{\pm}}(s,t)

exists and is a point in the
P(e)
-cylindrical end

U_{P(e)}(\mathfrak{X}_{P(v_{\pm})})
. Furthermore, the map
u_{v_{\pm}}

exponentially converges to a trivial cylinder with direction

\mathcal{T}(e)
in
the punctured neighborhood
U(w_{e}^{\pm})\backslash\{w_{e}^{\pm}\}
, that is,
there is a constant
c>0
such that

d(u_{v_{\pm}}(s,t),e^{\mp\mathcal{T}(e)(s+it)}x_{e}^{\pm})\leq ce^{-s},\quad s\geq s_{0},

using the cylindrical metric in
\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
. The proof is left to the reader.

Remark 4.27
.

(A different view of node matching)

In the special case
that
the multiple cut is a collection of orthogonally intersecting single
cuts, and each cut space is a manifold, the tropical node matching condition
(
4.22
)
splits into
the following conditions:

—

a
horizontal matching
condition on the intersection of
relative divisors, and

—

a
vertical matching
condition involving leading
order Taylor coefficients in the directions normal to each of the
relative divisors.

We consider a multiple cut given by a tropical moment map

\Phi:X\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
with cuts along the hypersurfaces
\Phi^{-1}_{i}(0)
,

i=1,\dots,k
. Denote the relative divisors by

Y_{i}:=\Phi^{-1}_{i}(0)/S^{1}
(though
Y_{i}
is a broken manifold).
Consider a broken map
u
with components
u_{+}
,
u_{-}
in the cut
spaces for the polytopes

P_{+}:=\cap_{i=1}^{k}\{\Phi_{i}\geq 0\},\quad P_{-}:=\cap_{i=1}^{k}\{\Phi_{i}\leq 0\}

respectively, sharing a node
w_{e}
. As discussed in Remark
4.25
, the map
u_{\pm}
extends over the puncture at the nodal point
w_{e}^{\pm}
. The
intersection multiplicities with the divisors
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}
are
the same for
u_{+}
,
u_{-}
, and are denoted by

\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. The matching condition consists of

—

(Horizontal matching) a horizontal condition which says
that

u_{+}(w^{+}_{e})=u_{-}(w^{-}_{e})\in X_{P(e)},

where
X_{P(e)}=\Phi^{-1}(0)/(S^{1})^{k}\simeq Y:=\cap_{i=1}^{k}Y_{i}
;

—

(Vertical matching) and a vertical condition which says
that for holomorphic coordinates

z_{\pm}:(U(w_{e}^{\pm}),w_{e}^{\pm})\to(\mathbb{C},0)

in a neighborhood of the nodal lifts that respect the framing (as
in (
4.21
)), for all
i
, the
(\mu_{i}+1)
-th
derivative normal to
Y_{i}
is equal for
u_{v_{+}}
and

u_{v_{-}}
. In the direction normal to
Y_{i}
, since derivatives up
to order
\mu_{i}
vanish, the
(\mu_{i}+1)
-th normal derivative, or
the
(\mu_{i}+1)
-jet normal to
Y_{i}
, denoted by

j^{\mu_{i}}_{Y_{i}}u_{\pm}(w_{e}^{\pm})\in N_{\pm}Y_{i}\backslash Y_{i}

is well-defined (see
[
26
, Section 6]
), where

N_{\pm}Y_{i}
is the normal bundle of
Y_{i}
in
\overline{X}_{P_{\pm}}
. The
vertical matching condition says that

j^{\mu_{i}}_{Y_{i}}u_{+}(w^{+}_{e})=j^{\mu_{i}}_{Y_{i}}u_{-}(w^{-}_{e}).

This view of the matching condition is relevant only in case of a
collection of orthogonal single cuts. In general cases, given a
node, both nodal lifts do not lie on the same set of relative
divisors. For example, in the broken map in Figure
2.22

corresponding to a Mikhalkin graph, there are edges of the form

—

e=(v_{+},v_{-})
,
\mathcal{T}(e)=(1,1)

—

with
P(v_{+})=P(v_{-})=P_{0}
which is the zero-dimensional
polytope; and

—

one of the lifts lies on a divisor of
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P_{0}}

corresponding to a facet with normal vector
(1,1)
and another
lift lies on the intersection of divisors corresponding to facets
with normal vectors
(0,-1)
and
(-1,0)
.

This ends the Remark.

Example 4.28
.

We unpack the horizontal and
vertical matching conditions from Remark
4.27
in the
first broken map in Figure
4.4
. The multiple cut
consists of two orthogonal cuts shown in Figure
1.2
, and
the node
w
lies between maps
u_{0}:C_{0}\to\overline{X}_{P_{2}}
,

u_{1}:C_{1}\to\overline{X}_{P_{0}}
. The nodes
u_{0}(w)
,
u_{1}(w)
lies in

\overline{X}_{P_{\cap}}
. Suppose the leading order Taylor term of
u_{0}
in
the direction normal to
X_{P_{12}}
resp.
X_{P_{23}}
is

a_{+}z_{+}^{\mu_{h}^{+}}
resp.
b_{+}z_{+}^{\mu_{v}^{+}}
, and suppose the
leading order Taylor term of
u_{1}
in the direction normal to

X_{P_{30}}
resp.
X_{P_{01}}
is
a_{-}z_{-}^{\mu_{h}^{-}}
resp.

b_{-}z_{-}^{\mu_{v}^{-}}
. Then the matching condition says that

—

(Matching of intersection multiplicities)
\mu_{h}^{+}=\mu_{h}^{-}
,
\mu_{v}^{+}=\mu_{v}^{-}
,

—

(Horizontal matching) the projections of the nodal evaluation maps to
X_{P_{\cap}}
are equal:

\pi_{P_{\cap}}(u(w^{+}))=\pi_{P_{\cap}}(u(w^{-}))\in X_{P_{\cap}}
,

—

(Vertical matching) and the leading order Taylor
coefficients are equal:
a_{+}=a_{-}
,
b_{+}=b_{-}
.

Abbildung 4.4:
Broken maps and their dual graphs in the broken manifold of Figure
3.9
.

Remark 4.29
.

(Comparison to Ionel’s refined matching) In
[
44
, p14]
,
Ionel states the edge-matching condition using a “refined
evaluation map”, which is analogous to the projected tropical
evaluation map defined in (
4.18
). Suppose a node

w_{e}
maps to the intersection of relative divisors
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}

and the intersection multiplicity with each of the divisors is

\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}
. (Here we work with the extension of the broken
map
u
over nodal lifts as in Remark
4.25
.) The
refined evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\operatorname{ref}}(u,0)
for the map
u
and the
nodal lift
0\in C
is a point in the weighted projective space

\operatorname{ev}_{\operatorname{ref}}(u,0)=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{P}_{(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})}(\oplus_{i}NY_{i}).

The point
\operatorname{ev}_{\operatorname{ref}}(u,0)
is well-defined since replacing the
domain coordinate
z
by
az
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
has the
effect of changing
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
to

(a^{\mu_{1}}x_{1},\dots,a^{\mu_{n}}x_{n})
. The projected tropical evaluation
in (
4.18
) and (
4.16
)

\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}(\operatorname{ev}^{\mu}(0))\in(Z\times\mathbb{R}^{n})/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}

is Ionel’s evaluation map written using cylindrical coordinates on
the target space, and the direction
\mathcal{T}(e)
of the edge
e
is the
vector
(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})
. The cylindrical viewpoint appears
more natural because in the image of the refined evaluation map none
of the coordinates
x_{i}
vanish. On the other hand, all points in
the cylinder
(Z\times\mathbb{R}^{n})/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
can possibly be in the
image of the evaluation map.

Remark 4.30
.

(Balancing property)
We discuss the generalization of the balancing condition
(
2.37
) for Mikhalkin graphs to broken maps.
First, consider the special case that the projection

(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v}):S_{v}^{\circ}\to X_{P(v)}
of a map component

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
is constant. Then, the edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
emanating from the vertex
v
satisfy

\sum_{e\ni v}\mathcal{T}(e)_{\operatorname{vert}}=0,

(4.29)

where
\mathcal{T}(e)_{{\operatorname{vert}}}\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)}
is the orthogonal projection of

\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e)}
to
\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)}
with respect to the inner product
(
3.10
) on
\mathfrak{t}_{P(e)}
.

We can generalize this property to non-constant projections,
assuming that the inner product (
3.10
) on
\mathfrak{t}
is rational,
in which case the compactification
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
of the broken
manifold is an orbifold, and
\overline{Z}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}
is an orbifold
torus bundle (see Remark
3.62
) for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
Then we have the equality

\sum_{e\ni v}\mathcal{T}(e)_{{\operatorname{vert}}}=c_{1}((\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v})^{*}\overline{Z}_{P(v)}\to\overline{X}_{P(v)})),

(4.30)

where

c_{1}((\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v})^{*}\overline{Z}_{P(v)}\to\overline{X}_{P(v)}))
is
the first Chern class of the pull-back bundle, viewed as a vector in

\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)}\simeq H^{2}(S_{v},\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)})
, and
S_{v}
is the possibly
orbifold compactification of
S_{v}^{\circ}
over which the map
u_{v}

extends.
Indeed, the map
u_{v}
gives a section of the
T_{P(v)}
-principal
bundle
(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v})^{*}\overline{Z}_{P(v)}\to\overline{X}_{P(v)}
on
the complement of nodal points
w_{e}
,
e\ni v
, and the monodromy
of the section around each such intersection is determined by

\mathcal{T}(e)_{\operatorname{vert}}
. This ends the Remark.

Remark 4.31
.

(A comparison with symplectic field theory) Holomorphic
buildings in symplectic field theory
[
11
]
are defined
slightly differently to broken maps on manifolds with a single
cut. The target space of a holomorphic building consists of
X^{+}
,

X^{-}
and
k-1
copies of the neck piece
Z(\mathbb{P}^{1})
for some

k\geq 1
:

\mathfrak{X}[k]:=\overline{X}_{+}\cup_{Y}Z(\mathbb{P}^{1})\cup_{Y}\dots\cup_{Y}Z(\mathbb{P}^{1})\cup_{Y}\overline{X}_{-},

and any pair of consecutive pieces are identified along a divisor

Y
. A holomorphic building
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}[k]
is a continuous map,
where nodes map to the divisor
Y
, and intersection multiplicities

m_{w_{e}^{\pm}}(u_{v_{\pm}},Y)
are equal on both sides.

A holomorphic building differs from a broken map in two ways: A
holomorphic building is a continuous map, and the data for a
holomorphic building includes an ordering for the neck piece
components. In the broken map view, this ordering is not important:
With suitable regularity assumptions a broken
u
map with
m

components in neck pieces can be glued to give a
2m
-dimensional
family of unbroken maps in
X^{\nu}
for any
\nu
. Any sequence of
maps
u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to X^{\nu}
lying in the glued family converges to
a broken map
u^{\prime}:C^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{X}
that is related to
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
by a
tropical symmetry (as in Definition
4.32
). In contrast,
the holomorphic building limits of different sequences in the glued
family may not all be the same, since the choice of the sequence

(u_{\nu})_{\nu}
determines the component of
\mathfrak{X}[k]
to which a curve
component
C_{v}\subset C
maps. For a broken map
u=(u_{v})
, the
ordering of the pieces
u_{v}
is not part of the data of the the map.
One effect of the differing definitions is the following: Unlike
holomorphic buildings, broken maps do not have components
u_{v}
that
are trivial cylinders in the sense that they map into a fiber of

\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)}
with only two marked points and so are unstable. In
holomorphic buildings, trivial cylinders have to be inserted
whenever there is a node
w_{e}
between components
C_{v_{+}},C_{v_{-}}

that are not in adjacent levels in order to achieve continuity.
This ends the Remark.

4.4
Symmetries of broken maps

The moduli space of broken maps of a fixed type
\Gamma
has the
action of a group, called the
tropical symmetry group
, arising
from torus actions on the “neck pieces” in the degeneration. The
tropical symmetry group
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
can be read off from the
tropical graph
\mathcal{T}
of
\Gamma
. The identity component of

T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
, is generated by the degrees of freedom of vertex
positions in the tropical graph
\mathcal{T}
. For example, the graph

\Gamma_{1}
in Figure
4.5
has one degree of freedom, and
therefore, the identity component of
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{1})
is a
one-dimensional complex torus. A tropical graph is
rigid
if there
is no way of moving the vertices while keeping the edge directions
fixed. Such graphs have a finite tropical symmetry group. For example,
in Figure
4.5
,
|T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{2})|=2
. The Mikhalkin
graphs from Section
2.4
give a class of examples of rigid
tropical graphs. We prove later in this section that the size of the
symmetry group for Mikhalkin graphs is equal to the multiplicity of
the graph.

The tropical symmetry group also incorporates a group action on the
framings of the broken map. Even one of the simplest kind of broken
maps occuring in a single cut, namely one whose tropical graph
consists of two vertices each lying in
P_{+}^{\vee}
and
P_{-}^{\vee}
(in
Figure
1.5
) connected by an edge of multiplicity
k

has a tropical group of size
k
arising from the action on framings.

Abbildung 4.5:
The dual complex
B^{\vee}
for the multiple cut
in Figure
1.2
is a
rectangle. The tropical graph
\Gamma_{1}
(left figure) is rigid, but

\Gamma_{2}
(right figure) is not rigid since the vertices inside the
square can be moved to the dotted positions.

Definition 4.32
.

(Tropical symmetry) A

tropical symmetry
for a tropical graph
\Gamma

is a tuple

(\underline{g},\underline{z})=((g_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)},(z_{e})_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}),\quad g_{v}\in T_{P(v),\mathbb{C}},\quad z_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}

consisting of a translation
g_{v}
for each vertex and a change of
local coordinate
z_{e}
for each tropical edge that satisfies

g_{v_{+}}g_{v_{-}}^{-1}=z_{e}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}\quad\forall e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma),

(4.31)

where we assume
z_{e}=1
for

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)\backslash\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. A tropical
symmetry
(g,z)
acts on a broken map
(u,{\operatorname{fr}})
as

u_{v}\mapsto g_{v}u_{v},\quad{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}\mapsto z_{e}{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}.

The group of tropical symmetries is denoted by

T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma):=\{((g_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)},(z_{e})_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)})|\text{\eqref{connecting}}\}.

(4.32)

The condition (
4.31
) is a necessary and sufficient
condition for the translations
(g_{v})_{v}
to preserve the matching
condition at nodes.

Remark 4.33
.

(Framing symmetry) There are finitely many tropical symmetries

(\underline{g},\underline{z})\in T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
, called
framing symmetries
,
for which the action on the unframed map
u
of type
\Gamma
is
trivial, that is
g_{v}=\operatorname{Id}
for all vertices
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
.
The group of such framing symmetries
(\underline{g},\underline{z})
is the
product

\prod_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{Z}_{\mu_{e}}\subset T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma),

(4.33)

where
\mu_{e}
is the multiplicity of the edge
e
(Definition

4.22
), and also the order of ramification of the broken
map at the nodal point
w_{e}
.

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
u_{0}
,
u_{1}
be broken maps of type
\Gamma
whose underlying
unframed broken maps are the same. Then,
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
are related
by an element of the framing symmetry group (from (
4.33
)).

Beweis.

By Remark
4.23
, the number of possible framings of an
unframed broken map is equal to

\prod_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)}\mu_{e}
. This number
is equal to the
size of the framing symmetry group from (
4.33
) which has a
free action on the set of broken maps.
∎

Remark 4.35
.

(Tropical symmetry for unframed broken maps) A tropical
symmetry on an unframed broken map is a tuple

\underline{g}=(g_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}

that satisfies

g_{v_{+}}g_{v_{-}}^{-1}\in\{z^{\mathcal{T}(e)}:z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}\quad\forall e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma).

(4.34)

The tropical symmetry group for unframed maps is equal to the
quotient of the tropical symmetry group by the group of framing
symmetries (
4.33
).

The next result shows that the identity component of the tropical
symmetry group is generated by tropical positions of vertices of the
tropical graph.

Lemma 4.36
.

(Tropical vertex positions generate tropical symmetries)

(a)

For a tropical graph
\Gamma
, the set of tropical vertex positions
\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)

(defined in
(
4.12
)) is convex.

(b)

If a
tropical graph
\Gamma
has two distinct tropical vertex position maps

(\mathcal{T}_{0}(v),v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)),\quad(\mathcal{T}_{1}(v),v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma))

then the difference
\mathcal{T}_{1}-\mathcal{T}_{0}
generates a real-two-dimensional
subgroup
\exp((\mathcal{T}_{1}-\mathcal{T}_{0})(\cdot))
of the tropical symmetry
group
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
(defined in (
4.32
)).

(c)

The subgroup

T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma):=\langle{\exp((\mathcal{T}_{1}-\mathcal{T}_{0})z)|\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\mathcal{W}(\Gamma),z\in\mathbb{C}}\rangle

(4.35)

generated by tropical vertex position maps
is the identity component of
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
.

Beweis.

For the first statement, if
\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)
are
vertex position maps for a tropical graph
\Gamma
, then for any

t\in[0,1]

(1-t)\mathcal{T}_{0}+t\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)

is also a vertex position map on
\Gamma
. Assume that
\mathcal{T}_{0}
,

\mathcal{T}_{1}
are distinct, and that
l_{e,i}\in\mathbb{R}
describe the
difference between the tropical vertex positions in the sense that

\mathcal{T}_{i}(v_{+})-\mathcal{T}_{i}(v_{-})=l_{i,e}\mathcal{T}(e),\quad i=0,1,e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma).

Then the set of elements

g:\mathbb{C}\to T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma),\quad z\mapsto\begin{cases}e^{(\mathcal{T}_{1}(v)-\mathcal{T}_{0}(v))z},\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\\
e^{(l_{1,e}-l_{0,e})z},\quad e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma)\end{cases}

(4.36)

is a non-trivial group of symmetries for broken maps modelled on

\Gamma
. In a similar way, one sees
that the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
is equal to the vector
space generated by differences of tropical vertex position maps

\mathcal{T}_{1}-\mathcal{T}_{0}
,
\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\mathcal{W}(\Gamma)
, from which
(
c
) follows.
∎

The following is a corollary of Lemma
4.36
.

Corollary 4.37
.

(Rigid tropical graphs) A
tropical graph is rigid if and only if its tropical symmetry group

T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
is finite.

Lemma 4.38
.

For any tropical graph
\Gamma
, the quotient

T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)/T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
is finite and the
group
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
has a finite number of connected components.

Beweis.

The quotient
T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)/T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
is discrete,
because by Lemma
4.36
,
T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
is the
identity component of
T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)
. Furthermore, every
connected component of
T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)
deformation retracts to a
component of the maximal compact subtorus

T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)^{im}:=\{(\underline{g},\underline{z})\in T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma):g_{v}\in T_{P(v)},|z_{e}|=1\}.

Indeed, an element
(g,z)\in T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)
can be written as

g_{v}=k_{v}e^{is_{v}},\quad k_{v}\in T_{P(v)},s_{v}\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)},\quad z_{e}=\theta_{e}e^{\alpha_{e}},\quad\theta_{e}\in S^{1},\alpha_{e}\in\mathbb{R}

The tuple
(k,\theta):=((k_{v})_{v},(\theta_{e})_{e})
is a tropical
symmetry element in
T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)^{im}
which is connected to

(g,z)
via the path

[0,1]\ni\tau\mapsto((k_{v}e^{i\tau s_{v}})_{v},(\theta_{e}e^{\tau\alpha_{e}})_{e})\in T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma).

The quotient
T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)/T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
is finite
because it is in bijection with the connected components of the
compact subgroup
T_{{\operatorname{trop}}}(\Gamma)^{im}
.
∎

Abbildung 4.6:
Rigid tropical graphs in Example
4.39
.

Example 4.39
.

The tropical graphs in Figure
4.6
are rigid, but have
non-trivial tropical symmetry groups. Suppose the tropical graph

\Gamma_{1}
has edge directions

\mathcal{T}(e_{1})=(-1,-1),\quad\mathcal{T}(e_{2})=(-1,2),\quad\mathcal{T}(e_{3})=(2,-1).

A tropical symmetry
(\underline{g},\underline{z})
on
\Gamma_{1}
satisfies the equations

g_{1}=g_{2}=g_{3}=\operatorname{Id},\quad g_{1}g_{0}^{-1}=z_{e_{1}}^{(-1,-1)},\quad g_{2}g_{0}^{-1}=z_{e_{2}}^{(-1,2)},\quad g_{3}g_{0}^{-1}=z_{e_{3}}^{(2,-1)},

and is therefore given by

g_{0}=(\omega,\omega),\quad z_{e_{1}}=z_{e_{2}}=z_{e_{3}}=\omega,

where
\omega\in e^{2\pi ik/3}
is a cube root of unity. Thus

|T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{1})|=3.

(4.37)

This tropical graph is similar to the example studied by
Abramovich-Chen-Gross-Siebert
[
2
, p51]
and Tehrani

[
33
, Section 6]
. The tropical graph
\Gamma_{2}
has edge
directions

\mathcal{T}(e_{1})=(-1,1),\quad\mathcal{T}(e_{2})=(-1,-1).

By a similar calculation
|T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{2})|=2
.

Recall from Section
2.4
that the multiplicity of a
Mikhalkin graph
\Gamma
is the product of multiplicities of the
vertices of
\Gamma
, that is,

\operatorname{mult}(\Gamma):=\prod_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}\operatorname{mult}(v).

Assuming that all vertices are trivalent, the multiplicity of a vertex

v
of
\Gamma
is the area of the parallelogram spanned by two of the
three edges incident on
v
. The next result, which was used in
Section
2.4
, shows that he multiplicity of the Mikhalkin
graph is equal to the size of the tropical symmetry group of the
augmented graph
\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
. We recall from the proof of Proposition

2.27
, that
\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
is a tropical graph for the
polyhedral decomposition of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
from Figure
2.21
. We
also recall that

\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})=\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Vert}_{\to}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})\cup\operatorname{Vert}_{1}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}),

where a vertex
v_{e}\in\operatorname{Vert}_{1}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
corresponds to a leaf
e
of the Mikhalkin graph,
and each vertex in
\operatorname{Vert}_{\to}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
has an interior marking, that is constrained to pass through a fixed point in the broken manifold.

Lemma 4.40
.

(Mikhalkin multiplicity and tropical symmetry)

Let
\Gamma
be a Mikhalkin graph all whose vertices are trivalent,
and whose leaf edges have multiplicity
1
. Let
\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
be
the augmentation of
\Gamma
. Then,

|T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})|=\operatorname{mult}(\Gamma).

Any two broken maps
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
modelled on
\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
are
related by a tropical symmetry
g\in T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
.

Beweis.

A tropical symmetry of a broken map with tropical graph

\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
is a tuple

(\underline{g},\underline{z})=((g_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})},(z_{e})_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})}),\quad g_{v}\in T_{P(v),\mathbb{C}},\quad z_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}

satisfying

g_{v_{+}}g_{v_{-}}^{-1}=z_{e}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}\quad\forall e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}),

(4.38)

see Definition
4.32
. We count the number of solutions of
(
4.38
). For vertices
v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\to}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})

containing markings, we must have
g_{v}=\operatorname{Id}
in order to ensure that
the evaluation of the marking satisfies the point constraint. We
solve for
(\underline{g},\underline{z})
, one vertex at a time, with vertex
ordering respecting the marking orientation defined
as part of the proof of Proposition
2.27
.
Consider a vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)

with incoming edges
e_{1}=(v_{1},v)
and
e_{2}=(v_{2},v)
, and outgoing
edge
e_{3}=(v,v_{3})
. Assuming the values of
g_{v_{1}}
,
g_{v_{2}}
to
be given, we solve the equations

g_{v}g_{v_{1}}^{-1}=z_{e_{1}}^{\mathcal{T}(e_{1})},\quad g_{v}g_{v_{2}}^{-1}=z_{e_{2}}^{\mathcal{T}(e_{2})}

for
g_{v}\in T_{P_{0},\mathbb{C}}\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
,
z_{e_{1}}
,

z_{e_{2}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and show that the number of solutions is

\operatorname{mult}(v)
. Use an integral basis of
\mathfrak{t}_{P_{0},\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
so that

\mathcal{T}(e_{1})=(p_{1},0),\quad\mathcal{T}(e_{2})=(q,p_{2})

for some integers

p_{1},p_{2},q
.
In this notation, the multiplicity
\operatorname{mult}(v)
is
|p_{1}p_{2}|
. We may
write
g_{v}=(g_{v}^{0},g_{v}^{1})
. We solve in the order
g_{v}^{1}
,
z_{e_{2}}
,

g_{v}^{0}
,
z_{e_{1}}
. For a fixed value of
g_{v_{1}}
,
g_{v_{2}}
, there
are
p_{1}p_{2}
solutions: There are
p_{1}
solutions for
z_{e_{2}}
, and
for each of these solutions, there are
p_{2}
solutions of
z_{e_{1}}
.
Thus we have shown that for a vertex

v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\subset\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
, the number of
solutions for
(g_{v},z_{e_{1}},z_{e_{2}})
is equal to the multiplicity of

v
. Finally, given a solution of (
4.38
) for vertices in
the set
\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Vert}_{\to}(\Gamma)
and connecting edges, it
remains to solve (
4.38
) for
g_{v}
,
z_{e(v)}
where

v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{1}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
is a univalent vertex corresponding to a
leaf of the Mikhalkin graph
\Gamma
and
e(v)\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})

is the edge incident on
v
. There is a unique solution of
(
4.38
) for
g_{v}\in T_{P(v),\mathbb{C}}
and

z_{e(v)}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
because the direction
\mathcal{T}(e(v))
of the edge

e(v)
is primitive, and
T_{P(v),\mathbb{C}}
is orthogonal to

T_{\mathcal{T}(e(v)),\mathbb{C}}
which contains the element
z^{\mathcal{T}(e(v))}_{e(v)}
.
This shows that

|T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})|=\prod_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}\operatorname{mult}(v).

To prove the second statement of the Lemma, consider two unframed
broken maps
u
,
u^{\prime}
modelled on
\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
. For vertices

v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\to}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
containing markings,
u_{v}=u_{v}^{\prime}
. Indeed,

u_{v}(z)=cz^{\mu}
for some
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and the point constraint
determines the constant
c\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
uniquely. For all other
vertices
v
, there exist elements
g_{v}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
satisfying

g_{v}u_{v}=u_{v}^{\prime}
as follows: For any trivalent vertex
v
we assume the
domain is parametrized so that
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
,
z_{3}\in C_{v}
are the
nodal points, and the edge incident at
z_{i}
has direction

\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then,

u_{v}=c\prod_{1\leq i\leq 3}(z-z_{i})^{\mu_{i}},\quad u_{v}^{\prime}=c^{\prime}\prod_{1\leq i\leq 3}(z-z_{i})^{\mu_{i}},

for some
c,c^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
, and therefore
g_{v}u_{v}=u_{v}^{\prime}
for
some
g_{v}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
. For a univalent vertex

v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{1}(\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}})
corresponding to an end of
\Gamma
, we
have
u_{v}(z)=c(z-z_{0})^{\mu}
,
u_{v}^{\prime}(z)=c^{\prime}(z-z_{0})^{\mu}
for some

c,c^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
, where
z_{0}
is the nodal point and

\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the direction of the edge
e(v)
.
The matching condition at nodes for unframed broken maps implies that
for any edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
, the condition (
4.34
) for a
tropical symmetry element for unframed maps, namely

g_{v_{+}}g_{v_{-}}^{-1}\in T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
, is satisfied. Finally
consider (framed) broken maps
u
,
u^{\prime}
modelled on
\Gamma_{\operatorname{aug}}
. The
preceding discussion for unframed maps implies that, after applying a
tropical symmetry element, the unframed maps underlying
u
,
u^{\prime}
are
the same, and therefore, by Lemma
4.34
,
u
,
u^{\prime}
are
related by a framing symmetry, which is an element of the tropical
symmetry group
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. This finishes the proof of the
Lemma.
∎

Kapitel 5
Stabilizing divisors

We use domain-dependent perturbations of the almost complex structure
to regularize the moduli space of pseudoholomorphic disks or spheres.
Moduli spaces of maps are defined modulo automorphisms of the domain
curve, and in order to define domain-dependent
perturbations on every component of the map, we would like the domain
components to be stable. As in Cieliebak-Mohnke
[
26
]
, we
consider domains with interior markings corresponding to points at
which the pseudoholomorphic map intersects a Donaldson divisor.
As a consequence, the almost complex structure can be perturbed to attain
regularity on all domain components. Of course, one could envision
using any of the current perturbation schemes to achieve virtual
fundamental chains; the stabilizing divisor approach is merely a
convenience.

5.1
Stabilizing divisors in symplectic manifolds

We recall results about stabilizing divisors for unbroken manifolds.
We consider divisors of Donaldson type, meaning Poincaré dual to

k[\omega]
where
k\gg 0
is a large integer. To define and construct
Donaldson divisors we assume that all the symplectic forms are
rational.

Definition 5.1
.

A symplectic orbifold
(X,\omega)
is

rational
if
[\omega]\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, and
integral
if

[\omega]\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Given an integral form
\omega
, a

prequantum line bundle
is a line-bundle-with-connection

\mathcal{L}_{X}\to X
whose curvature is

\operatorname{curv}(\mathcal{L}_{X}\to X)=(2\pi/i)\omega\in\Omega^{2}(X,i\mathbb{R}).

A Lagrangian
L\subset X
disjoint from the singular locus of
X
is

rational
if there exists a prequantum bundle
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, an integer

k
and a flat section
s:L\to\mathcal{L}_{X}^{\otimes k}
of the restriction

\mathcal{L}_{X}^{\otimes k}|L
.

A Donaldson divisor
D
is a
stabilizing divisor
if there is an
almost complex structure
J
for which
D
is
J
-holomorphic, and
D

intersects all
J
-holomorphic spheres and disks at sufficiently many
isolated points. In other words, the domains of
J
-curves can be
stabilized by treating intersection points with the divisor
D
as
markings.

Definition 5.2
.

(Stabilizing pair)

(a)

A
divisor
in
X
is a symplectic suborbifold

D\subset X
of real codimension
\operatorname{codim}(D)=2
such that
locally if
X
is the quotient of a symplectic manifold
X
by a
finite group
\Gamma
, then the divisor
D
is the quotient of a

\Gamma
-invariant divisor in
X
. A
Donaldson divisor
is a
divisor
D
that is Poincaré dual to
k[\omega]
for some large
integer
k>0
, and
k
is called the
degree
of the divisor

D
.

(b)

An almost complex structure
J
on
X
is
adapted
to
D

if
D
is a
J
-almost complex submanifold, that is,

J(TD)=TD
. The space of
D
-adapted almost complex structures is
denoted by

\mathcal{J}(X,D)=\{J\in\mathcal{J}(X)\ :\ J(TD)=TD\}.

(c)

Let
D\subset X-L
be a divisor disjoint from the Lagrangian

L
. For
E>0
, a
D
-adapted almost complex structure
J
is

E
-stabilizing
for the divisor
D
if and only if

(i)

(No non-constant spheres)
D
does not contain any

J
-holomorphic
orbifold sphere

u:C\to X
with symplectic
area
<E
(by an orbifold sphere we mean that
C
is an orbifold
Riemann surface of genus zero), and

(ii)

(Sufficient intersections) any
J
-holomorphic orbifold
sphere in
X
with symplectic area
<E
has at least
3

distinct points of intersection with
D
, and any

J
-holomorphic disk with symplectic area less than
E
has at
least one intersection with
D
.

A pair
(J,D)
consisting of a divisor
D
and an
\omega
-tamed

J\in\mathcal{J}(X,D)
is
stabilizing
if
J
is
E
-stabilizing for
all
E>0
. We call the divisor
D
in a stabilizing pair a
stabilizing divisor
.

Proposition 5.3
.

(Existence of a stabilizing pair,

[
16
, Section 4]
,
[
26
, Section 8]
)

Suppose
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(X)
is a rational symplectic form on an
orbifold
X
and
L\subset X
is a compact rational Lagrangian
submanifold disjoint from the singular locus of
X
. Then, there
exists

(a)

a divisor
D\subset X-L
such that
L
is exact in
X-D
; and

(b)

a tamed almost complex structure
J_{0}\in\mathcal{J}(X,D)
such
that
(J_{0},D)
is a stabilizing pair and for every
E>0
there is
an open neighbourhood

\mathcal{J}(X,D;J_{0},E)\subset\{J\in\mathcal{J}(X,D)\ :\ J|TD=J_{0}|TD\}

of
J_{0}
consisting of
E
-stabilizing almost complex structures
adapted to
D
.

The proof of the Proposition is outlined below. The construction of a
stabilizing pair uses the following notion of a degree bound.

Definition 5.4
.

A constant
k_{*}>0
is a

degree bound
for a tamed almost complex structure
J
on

(X,\omega)
if for any
J
-holomorphic orbifold sphere
u:C\to X
,

c_{1}(u):=\int_{C}u^{*}c_{1}(TX)\leq k_{*}\omega(u).

Lemma 5.5
.

(Existence of uniform degree bounds) (
[
26
, Lemma 8.11]
)

Let
J_{0}
be a compatible almost complex structure on the compact
symplectic orbifold
(X,\omega)
. For any
0<\epsilon<1
, there exists a
constant
k_{*}
which is a degree bound for all tamed almost complex
structures
J
satisfying
\lVert J-J_{0}\rVert_{C^{0}(X)}<\epsilon
.

The proof in
[
26
]
, carried out for the manifold case,
extends verbatim to the case of orbifolds. We reproduce the proof
since an adaptation of the technique is used in the case of broken
manifolds.

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Let
\gamma\in\Omega^{2}(X)
be a closed two-form in the class of the
first Chern class
c_{1}(TX)\in H^{2}(X)
. Using the norm

|v|^{2}:=\omega(v,J_{0}v)
on
TX
, and a
C^{0}
norm on forms, for any

J\in B_{\epsilon}(J_{0})
and
v\in TX
, we have the bounds

\displaystyle\gamma(v,Jv)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\gamma\rVert(1+\lVert J-J_{0}\rVert)|v|^{2}\leq\lVert\gamma\rVert(1+\epsilon)|v|^{2},

\displaystyle\omega(v,Jv)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\lVert J-J_{0}\rVert)|v|^{2}\geq(1-\epsilon)|v|^{2}.

Define
k_{*}:=\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}
.
We have

\gamma(v,Jv)\leq k_{*}\omega(v,Jv).

For a
J
-holomorphic orbifold sphere
u:C\to X
this implies

c_{1}(u)\leq k_{*}\omega(u)
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

(Using the degree bound for
stabilizing)
The degree bound on almost complex structures is related to the
stabilizing property in Definition
5.2
as follows. In
the manifold case, by
[
26
, Lemma 8.13]
, if a divisor
D

is Poincaré dual to
k[\omega]
, and a
D
-adapted tamed almost
complex structure
J
has a degree bound
k_{*}
satisfying

k>2\max\{k_{*},k_{*}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}(\dim(X))-2\},

(5.1)

then the expected dimension of moduli space of
J
-holomorphic
spheres that are not stabilized by
D
is negative. The same holds
in the orbifold case also. Indeed, for an orbifold sphere

u:C\to X
, in the Hirzebruch-Riemann-Roch formula for the expected
dimension of the moduli space of maps, the Chern number
c_{1}(u^{*}TX)

is replaced by the first Chern number of the “de-singularization”
of
u^{*}TX
, and the latter is lesser than the former, see

[
19
, p24]
,
[
20
, Proposition 4.2.1]
. Therefore if

J
were chosen generically,
(J,D)
would be a stabilizing pair.

Outline of proof of Proposition
5.3
.

In the case when
X
is a manifold, the adaptation
of Donaldson’s construction in Auroux-Gayet-Mohsen

[
5
]
produces an approximately holomorphic
divisor in
X-L
. By
[
16
, Theorem 3.6]
, if the
Lagrangian
L
is rational, then for any divisor
D
in
X-L

produced by Auroux-Gayet-Mohsen
[
5
]
,
L
is
exact in
X-D
. (We refer the reader to
[
16
, Example 3.2]

for the definition of “exactness”.) Exactness allows the following
relation of area to intersection numbers: If
L
is exact in

X-D
, then, the intersection number of a disk

u:(C,\partial C)\to(X,L)
with the divisor
D
is proportional to
the area of the disk: If
[D]^{\vee}=k[\omega]
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
then,

\#u^{-1}(D)=k\int_{C}u^{*}\omega.

(5.2)

See
[
16
, Lemma 3.4]
. As a consequence, for any
J
that is
adapted to
D
, any non-constant
J
-holomorphic disk is
automatically stabilized since it has at least one marked point.

Donaldson’s divisor construction is extended to the orbifold case by
Gironella-Muñoz-Zhou
[
37
]
. Since the Lagrangian
is smooth the modifications of Auroux-Gayet-Mohsen

[
5
]
do not interfere with the arguments in
Gironella-Muñoz-Zhou
[
37
]
. The relation between
symplectic area and the number of divisor intersections extends to
the orbifold case if the Lagrangian does not contain orbifold
singularities, the proof from
[
16
]
carries over verbatim.

To construct a stabilizing pair one starts with a preliminary almost
complex structure
J^{\operatorname{pre}}
that is
\omega
-compatible. For any

\epsilon>0
there exists a constant
k_{*}:=k_{*}(\epsilon,J^{\operatorname{pre}})
that is a
degree bound for any
\omega
-tamed almost complex structure
J
on
X

satisfying
\lVert J-J^{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{C^{0}}\leq\epsilon
. This fact gives enough
wiggle room to find a stabilizing pair as follows: For any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0

there is a constant
k_{d}(\epsilon^{\prime})
such that if the degree of the
Donaldson divisor
D
is
\geq k_{d}(\epsilon^{\prime})
, then there is a tamed
almost complex structure
J_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(J^{\operatorname{pre}})
that is

D
-adapted. We choose the degree
k
of the Donaldson divisor to
be high enough that it satisfies the bound (
5.1
), that
is,

k>2\max\{k_{*},k_{*}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}(\dim(X))-2\}

and
k\geq k_{d}(\epsilon/2)
. Then, there is an open subset of
\mathcal{J}(X,D)

contained in
B_{\epsilon}(J^{\operatorname{pre}})
, and for any

J\in\mathcal{J}(X,D)\cap B_{\epsilon}(J^{\operatorname{pre}})
,
k_{*}
is a degree bound in the
sense of Definition
5.4
. Therefore by Remark

5.6
, for a generic
J_{0}
in

\mathcal{J}(X,D)\cap B_{\epsilon}(J^{\operatorname{pre}})
, the pair
(J_{0},D)
is stabilizing for
all orbifold spheres. See
[
26
, Section 8]
for details.
∎

5.2
Cylindrical almost complex structures, without gluability

Donaldson’s construction of approximately holomorphic divisors
requires a rational symplectic manifold with a compatible almost
complex structure. Broken manifolds with cylindrical almost complex
structures, as defined in Chapter
3
, do not meet this
requirement because they do not have natural embeddings into
symplectic broken manifolds. We define a modified version of
cylindricity, called
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindricity
for almost complex
structures, so that symplectic broken manifolds possess

\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex structures. We point out that an

\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex structure is not
gluable
,
that is, it can not be glued on the ends to yield
neck-stretched almost complex structures on
X^{\nu}
. Indeed, the

\mathfrak{t}
-inner product underlying an
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex
structure is not the same for all the components
\overline{X}_{P}
of the
broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
, and has the following form.

Definition 5.7
.

(
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product)

Let
g_{Q}^{P}:\mathfrak{t}_{Q}\times\mathfrak{t}_{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}
be a collection of inner products for all
Q\subset P
, where
P\in\mathcal{P}^{0}
ranges over top-dimensional polytopes, that satisfy

(a)

(Restriction)

R\subset Q\implies g_{R}^{P}|\mathfrak{t}_{Q}=g_{Q}^{P}
; and

(b)

(Orthogonality) for
Q\subset P
,
\dim(Q)=0
, which is the intersection of facets
Q=\cap_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}
of
P
, the inner product
g_{Q}^{P}
satisfies the condition that the outward normals to
Q_{i}
are orthogonal.

We define symplectic cylindrical structure maps on symplectic cut spaces
\{\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
using
\mathfrak{X}
-inner products for the consistency condition.

Definition 5.8
.

(
\mathfrak{X}
-symplectic cylindrical structure maps)

For any top-dimensional polytope
P
, let
(\phi^{P}_{Q})_{Q\subset P}

be a collection of symplectic cylindrical structure maps

U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q})\xrightarrow{\phi^{P}_{Q}}((\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P\times\overline{Z}_{Q}^{\omega})/\sim,\omega_{{\tilde{Q}}}),\quad\omega_{{\tilde{Q}}}:=\omega_{X_{Q}}+d\langle{\alpha_{Q}^{P},\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}}}\rangle

(5.3)

on neighborhoods
U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})\subset\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
of
\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
, where

—

\overline{Z}_{Q}^{\omega}\to\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
is the
T_{Q}
-bundle from (
3.17
),

—

\alpha_{Q}^{P}\in\Omega^{1}(\overline{Z}_{Q}^{\omega},\mathfrak{t}_{Q})
is a connection one-form, where the collection

(\alpha_{Q}^{P})_{Q\subset P}
satisfies the consistency condition
(
3.23
) with respect to the
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product; and

—

\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}}:\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\to\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}
is the
projection on
\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P\subset\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
, and so,

\langle\alpha_{Q},\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}}\rangle
is a one-form on

(\overline{Z}_{Q}^{\omega}\times\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P)
;

—

for any facet
R\subset P
with
Q\subseteq R
,
the equivalence
\sim
mods out the boundary
\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}R\subset\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P

by the action of
T_{R}\simeq S^{1}
; and

1
1

1

By Lerman’s construction
(\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P\times\overline{Z}^{\omega}_{Q},\omega_{{\tilde{Q}}})/\sim
is a manifold resp. orbifold if
\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P
is Delzant resp. simple.

—

\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
is identically mapped by
\phi_{Q}^{P}
to

Q\times\overline{Z}^{\omega}_{Q}/T_{Q}
.

The map
\phi_{Q}^{P}
induces a projection map

\pi_{Q}^{P}:U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})\to\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}.

(5.4)

Finally, we define
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible almost complex structures. In
addition to being
\omega
-compatible on each piece, in neighborhoods

U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})
of boundary submanifolds these almost
complex structures are integrable on the fibers of the map
\pi_{Q}^{P}
.

Definition 5.9
.

(
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex
structures) Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a broken symplectic
manifold equipped with
\mathfrak{X}
-symplectic cylindrical structure maps
as in (
5.3
) and resulting projection maps

\pi_{Q}^{P}:U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})\to\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}

on neighborhoods

U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})\subset\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
of

\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
for
Q\subset P
.

(a)

(
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical)

An
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical

almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}=(\mathfrak{J}_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathfrak{X}
consists
of a cylindrical almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}_{P}
on each compactified component
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
of
\mathfrak{X}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
whose projection to
\overline{X}_{P}
(as in (
3.18
)) is denoted by
J_{P}
, and the collection
(J_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
is such that

(i)

(Restriction)
for any pair
Q\subset P
of polytopes,

\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
is a
J_{P}
-holomorphic submanifold of
\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
,
and
J_{P}|\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}=J_{Q}
, and

(ii)

(Cylindrical structure) for any pair
Q\subset P
of
polytopes, the fibers of
\pi_{Q}^{P}
are
\mathfrak{J}
-holomorphic and
\mathfrak{J}

is integrable on each of the fibers.

(b)

(
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatibility and tamedness)

An
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex
structure
\mathfrak{J}=(\mathfrak{J}_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
is
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible
resp.

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-tamed
if
for any
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
J_{P}
is
\omega_{X_{P}}
-compatible resp.
\omega_{X_{P}}
-tamed. The space of

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible resp.
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-tamed almost complex structures is denoted by

\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}_{\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}}(\mathfrak{X})\quad\text{resp.}\quad\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}_{\omega_{\mathfrak{X}},\tau}(\mathfrak{X}).

Remark 5.10
.

A collection of
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex structures can
not be glued along the necks because the underlying
\mathfrak{t}
-inner
products and connection one-forms vary across cut spaces.

Lemma 5.11
.

An
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible almost complex structure exists.

Beweis.

Starting from a broken symplectic manifold
\mathfrak{X}
, we construct an
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product,
\mathfrak{X}
-symplectic cylindrical structure maps, and an
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}
. We first construct these structures in the neighborhoods of vertices
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{(0)}
. Consider a top-dimensional polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
and a vertex
Q\in P
which is the intersection of facets
Q_{1},\dots Q_{q}\subset P
.
The symplectic cylindrical structure map at the corner
Q
is a

T_{Q}
-equivariant symplectic embedding

U_{X^{\omega}_{Q}}\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}\xrightarrow{\phi_{Q}^{P}}(\mathbb{C}^{q}/\Gamma,\omega_{\operatorname{std}}),

where
\Gamma
is a finite group acting on
\mathbb{C}^{q}
(
\Gamma
is
trivial if
Q
is a smooth corner of
P
as in (
3.2
)),

T_{Q}
is the torus acting on
U_{X^{\omega}_{Q}}\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
with an
identification
T_{Q}=(S^{1})^{q}/\Gamma
, and
(S^{1})^{q}
has a standard
action on
\mathbb{C}^{q}
that descends to a
T_{Q}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{q}/\Gamma
.
On
U_{X^{\omega}_{Q}}\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
, we define the almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}
by pulling back via
\phi_{Q}^{P}
; the inner product
g_{Q}^{P}
is automatically defined. We may similarly define

\mathfrak{J}
for all vertex neighborhoods
U_{X^{\omega}_{Q}}\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
,
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{(0)}
so that
the
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product satisfies the
(Restriction)

condition.

With the
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product fixed, we proceed to extend the

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}
to all of the
broken manifold by induction on the face structure of the polytopes
in the polyhedral decomposition. Consider a polytope

Q\in\mathcal{P}\backslash\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
that is not top-dimensional, and assume
that an
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible collection of almost complex
structures

\mathfrak{J}|\left(\cup_{R\subset Q,P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}:Q\subset P}U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{R}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})\right)

(5.5)

is already defined. First, we extend

\mathfrak{J}|\cup_{R\subset Q}U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{R}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q})
to an

\omega_{X_{Q}}
-compatible almost complex structure over
\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
.
Next, we extend the definition of
\mathfrak{J}
to neighborhoods

U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})
for any
P\supset Q
,
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
as
follows. Suppose
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{q}\in\mathcal{P}
are facets of
P
such
that
\cap_{i}Q_{i}=Q
. Identify

T_{Q}\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{q}T_{Q_{i}}\simeq(S^{1})^{q}
.
Choose a symplectic cylindrical structure map
\phi_{Q}^{P}
(as in
(
5.3
))
which is consistent with the
existing maps, and let
\pi_{Q}^{P}
be the resulting projection map
as in (
5.4
).
From
\phi_{Q}^{P}

we obtain a family of
T_{Q}
-equivariant symplectic embeddings
for
x\in\overline{X}_{Q}^{\omega}

(\pi_{Q}^{P})^{-1}(x)\xrightarrow{\phi_{x}}(\mathbb{C}^{q}/\Gamma,\omega_{\operatorname{std}})

where
\Gamma
is a finite group and there is an identification
T_{Q}\simeq(S^{1})^{q}/\Gamma
so that

T_{Q}
has a standard action on
\mathbb{C}^{q}/\Gamma
.
The embeddings
\phi_{x}
vary smoothly with
x\in Q
, and

(\pi_{Q}^{P})^{-1}(x)\cap\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q_{i}}
is mapped to the subspace

\{z_{i}=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{q}
. The fibers of the projection

\pi_{Q}^{P}:U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})\to\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
are equipped with the
complex structure pulled back by
\phi_{x}
. For any

y\in U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})
, on the
\omega
-complement of

\operatorname{ker}(d\pi_{Q}^{P})_{y}
in the tangent space
T_{y}\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
, define

\mathfrak{J}
to be equal to
\mathfrak{J}_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\pi_{Q}^{P}(y))
.
The definition of
\mathfrak{J}
in neighborhoods of
\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}
agrees with
the existing definition in neighborhoods of
\overline{X}^{\omega}_{R}
for any

R\subset Q
. Indeed, for any
R\subset Q
, and top-dimensional
polytope
P\supset Q
, the definition of the
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product
implies that
\mathfrak{J}|U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})
agrees with

\mathfrak{J}|U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{R}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})
from (
5.5
) in the overlaps. In
the concluding step of the induction, for each top-dimensional
polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
, we extend

\mathfrak{J}|\cup_{Q\subset P}U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P})
to an

\omega_{X_{P}}
-compatible almost complex structure on
\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}
.
∎

5.3
Stabilizing divisors in broken manifolds

In this Section, we construct stabilizing divisors in a broken
manifold by a modification of Donaldson’s construction. A broken
divisor
\mathfrak{D}
in a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
is a Donaldson divisor
D_{P}

in each cut space
\overline{X}_{P}
of
\mathfrak{X}
that is cylindrical in the ends
of
\overline{X}_{P}
. Such submanifolds are constructed as approximately
holomorphic submanifolds with respect to a compatible almost complex
structure.

Definition 5.12
.

(Broken Donaldson divisor) Let
(X,\mathcal{P})
be a tropical Hamiltonian
manifold, where on any symplectic cut space
\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
,

P\in\mathcal{P}
, the symplectic form
\omega_{X_{P}}
is rational. Let

\mathfrak{X}:=\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
be the broken manifold in the sense of Definition

3.53
. For
k\gg 0
, a
broken Donaldson divisor

of degree
k
on
\mathfrak{X}
is a collection of codimension two
submanifolds

\mathfrak{D}=\{\mathfrak{D}_{P}\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P},\quad P\in\mathcal{P}\}

such that

(a)

(Cylindricity on neck pieces) for any
P\in\mathcal{P}
, there is a codimension two submanifold

D_{P}\subset X_{P}\quad\text{such that}\quad\mathfrak{D}_{P}=\pi_{P}^{-1}(D_{P}),

where
\pi_{P}:\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to X_{P}
is the projection map;

(b)

(Cylindricity on ends) for any pair
of polytopes
Q\subset P
, the divisor
D_{P}\subset X_{P}
is

Q
-cylindrical in the
Q
-cylindrical end
U_{Q}(X_{P})
, that is,

D_{P}\cap U_{Q}(X_{P})=(\pi_{Q}^{P})^{-1}(D_{Q})
, where the projection

\pi_{Q}^{P}:U_{Q}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}
is from (
3.56
); and

(c)

(Poincaré dual) for any map
F:X_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}

that respects the cylindrical structure at the ends of
X_{P}
(in
the sense of Remark
3.57
(
a
)), the
closure
\overline{F(D_{P})}\subset\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
is a suborbifold that
is Poincaré dual to
k[\omega_{X_{P}}]
.

In the rest of this section, we construct a stabilizing divisor in the
cut spaces of a broken manifold. The construction of the divisors is
via a slight modification of Donaldson’s technique
[
29
]
. We
give an outline of Donaldson’s construction following Auroux

[
4
]
. Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold with a
compatible almost complex structure
J
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{X}\to X
be a
Hermitian line-bundle with connection
\alpha
over
X
whose
curvature two-form
\operatorname{curv}(\alpha)
satisfies

\operatorname{curv}(\alpha)=(2\pi/i)\omega
. Since our symplectic manifolds are
rational we may always assume this to be the case after taking a
suitable integer multiple of the symplectic form. We will construct
approximately holomorphic sections
s_{k}
of the line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}

for large
k
that are transverse in the sense that

|\overline{\partial}s_{k}|\ll|\partial s_{k}|
on the zero set
s_{k}^{-1}(0)
. Then
for large enough
k
the zero set
s_{k}^{-1}(0)
is transversally cut
out, and is a divisor of
X
with degree
k
. To study sections on the
bundle
\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}
, we use the metric

g_{k}:=k\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)

on
X
. Under this metric, the effect of the non-integrability of
J

becomes negligible as
k
increases. We define the notions of

approximate holomorphicity
and
transversality
:

Definition 5.13
.

(Asymptotically holomorphic sequences of
sections) Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold with

\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J
and a prequantum line
bundle
\mathcal{L}_{X}\to X
. Let
(s_{k})_{k\geq 0}
be a sequence of
sections of
\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}\to X
.

(a)

The sequence
(s_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is
asymptotically
holomorphic
if there exists a constant
C
and integer
k_{0}
such
that for
k\geq k_{0}
,

|s_{k}|+|\nabla s_{k}|+|\nabla^{2}s_{k}|\leq C,\quad|\overline{\partial}s_{k}|+|\nabla\overline{\partial}s_{k}|\leq Ck^{-1/2}.

(5.6)

(b)

The sequence
(s_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is
uniformly transverse
to

0
if there exists a constant
\eta
independent of
k
, and

k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for any
x\in X
and
k\geq k_{0}
with

|s_{k}(x)|<\eta
, the derivative
\nabla s_{k}
of
s_{k}
is
surjective at any point and satisfies
|\nabla s_{k}(x)|\geq\eta
.

In both definitions, the norms of the derivatives
\nabla s_{k}
are
evaluated using the metric
g_{k}=k\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)
.

Donaldson’s construction uses a family of asymptotically holomorphic
sections on
\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}
, that are concentrated at a given point in
X
:
The Gaussian section centered at
x\in X
is constructed by choosing
Darboux coordinates
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}):(U_{x},x)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
that are
approximately holomorphic in the following sense:
Assuming that the Darboux coordinates map to a neighborhood
B\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
of the origin, and

\psi:\mathbb{C}^{n}\supset B\to X
is the inverse map,
then the norm
|\psi^{*}J-i|
of
the difference between the complex structures
\psi^{*}J
and
i
is at
most
c|z|
, where
c
is a uniform constant independent of
x
, and
the derivative
|\nabla(\psi^{*}J-i)|
is uniformly bounded. We choose
a trivialization of the bundle
\mathcal{L}_{X}
so that the Hermitian
connection is
\sum_{i}(z_{i}d\overline{z}_{i}-\overline{z}_{i}dz_{i})
plus terms of
higher order in
z,\overline{z}
. The Gaussian section is then defined as

\sigma_{k,x}:=\beta_{k,x}\cdot e^{-k|z|^{2}}

(5.7)

on
U_{x}
, where
\beta_{k,x}
is a cut-off function vanishing at a

g_{k}
-distance of
k^{1/6}
from
x
, and extended by zero outside

U_{x}
.

The globalization process in Donaldson’s construction uses the
following result:

Lemma 5.14
.

(Quantitative Sard’s theorem,

[
29
, Theorem 20]
) Suppose
0<\delta<\frac{1}{4}
,
and
f:B_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
is a function defined on a ball

B_{+}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
of radius
\frac{11}{10}
that satisfies

\lVert f\rVert_{C^{1}}\leq\eta
, where

\eta:=\delta\log(\delta^{-1})^{-p}
. Then, there exists

w\in\mathbb{C}
,
|w|\leq\delta
such that
f-w
is
\eta
-transverse
to
0
over the interior ball
B
of radius
1
.

Lemma
5.14
is used to modify an approximately
holomorphic section in order to achieve uniform transversality in the
neighborhood of a given point: Given an asymptotically holomorphic
section
s_{k}:X\to\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}
and a point
x\in X
, by applying Lemma

5.14
to the section

f_{k}:=s_{k}/\sigma_{k,x}:B_{g_{k}}(x,\frac{11}{10})\to\mathbb{C}
, we obtain a
constant
w_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
f_{k}-w_{k}
is uniformly transverse to
the zero section. As a consequence, the difference
s_{k}-w_{k}\sigma_{k,x}

is also uniformly transverse to the zero section in a neighborhood of

x
.

Uniform transversality on the entire manifold is obtained by
iteratively adding successively smaller contributions, so that the
transversality over previous neighborhoods is not disturbed. In
particular, Donaldson’s construction chooses a lattice of points

\Lambda_{k}\subset X
such that
X
is covered by the unit balls

B_{g_{k}}(x,1)
,
x\in\Lambda_{k}
; and partitions the lattice into
N

sets (where
N
is
k
-independent)

\Lambda_{k}=I_{1}^{k}\cup\dots\cup I_{N}^{k}

such that any two points in a set
I_{j}^{k}
are separated by a uniform

g_{k}
-distance guaranteeing the following: For any
I_{j}^{k}
, the
constants
w_{k,x}
for the balls centered at points
x\in I_{j}
can
be chosen simultaneously and independently of each other. If

j_{1}<j_{2}
, the constants
w_{k,x}\in\mathbb{C}
for
x\in I^{k}_{j_{2}}
are
chosen to be small enough to not break the transversality in the balls
belonging to
I_{k}^{j_{1}}
. We start with the zero section, and run the
iteration described above. The iteration terminates in
N
steps,
which is
k
-independent, and the resulting section is a sum

\sigma_{k}:=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{k}}w_{k,x}\sigma_{k,x}.

that is uniformly transverse on
X

for large
k
.

Remark 5.15
.

In case of a symplectic orbifold
(X,\omega)
, we consider the
pre-quantum orbibundle
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, and a sequence of sections

s_{k}:X\to\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}
for
k
such that
\mathcal{L}_{X}^{k}
is a line bundle on

X
. Notions of asymptotic holomorphicity and transversality extend
naturally to the orbifold case and are defined in
Gironella-Muñoz-Zhou
[
37
]
.

Next, we give a preliminary result constructing a gluable cylindrical
almost complex structure on the broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
, that will be
used to construct Donaldson divisors. We start with compatible almost
complex structures on the symplectic cut spaces (as in Definition

5.9
and constructed in Lemma
5.11
), and
produce gluable almost complex structures by twisting the inner
product on
\mathfrak{t}
in a truncation of the cylindrical end.

Lemma 5.16
.

(Taming map) Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a broken manifold with cylindrical
end
U_{Q}(X_{P})\subset X_{P}
and projections

\pi_{Q}:U_{Q}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}
for each pair
Q\subset P
, and an

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}=(\mathfrak{J}_{P})_{P}
with projections
J_{P}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
, as in
Definition
5.9
. Then, there exist

(1)

truncated cylindrical ends

U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})\subset U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})\subset U_{Q}(X_{P}),

(2)

an almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{J}_{P}^{\prime})_{P}
on
\mathfrak{X}
with projections
(J_{P}^{\prime})_{P}
, that is
strongly compatible (as in Definitions
3.53
(
d
),
3.49

(
b
)) and
X
-cylindrical (Definition
3.53
(
d
))
on the ends

U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
for all
Q\subset P
, and

(3)

maps

\phi_{P}:X_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}

such that

(a)

on
X_{P}\backslash U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
,
\phi_{P}
is a diffeomorphism onto
X_{P}^{\omega}
, and

\phi_{P}^{*}J_{P}=J_{P}^{\prime}
, and

(b)

on
U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
,
\phi_{P}
is
projection to
X_{Q}
, that is,

\phi_{P}|U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})=\phi_{Q}\circ\pi_{Q}
.

Beweis.

Starting from an
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product on the cylindrical end, we
define domain-dependent inner products that are gluable near the
infinite ends. Choose truncated cylindrical ends
U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
,

U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
for all pairs
Q\subset P
, and choose
domain-dependent inner products

g_{Q}^{P}:U_{Q}(X_{P})\to(t_{Q}\otimes t_{Q})^{\vee},

each of which is

—

constant and equal to the
X
-inner product
g_{Q}
from
(
3.10
) on
U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
; and

—

is constant and equal to an
\mathfrak{X}
-inner product (Definition

5.7
)
g_{Q}^{P}
on
U_{Q}(X_{P})\backslash U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
.

On cylindrical ends, the maps
\phi_{P}
are determined by maps on
cones. For example, for a pair
Q\subset P
, on the domain of
\phi_{P}
, we have

U_{Q}(X_{P})=(0,\infty)^{k}\times(Z_{Q}/T_{P}),\quad U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})=\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(\delta_{i},\infty)\times(Z_{Q}/T_{P}),

(5.8)

where
k=\operatorname{codim}_{P}(Q)
, and on the target space of
\phi_{P}
,

U_{\overline{X}_{Q}^{\omega}}\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}=(\Pi_{i=1}^{k}(-\epsilon_{i},0]\times(Z_{Q}/T_{P}))/\sim,

(5.9)

where
\sim
mods out boundaries by circle actions as in
(
5.3
). The map
\phi_{P}
is coordinate-wise given by
maps
(0,\infty)\to(-\epsilon_{i},0]
that are equal to
0
on

[\delta_{i},\infty)
, and strictly increasing on
(0,\delta_{i})
. On
the complement of the cylindrical ends, the map
\phi_{P}
is standard
as in (
3.48
).

The almost complex structure
J_{P}^{\prime}
is defined to be the pullback

\phi_{P}^{*}J_{P}
on the complement of
\cup_{Q}U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
. The pullback
can be extended to the ends
\cup_{Q}U_{Q}(X_{P})
by keeping the base almost
complex structures
d\pi_{Q}(J_{P})
fixed on
U_{Q}(X_{P})
, ensuring

X
-cylindricity on
U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
, and interpolating the inner
products and connection one-forms in the intervening subset

U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})\backslash U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
.
∎

The following is the main result of the section.

Proposition 5.17
.

(Construction of a broken divisor)

Let
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
be a cylindrical almost complex structure as constructed
in Lemma
5.16
. For any
\theta>0
, and a large
enough
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a cylindrical Donaldson-type divisor

\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathfrak{X}
that is
\theta
-approximately holomorphic, and
such that on any cut space
\overline{X}_{P}
, the induced divisor

D_{P}\subset\overline{X}_{P}
is Poincaré dual to
k[\omega_{X_{P}}]
.

We refer the reader to
[
29
]
for the definition of

\theta
-approximate holomorphicity.

Proof of Proposition
5.17
.

The sections are constructed by running Donaldson’s procedure
simultaneously for all the manifolds in the set

\{\overline{X}_{P}\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
. Our modification of Donaldson’s
algorithm is limited to choosing appropriate Gaussian sections in
order to ensure
(Cylindricity on ends)
is
satisfied. The step of achieving global transversality by applying
Lemma
5.14
is the same as the original algorithm, and
therefore not discussed.

The prequantum bundle on a cut space
\overline{X}_{P}
is defined as the
pullback by a map to the the symplectic cut space
\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
.
Lemma
5.16
constructs a map

\phi_{P}:\overline{X}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
and produces an almost complex
structure
J_{P}
such that

—

a truncated cylindrical end
U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})\subset U_{Q}(X_{P})
is
mapped by
\phi_{P}
to
X_{Q}
via a projection map,

—

on the complement of the truncated cylindrical end,
\phi_{P}

is a diffeomorphism onto its image and
J_{P}
is

\phi_{P}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}
-compatible, and

—

\phi_{P}|X_{Q}=\phi_{Q}
.

Assuming that
\mathcal{L}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
is a prequantum line bundle
with connection, we will construct asymptotically holomorphic
sections on the pullback bundle
\phi_{P}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{P}\to X_{P}
. On

U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
, the connection on the pullback bundle is flat on the
fibers of
\pi_{Q}
, and we will define sections that are constant in
the fiber direction.

The notions of asymptotically holomorphicity and transversality are
defined with respect to a dilated metric on the cut spaces. On any

X_{P}
, the metric
g
is equal to
\omega_{X_{P}}(\cdot,J_{P}\cdot)
in
the complement of the cylindrical ends
U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
,
Q\subset P
,
and on the cylindrical end
g_{P}
is a product metric on the
fibration
U_{Q}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}
that extends the metric
g_{Q}
on
X_{Q}
.
For any
k
, define a dilated metric

g_{k}:=kg.

We describe a set of lattice points at which the Gaussian section
for each tensor power of the given line bundle are centered. Given

k\gg 0
, a set of lattice points

\Lambda_{k,P}\subset X_{P}\backslash\cup_{Q\subset P}U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})

is defined so that it contains
Ck^{\dim(\overline{X}_{P})}
number of
points, and there is a covering

X_{P}\backslash\left(\cup_{Q\subset P}B_{g_{k}}(U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P}),1)\right)=\left(\cup_{x\in\Lambda_{k,P}}B_{g_{k}}(x,1)\right).

(The set
\Lambda_{k,P}
is called a lattice because it resembles a
lattice locally in Darboux coordinates.) For any

p\in\Lambda_{k,Q}
, we will write down Gaussian sections

\sigma_{k,p,P}:\overline{X}_{P}\to\phi_{P}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{P}

centered at
p
for all
P\supseteq Q
, and determine coefficients

w_{p,k}\in\mathbb{C}
so that

\sum_{p\in\Lambda_{Q},Q\subseteq P}w_{p,k}\sigma_{k,p,P}:\overline{X}_{P}\to\phi_{P}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{P}

is asymptotically holomorphic and uniformly transverse on
\overline{X}_{P}
.
The set
\Lambda_{k}
is partitioned into subsets
I_{1},\dots,I_{N}
where

N
is independent of
k
while satisfying the following: For any
pair
