x\in\Lambda_{k,P}\backslash\cup_{Q\subset P}\Lambda_{k,Q},\quad y\in\cup_{Q\subset P}\Lambda_{k,Q}

we have

x\in I_{\alpha},y\in I_{\beta}\implies\beta<\alpha,

and at the
\alpha
-th iteration the coefficients
w_{k,p}
are
fixed for
p\in I_{\alpha}
. As a result, coefficients of the
Gaussian sections centered at lattice points on
X_{Q}
are fixed
before those centered at lattice points on
X_{P}
if
Q\subset P
.

We define Gaussian sections concentrated at the lattice points.
Consider
p\in\Lambda_{k,Q}
. We
wish to construct
Gaussian sections
\sigma_{k,p,P}:X_{P}\to\phi_{P}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{P}^{k}
for all

P\supseteq Q
such that

\sigma_{k,p,P}|X_{Q}=\sigma_{k,p,Q}.

We will first define
\sigma_{k,p,Q}
and then define
\sigma_{k,p,P}

so that it is constant on the fibers of the projection

U_{Q}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}
, and and decays at an exponential rate along the
fiber
U_{Q}(X_{P})\backslash U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}
. The detailed construction
of the sections is as follows: For any
P\supseteq Q
, choose
Darboux coordinates
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n(P)})
on a neighborhood

U_{p}\subset\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
centered at
p
, where

n(P):={\tfrac{1}{2}}\dim(X_{P})
, and the first
n(Q)
coordinates are Darboux
coordinates on
X_{Q}^{\omega}
. Choose a trivialization of
\mathcal{L}_{P}^{k}|U_{P}

for which the connection is
k\sum_{i}(\overline{z}_{i}dz_{i}-z_{i}d\overline{z}_{i})
.
Let

s_{k,p,P}(z):=\beta_{k,z}e^{-k|z|^{2}}:\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}\to\mathcal{L}_{P}^{k},

where
\beta_{k,z}
is a cut-off function vanishing at a

g_{k}
-distance of
k^{1/6}
from
x
, and extended by zero outside

U_{p}
, and let

\sigma_{k,p,P}:=\phi_{P}^{*}s_{k,p,P}:X_{P}\to\phi_{P}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{P}^{k}.

As a consequence,
\sigma_{k,p,Q}
is an ordinary Gaussian section.
For
P\supset Q
, the section
\sigma_{k,p,P}
is constant on the
fibers decays outside
U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
.

The globalization process consists of finding coefficients

\{w_{p}\in\mathbb{C}\}_{p\in\Lambda_{k}}
such that

\sigma_{k,P}:=\sum_{p\in\Lambda_{k,P}}w_{p}\sigma_{k,p,P}

is a uniformly transverse sequence of sections for each
P
. The
proof of globalization carries over from
[
29
]
. The only
new feature is to determine each coefficient
w_{p}
in a

P
-independent way. This can be done by Lemma
5.18
which
is a modification of Lemma
5.14
.

Finally, for any pair
Q\subset P
, the zero set

\sigma_{k,P}^{-1}(0)
is
Q
-cylindrical, because a lattice point

p\in\Lambda_{k,P}
lies in the complement of the
Q
-cylindrical
end, and the section
\sigma_{k,p,P}
is supported in

B_{g_{k}}(p,k^{1/6})
, which is equal to
B_{g}(p,k^{-1/3})
.
Therefore, there is a truncated cylindrical end

U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})\subset U_{Q}^{\prime}(X_{P})
on which, for large enough
k
,

\sigma_{k,P}^{-1}(0)
is
Q
-cylindrical, since
U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
is
disjoint from
B_{g}(p,k^{-1/3})
for all
p\in\Lambda_{k,P}
.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

(Quantitative Sard’s theorem)
Given a tuple of positive integers
(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
there
is an integer
p
for which the following is satisfied. Suppose

0<\delta<\frac{1}{4}
, and
f_{i}:B_{+}^{n_{i}}\to\mathbb{C}
is a set of
k

functions on balls
B_{+}^{n_{i}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}
of radius

\frac{11}{10}
that satisfy
\lVert f_{i}\rVert_{C^{1}}\leq\eta
, where

\eta:=\delta\log(\delta^{-1})^{-p}
. Then, there exists

w\in\mathbb{C}
,
|w|\leq\delta
such that
f_{i}-w
is

\eta
-transverse to
0
over the interior ball
B^{n_{i}}
of radius

1
for each
i
.

The case
k=1
is Theorem 20 of
[
29
]
, and is stated as
Lemma
5.14
. The proof in the case
k=1
is by bounding
the size of the image
f(B_{+})
in the range. For a finite
k
, the
volume is multiplied by a constant, and the proof in
[
29
]

can be replicated by altering the constants.

Remark 5.19
.

The construction of a broken divisor in Proposition

5.17
extends to the orbifold case. Indeed, in the
orbifold adaptation of the Donaldson construction in
Gironella-Muñoz-Zhou
[
37
]
, the new features are
the choice of an appropriate lattice compatible with the
stratification of the orbifold; and adjusting the uniform
transversality constants. Both of these features are compatible with
the modifications we have introduced in the proof of Proposition

5.17
.

Abbildung 5.1:
The polyhedral decomposition of a single cut.

Abbildung 5.2:
Multiple cut case.

Remark 5.20
.

(On the construction of broken stabilizing divisors) This is a
technical remark justifying the proof technique of Proposition

5.17
wherein we construct asymptotically holomorphic
sequences of sections
\sigma_{k,P}
simultaneously for all polytopes

P\in\mathcal{P}
rather than construct one sequence
(\sigma_{k,P})_{k}
at a
time. We point out that the latter approach, which appears cleaner,
is taken in
[
17
]
for the case of single breaking.
Consider a single cut with polyhedral decomposition shown in Figure

5.1
. Given a sequence of sections

(\sigma_{k,{P_{0}}}:\overline{X}_{P_{0}}\to\mathcal{L}_{\overline{X}_{P_{0}}}^{k})_{k},

[
17
, Lemma 7.15]
constructs an extension of the sequence
of sections to

(\sigma_{k,{P_{\pm}}}:\overline{X}_{P_{\pm}}\to\mathcal{L}_{\overline{X}_{P_{\pm}}}^{k})_{k}.

The first step in the construction is to consider the extension

\sigma_{k,{P_{0}}}e^{-k|x|^{2}}
and then turn on the contributions from
Gaussians centered away from the divisor
\overline{X}_{P_{0}}\subset\overline{X}_{P_{\pm}}

so that the sections become transverse. If we apply this approach to
spaces corresponding to the
polytopes shown in Figure
5.2
,
we would construct a sequence in the order
\sigma_{k,{P_{0}}}
, then

\sigma_{k,{P_{1}}}
,
\sigma_{k,{P_{2}}}
and then finally

\sigma_{k,{P_{3}}}
. We would like the sequence
\sigma_{k,{P_{3}}}
on

\overline{X}_{P_{3}}
to be an extension of
\sigma_{k,{P_{0}}}
,
\sigma_{k,{P_{1}}}

and
\sigma_{k,{P_{2}}}
. Therefore, we would like
to start Donaldson’s globalization iteration with a sequence of Gaussian sections that are equal to

\sigma_{k,{P_{0}}}e^{-k(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2})}
in a neighborhood of

\overline{X}_{P_{0}}
, equal to
\sigma_{k,{P_{1}}}e^{-k|z_{1}|^{2}}
in a neighborhood
of
\overline{X}_{P_{1}}
, and equal to
\sigma_{k,{P_{2}}}e^{-k|z_{2}|^{2}}
in a
neighborhood of
\overline{X}_{P_{2}}
. The approach fails because the three
sections do not agree on overlaps. Indeed, to define the sequence

\sigma_{k,{P_{1}}}
, we would have used
\sigma_{k,{P_{0}}}e^{-k|z_{2}|^{2}}

as a starting sequence, but then these sections would have been
modified when contributions from Gaussians in nearby balls are
turned on to achieve transversality.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
be the
X
-cylindrical almost complex structure from
Lemma
5.16
. There is a diffeomorphism

\phi:X\to X
that preserves the tropical structure (in the sense
of Remark
5.22
below) and such that gluing
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}

along the ends produces a sequence
(J^{\prime})^{\nu}
of almost complex
structures on
X
, that is neck-stretching with respect to the
symplectic form
\phi^{*}\omega
, and locally
\phi^{*}\omega
-compatible.

We recall that “
(J^{\prime})^{\nu}
being neck-stretching with respect to the
symplectic form
\phi^{*}\omega
” means that there is a symplectic
cylindrical structure (Definition
3.37
) on

(X,\phi^{*}\omega)
which gives rise to neck-stretched manifolds
(Definition
3.42
) on which
(J^{\prime})^{\nu}
is cylindrical.

Remark 5.22
.

A diffeomorphism
\phi:X\to X
preserves the tropical structure

\mathcal{P}
on
X
if there are neighborhoods
U_{P}\subset X
of

\Phi^{-1}(P)
on which
\phi
is
T_{P}
-equivariant and

\Phi_{P}\circ\phi=\Phi_{P}
where
\Phi_{P}:U_{P}\to\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
is the
projection of the tropical moment map
\Phi
to
\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\vee}
.

Beweis.

We recall that in Lemma
5.16
, we start out with a

\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}
, which is locally

\omega
-compatible with cylindrical end
U_{Q}(X_{P})
for any pair
Q\subset P
,
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
For any top-dimensional polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
the corresponding symplectic
\mathfrak{X}
-cylindrical structure on
X_{P}^{\omega}
is
given by maps

U_{\overline{X}^{\omega}_{P}}(\overline{X}^{\omega}_{Q})\xrightarrow{\phi^{P}_{Q}}((\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P\times\overline{Z}_{Q}^{\omega})/\sim,\omega_{{\tilde{Q}}}),\quad\omega_{{\tilde{Q}}}:=\omega_{X_{Q}}+d\langle{\alpha_{Q}^{P},\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}}}\rangle,

for all
Q\subset P

where the various terms are as in (
5.3
).

As a first step of the proof, we produce a symplectic

X
-cylindrical structure on symplectic cut spaces for which the almost complex
structure
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
is locally compatible. We recall from the
proof of Lemma
5.16
that
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
is obtained from

\mathfrak{J}
to changing the connection one-form to be
X
-cylindrical in
the truncated cylindrical end
U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
and interpolating between
the two in the intervening subset
U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})\backslash U_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(X_{P})
, where
Q\subset P
and
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
.
We may alter the connection one-form underlying the symplectic
cylindrical structure in a similar way to obtain the following: For
any top-dimensional
P\in\mathcal{P}
, there is a symplectic form

\omega_{P,1}
on
\tilde{X}_{P}^{\omega}
, which is equal to
\omega_{X_{P}}
in the
complement of
U_{\overline{X}_{Q}^{\omega}}(\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega})
, and on

U_{\overline{X}_{Q}^{\omega}}(\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega})
,

((\phi_{Q}^{P})^{-1})^{*}\omega_{P,1}=\omega_{X_{Q}}+d\langle{\alpha^{P}_{Q,\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}},\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}}}\rangle,

(5.10)

where
\alpha^{P}_{Q,\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}}
is a
T_{Q}
-connection one-form on
the
T_{Q}
-bundle
(\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P\times\overline{Z}_{Q}^{\omega})
, and for which

\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
is locally
\omega_{1,P}
-compatible. (The subscript
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}

denotes dependence on the coordinate in

\operatorname{Cone}_{Q}P\subset\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}
.) Comparing (
5.10
),
(
5.3
), we see that the forms
\omega_{Q,1}
,
\omega_{X_{Q}}

are cohomologous. Furthermore, assuming that the cylindrical end is
small enough the family

\{(1-t)\omega_{Q,1}+t\omega_{X_{Q}}:t\in[0,1]\}
consists of
symplectic forms on
\overline{X}_{P}
. Applying Moser’s theorem, we obtain a
symplectomorphism

\psi_{P}:(\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega},\omega)\to(\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega},\omega_{1}).

Note that
\phi_{Q,1}^{P}:=\phi_{Q}^{P}\circ\psi_{P}
is a symplectic

X
-cylindrical structure.

We glue the symplectic cut spaces
X_{P}^{\omega}
using the symplectic

X
-cylindrical structure, and call the resulting symplectic
manifold
(X,\omega_{1})
. The almost complex structures

\{(J^{\prime})^{\nu}\}_{\nu}
obtained by gluing
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
along the ends is
locally
\omega_{1}
-compatible.
It remains to prove that the manifold
(X,\omega_{1})
is
symplectomorphic to the original manifold
(X,\omega)
. By Proposition

3.67
, there is a symplectic
X
-cylindrical structure

\{\phi_{P}\}_{P}
on
(X,\omega)
. Applying the multiple cut
\mathcal{P}
to

(X,\omega)
, we obtain cut spaces
\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
equipped with an
symplectic
X
-cylindrical structure denoted by

\phi_{Q,0}^{P}
. There is a family of
X
-cylindrical structures

\{\phi_{Q,t}^{P}\}
connecting
\phi_{Q,0}^{P}
and

\phi_{Q,1}^{P}
. Gluing along each of these produces a family

(X,\omega_{t})
with cohomologous symplectic forms. The Lemma follows
from Moser’s theorem.
∎

5.4
Stabilizing pairs in neck-stretched manifolds

In this Section, we prove the existence of a stabilizing pair

(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
on a broken manifold such that the family

(J^{\nu},D^{\nu})
obtained by gluing the pair
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
consists of
stabilizing pairs on neck-stretched manifolds. We recall from Section

5.1
that in a smooth symplectic manifold

(X,\omega_{X})
, a stabilizing pair
(J,D)
consists of a Donaldson-type
divisor
D\subset X
and a
\omega_{X}
-tamed almost complex structure
J

that is adapted to
D
and for which any non-constant
J
-holomorphic
sphere intersects
D
at least three points and is not contained in

D
. We start by defining the analogous notion for broken manifolds.

Definition 5.23
.

(Adapted broken almost complex structure)

Given a broken
divisor
\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathfrak{X}
as in Definition
5.12
, we denote by

\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D})=\{\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})\ :\ \mathfrak{J}(T\mathfrak{D}_{P})=T\mathfrak{D}_{P}\text{ on $\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}$ }\ \forall P\in\mathcal{P}\}

the space of cylindrical almost complex structures that are adapted
to

\mathfrak{D}
.

A
stabilizing pair
in a broken manifold consists of a broken Donaldson divisor and a broken almost complex structure such that the restriction of the pair to any cut space is a stabilizing pair in the sense of Definition
5.2
.

Definition 5.24
.

(Stabilizing pair in a broken manifold)

Let
\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathfrak{X}
be a broken divisor as in Definition

5.12
which is disjoint from the Lagrangian

L\subset\mathfrak{X}
. For
E>0
, an adapted almost complex structure

\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D})
, is
E
-stabilizing
if for every
polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
, the almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}|\overline{X}_{P}
is

E
-stabilizing in
(\overline{X}_{P},\omega_{X_{P}},D_{P})
. A pair
(\mathfrak{J},\mathfrak{D})

is
stabilizing
if
\mathfrak{J}
is locally strongly tamed and is

E
-stabilizing for all
E>0
. We call a divisor
\mathfrak{D}
appearing in
a stabilizing pair a
stabilizing divisor.

The following is the main result of the Section where we construct
stabilizing pairs on broken manifolds. We adapt the construction of
stabilizing pairs in smooth manifolds outlined in Section

5.1
.

Proposition 5.25
.

(Stabilizing pair in a broken manifold)

Suppose
\mathfrak{X}:=\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a broken manifold, such that on each cut space

\overline{X}_{P}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
the symplectic form
\omega_{X_{P}}
is
rational. There exists a stabilizing pair

(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
in
\mathfrak{X}
such that

(a)

the family
(J^{\nu},D^{\nu})
obtained by
gluing consists of stabilizing pairs for all
\nu\in[1,\infty)
, and

(b)

for every
E>0
there is a neighbourhood

\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D};\mathfrak{J}_{0},E)\subset\{\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D}):\mathcal{J}|T\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{J}_{0}|T\mathfrak{D}\}

of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
consisting of
E
-stabilizing locally tamed
cylindrical almost complex structures adapted to
\mathfrak{D}
.

It follows from Lemma
5.21
that the family of almost complex structures
\{J^{\nu}\}_{\nu}

produced by Proposition
5.25
are neck-stretched in the sense of Definitions in Section
3.4
.

Proof of Proposition
5.25
.

Step 1
: We start with a preliminary almost complex
structure, and find a uniform degree bound on its neighborhood, in
order to determine the degree of the Donaldson divisor that we
require. Suppose
\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\omega}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathfrak{X})
is a

\omega_{\mathfrak{X}}
-compatible almost complex structure (Definition

5.9
). By Lemma
5.16
, there are maps

\phi_{P}:X_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
from cut spaces to symplectic cut
spaces, and a locally compatible (hence gluable, see Definitions

3.53
(
d
),
3.49

(
b
)) almost complex structure on
(X_{P})_{P}
, which
we denote by
\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}
. The almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}

is cylindrical on a truncation of the cylindrical ends, henceforth
in this proof, the cylindrical ends are replaced by these
truncations, and are denoted by
U_{Q}(X_{P})\subset X_{P}
for any pair

Q\subset P
. We recall that cylindrical ends are equipped with a
projections
\pi_{Q}:U_{Q}(X_{P})\to X_{Q}
. For any
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}
,
Lemma
5.16
says that
\phi_{P}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}
is a

basic
form on the cylindrical ends of
X_{P}
, in the sense that
for any
Q\subset P
, on
U_{Q}(X_{P})
,
\phi_{P}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}
is a
pullback of a form by
\pi_{Q}
. Gluing the forms

\phi_{P}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}
along the ends produces

\omega_{\nu}\in\Omega^{2}(X^{\nu})
which is cohomologous to

\omega_{X}\in\Omega^{2}(X)
, and which is basic in the cylindrical
subsets. To derive the bound we also choose representatives of the
first Chern classes
[c_{1}(T\overline{X}_{P})]
which are basic on
cylindrical ends. That is, we choose forms

\gamma=(\gamma_{P})_{P\in\mathcal{P}},\quad\gamma_{P}\in\Omega^{2}(\overline{X}_{P}),\quad[\gamma_{P}]=c_{1}(T\overline{X}_{P}).

We choose forms on the neck-stretched manifolds

\gamma_{\nu}\in\Omega^{2}(X^{\nu}),\quad\gamma_{\nu}\in[c_{1}(TX)],

which are basic in the the sense that
on the
P
-cylindrical subset
\pi_{P}^{\nu}:X_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\nu}\to X_{P}
,
each of the forms
\gamma_{\nu}
is the pullback of the same form on
X_{P}
.

For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
, the supremum of the ratio of
the first Chern class form and the basic symplectic form
given by

k_{*}(\epsilon,P):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{J}:\lVert\mathfrak{J},\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{C^{0}}<\epsilon\\
0\neq v\in TX_{P}\end{subarray}}\tfrac{\gamma_{P}(v,\mathfrak{J}v)}{\omega_{X_{P}}(v,\mathfrak{J}v)}

is finite. The proof of finiteness is as in the proof
of Lemma
5.5
, together with the fact that the forms
\gamma_{P}
and
\omega_{X_{P}}

are basic on
X_{P}
.
Similarly, the supremum

k_{*}(\epsilon,X):=\sup_{\nu}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{J}:\lVert\mathfrak{J},\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{C^{0}}<\epsilon\\
0\neq v\in TX^{\nu}\end{subarray}}\tfrac{\gamma_{\nu}(v,J^{\nu}v)}{\omega_{\nu}(v,J^{\nu}v)},

where
J^{\nu}
is given by gluing
\mathfrak{J}
on the cylindrical ends,
is finite.
We choose
k_{*}

satisfying

k_{*}\geq k_{*}(\epsilon,X),k_{*}(\epsilon,P)\quad\forall P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}.

(5.11)

Step 2
: We find a stabilizing divisor in the broken
manifold.
We will find a stabilizing divisor of degree

k>2\max\{k_{*},k_{*}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}(\dim(X))-2\}.

(5.12)

for reasons explained in Remark
5.6
. Let
\theta_{0}>0

be such that for any
\theta_{0}
-approximately

\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}
-holomorphic divisor, there is an adapted tamed almost
complex structure that is
\epsilon/2
-close to
\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}
. In
addition to satisfying (
5.12
), we may choose
k
to be
large enough that a Donaldson divisor of degree
k
is

\theta_{0}
-approximately holomorphic.
By Proposition
5.17
,
there is a divisor
\mathfrak{D}

cylindrical in
\{U_{Q}(X_{P})\}_{Q\subset P}
.
There is an adapted strongly tamed almost complex structure

\mathfrak{J}_{0}\in B_{\epsilon/2}(\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{pre}}),

and furthermore, there is a
neighborhood of adapted almost complex structures

U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}:=\{\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D}):\text{$\mathfrak{J}$ is locally strongly tamed},\lVert\mathfrak{J}-\mathfrak{J}_{0}\rVert_{C^{0}}<\epsilon/2\}

that satisfy the degree bound of
k_{*}
on the cut spaces

\{x_{P}\}_{P}
, and their gluings satisfy the same degree bound on the
neck-stretched manifolds
\{X_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
.

Step 3
: We finish the proof closely following the method
in the unbroken case in Cieliebak-Mohnke
[
26
]
, which is
outlined in Proposition
5.3
. So far, we have shown
that there is a neighborhood
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
in the space of locally
strongly tamed
\mathfrak{D}
-adapted cylindrical almost complex structures
on
\mathfrak{X}
such that for any
\mathfrak{J}\in U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
,
k_{*}
is a degree
bound for the glued family
J^{\nu}
. By Remark
5.6

generic almost complex structures in the set
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
are
stabilizing.

Next, we claim that for any
E>0
, an open and dense subset of

U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
is
E
-stabilizing for all neck lengths. The subset

\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E,\infty}=\{\mathfrak{J}\in U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}:\mathfrak{J}\text{ is $E$-stabilizing on $\mathfrak{X}$}\}

is open and dense: Openness is a consequence of Gromov convergence
applied to each of the cut spaces
\overline{X}_{P}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
(see the
proof of
[
26
, Corollary 8.16]
), and denseness follows
because the
E
-stabilizing condition is generic. The subset

\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E}=\{\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E,\infty}:J^{\nu}\text{ is
$E$-stabilizing on $X^{\nu}$ for $\nu\in[1,\infty)$}\}.

is also open in
\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E,\infty}
by Gromov
compactness. Openness at the infinite neck length parameter is
proved by Lemma
5.26
. The subset

\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E}\subset\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E,\infty}
is comeager by an
application of Sard’s theorem : The universal moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}:=\{u_{\nu}:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to X^{\nu}:u_{\nu}\text{ is
$J^{\nu}$-holomorphic},\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E,\infty},\nu\in[1,\infty)\}

is a Banach manifold and the projection

\pi_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{J}}\to\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E,\infty}
is a Fredholm map. Therefore,
the subset
\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E}
of regular values of
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}
is comeager.

Finally, let
E_{k}\to\infty
be any sequence of real numbers with
limit infinity. The set of almost complex structures

\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}}}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}},E_{k}}

that is stabilizing for all
\nu\in[1,\infty]
is the
intersection of the set of
E_{k}
-stabilizing almost complex
structures for all
k
. The intersection
\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{reg}}}
is non-empty
because each of the sets in the intersection is open and dense.
∎

The following Lemma, used above in the proof of Proposition

5.25
, is an openness statement for stabilizing
almost complex structures at
\nu=\infty
.

Lemma 5.26
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathfrak{X}
is a cylindrical broken divisor, and

\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D})
is a tamed adapted almost complex
structure that is
E
-stabilizing. Suppose the divisors

D^{\nu}\subset X^{\nu}
are obtained by gluing, and the sequence

J^{\nu}\in\mathcal{J}(X^{\nu},D^{\nu})
converges to
\mathfrak{J}
. Then, there exists

\nu_{0}
such that
J^{\nu}
is
E
-stabilizing for
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
.

The proof of the Lemma requires terminology from Chapter

7
and Section
8.2
. The proof is by a
hard rescaling argument similar to the one used in the proof of Gromov
convergence for breaking maps.

Proof of Lemma
5.26
.

Suppose

u_{\nu}:C\to X^{\nu}
is a sequence of non-constant

J^{\nu}
-holomorphic spheres with area
\leq E
that are not
stabilized. That is, either the images are contained in the divisor

D^{\nu}
or they have
\leq 2
distinct points of intersection with
the divisor.

First, consider the situation that the derivatives of
u_{\nu}
are
uniformly bounded. By Lemma
8.18
,
after passing to a subsequence,
the sequence
u_{\nu}
converges horizontally in
some polytope
P
.
By Lemma
8.17
, there is a sequence of
translations
t_{\nu}\in\nu P^{\vee}
such that a subsequence of

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t_{\nu}}u_{\nu}
uniformly converges to a non-constant

\mathfrak{J}_{P}
-holomorphic map
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, that is unstabilized. The
projection
\pi_{P}\circ u:C\to X_{P}
is non-constant, because it
is not possible for the image of
u
to be non-constant and lie in a
single fiber of
\pi_{P}
, which is isomorphic to
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
. By the
definition of
P
-Hofer energy, for closed curves in
X_{P}
,

\langle{(\pi_{P}\circ u)_{*}[C],\omega_{X_{P}}}\rangle\leq E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(\pi_{P}\circ u).

We have

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(\pi_{P}\circ u)\leq\lim_{\nu}E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u_{\nu})=\langle{(u_{\nu})_{*}[C],\omega_{X}}\rangle\leq E,

where the first inequality is by Proposition

7.27
, and from the fact that Hofer energy is
monotonic for maps that are holomorphic with respect to a locally
strongly tamed almost complex structure (Lemma

7.22
), and the equality is from Remark

7.19
. As a consequence,

{\operatorname{Area}}(u)=\langle{(\pi_{P}\circ u)_{*}[C],\omega_{X_{P}}}\rangle\leq E.

If the derivatives on
u_{\nu}
are not uniformly bounded, we produce
a sphere by hard rescaling. By following the procedure in Step 2 of
the proof of Theorem
8.2
, we obtain a rescaled
sequence of
J^{\nu}
-holomorphic maps
v_{\nu}:B_{r_{\nu}}\to X^{\nu}

on balls
B_{r_{\nu}}
that exhaust
\mathbb{C}
, a polytope
P
, and a
sequence of translations
t_{\nu}\in\nu P^{\vee}
such that

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t_{\nu}}u_{\nu}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}
to a non-constant
map
v:\mathbb{C}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. In this preceding step, the rescaled maps are
found in the same way as in
[
55
, Lemma 4.6.5]
, the polytope

P
and translation sequences are given by Lemma
8.17
. As
in Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
8.2
, the map

v
has finite
P
-Hofer energy, and by Proposition

7.1
, the infinite end of
v
is asymptotic to a
trivial cylinder. Therefore, the projection
\pi_{P}\circ v
extends
to
\pi_{P}\circ v:C_{0}\to\overline{X}_{P}
, for some weighted projective
space
C_{0}=\mathbb{P}(1,n)
. The projection

\pi_{P}\circ v:C_{0}\to\overline{X}_{P}
is non-constant: Otherwise, the
image of
v(\mathbb{C})
is in a fiber
V_{P^{\vee}}
which is a toric
variety, and the map extends to
v:C_{0}\to V_{P^{\vee}}
with only
one point
\infty\in C_{0}
that maps to toric divisors

V_{Q^{\vee}},Q\supset P
of
V_{P^{\vee}}
, and therefore,
v
is
constant. By the argument in the previous paragraph,

{\operatorname{Area}}(v)=\langle{(\pi_{P}\circ v)_{*}[C_{0}],\omega_{X_{P}}}\rangle\leq E
.

In both cases, the limit (orbifold) sphere is not stabilized and the
Lemma follows.
∎

Kapitel 6
Coherent perturbations and regularity

In order to obtain the necessary transversality for moduli spaces of
treed holomorphic maps, we use the Cieliebak-Mohnke perturbation
scheme
[
26
]
. This scheme has been adapted to define Fukaya
algebras of Lagrangians by Charest-Woodward
[
16
]
. The Morse
function on the Lagrangian submanifold and the almost complex
structure on the broken manifold are allowed to be
domain-dependent. Stabilizing divisors from the previous chapter are
essential in defining domain-dependent perturbations.

A domain-dependent perturbation is defined as a map from a universal
curve to the space of tamed almost complex structures. We recall from
Chapter
4
that domain curves are treed disks. For
any type
\Gamma
of treed disks the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
of treed
disks has a universal curve

\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}

whose fiber over a point
[C]\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is isomorphic to
C
.
The perturbation datum is then a map from
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
to the space of
cylindrical almost complex structures on the broken manifold and Morse
functions on the Lagrangian submanifold. Under this perturbation
scheme, marked points on the domain curve are the inverse images of
the intersection of the holomorphic map with the stabilizing
divisor. The stabilizing divisor is Poincaré dual to a large
multiple of the symplectic form. This ensures that generically there
are no holomorphic spheres in the divisor, and that any non-constant
holomorphic sphere has enough divisor intersections to ensure that its
domain is stable.

Domain-dependent perturbations are necessary for solving the multiple
cover problem. Indeed, for a fixed almost complex structure the
compactification of the moduli space of pseudoholomorphic maps may
contain nodal maps with components that are multiple covers of maps
with negative Chern class. It is not possible to achieve
transversality for the moduli space of such maps. Multiple covers can
be perturbed away by domain-dependent perturbations. As a toy example,
suppose for a domain-independent almost complex structure
J_{0}
, there
is a simple
J_{0}
-holomorphic sphere
S\subset X
with negative Chern
number and one intersection point with the stabilizing divisor. Via a
domain-dependent perturbation of
J_{0}
, we can ensure that an

n
-covered sphere (
n>1
) homologous to
n[S]
does not occur in the
moduli space of perturbed holomorphic maps. Indeed, for such a cover
the domain would have
n
marked points labelled
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
, and
the perturbation is not required to be invariant under automorphisms
of the domain curve that permute the marked points. Breaking the
permutation symmetry adds a multiplicative factor of
\frac{1}{n!}
to
the curve count (see Remark
6.14
).

6.1
Domain-dependent perturbations

A domain-dependent perturbation consists of a domain-dependent almost complex structure on the surface parts and a domain-dependent Morse function on the treed segments of treed disks.
Domain-dependent perturbations
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
are defined for each
domain type
\Gamma
, and later in the section we describe a set of

coherence
conditions on the set of perturbation data

(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
to ensure that the perturbations on various
strata fit in together in an expected manner.

To describe perturbations for a domain type,
we first fix subsets of the universal treed disk outside of which
perturbations vanish. Let
\Gamma
be a combinatorial type of treed
disk and
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}=\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\cup\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{\Gamma}
its
universal curve from (
4.6
). Fix a compact subset

\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\operatorname{cp}}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\Gamma}

in the complement of breaking points and disk nodes (that is, disk
nodes
w_{e}
where the length
\ell(e)
of the treed segment is zero),
and containing in its interior, for every edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
and every curve

C\subset\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
, at least one point
z\in T_{e}\subset C
on
any infinite segment. Also fix a compact subset

\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{cp}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}-\{w_{e}\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma},\ e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)\}

disjoint from the boundary and spherical nodes

w_{e}\in C,e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)
, containing in its interior at least
one point
z\in S_{v}
on each sphere and disk component

S_{v}\subset C
for each fiber
C\subset\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
.
Furthermore, the complement

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\Gamma}-\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\operatorname{cp}}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\Gamma}

is a neighbourhood of the boundary and nodes; these neighborhoods must
be chosen compatibly with those already chosen on the boundary for the
inductive construction later.

Following Floer
[
35
]
, we use a
C^{\varepsilon}
-topology on the
space of almost complex structures. For a section
\xi
of a vector
bundle
E\to X
, the
C^{\varepsilon}
-norm is

\lVert\xi\rVert_{C^{\varepsilon}}:={\textstyle\sum}_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{k}\lVert\xi\rVert_{C^{k}(X,E)}.

Here
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a fixed sequence of positive
numbers that converges fast enough to
0
as
i\to\infty
. If the
convergence is sufficiently rapid, then the space of sections with a
bounded norm is a Banach space
[
35
, Lemma 5.1]
and
contains sections supported in arbitrarily small neighbourhoods of

X
.

Definition 6.1
.

(Perturbation data)

(a)

(Domain-dependent Morse functions) Suppose that
\Gamma
is a type of stable
treed disk, and
\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
is the
tree part of the universal treed disk. Let

(F_{0}:L\to\mathbb{R},G:T^{\otimes 2}L\to\mathbb{R})

be a Morse-Smale pair. For an integer
l\geq 0
a

domain-dependent perturbation
of
F_{0}
of class
C^{l}
is a
C^{l}

map

F_{\Gamma}:\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\Gamma}\times L\to\mathbb{R}

(6.1)

equal to the given function
F
away from the compact part:

F_{\Gamma}|(\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\Gamma}-\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\operatorname{cp}}_{\Gamma})=\pi_{2}^{*}F_{0}

where
\pi_{2}
is the projection on the second factor in
(
6.1
). Here
F_{0}
is called the
background Morse
function
for the domain-dependent perturbation
F_{\Gamma}
. The
set of critical points of the background Morse function is denoted
by

\mathcal{I}(L):={\operatorname{crit}}(F).

(6.2)

(b)

(Domain-dependent almost complex structure)

Let
\mathfrak{J}_{0}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
be a locally strongly tamed
cylindrical almost complex structure. A
domain-dependent
almost complex structure
of class
C^{\varepsilon}
for treed disks of
type
\Gamma
with
background almost complex structure

\mathfrak{J}_{0}

is a map from the
two-dimensional part
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\Gamma}
of the universal curve

\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}_{\Gamma}
to
\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
given by

\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}:\ \overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})

(6.3)

equal to the given
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
away from the compact part:

\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}|(\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{\Gamma}-\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\operatorname{cp}}_{\Gamma})=\mathfrak{J}_{0},

(6.4)

and on any fiber
S_{\Gamma}\subset\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}
,
\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}-\mathfrak{J}_{0}

has finite norm in
C^{\varepsilon}(S_{\Gamma}\times\mathfrak{X},\operatorname{End}(T\mathfrak{X}))
.

(c)

(Perturbation data) A perturbation datum for a type
\Gamma
of stable treed disks is a
pair
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}=(F_{\Gamma},\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma})
consisting of a
domain-dependent Morse function
F_{\Gamma}
and a domain-dependent
almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}
.

The following morphisms on the set of combinatorial types of stable
treed disks are used to define coherence of perturbations.

Definition 6.2
.

(Morphisms on treed disk types)

(a)

(Cutting edges)

There is a (Cutting edges) morphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
between
combinatorial types
\Gamma
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
of stable treed disks
if and only if
\Gamma
is
obtained by cutting a boundary edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}

that contains a breaking; see Figure
6.1
.

(b)

(Collapsing edges)

A morphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a (Collapsing edges) morphism if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by collapsing an interior edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
or a boundary edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
with length zero,
\ell(e)=0
.

(c)

(Making an edge length finite or
non-zero)

A morphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a (Making an edge length finite or non-zero) morphism if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by
changing the edge length of a boundary edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
from infinite or zero to finite non-zero.

Abbildung 6.1:
Cutting an edge
e
relabels the boundary and interior markings while preserving their ordering on the pieces
\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
.

Perturbation data
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
for various treed disk types
\Gamma

must be chosen coherently to obtain compactness. For any two
treed types
\Gamma
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
related by a morphism from Definition
6.2
, there is a
coherence condition relating
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Definition 6.3
.

(Morphisms of perturbation data)

(a)

(Collapsing edges/Making an edge length finite/non-zero)
Let
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
be a (Collapsing
edges/making an edge length finite/non-zero) morphism.
For perturbation data
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
,

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
there is a morphism
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
if

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is induced by pullback of
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
under the
natural inclusion of the universal curve

\iota_{\Gamma}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}:\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}.

(b)

(Cutting edges)

Suppose
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a (Cutting edges) morphism, where an edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
is cut to yield leaf edges

e_{+},e_{-}\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma^{\prime})
. There is a morphism of perturbation data
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}\to\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

if
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is obtained by pushing forward
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
under the map

\pi_{\Gamma}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}:\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}\to\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

defined by gluing at the leaf edges
e_{\pm}
to form a single
non-leaf edge
e
with
\ell(e)=\infty
. That is, define

\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},x)=\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}(z,x),\quad\forall z\in(\pi_{\Gamma}^{\Gamma^{\prime}})^{-1}(z).

The definition for the perturbed Morse datum
F_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is similar.

We are now ready to define coherent collections of perturbation data.
These are data that behave well with each type of operation in
Definition
6.2
.

Definition 6.4
.

(Coherent families of
perturbation data) A collection of perturbation data

\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
is
coherent
if it is
compatible with the morphisms of moduli spaces of different types in
the sense that

(a)

(Collapsing edges)/(Making an
edge length finite/non-zero)
if
\Gamma
is
obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by collapsing an edge or making an edge
finite/non-zero, then
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is the pullback of

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
;

(b)

(Cutting edges)
if
\Gamma
is obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by
cutting a boundary edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}^{\infty}(\Gamma^{\prime})

of infinite length, then
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is the push-forward of

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
. Assuming
\Gamma
is the union of types

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
is obtained from

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma_{2}}
as follows: For
k=1,2
, let

\pi_{k}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{1}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{2}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{k}}

denote the projection on the
k
-th factor, and therefore,

\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
is the union of
\pi_{1}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma_{1}}
and

\pi_{2}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma_{2}}
. We require that
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
is equal
to the pullback of
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma_{k}}
on
\pi_{k}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma_{k}}
:

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}|\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma_{k}}=\pi_{k}^{*}\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma_{k}}.

(6.5)

We also require the perturbation data to satisfy the following
locality axiom which ensures that the perturbations on any component
only depend on special points on that component, and the length of the
treed segments on the boundary of the disk. We first set up some
notation: For a type
\Gamma
underlying treed disks, and a vertex

v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
, let
\Gamma(v)
be a graph with a single vertex

v
and markings

\{e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma):v\in e\}.

corresponding to each edge incident on
v
. Let

\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,v}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
be a fibration over
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}

whose fiber over
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
consists of the curve component
represented by
v
. Define a map

\pi_{v}:\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,v}\to\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma(v)}\times([0,\infty])^{|\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)|},

(6.6)

whose first component
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,v}\to\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma(v)}
is the
natural projection map, and the second component is the length
function on boundary edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
.

(Locality Axiom)

The restriction of the perturbation
datum
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
to
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,v}
is the pullback via
\pi_{v}
of
some datum on

\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma(v)}\times([0,\infty])^{|\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)|}
.

This ends the Definition.

Let
C
be a possibly unstable treed disk of type
\Gamma
. The

stabilization
of
C
is the stable treed disk
\operatorname{st}(C)
of some type

\operatorname{st}(\Gamma)
obtained by collapsing unstable surface and tree
components. Thus the stabilization
\operatorname{st}(C)
of any treed disk
C
is
the fiber of a universal treed disk
\mathcal{U}_{\operatorname{st}(\Gamma)}
. Given a
perturbation datum for the type
\operatorname{st}(\Gamma)
, we obtain a
domain-dependent almost complex structure and Morse function for
C
,
still denoted
\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma},F_{\Gamma}
, by pull-back under the map

C\to\mathcal{U}_{\operatorname{st}(\Gamma)}
. If
\Gamma
does not contain vertices,
i.e. if
C
is a single infinite segment
T_{e},e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)
,
then the perturbation
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
vanishes on
C
.

Remark 6.5
.

Coherence conditions for perturbation data are motivated as follows.

(a)

If a sequence
C_{\nu}
of (domain) curves of type
\Gamma

converges to a limit curve
C
of type
\Gamma^{\prime}
, we would like
the perturbation data on
C_{\nu}
to converge to the perturbation
datum on
C
. If
\Gamma^{\prime}\neq\Gamma
then there is a

(Collapsing edges)/(Making an edge
length finite/non-zero)
morphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
, and the
convergence of the perturbation datum is ensured by coherence
under
(Collapsing edges)/(Making an
edge length finite/non-zero)
.

(b)

Suppose
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a cutting edges morphism, and
suppose the disconnected type
\Gamma^{\prime}
has components

\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
,
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
. Once moduli spaces of perturbed
holomorphic maps in a symplectic manifold
X
are defined, we
would like to be able to say that the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(X)

of maps with domain type
\Gamma
is a product

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(X)=\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}}(X)\times\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}(X),

The product relation would hold only if the perturbation data is
coherent under
(Cutting edges)
.

Remark 6.6
.

(On the locality axiom) The locality axiom ensures that
forgetting a marking
z_{e^{\prime}}
on a treed curve
C
affects the
perturbation datum only on the component containing
z_{e^{\prime}}
. This
feature is used in Proposition
8.51
. The dependence
on the boundary edge lengths is useful for the following
reason. Suppose
\Gamma
is a combinatorial type of treed disk
depicted in Figure
6.1
. By cutting an edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)
, we obtain two identical types. The cutting
edge axiom requires that a coherent perturbation datum
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}

for
\Gamma
is equal after restriction to the universal curve for
the type
\Gamma^{\prime}
on both sides of the edge
e
. If in the locality
axiom, the perturbation is
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
defined by pulling back by
the map
\pi_{v}:\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma,v}\to\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma(v)}
, then the
perturbation datum on both surface components will be required to be
equal even when the edge length of
e
is finite. This creates a
problem, because in order to obtain transversality in the case

\ell(e)=0
, we need the perturbation datum on both surface
components to be independent of each other.

Remark 6.7
.

(Perturbations for moduli spaces of spheres) The coherence
conditions required to define moduli spaces of spheres are much
simpler than those required for treed disks: The only coherence
condition is
(Collapsing edges)
, which
translates to the statement that the perturbation datum is
continuous on the compactified moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
of
n
-marked
spheres. An important difference from the disk case is that the
perturbation datum on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
can be defined independently of

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
for any
k<n
.

We require perturbations to be adapted to the stabilizing pair

(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
in the sense that
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
is the background almost
complex structure; the domain-dependent almost complex structures

\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}
are adapted to the stabilizing divisor (as in Definition

5.23
) in that the tangent space of the stabilizing
divisor is
\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}(z)
-invariant for all points
z\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
;
and finally, the domain-dependent almost complex structures take
values in small enough neighborhoods of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
, with the size of these
neighborhoods being smaller for types
\Gamma
with more interior
markings. The reason for the last requirement is explained later in
Remark
6.10
after perturbed holomorphic maps are defined.

Definition 6.8
.

(Perturbations adapted
to a stabilizing divisor) Let
k\gg 0
, and
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
be a
stabilizing pair on the broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
(as in Definition

5.24
), such that
D_{P}\subset\overline{X}_{P}
is dual to

k[\omega_{X_{P}}]
for all polytopes
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, let

\mathfrak{D}
be disjoint from the Lagrangian submanifold
L
. Suppose

\Gamma
is a type of treed disk. A perturbation datum

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}=(\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma},F_{\Gamma})
on
(\mathfrak{X},L)
is adapted to the
pair
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
if

—

\mathfrak{J}_{0}
is the background almost complex structure for

\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}
, meaning that each
\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}
is a domain-dependent
perturbation of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
, and

—

for any treed curve
C=S\cup T
, and a connected
component
S^{\prime}\subset S
with
d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(S^{\prime})
interior markings,

\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}(S^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D};\mathfrak{J}_{0},\tfrac{1}{k}d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(S^{\prime}))\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}.

(6.7)

Here

\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D};\mathfrak{J}_{0},\tfrac{1}{k}d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(S^{\prime}))\subset\{\mathfrak{J}\in\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D}):\mathfrak{J}|T\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{J}_{0}|T\mathfrak{D}\}

is the neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
consisting of
\frac{1}{k}d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(S^{\prime})
-stabilizing almost complex structures (see
Proposition
5.25
), and
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
is a neighborhood of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
on which the results on Hofer energy hold (see Lemma
7.21
).

The set of perturbation data adapted to
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
is denoted by

\mathscr{P}_{\Gamma}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
.

6.2
Perturbed maps

Given domain-dependent perturbations adapted to a stabilizing divisor
as in the previous section, we define
perturbed pseudoholomorphic maps and their moduli spaces.

Definition 6.9
.

(Perturbed holomorphic treed disks)

Given a rational symplectic manifold
(X,\omega_{X})
, a rational Lagrangian
L\subset X
,
a coherent perturbation datum
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}=(J_{\Gamma},F_{\Gamma})\}_{\Gamma}
on
X

adapted to a stabilizing divisor
D
(as in Definition

6.8
), a

perturbed holomorphic treed disk

u
of stable domain type

\Gamma
consists of

(a)

a treed disk
C=S\cup T
of type

\Gamma
, and

(b)

a continuous map

u:C\to X,

such that the following hold:

(a)

(Boundary condition) The tree components and the
boundary of the surface components are mapped to the Lagrangian
submanifold :

u(\partial S\cup T)\subset L.

(b)

(Surface equation) On the surface part
S
of
C
, the
map
u
is holomorphic with respect to the given domain-dependent
almost complex structure: if
j
denotes the complex structure on

S
then

J_{\Gamma}(z,u(z))\ {\operatorname{d}}u_{S}={\operatorname{d}}u_{S}\ j.

(6.8)

(c)

(Boundary tree equation) On the boundary tree part
T\subset C
the map
u
is a collection of gradient trajectories:

\frac{d}{ds}u_{T}=-\operatorname{grad}_{F_{\Gamma}(s,u(s))}u_{T}

(6.9)

where
s
is a coordinate on the segment so that the segment has
the given length. Thus, for each treed edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
the length of the trajectory

u|_{T_{e}}
is equal to
\ell(e)
.

(d)

(Adapted to stabilizing divisor)

Each interior marking

z_{e},e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},\to}(\Gamma)
maps to
D
under
u
and
each connected component
C^{\prime}
of
u^{-1}(D)\subset C
contains an
interior marking.

We say that the map
u
is
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
-holomorphic
.
A perturbed holomorphic disk
u
with an unstable domain type
\Gamma

is defined
similarly except that
u_{v},u_{e}
are

\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{st}{\Gamma}}
-holomorphic, where
\operatorname{st}{\Gamma}
is the
stabilization of
\Gamma
. On surface and treed components that get collapsed in the stabilization, the map
u
is pseudoholomorphic with respect to the background data
(J_{0},F_{0})
.

Remark 6.10
.

Our domains below are stable on the
surface parts because of the additional markings arising from
intersections with the stabilizing divisor.
So the content of the last sentence is that
on broken Morse trajectories, for segments that are infinite in both
directions the gradient flow equation is unperturbed.

Perturbed holomorphic disks in broken manifolds are defined analogously to the smooth version in Definition

6.9
.

Definition 6.11
.

(Perturbed holomorphic
broken treed disks)
Given
a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}:=\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
with rational symplectic cut spaces
X_{P}
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
, a rational Lagrangian

L\subset X_{P_{0}}
,
a coherent perturbation datum

\mathfrak{p}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}=(\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma},F_{\Gamma})\}_{\Gamma}
on
\mathfrak{X}
that is adapted to a broken stabilizing
divisor
\mathfrak{D}
(as in Definition
6.8
),
a
perturbed holomorphic broken treed disk

u
of stable domain type
\Gamma

consists of

(a)

a treed disk
C=S\cup T
of type
\Gamma
;

(b)

a tropical structure
\mathcal{T}
on
\Gamma
;

(c)

a collection of maps on surface components

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)},\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)

that are
\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma}
-holomorphic as in (
6.8
) where

S_{v}^{\circ}
is the punctured domain curve with lifts of tropical
nodal points deleted (see (
4.14
)), satisfy the Lagrangian
boundary condition
u_{v}(\partial S_{v})\subset L
for all

v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
and are adapted to the stabilizing divisor
\mathfrak{D}
(as in Definition
6.9
(
d
)); and

(d)

a collection of
F_{\Gamma}
-gradient flow lines as
in (
6.9
)

u_{e}:T_{e}\to L\subset X_{P_{0}},\quad e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)

on the treed parts,
that satisfy all the matching conditions on
broken treed holomorphic maps (Definition
4.13
).

We say that the map
u
is
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
-holomorphic
.

Broken maps on unstable domain types are defined similarly as the unbroken case in
Definition
6.9
.

Remark 6.12
.

We explain why we use
\frac{1}{k}d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(S^{\prime})
-stabilizing almost
complex structures in (
6.7
). Let

\underline{J}=(J_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
be a collection of coherent
domain-dependent almost complex structures satisfying
(
6.7
). Let
S_{\nu}
be a sequence of nodal curves of type

\Gamma
with
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})
markings. Then the limit
u
of any
sequence
u_{\nu}:S_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}
of
J_{\Gamma}
-holomorphic broken maps
can not have an unstable domain component. Indeed, the area of the
maps
u_{\nu}
is
\frac{d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}{k}
, and so an unstable component

u_{v}
of the
u
has area
\leq\frac{d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}{k}
. Therefore,
u

is holomorphic with respect to a domain-independent almost complex
structure
\mathfrak{J}_{v}
which, by (
6.7
), is

\frac{d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}{k}
-stabilizing. This contradicts the existence of
a non-constant map
u_{v}
.

We now define moduli spaces of perturbed broken treed holomorphic disks.
The moduli space of such maps is stratified by combinatorial type.

Definition 6.13
.

The
combinatorial type of
an perturbed holomorphic broken treed disk

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
adapted to a
broken divisor
\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathfrak{X}-L
consists of

(a)

the combinatorial type
\Gamma
of its domain
C
,

(b)

the tropical structure on
\Gamma
, which consists of an
assignment of polytopes for vertices, and directions for tropical
edges :

\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\ni v\mapsto P(v)\in\mathcal{P},\quad\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\ni e\mapsto\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}};

(c)

a labelling

d(v):=(\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v})_{*}[(S_{v},\partial S_{v})]\in\begin{cases}H_{2}(\overline{X}_{P_{0}},L),\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma),\\
H_{2}(\overline{X}_{P(v)}),\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)\end{cases}

that assigns to each vertex
v
of
\Gamma
the homology class
of the disk/sphere obtained by extending

\pi_{P(v)}\circ u_{v}:S^{\circ}_{v}\to X_{P(v)}
over the
punctures corresponding to nodal lifts;
2
2

2

In case the
space
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
has an orbifold compactification

\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
(see Remark
3.62
), the homology
class of
(u_{v})_{*}[S_{v}]\in H_{2}(\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)})
can be read
off from
d(v)
and the tropical graph.

(d)

a labelling

\mu_{\mathfrak{D}}:\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},\rightarrow}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

that records the order of tangency of the map
u
to the divisor

\mathfrak{D}
at markings that do not lie on horizontally constant
components (with the convention that a transverse intersection
has order
1
).

The type is denoted simply as
\Gamma
, suppressing

\mathcal{T},d,\mu_{\mathfrak{D}}
in the notation, or by
\Gamma_{X}
if we wish to
distinguish a type of map
\Gamma_{X}
from a type of treed disk

\Gamma
.

We introduce the following notations for moduli spaces.
Given a collection of coherent perturbation data
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
,
let

\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})
be the moduli space of isomorphism classes of
stable treed broken holomorphic disks in
\mathfrak{X}
with boundary in

L
, where isomorphism is modulo
reparametrizations of domains and is defined in Definition

4.15
(
c
).
Let

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})\subset\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})

be the
moduli space of broken maps
of combinatorial type

\Gamma
modulo the action of domain reparametrizations.
We drop the perturbation datum
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
from the notation if it is obvious from
the context. We recall
that domain reparametrizations are isomorphisms of broken maps as in
Definition
4.15
(
c
). The group of tropical symmetries

T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
acts naturally on
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})
.
The quotient

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma,{\operatorname{red}}}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}):=\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})/T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)

is the
reduced moduli space of broken maps
of combinatorial type

\Gamma
. For
\underline{x}\in\mathcal{I}(L)^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+1}
, let

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma},\underline{x})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})

(6.10)

denote the subset of perturbed holomorphic treed disks of type

\Gamma
with limits

\underline{x}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})})\in\mathcal{I}(L)
along the root
and leaves. The union over all types with
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
incoming leaves
is denoted

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}):=\bigcup_{\Gamma,\underline{x}}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma},\underline{x}).

The space
{\mathcal{M}}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})
has a natural topology
for which convergence is a version of Gromov convergence defined in
Chapter
8
. For a type
\Gamma
and labels
\underline{x}
on leaves, the expected dimension of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma},\underline{x})
of broken maps is given by the index
i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})
defined in (
6.31
) in Section
6.4
. For
d=0,1
, we denote by

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})_{d}:=\bigcup_{(\Gamma,\underline{x}):i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})=d}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma},\underline{x})

(6.11)

the union of
d
-dimensional strata with
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
incoming boundary edges in the moduli space of broken maps. In this book, we show that the
compactifications of zero and one-dimensional components of the moduli
space
\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})^{<E}
are manifolds. For
one-dimensional components, the boundary consists of configurations
with disk bubbling. For zero and one-dimensional components of

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}^{{\operatorname{brok}}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})
the symmetry tropical group is finite.

Remark 6.14
.

(Perturbed maps and curve counts) A perturbed holomorphic map

u
in a zero-dimensional moduli space makes a contribution of

\frac{1}{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})!}
to curve counts. Here
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})
is the
number of interior leaves of
u
, and hence is the number of
intersections of the map
u
with the stabilizing divisor. To
justify this multiplicative factor, let us consider the special case
where for a certain type of map, the moduli space can be regularized
using a domain-independent perturbation. Then there are
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})!

ways of labelling the divisor intersections as

z_{1},\dots,z_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}
. Each of these labellings is a different
map in the moduli space, and therefore a corrective factor of

\frac{1}{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})!}
needs to be inserted.

6.3
Fredholm theory for broken maps

We introduce a weighted Sobolev space needed for the transversality
result.
The norm is defined by viewing the domain as having punctures
that map to cylindrical ends in the target. With this Sobolev
completion, we can enforce higher order tangencies with relative
divisors. We focus on surface components of a broken map, since the
transversality and gluing results use a standard norm on tree components.
Moduli spaces of broken holomorphic maps are cut out as zero sets of a Cauchy-Riemann operator on the weighted Sobolev space.
Later in the section, we define another Cauchy-Riemann operator on the
Sobolev completion of the space of maps on compact curves, and show that
the linearizations of both Cauchy-Riemann operators have kernels and
cokernels of the same dimension as each other. The latter operator is
often useful for index computations.

The Sobolev norm is defined component-wise for a broken map. We start
by defining a
relative map
, which is a single component of a
broken map. Tropical nodes in the broken map appear as markings on a
relative map, called
tropical markings
; they correspond to
one-sided edges
\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
on the combinatorial type

\Gamma
of the relative map.

Definition 6.15
.

(Relative map) A
relative type
is a graph
\Gamma
with a
single vertex
v
, a collection
\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
of edges each
with a single end-point, a polytope
P(v)\in\mathcal{P}
, directions for the
edges

\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}},\quad e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma),

and homology data
d(v)\in H_{2}(\overline{X}_{P(v)})
. A
relative
map
is a map
u:C^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
from a punctured
domain
C^{\circ}:=C\backslash\{z_{e}:e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}
where

C
is a disk or sphere, to the manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
with
cylindrical ends, such that at a puncture
z_{e}

u
is asymptotic to
a
\mathcal{T}(e)
-cylinder, and the extension of
\pi_{P(v)}\circ u
over the
punctures has homology class
d(v)\in H_{2}(\overline{X}_{P(v)})
.

We introduce the space of smooth relative maps whose Sobolev
completion will be given later. Let
(C,j)
be a connected Riemann
surface with a set
\{z_{e}:e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}
of tropical
markings. Let

C^{\circ}:=C\backslash\{z_{e}:e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}

(6.12)

be the curve with the tropical marked points removed. For the purpose
of defining weighted Sobolev norms, we introduce cylindrical
coordinates

(s_{e},t_{e}):U_{z_{e}}\backslash\{z_{e}\}\to[0,\infty)\times S^{1}

(6.13)

on the neighborhood
U_{z_{e}}\subset C
of every puncture
z_{e}
. Let

\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})

be the set of relative maps, which at a puncture

z_{e}\in C\backslash C^{\circ}
corresponding to an edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
is asymptotically close to a

\mathcal{T}(e)
-cylinder

u_{\operatorname{vert},e}:[0,\infty)\times S^{1}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)},\quad(s_{e},t_{e})\mapsto e^{\mathcal{T}(e)(s_{e}+it_{e})}x_{e}

for some point
x_{e}\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
. Here, we recall that the
punctured neighborhood at
z_{e}
in
C^{\circ}
maps to a

P(e)
-cylindrical end of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
and the
P(e)
-cylindrical end is
identified to
\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
, see (
3.51
). An infinitesimal
deformation of a
\mathcal{T}(e)
-cylinder is given by a vector

\xi_{e}\in T_{x_{e}}\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
. It defines a section

\xi_{e,\mathcal{T}(e)}:=\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}(e^{\mathcal{T}(e)(s+it)}\exp_{x_{e}}(\tau\xi_{e}))|_{\tau=0}\in\Gamma(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1},u_{\operatorname{vert},e}^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}).

(6.14)

The tangent space of
\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})
at
u
consists of
sections

\xi\in\Gamma(C^{\circ},u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P})

which, for any edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
, have limits

\xi_{e}:=\lim_{z\to z_{e}}\xi\in T_{x_{e}}\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}

(6.15)

in the sense that a section
\xi
is asymptotically close to the section

\xi_{e,\mathcal{T}(e)}
(
6.14
) as
z\to z_{e}
.

Remark 6.16
.

The quantity
\xi_{e}
defined in (
6.15
) is the
infinitesimal change in the tropical evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{z_{e}}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}(u)\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
(see (
4.15
)) when
the broken map
u
is infinitesimally deformed by
\xi
. Since the
tropical evaluation map depends on the choice of cylindrical
coordinates (
6.13
) in the punctured neighborhood of
z_{e}

in the domain curve, the quantity
\xi_{e}
depends on infinitesimal
changes in the domain cylindrical coordinates. On the other hand,
the projected evaluation map

\pi_{\mathcal{T}(e)}^{\perp}(\operatorname{ev}_{z_{e}}(u))\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
is
independent of the domain cylindrical coordinates, and the same is
true of its infinitesimal version

\pi_{\mathcal{T}(e)}^{\perp}(\xi_{e})\in T(\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}})
.

We now define the Sobolev norm. Define a cutoff function

\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},[0,1]),\quad\begin{cases}\beta(s)=0&s\leq 0\\
\beta(s)=1&s\geq 1\end{cases}.

(6.16)

Let

\kappa:C^{\circ}\to\mathbb{R}

(6.17)

be equal to
\beta(s_{e})s_{e}
near the puncture corresponding to

z_{e}
, for any edge
e
, and
0
outside all the edge neighbourhoods.
For a section
\xi:C^{\circ}\to E
of a vector bundle
E
with
connection
\nabla
, integers
k\geq 0
,
p>1
and constant

\lambda\in(0,1)
define the
W^{k,p,\lambda}
-norm of
\xi
as

\lVert\xi\rVert_{W^{k,p,\lambda}}^{p}:=\sum_{0\leq i\leq k}\int_{C^{\circ}}|\nabla^{i}\xi|^{p}\exp(\lambda\kappa p)\operatorname{dvol}_{C^{\circ}}.

The norm on a section
\xi
is

\lVert\xi\rVert^{\circ}_{\Gamma}:={\textstyle\sum}_{e}|\xi_{e}|+\lVert\xi-{\textstyle\sum}_{e}\beta(s_{e})\mathbb{T}_{u}^{e}\xi_{e,\mathcal{T}(e)}\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}},

(6.18)

where

\xi_{e}:=\lim_{z\to z_{e}}\xi\in T_{x_{e}}\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}

(6.19)

near the puncture
z_{e}
as in (
6.19
),
u
is asymptotic to a vertical cylinder

u_{\operatorname{vert},e}
,

\mathbb{T}_{u}^{e}:u_{\operatorname{vert},e}^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P}

is the parallel transport map along geodesics, and
\xi_{e,\mathcal{T}(e)}
is a section of
u_{\operatorname{vert},e}^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P}
as in (
6.14
).

There are similar Sobolev spaces of maps and one-forms. Let

\operatorname{Map}^{1,p,\lambda}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})
be the Banach completion of
the space of maps
\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})
under the norm
(
6.18
). That is, for a smooth map

u\in\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})
and a section

\xi\in\Gamma(C^{\circ},u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P})
satisfying

\lVert\xi\rVert_{\Gamma}^{\circ}<\infty
, the map
\exp_{u}\xi
belongs to the
completion
\operatorname{Map}^{1,p,\lambda}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})
. Let

\mathcal{E}^{p,\lambda}\to\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}^{1,p,\lambda}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})

be the vector bundle whose fiber

\mathcal{E}^{p,\lambda}_{u}=\Omega^{0,1}(C^{\circ},u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P})_{L^{p,\lambda}}

is the space of
(0,1)
-forms with respect to
(j,J)
, where
J
is a
fixed cylindrical almost complex structure. The vertical projection
of the linearization of the Cauchy-Riemann operator
\overline{\partial}_{j,J}
at

u

D_{u}^{\circ}:T_{u}\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}^{1,p,\lambda}\to\mathcal{E}_{u}^{p,\lambda}

is a Fredholm operator by results of Lockhart-McOwen
[
51
]
.

Next, we describe a linearized
\overline{\partial}
-operator on the compact curve

C
, denoted by
D_{u}
. This operator will be useful
for index computations. It is not used for transversality and gluing
proofs where we stick to
D_{u}^{\circ}.
Given a relative map

u:C^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, we define an extension of the pullback bundle

u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to C^{\circ}
to a bundle over
C
, as follows. Define a (partial)
almost complex orbifold compactification of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, denoted by

\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}
, as the union

\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}:=\mathfrak{X}_{P}\cup\cup_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}

(6.20)

obtained by adding a divisor at infinity
Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}
corresponding to
every tropical marking
z_{e}
.
3
3

3

The space
\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}
is a different compactification
of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
compared to
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
(when it exists).

The divisor
Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}
is
defined as

Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}:=\{[x]=T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}x\ :\ x\in\mathfrak{X}_{P}\},

(6.21)

where each
[x]
is the limit point of a
T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
-orbit, that
is,
\lim_{s\to\infty}e^{(s+it)\mathcal{T}(e)}x=[x]
, and

Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}=Y_{\mathcal{T}(e^{\prime})}
if
\mathcal{T}(e)=\lambda\mathcal{T}(e^{\prime})
for some

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. The map
u
extends over all tropical markings

z_{e}
to yield
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}
, with
u(z_{e})\in Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}
.
The extension of
u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to C^{\circ}
is the pullback bundle

u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}\to C
. Recall from Definition
4.22

that for any tropical marking
z_{e}\in C\backslash C^{\circ}
, the direction

\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}}
is the product

\mathcal{T}(e)=\mu_{e}\mathcal{T}(e)_{{\operatorname{prim}}},

(6.22)

of the primitive direction
\mathcal{T}(e)_{{\operatorname{prim}}}\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(e),\mathbb{Z}}
and a multiplicity

\mu_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Denote by

D_{u}:W^{k,p}(\Omega^{0}_{\underline{\mu}}(C,u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}))\to W^{k-1,p}(\Omega^{0,1}_{\underline{\mu}-1}(C,u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}))

(6.23)

the linearization of the
\overline{\partial}
-operator defined on the space of sections on the compactification
\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}
.
Here,

\underline{\mu}=(\mu_{e}\in\mathbb{N})_{e}
, and the subscript in the domain and
codomain indicate the order of vanishing of the sections at the
tropical markings
z_{e}
,
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
in the direction
normal to the divisor at infinity
Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}
; and the Sobolev
indices are such that

k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\quad p>1,\quad k>\mu_{e}+2/p\kern 5.0pt\forall e

so that the sections with prescribed orders of vanishing at the divisors are well-defined.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
u\in\operatorname{Map}_{\Gamma}(C^{\circ},\mathfrak{X}_{P})
be a relative holomorphic map.
There are isomorphisms

\operatorname{ker}(D_{u})\simeq\operatorname{ker}(D_{u}^{\circ}),\quad\operatorname{coker}(D_{u})\simeq\operatorname{coker}(D_{u}^{\circ}).

Beweis.

Since the proof is by a local computation near each of the special
points, it is enough to prove the result for a map whose domain is a
single curve component with a single tropical marking
z_{e}
. By the
description of
D_{u}
(see (
6.23
)), the curve
u
extends
over
z_{e}
to yield
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}:=\mathfrak{X}_{P}\cup Y
(as in
(
6.20
)), with
u(z_{e})
mapping to the divisor at
infinity
Y
, and
u
intersecting the divisor
Y
at
z_{e}
with
multiplicity
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
. We abbreviate
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}:=\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}
.
We assume that the marked point is
0\in C
.

The proof is based on the fact that in a neighborhood of the divisor
the tangent space splits into a vertical and a horizontal subspace.
Indeed, because of the cylindrical almost complex structure, there
is a neighborhood
U_{Y}\subset\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
of
Y
for which there is a
projection
\pi_{Y}:U_{Y}\to Y
with holomorphic fibers. The tangent
space splits into a
J
-holomorphic horizontal and vertical part:

T\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}|U_{Y}\simeq V\oplus H,\quad H:=\pi_{Y}^{*}TY,\quad V:=\operatorname{ker}(d\pi_{Y}).

(6.24)

Under
the splitting (
6.24
),

D_{u}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{\partial}&A\\
0&D_{u}^{Y}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)

(6.25)

in a neighbourhood
U_{0}\subset C
of the marked point. Here

\overline{\partial}:\Gamma(U_{0},u^{*}V)\to\Omega^{0,1}(U_{0},u^{*}V)
is the standard
Cauchy-Riemann operator,

A:\Gamma(U_{0},u^{*}H)\to\Omega^{0,1}(U_{0},u^{*}V)
is multiplication with
a tensor and

D_{u}^{Y}:\Gamma(U_{0},u^{*}H)\to\Omega^{0,1}(U_{0},u^{*}H)

is the lift of the linearized
operator
D_{u_{Y}}
, where
u_{Y}:=\pi_{Y}\circ u
.

The correspondence for the kernels follows by decay estimates. For
an element
\xi\in W^{k,p}(C,u^{*}T\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P})
, we denote the horizontal
part and vertical part by

\xi_{h}\in W^{k,p}(U_{0},u^{*}H),\quad\xi_{v}\in W^{k,p}(U_{0},u^{*}V).

in the neighbourhood
U_{0}\subset C
of
0
.
Denote by
|\cdot|
resp.
|\cdot|^{\circ}
the ordinary metric on
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}

resp. the cylindrical metric on
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. Then, we have

|\xi_{h}(z)|^{\circ}\sim|\xi_{h}(z)|,\quad|\xi_{v}(z)|^{\circ}\sim|\xi_{v}(z)|/|u(z)|,\quad z\neq 0,

(6.26)

where the norm on
u(z)
is a norm on the fiber of the projection

U_{Y}\to Y
. Since

|\xi_{h}|\leq c,\quad|\xi_{v}(z)|\leq c|z|^{\mu},\quad|u(z)|\sim c|z|^{\mu},

we conclude that
|\xi_{h}|^{\circ}
and
|\xi_{v}(z)|^{\circ}
are uniformly
bounded and have a limit as
z\to 0
. Since
\xi
is smooth on
C
,
the convergence to
\xi(0)
is exponential on
C^{\circ}
:

|\xi(z)-\xi(0)|^{\circ}\sim c|z|\sim ce^{-s},

where
(s,t)
are cylindrical coordinates on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0\}
near
the puncture, and

|\nabla\xi_{h}(z)|^{\circ},|\nabla\xi_{v}(z)|^{\circ}\sim ce^{-s}

since the derivative is with respect to the cylindrical coordinates on
C^{\circ}
.
We conclude

\xi\in W^{k,p}_{\Gamma}(C,\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P})\implies\xi-\xi(0)\in W^{1,p,\lambda}(C^{\circ},u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P})

for any
0<\lambda<1
, which implies
\operatorname{ker}D_{u}\subset\operatorname{ker}D_{u}^{\circ}
.
The converse
\operatorname{ker}D_{u}^{\circ}\subset\operatorname{ker}D_{u}
is proved using
removal of singularity, elliptic regularity, and the estimate (
6.26
).

A similar argument holds for the cokernels. We first prove the
inclusion
\operatorname{coker}(D_{u})\subset\operatorname{coker}(D_{u}^{\circ})
. Consider

\eta\in\operatorname{coker}(D_{u})
. We view
\operatorname{coker}(D_{u})
as a subspace in the

(W^{k-1,p})^{\vee}
-completion of
\Omega^{1,0}(C,u^{*}(T^{*}\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}))
,
which is a space of distributions. (Here we use
\Omega^{1,0}(u^{*}(T^{*}\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}))

instead of
\Omega^{0,1}(u^{*}T\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P})
to avoid making choices of metrics on

C
and
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
.) By elliptic regularity, the distribution

\eta
is represented by a smooth section in the complement of
marked points. So we focus attention in a neighbourhood

U_{0}\subset C
of
0
. For any
W^{k,p}
-section

\xi:C\to u^{*}T\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
that is supported in
U_{0}
, and is
vertical, we have
z^{\mu}\xi\in W^{k,p}_{\Gamma}(C,u^{*}T\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P})
. So for any such
\xi
,

0=\int_{C}(\overline{\partial}(z^{\mu}\xi),\eta_{v}).

Therefore,
\overline{\partial}(z^{\mu}\eta_{v})=0
weakly in
U_{0}
and so,

z^{\mu}\eta_{v}
can be represented by a smooth function. Next, using
the split form (
6.25
), we observe that any section
\xi

that is supported in
U_{0}
and is horizontal satisfies

(A\xi,\eta_{v})+(D_{u}^{Y}\xi,\eta_{h})=0,

and so,
A^{*}\eta_{v}+(D_{u}^{Y})^{*}\xi_{h}=0
weakly in
U_{0}
. The tensor

A
vanishes to order
\mu
at
0\in C
in the
|\cdot|
-norm, as a
consequence of the transformation relation (
6.26
) and the
fact that
A
is bounded in the
|\cdot|^{\circ}
norm. So,

A^{*}\eta_{v}
is smooth in
U_{0}
. By elliptic regularity
\xi_{h}
is
smooth in
U_{0}
. Finally,
\eta
is in
\operatorname{coker}D_{u}^{\circ}
because of
the following transformations valid in
U_{0}
:

|\eta_{h}(z)|^{\circ}\sim|\eta_{h}(z)|,\quad|\eta_{v}(z)|^{\circ}\sim|\eta_{v}(z)|\cdot|u(z)|,\quad z\neq 0.

(6.27)

Indeed,
|\eta(z)|^{\circ}
is bounded, and therefore is in

L^{p^{*},-\lambda}
the dual space of
L^{p,\lambda}
. The reverse
inclusion
\operatorname{coker}(D_{u}^{\circ})\subset\operatorname{coker}(D_{u})
follows formally
from the inclusion relation

W^{k-1,p}_{\mu-1}(\Omega^{0,1}(C,u^{*}T\hat{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}))\to L^{p,\lambda}(\Omega^{0,1}(C^{\circ},u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P}))

between the target spaces of
D_{u}
and
D_{u}^{\circ}
.
∎

6.4
The index of a broken map

The index of a broken map is the expected dimension of the moduli
space containing the broken map. In this section, we give an
expression for the index in terms of the Fredholm index of the
linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator defined in the last section. We
also prove that collapsing tropical edges in the tropical graph of the
broken map does not change the expected dimension of the moduli space.

We start by stating the expected dimension of the moduli space of
relative maps, which are single components of broken maps. We recall
that the
boundary Maslov index
I(E,F)

is an integer
assigned to a pair
(E,F)
consisting of a complex vector bundle
E

on a Riemann surface
C
with boundary, and a totally real subbundle

F\subset E|_{\partial C}
on the boundary (see
[
55
, Appendix
C]
). In case
\partial C=\emptyset
, the Maslov index

I(E,\emptyset)
is twice the first Chern number
c_{1}(E)
.

Lemma 6.18
.

(Expected dimension for relative maps)

Let
u
be a relative map of type

\Gamma_{v}
with a collection of tropical markings
z_{e}

corresponding to
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{v})
, each with
multiplicity
\mu_{e}\in\mathbb{N}
, and with simple intersections with the
stabilizing divisor
\mathfrak{D}
. Then, the expected dimension of the
moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{v}}(\mathfrak{X})
of relative maps of type

\Gamma_{v}
is

i(\Gamma_{v})=\begin{cases}(d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+1)+i_{\operatorname{Morse}}(\underline{x})+I_{\operatorname{adj}}(\Gamma_{v})-\dim\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb D^{2}),&\text{$\Gamma_{v}$ is a disk },\\
\dim(X)+I_{\operatorname{adj}}(\Gamma_{v})-\dim\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^{1}),&\text{$\Gamma_{v}$ is a sphere}\end{cases}

(6.28)

where
I_{\operatorname{adj}}(\Gamma_{v})
is the adjusted Maslov index defined as

{\text{{(Adjusted Maslov index)}}}\quad I_{\operatorname{adj}}(\Gamma_{v}):=I_{\operatorname{adj}}(u):=I((u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(v),\mathcal{T}}),(\partial u)^{*}TL)\\
-\sum_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}2(\mu^{\operatorname{trop}}_{e}-1);

(6.29)

the Morse index of the tuple is the difference

i_{\operatorname{Morse}}(\underline{x}):=i_{\operatorname{Morse}}(x_{0})-{\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}i_{\operatorname{Morse}}(x_{i}),

and
i_{\operatorname{Morse}}(x_{i})
is the dimension of the stable manifold of

x_{i}
in
L
. Furthermore,

I_{\operatorname{adj}}(u)=\operatorname{ind}(D_{u})+2|\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)|.

(6.30)

Beweis.

We recall from the paragraph preceding (
6.23
) that,
assuming the tropical markings are fixed, the moduli space of
relative maps of type
\Gamma_{v}
is cut out as the zero set of
D_{u}

from the Banach space of sections

W^{k,p}_{\underline{\mu}}(C,u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}})
that vanish to order
\mu_{e}

in the direction normal to the divisor
Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}
at the tropical
marking
z_{e}
, and therefore the expected dimension is

\operatorname{ind}(D_{u})
. Since the tropical markings are free to move on the
domain, the expected dimension of
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{v}}(\mathfrak{X})
is given by
the first expression in (
6.30
). To obtain the second
expression, we observe that the expected dimension of moduli space
of holomorphic maps
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}}
is the Maslov index

I(u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P,\mathcal{T}},(\partial u)^{*}TL)
, and for any

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma_{v})
, the choice of
z_{e}
on
C
adds
2

to the expected dimension, and the requirement that there is a
tangency of order
\mu_{e}
with the divisor
Y_{\mathcal{T}(e)}
at
z_{e}

subtracts
2\mu_{e}
.
∎

Example 6.19
.

Suppose

\overline{X}_{P(v)}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
with a Hamiltonian
(S^{1})^{2}
-action,

X_{P(v)}
is the complement of the 3 torus-invariant divisors,

L\subset\overline{X}_{P(v)}
is a toric Lagrangian, and

u:(D,\partial D)\to(\overline{X}_{P(v)},L)
is a disk through the point

[1:0:0]
whose boundary Maslov index is
4
. In contrast, the
adjusted Maslov index
I_{\operatorname{adj}}(u)
is
2
, because the adjusted
Maslov index accounts for the constraint that the disk passes
through the point
[1:0:0]
which cuts down the index by
2
. The
reason for the discrepancy is that the pullback bundle

u^{*}T\overline{X}_{P(v)}
is different from
u^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(v),\mathcal{T}}
since a
tropical marking maps to the intersection of two divisors in

\overline{X}_{P(v)}
, which is replaced by a single divisor in

\mathfrak{X}_{P(v),\mathcal{T}}
.

For a type of broken maps the expected dimension of the moduli space
is the same as that of the glued type of unbroken maps. We define

glued type
:

Definition 6.20
.

(Glued type) Let

\Gamma
be the combinatorial type of a broken map. Then the

glued type

\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}
is the type of the unbroken map
obtained by collapsing the tropical edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
in
\Gamma
.

Proposition 6.21
.

(Expected dimension) Suppose
\Gamma
is the combinatorial type
of a broken map.

(a)

The expected dimension of the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})
