. This is in contrast with the
polyhedral decomposition of Chapter
3
which was defined
for the entire vector space. A decomposition of the vector space
V

induces a decomposition of
Q\subset V
as we now describe:

Let
V
be a vector space and let
Q\subset V
be a polytope of the
same dimension as
V
. A polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of the vector
space
V
for which all top-dimensional polytopes
P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}

intersect
Q^{\circ}
induces a polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}(Q)
of
Q

whose top-dimensonal polytopes are

\mathcal{P}(Q)^{(0)}:=\{P\cap Q:P\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}\},

and whose lower dimensional polytopes are faces of polytopes in

\mathcal{P}_{Q}^{(0)}
; see Figure
7.3
.

Abbildung 7.3:
Left: A polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of
V
induces a polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}(Q)
of
Q\subset V
. Right : The corresponding dual complexes.

In Lemma
7.14
, we will construct a sequence of undilated
squashing maps
\aleph_{\nu}:V\to Q
whose underlying polyhedral
decomposition of
Q
is
\mathcal{P}(Q)
described in the previous paragraph.
The squashing maps will have the property that for any two
top-dimensional polytopes
Q_{i},Q_{j}
of
\mathcal{Q}
, the distance between

\aleph^{-1}(Q_{i}^{\circ})
,
\aleph^{-1}(Q_{j}^{\circ})
goes to infinity as

\nu\to\infty
.

Lemma 7.14
.

(From a dual complex to squashing maps)
Let
Q
,
V
,
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{P}(Q)
be as above. Suppose
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a dual complex of
\mathcal{P}
that respects the metric on
V
(as in Remark
3.26
).

(a)

There is a dual complex

B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}(Q)}
of
\mathcal{P}(Q)
that respects the metric on
V
and
for which
B^{\vee}
is a subcomplex.

(b)

For any
\nu\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, there is a
squashing map
\aleph_{\nu}:V\to Q
such that for any polytope

P\in\mathcal{P}(Q)
,

\aleph^{-1}_{\nu}(P)\xrightarrow{\text{isomorphic}}P\times\nu P^{\vee},

(7.23)

where
P^{\vee}\subset V
is the dual polytope of
P
in

B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}(Q)}
.

(c)

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}(Q)^{(0)}
be a
top-dimensional polytope, and for any
\nu
, let
i_{\nu}:P\to V

be an embedding (which is a translation). For any
\nu
, there is
a unique squashing map that satisfies (
7.23
) and the
condition that
\aleph_{\nu}\circ i_{\nu}=\operatorname{Id}_{P}
. For any two
top-dimensional polytopes
Q_{i},Q_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(Q)^{(0)}
,

d_{V}(\aleph^{-1}(Q_{i}^{\circ}),\aleph^{-1}(Q_{j}^{\circ}))\to\infty\quad\text{as $\nu\to\infty$.}

(7.24)

Beweis.

To construct
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}(Q)}
, we start with

B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
, and add polytopes corresponding to

P_{0}\cap Q_{0}
where
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
and
Q_{0}
is a proper face of

Q
. First consider the case that
Q_{0}
is a facet and
P_{0}
is
top-dimensional. Then, the one-dimensional polytope

(P_{0}\cap Q_{0})^{\vee}
is a ray pointing in the outward normal
direction to
Q_{0}
, whose starting point is

P_{0}^{\vee}\in B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}
. Next, consider the general case when
the face
Q_{0}\subset Q
is the intersection of facets

Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k}
, and the dual polytope of
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
in

B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}
has vertices
P_{1},\dots,P_{\ell}
. Then, we define

(P_{0}\cap Q_{0})^{\vee}:=\operatorname{Convex-hull}(\{(P_{j}\cap Q_{i})^{\vee}\}_{i,j}),

which has the right dimension for the following reason: For any
j
,
the convex hull of
C_{j}:=\{P_{j}\cap Q_{i}\}_{i}
is a cone spanned by
the rays
(P_{j}\cap Q_{i})^{\vee}
with vertex
P_{j}^{\vee}
. Denote

C:=C_{j}
, since for any
j^{\prime}
,
C_{j^{\prime}}
is a translate of
C_{j}
. The
convex hull of
\{P_{j}\cap Q_{i}\}_{i,j}
is the product

C\times P_{0}^{\vee}
, and therefore, has codimension

k+\operatorname{codim}(P_{0})
. Here,
P_{0}^{\vee}
is the dual polytope of
P_{0}

in
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

To prove (
b
), we observe that for any
\nu>0

there is an isomorphism

\left(\sqcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(Q)}(\nu P^{\vee}\times P)/\sim\right)\xrightarrow{i_{\nu}}V,

(7.25)

which is uniquely defined up to a translation in
V
. Here,

P^{\vee}
is the dual polytope of
P
in
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}(Q)}
, and for
any pair
Q\subset P
with
\operatorname{codim}_{P}Q=1
,
\sim
identifies

(\nu P^{\vee}\times Q)
, which is a facet in both

(\nu P^{\vee}\times P)
and
(\nu Q^{\vee}\times Q)
. In
(
7.25
), each of the maps
i_{\nu}|(\nu P^{\vee}\times P)

is a translation. The squashing map
\aleph_{\nu}
is defined as
projection to
P
on the subset
i_{\nu}(\nu P^{\vee}\times P)
.

To prove (
c
), we observe that (
7.25
) is
uniquely defined up to translation in
V
. The condition

\aleph_{\nu}\circ i_{\nu}=\operatorname{Id}_{P}
cuts down this translation freedom
and fixes a unique
\aleph_{\nu}
. The property (
7.24
)
follows from the construction of
\aleph_{\nu}
, since the two sets
being considered are separated by a dual polytope scaled up by

\nu
. This finishes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Abbildung 7.4:

The undilated squashing map
\aleph:V\to Q
corresponding to the dual complex
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}(Q)}
in Figure
7.3
.
In
V
, solidly shaded regions are mapped to points,
ruled regions are mapped to lines by contracting each ruling to a
point, and blank regions are mapped isometrically.

The following result constructs a polyhedral decomposition (with dual
complex) of a polytope
Q
that contains a prescribed top-dimensional
piece. The polyhedral decomposition is used to construct squashing
maps in the proof of Proposition
7.27
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
V
be a vector space of dimension
n
equipped with a Euclidean
metric. Let
C\subset V
be a
k
-dimensional cone, that is,
C
is
the convex hull of
k
rays originating at
0\in V
and pointing in
linearly independent directions. Let
C^{\vee}\subset V
be an

(n-k)
-dimensional simplex that is orthogonal to
P
. Then, there
is a polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of
V
that contains

P\times P^{\vee}
as a top-dimensional polytope, and which has a
dual complex
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}
respecting the metric on
V
.

Beweis.

First, we construct a polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{pre}}
of
V

that contains
C
as a polytope, and whose dual complex is an

n
-simplex. Assuming that the cone
C
is the convex hull of rays
pointing in the directions
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
, choose vectors

e_{k+1},\dots,e_{n+1}
such that
V
is the convex hull of the
rays
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n+1
, and (a translate of)

C^{\vee}
is a cross-section of the cone spanned by the rays

\{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e_{i}\}_{k+1\leq i\leq n+1}
. Define a polyhedral
decomposition

\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{pre}}:=\{P_{I}:=\mathbb{R}_{+}\langle{x_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}\subset V:I\subset\{1,\dots,n+1\}\}

of
V
, and observe that its dual complex (respecting the metric) is
an
n
-simplex denoted by
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{pre}}}
. Define the
polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
so that its top-dimensional polytopes
are

\tilde{C}:=C^{\vee}+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\langle{e_{i}}\rangle_{1\leq i\leq k}\simeq C\times C^{\vee},

and
P\backslash\tilde{C}
for all top-dimensional polytopes

P\in\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{pre}}^{(0)}
; see Figure
7.5
. The
polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
has a dual complex
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
which is obtained by adding a vertex
\tilde{C}^{\vee}
to the face

P^{\vee}_{1,\dots,k}
such that the convex hull of

\tilde{C}^{\vee}\cup P^{\vee}_{\{k+1,\dots,n+1\}}
is perpendicular
to
P^{\vee}_{\{1,\dots,k\}}
. The other polytopes in
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{P}}

are spanned by the given set of vertices, and can be determined
combinatorially.
∎

Abbildung 7.5:
Constructing
\mathcal{P}
from
\mathcal{P}_{\operatorname{pre}}
in Lemma

7.15
. Here
n=2
,
k=1
.

7.3
Multi-directional Hofer energy

Hofer energy for a multiple cut is defined as the supremum over
pullbacks of symplectic forms by squashing maps between manifolds that
are induced by squashing maps between polytopes. The definition of
Hofer energy uses squashing maps from
\omega
-complexes to

J
-complexes. In this section, we define these complexes for
neck-stretched and broken manifolds, and prove some useful properties
satisfied by Hofer energy.

In the last section, we defined squashing maps as maps from an affine
space to a compact polytope. The definition extends naturally to maps
between polytopes, and later, complexes.

Definition 7.16
.

(Squashing map between polytopes) Let
P,Q\subset V
be
polytopes, and let
Q
be compact. A squashing map

\aleph:P\to Q
is the restriction of a squashing map

\overline{\aleph}:V\to Q
for which the subset
V_{0}\subset V
on which

\overline{\aleph}
is a dilation is contained in
P
.

7.3.1
Hofer energy for neck-stretched manifolds

We recall from Section
7.1
that for a neck-stretched
manifold
X^{\nu}
, the
\omega
-complex is the dual complex
B^{\vee}
and
the
J
-complex is
\nu B^{\vee}
.

Definition 7.17
.

(Hofer energy for
neck-stretched manifolds) Let
\{X^{\nu}\}_{\nu}
be a family of
neck-stretched manifolds. For any
\nu\geq 1
, the
Hofer
energy
of a map
u:C\to X^{\nu}
is

E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)=\sup_{\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}}\int_{C}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X},

where the supremum is over all maps of complexes

\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
for which

\aleph|\nu P^{\vee}:\nu P^{\vee}\to P^{\vee}
is a squashing map for
each polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
(as in Definition

7.16
); and

\psi_{\aleph}:X^{\nu}\to(X,\omega_{X})

is the map induced by
\aleph
as in (
7.13
). For any
squashing map
\aleph
, the form
\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{X}
is called a

squashed area form
. The squashing map
\aleph
and the squashed
area form are called
unpartitioned
if for each polytope
P
, the
squashing map
\aleph|\nu P^{\vee}
is unpartioned in the sense of
Definition
7.10
(
c
). The supremum over
unpartitioned squashed area forms is called
unpartitioned Hofer
energy
and is denoted by

E^{*}_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)=\sup_{\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}\text{ is unpartitioned}}\int_{C}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X}.

See Figures
7.6
and
7.7
for examples of
squashing maps for neck-stretched manifolds. This ends the Definition.

Abbildung 7.6:
Examples of squashing maps
\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
. In both examples, solidly
shaded regions are mapped to points, ruled regions are mapped to
lines by contracting each ruling to a point, and blank regions are
mapped isometrically. The map in the left is unpartitioned, the map in the right is not.

Abbildung 7.7:
A squashing map
\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
where there are two top-dimensional dual polytopes
Q_{0}^{\vee}
,
Q_{1}^{\vee}
in
B^{\vee}
. The maps
\aleph|\nu Q_{0}^{\vee}
,
\aleph|\nu Q_{1}^{\vee}
are equal on
\nu P^{\vee}
. The map
\aleph
is unpartitioned.

For any squashing map
\aleph
, the map of manifolds

\psi_{\aleph}:X^{\nu}\to X

is continuous and piecewise-smooth
with a finite number of pieces. Therefore, the integral computing
squashed area is well-defined.

Remark 7.18
.

(When is squashed area non-degenerate) Given a squashing map

\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
, the squashed area form is
non-degenerate on the subset
S\subset\nu B^{\vee}
where
\aleph

is dilation by a positive factor. For any polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
,
suppose

\aleph_{P}:=\aleph|\nu P^{\vee}:\nu P^{\vee}\to P^{\vee}

has an underlying decomposition
\mathcal{Q}_{P}
of
P^{\vee}
(as in
(
7.21
)). Then, for any top-dimensional polytope

Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{P}
,
\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{X}
is non-degenerate on

\pi_{\nu B^{\vee}}^{-1}(\aleph_{P}^{-1}(Q^{\circ}))
.

Remark 7.19
.

For a compact curve
C
with boundary, and a map

u:(C,\partial C)\to(X^{\nu},L)
, the Hofer energy is equal to the
area of
u
:

E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u)=\langle{(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)_{*}[C],[\omega_{X}]}\rangle

for any squashing map
\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
. The
quantity
E_{\operatorname{Hof}}
is independent of
\aleph
, because any pair of
squashing maps
\aleph_{0}
,
\aleph_{1}
are isotopic via a family

\{\aleph_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of continuous piecewise smooth maps,
and therefore, the maps
(\psi_{\aleph_{0}}\circ u)
,

(\psi_{\aleph_{1}}\circ u):C\to(X,\omega_{X})
are isotopic.

We point out that squashing maps satisfy the increasing property
(Definition
7.3
), which we initially set out to
achieve.

Lemma 7.20
.

(Squashed area forms are weakly taming)

Suppose
\mathfrak{J}_{0}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
is a locally strongly tamed
cylindrical almost complex structure, and suppose

U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(\mathfrak{X})
is a
C^{0}
-neighborhood of

\mathfrak{J}_{0}
from Lemma
7.8
. Then, for any
\nu
and any
squashing map
\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
, the squashed area
form
\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{X}
is weakly taming (Definition

7.4
) for any
J^{\nu}\in\mathfrak{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(X^{\nu})
obtained
by gluing
\mathfrak{J}\in U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
at cylindrical ends.

Beweis.

A squashing map
\aleph:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
satisfies the
increasing property on
\aleph|\nu P^{\vee}
. Indeed, it is enough to
check this property for squashing maps on vector spaces, which in
turn, follows from the increasing property on undilated squashing
maps, and finally, an undilated squashing map is piecewise smooth,
and on each of these pieces, it is an orthogonal projection.
∎

The next result, which is a consequence of Lemma

7.20
, says that the squashed area

(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X}
is pointwise non-negative if the map

u
is pseudoholomorphic with respect to an almost complex structure
that is close to a locally strongly tamed almost complex structure.

Lemma 7.21
.

(Monotonicity of Hofer
energy) Suppose
\mathfrak{J}_{0}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
is a
locally strongly tamed cylindrical almost complex structure, and
suppose
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(\mathfrak{X})
is a
C^{0}
-neighborhood
of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
from Lemma
7.8
. Let
u:C\to X^{\nu}
be a
map that is holomorphic with respect to the domain dependent almost
complex structure
J^{\nu}:C\to U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
. (Here, for any

z\in C
,
J^{\nu}(z)
denotes both a broken almost complex
structure
\mathfrak{J}_{z}
on
\mathfrak{X}
and the almost complex structure

J_{z}^{\nu}
on
X^{\nu}
obtained by gluing
\mathfrak{J}_{z}
on the neck with
neck length parameter
\nu
.) For any open subset
\Omega\subset C
,

E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u,\Omega)\leq E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u,C).

For some results in Chapter
5
, we consider maps that
are holomorphic with respect to a locally strongly tamed almost
complex structure, in which case, monotonicity of Hofer energy holds
without restricting to a
C^{0}
-neighborhood. The relevant statement is
as follows, and the proof is a consequence of Lemma

7.5
.

Lemma 7.22
.

(Monotonicity in the locally strongly tamed case) Suppose

\mathfrak{J}_{0}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
is a locally strongly tamed cylindrical
almost complex structure. Let
u:C\to X^{\nu}
be a

J_{0}^{\nu}
-holomorphic map. Here, the almost complex structure

J_{0}^{\nu}
on
X^{\nu}
is obtained by gluing
\mathfrak{J}_{0}
on the neck with
neck length parameter
\nu
. For any open subset
\Omega\subset C
,

E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u,\Omega)\leq E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u,C).

7.3.2
Hofer energy on a broken manifold

Hofer energy on broken manifolds is defined in a similar way as that
on neck-stretched manifolds. The only new feature is that the

\omega
-complex and
J
-complex are different.

We describe the
\omega
-complexes for pieces of the broken manifold.
For a polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
, the
\omega
-complex of the cut space

X_{P}^{\omega}
is the subset

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}:=\pi_{B^{\vee}}(X_{P}^{\omega})\subset B^{\vee},

where
\pi_{B^{\vee}}:(X,\omega_{X})\to B^{\vee}
is the projection to the
dual complex from (
7.10
) and we recall
that the symplectic cut space
X_{P}^{\omega}=\Phi^{-1}(P)
is a subset of

(X,\omega_{X})
. (We recall that the symplectic cut space and symplectic
broken manifolds have a superscript
\omega
, see
Notation
3.9
.)
The complex

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}
may alternately be defined as

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}:=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{P},\dim(Q)=0,Q\in P}i_{{\tilde{Q}}}^{-1}(P)\subset B^{\vee},

(7.26)

where
i_{{\tilde{Q}}}:Q^{\vee}\to\operatorname{im}(\Phi)\subset\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
is the
embedding from (
3.13
), noting that for a zero-dimensional
polytope
Q
,
{\tilde{Q}}=Q^{\vee}
. See Figure
7.8
. The space

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}
inherits the structure of a complex from
B^{\vee}
. It
is a union of polytopes

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}=\cup_{Q\in\mathcal{P}:Q\subseteq P}\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,Q},\quad\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,Q}:=\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}\cap Q^{\vee},

(7.27)

and for any pair
R\subset Q\subseteq P
,
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,Q}
is
identified to a face of
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,R}
. Note that

\dim(\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P})=\dim(P)
, and

\dim(\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,Q})=\dim(P)-\dim(Q)
.

Abbildung 7.8:
For a polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
of a tropical
manifold,
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}
resp.
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}}}
is the

\omega
-complex of the cut space
X_{P}
resp. Broken manifold

\mathfrak{X}_{P}
for
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

Abbildung 7.9:
Polytopes in the complex
\mathbb{B}_{P_{0}}^{\vee}
from Figure
7.8
.

Abbildung 7.10:
Some
J
-complexes for the polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{P}
in Figure
7.8
.

We also associate an
\omega
-complex to thickened complexes
{\tilde{P}}
in
order to define Hofer energy for maps in the broken manifold

\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. We define
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}}}
as a thickening of
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}

:

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}}}:=\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}\times P^{\vee},

where
P^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}
is small enough that
there is an embedding

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}}}\to B^{\vee}
whose image is a tubular neighborhood of

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}\subset B^{\vee}
(if needed we assume that
P^{\vee}
is a scaling of the dual polytope of
P
). The space
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}}}
inherits
the structure of a complex from
B^{\vee}
, and thus, consists of
polytopes

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}},Q}:=\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,Q}\times P^{\vee}\quad\forall Q\subseteq P.

The choice of the
\omega
-complexes
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P}
,
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}}}
is
justified by the fact that the symplectic broken manifold or the symplectic cut space
can be re-constructed from the complexes as

\begin{split}\left(\cup_{Q\subseteq P}\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{{\tilde{P}},Q}\right)/\sim=\Phi^{-1}(P^{\circ})=(\mathfrak{X}_{P}^{\omega},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}),\\
\left(\cup_{Q\subseteq P}\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P}\times\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,Q}\right)/\sim=\Phi^{-1}(P^{\circ})/T_{P}=(X_{P}^{\omega},\omega_{X_{P}}),\end{split}

(7.28)

where
\sim
is an identification on boundaries, which is a
restriction of the equivalence relation in (
3.28
). The
decomposition in (
7.28
) is a consequence of the
decomposition of
(X,\omega_{X})
in (
7.9
).

Next, we describe the
J
-complexes. For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
, the

J
-complex for the broken manifold
X_{P}
is

\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}:=\left(\bigcup_{Q\subseteq P}\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\right)/\sim,

where, for any pair
Q_{0}\subset Q_{1}
, the equivalence relation
\sim

identifies
\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}_{1}
to a face of

\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}_{0}
. The
J
-complex for the cut space
X_{P}

is the corresponding normal cone

\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}:=\left(\bigcup_{Q\subseteq P}\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\right)/\sim,

and thus
\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}
is a product of orthogonal spaces

\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}=\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{t}_{P}.

The almost complex broken manifold and cut spaces can be reconstructed from the
J
-complexes

\begin{split}\mathfrak{X}_{P}&=\left(\cup_{Q\subseteq P}\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\right)/\sim,\\
X_{P}&=\left(\cup_{Q\subseteq P}\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})/T_{P}\times\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\right)/\sim,\end{split}

(7.29)

where, for any facet
Q\subset R
,
\sim
identifies the boundary component

\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}(Q^{\vee})\subset\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}(Q^{\vee})

with the boundary component

\Phi^{-1}(Q^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}(Q^{\vee})\subset\Phi^{-1}(R^{{\includegraphics[width]{blacksq.pdf}}})\times\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}(R^{\vee})

by the identity map. Here we use the viewpoint that broken manifolds
are degenerate limits of neck-stretching, and therefore the
decomposition in (
7.29
) is the limit of the

J
-decomposition in (
7.11
). As a consequence of the
decomposition in (
7.29
), there are projection maps

\pi_{\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}:\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee},\quad\pi_{\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}:X_{P}\to\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}

(7.30)

for all polytopes
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

Definition 7.23
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
be a
broken manifold and let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be a polytope.

(a)

(Squashing map for cut spaces)
A
squashing map for the cut space

X_{P}
is a map of complexes

\aleph:\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}

for which

\aleph(\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee})\subset\mathbb{B}_{P,Q}^{\vee}
for any
polytope
Q\subseteq P
, and

\aleph:\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P,Q}^{\vee}
is a
squashing map of polytopes.

(b)

(Squashing map for a broken manifold) A
squashing
map
for the component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of the broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a map

\aleph=(\aleph^{P},\aleph^{P^{\vee}}):\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}

where
\aleph^{P}:\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
is a squashing map for the cut space
X_{P}
,
and
\aleph^{P^{\vee}}:\mathfrak{t}_{P}\to P^{\vee}
is a squashing map of polytopes. Note that

\aleph
is itself a squashing map of complexes.

See Figure
7.11
for examples.

Abbildung 7.11:
Squashing maps for a broken manifold.

\aleph_{1}:\operatorname{Cone}_{P_{0}^{\vee}}R_{0}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P_{0}}^{\vee}
,

\aleph_{2}:\operatorname{Cone}_{Q_{1}^{\vee}}R_{0}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{Q_{1}}^{\vee}
,

\aleph_{3}:\operatorname{Cone}_{P_{3}^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P_{3}}^{\vee}
with

\omega
-complexes and
J
-complex from Figures
7.8
,

7.10
.

Via the decompositions (
7.28
), (
7.29
) of
a cut space into a union of fibrations over polytopes, a squashing map

\aleph
for the almost cut space
X_{P}
induces a map of manifolds

\psi_{\aleph}:X_{P}\to X_{P}^{\omega},

that is, piecewise, a smooth submersion onto the symplectic cut space

X_{P}^{\omega}
. Similarly for a component
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of an almost complex
broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
, a squashing map
\aleph
induces a map of
manifolds

\psi_{\aleph}:\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}^{\omega}

onto the symplectic broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
.

Definition 7.24
.

(Hofer energy for a broken manifold)

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be a polytope. The
P
-Hofer energy
of a map

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)=\sup_{\aleph}\int_{C}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}},

where the supremum is over all squashing maps
\aleph
for
\mathfrak{X}_{P}

(as in Definition
7.23
). The
unpartitioned

P
-Hofer energy
of
u
is defined as

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(u)=\sup_{\aleph\text{ is unpartitioned}}\int_{C}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}},

where the squashing map
\aleph
is said to be unpartitioned if for
each
Q\subseteq P
,
\aleph|\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
is
unpartitioned (as in Definition
7.10

(
c
)). The
P
-Hofer energy resp. unpartitioned

P
-Hofer energy of a holomorphic map
u:C\to X_{P}
to a cut space

X_{P}
, also denoted by
E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)
resp.
E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}
, is
analogously defined.

The proof of the following monotonicity result is the same as the
proof in the neck-stretched case (Lemma
7.21
).

Lemma 7.25
.

(Monotonicity of Hofer energy for broken manifolds)

Suppose
\mathfrak{J}_{0}\in\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}(\mathfrak{X})
is locally strongly tamed. Then
there is a
C^{0}
-neighborhood
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{J}^{\operatorname{cyl}}
of
\mathfrak{J}_{0}

from Lemma
7.8
such that for any
\mathfrak{J}\in U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
,

P\in\mathcal{P}
and any map

\psi_{\aleph}:(\mathfrak{X}_{P},\mathfrak{J})\to(\mathfrak{X}_{P}^{\omega},\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}})

induced by a squashing map

\aleph:\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}
,
\mathfrak{J}|\mathfrak{X}_{P}

satisfies

\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}(v,\mathfrak{J}v)\geq 0

for all

v\in T\mathfrak{X}_{P}
.

Often, it is useful to have squashing area forms that are
non-degenerate on a given compact subset of the cut space
X_{P}
.

Lemma 7.26
.

For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
and a compact subset
K\subset X_{P}
, there is an unpartitioned squashing map

\aleph:\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
such that the squashed area form

\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}
is non-degenerate on
K
.

Beweis.

The requirement in the Lemma is satisfied by a squashing map

\aleph
that is a dilation on

\pi_{\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}(K)
. Such a map exists by taking the
dilation constant
t
(from (
7.22
)) to be large
enough.
∎

Proposition 7.27
.

(Limit of maps and Hofer energy)
Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be a compact set, and let

u_{\nu}:\Omega\to X^{\nu}
be such that there is a polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}

and a sequence of translations
t_{\nu}\in\nu P^{\vee}
such that

d(t_{\nu},\nu P_{0}^{\vee})\to\infty,\quad\forall P_{0}\supset P

(7.31)

and the sequence of translated maps
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t_{\nu}}u:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}

2
2

2

From (
3.59
),

e^{-t_{\nu}}:X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is an embedding of the

P
-cylindrical subset
X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\subset X^{\nu}
.
converges
uniformly on compact subsets to a limit
u:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. Then,

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(u)\leq\lim_{\nu}E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u_{\nu}).

Here
E^{*}_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}
is unpartitioned
P
-Hofer energy from
Definition
7.24
.

Beweis.

Consider an unpartitioned squashing map

\aleph:\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}
. To prove the
result, we need to construct a sequence of squashing maps

\aleph_{\nu}:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
for which

\lim_{\nu}u_{\nu}^{*}(\phi_{\aleph_{\nu}}^{*}\omega_{X})=u^{*}(\phi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}).

(7.32)

In particular, we will construct
\aleph_{\nu}
that fits into a
diagram (whose commutativity we will describe later)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu B^{\vee}\supseteq\cup_{Q\subseteq P}\nu Q^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\vee},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\aleph}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\nu}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\aleph_{\operatorname{inv}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\aleph_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\nu}}

where

—

i_{\nu}:={\mathbb{e}}^{-t_{\nu}}_{P}
from (
3.60
), the inverse
i_{\nu}^{-1}
is defined on the image of
i_{\nu}
,

—

\aleph_{\operatorname{inv}}
is the right inverse of the unpartitioned squashing map
\aleph
(see Remark
7.12
),

—

and
i:\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{P}}^{\vee}\hookrightarrow B^{\vee}
is a tubular neighborhood of
\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
in
B^{\vee}
.

By the hypothesis (
7.31
) on
t_{\nu}
, the images of

i_{\nu}
exhaust
\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}
. We will define

\aleph_{\nu}
so that there is a sequence of increasing open subsets

S_{\nu}\subset\operatorname{image}(i_{\nu})
that exhaust

\operatorname{Cone}_{B^{\vee}}P^{\vee}
and on which the diagram commutes, that
is,

\aleph=\aleph_{\nu}\circ i_{\nu}\quad\text{on $S_{\nu}$}.

(7.33)

This condition ensures that for any point
x\in\mathfrak{X}_{P}
for which

\pi_{\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}(x)\in S_{\nu}
, there is an equality
of squashed forms

(\phi_{\aleph_{\nu}}^{*}\omega_{X})|_{({\mathbb{e}}^{-t_{\nu}})^{-1}x}=(\phi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}})_{x},

and consequently (
7.32
) holds.

We assume that
B^{\vee}
has a single top-dimensional
polytope, and therefore
B^{\vee}
itself may be viewed as a
polytope. The general case is a natural extension and is left to the
reader.

The squashing map
\aleph_{\nu}
is constructed using a polyhedral
decomposition of the target space
\nu B^{\vee}
that possesses a
dual complex, as in Figures
7.3
and
7.4
.
By Lemma
7.15
and Lemma
7.14

(
a
), there exists a polyhedral decomposition
\mathcal{Q}

of
B^{\vee}
for which
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{P}}^{\vee}
is a top-dimensional
piece, and which has a dual complex

B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{Q}}

3
3

3

The dual complex
B^{\vee}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is that of
the polyhedral decomposition of
B^{\vee}
. The space
B^{\vee}

itself happens to be a dual complex, but in this discussion it
plays the role of the target space of a squashing map.
. Note
that Lemma
7.15
proves the result for a
decomposition of a vector space, and Lemma
7.14

(
a
) extends the construction of a dual complex for
the decomposition of a polytope. The squashing maps
\aleph_{\nu}
are
constructed by applying Lemma
7.14

(
b
) to the polyhedral decomposition induced on

B^{\vee}
by
\mathcal{Q}
. By Lemma
7.14

(
b
), (
c
) there is a unique
family of squashing maps
\{\aleph_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
for which

\aleph=\aleph_{\nu}\circ i_{\nu}^{-1}\quad\text{on $\aleph^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee,\circ})$},

and such that for any top-dimensional polytope
P_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}
other than
\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{P}}^{\vee}
,

d(\aleph_{\nu}^{-1}(P_{0}^{\circ}),\aleph_{\nu}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee,\circ}))\to\infty,

(7.34)

see (
7.24
). By (
7.34
), the commutativity
(
7.33
) holds on

S_{\nu}:=\cup_{P_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},P_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}}\aleph_{\nu}^{-1}(P_{1}).

Informally, the reason for the commutativity on
S_{\nu}
is that the
squashing map sends
S_{\nu}
to
\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}
, and so

\aleph_{\nu}
does not see the effect of other top-dimensional
polytopes of
\mathcal{Q}
on
S_{\nu}
. Thus, the behavior of
\aleph_{\nu}
is
exactly like
\aleph
with a domain translation given by
i_{\nu}
. See
Figures
7.12
and
7.13
for examples. This
finishes the proof of Proposition
7.27
.
∎

Abbildung 7.12:
The squashing maps
\aleph_{\nu}:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}
(constructed in the proof of of Proposition
7.27
) converge to
\aleph:\operatorname{Cone}_{P_{1}^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P_{1}}^{\vee}
as
\nu\to\infty
. Note that
\mathbb{B}_{P_{1}}^{\vee}=\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}_{1}}^{\vee}
since
\operatorname{codim}(P_{1})=0
.

Abbildung 7.13:
The squashing maps
\aleph_{\nu}:\nu B^{\vee}\to B^{\vee}

(constructed in the proof of of Proposition

7.27
) converge to

\aleph:\operatorname{Cone}_{P_{4}^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}_{4}}^{\vee}
as

\nu\to\infty
, and
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\to\infty
. Here
\mathcal{P}
and

B^{\vee}
are from Figure
7.12
.

Proposition 7.28
.

(Hofer energy and quotients) Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be a polytope with

\operatorname{codim}(P)>0
. Let
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
be the
C^{0}
-neighborhood of
cylindrical almost complex structures from Lemma
7.25

for which Hofer energy is monotonic. Let
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
be a
holomorphic map with respect to a domain-dependent almost complex
structure
J:C\to U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
. Let
\pi_{P}:\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to X_{P}
be the
quotient under the action of
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
. Then,

(a)

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(\pi_{P}\circ u)\leq E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)
, and

(b)

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(\pi_{P}\circ u)\leq E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(u)

where
E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}
denotes
P
-Hofer energy, and the superscript
*

refers to unpartitioned Hofer energy, see Definition
7.24
.

Beweis.

We recall that a squashing map for
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is a product map

\aleph=(\aleph^{P},\aleph^{P^{\vee}}):\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}

where
\aleph^{P}:\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
is a
squashing map for the cut space
X_{P}
, and

\aleph^{P^{\vee}}:\mathfrak{t}_{P}\to P^{\vee}
is a squashing map of
polytopes. The
P
-Hofer energy is the supremum over squashed areas
induced by
\aleph_{P}
, and Hofer energy is the supremum over
squashed areas induced by
\aleph
.

To prove the Proposition, consider a squashing map

\aleph^{P}:\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
. Suppose
the projection

\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}}:\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}\to P^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}
maps

\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}\subset\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}
to the point
c_{P}\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
.
For a map

\aleph=(\aleph^{P},c_{P}):\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{{\tilde{P}}}^{\vee}

(7.35)

and any map
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, we have

(\pi_{P}\circ u)^{*}(\psi_{\aleph^{P}}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}})=u^{*}(\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}).

(7.36)

Adding a translation to the squashing map
\aleph
alters the
squashed area by a bounded multiplicative factor: Let
c_{P}^{\prime}
be a
vertex of the polytope
P^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P}
. For the map

\aleph^{\prime}=(\aleph^{P},c_{P}^{\prime}):\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{\tilde{P}}^{\vee}

Lemma
3.51
shows that there is a constant
c>0
such
that

\psi_{\aleph^{\prime}}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}(v,Jv)\geq c^{-1}\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}(v,Jv)

for any
J\in U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}
and
v\in T\mathfrak{X}_{P}
.

Next, we construct a sequence of squashing maps that approximate

\aleph^{\prime}
. (Note that
\aleph^{\prime}
itself is not a squashing map since
it is not surjective.) Define

\tilde{\aleph}_{\nu}:=(\aleph^{P},\aleph_{\nu})

where
\aleph_{\nu}:\mathfrak{t}_{P}\to P^{\vee}
is an unpartitioned squashing
map whose right inverse
\aleph_{\nu,inv}:P^{\vee}\to\mathfrak{t}_{P}
is a
translation such that the image
\aleph_{\nu,inv}(P^{\vee})
goes to

\infty
in
\mathfrak{t}_{P}
in the direction
-c_{P}^{\prime}
. For example, we may
take
\aleph_{\nu,inv}:=\aleph_{0,inv}-\nu c_{P}^{\prime}
for any
\nu
.
Consequently, there exist increasing open sets

U_{\nu}\subset\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}
that exhaust

\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}
such that

\aleph_{\nu}|U_{\nu}=(\aleph^{P},c_{P}^{\prime})=\aleph^{\prime}.

The Lemma now follows: For any squashing map
\aleph^{P}
of
X_{P}
,
define a sequence of squashing maps

\tilde{\aleph}_{\nu}:=(\aleph^{P},\aleph_{\nu})
of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. We have

E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u)\geq\int_{C}(\psi_{\tilde{\aleph}_{\nu}}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}\geq\int_{C_{\nu}}(\psi_{\aleph^{\prime}}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}\\
\geq c\int_{C_{\nu}}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}=\int_{C_{\nu}}(\psi_{\aleph_{P}}\circ\pi_{P}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X_{P}},

where
C_{\nu}:=(\pi_{\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}}\circ u)^{-1}(U_{\nu})
.
Since the sets
C_{\nu}
exhaust the domain
C
, we conclude that for
any squashing map
\aleph_{P}
of
X_{P}
,

\int_{C}(\psi_{\aleph_{P}}\circ\pi_{P}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}\leq E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u),

and consequently
E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(\pi_{P}\circ u)\leq E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u)
.
∎

7.4
Removal of singularities

In this section, we prove the removal of singularities result,
Proposition
7.1
, for punctured holomorphic maps in a
piece of a broken manifold.
4
4

4

An approach to proving this
result by embedding the broken almost complex manifold into a
compact symplectic manifold fails, because the almost complex
manifold
X_{P}
can not be embedded into the symplectic cut space

\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
via increasing maps. For any vertex
Q\in\mathcal{P}
of

P
, an increasing map may not exist in the
Q
-cylindrical corner
of
X_{P}
because the fixed
\mathfrak{t}
-inner product from (
3.10
)
is not equal to the natural
\mathfrak{t}
-inner product at the

Q
-corner. The natural inner product is the one for which the edges

P_{1}\subset P
emanating from
Q
form an orthogonal basis.
Consequently, a Hofer energy bound in
X_{P}
does not translate to
a bound on symplectic area in
\overline{X}_{P}^{\omega}
.
We prove that any
punctured holomorphic map with finite Hofer energy lies in some

Q
-cylindrical region and the image of the projection to
X_{Q}
lies
in a compact set in the complement of relative divisors. The removal
of singularities result applies on the projected map, and consequently
on the original map.

Monotonicity for pseudoholomorphic maps is the main technical tool in
the proof of Proposition
7.1
. We state the monotonicity
result (see for example
[
86
, Proposition 3.12]
).

Proposition 7.29
.

(Monotonicity) Let

(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold,
J_{0}
an
\omega
-tamed
almost complex structure, and let

U_{J_{0}}:=\{J\text{ is $\omega$-tamed}:\lVert J-J_{0}\rVert_{C^{0}}<\epsilon\}

for some
\epsilon>0
be a
C^{0}
-neighborhood on the space of tamed
almost complex structures. There exist constants
c,r_{0}>0
such
that for any
x\in X
,
0<r\leq r_{0}
, a
Riemann surface
C
with boundary
\partial C
and a
pseudoholomorphic map
u:C\to X
with respect to a domain-dependent
almost complex structure
J:C\to U_{\mathcal{J}}
whose image contains
x
and

u(\partial C)\subset\partial B(x,r)
,

\int_{C}u^{*}\omega\geq cr^{2}.

We first prove the removal of singularities result (Proposition

7.1
) in the case of a single cut to serve as a warm-up
for the more complicated proof in the case of multiple cuts.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
in the case of a single
cut.

We set up some notation first. We consider a single cut with
polyhedral decomposition

\mathcal{P}=\{P_{+}:=(-\infty,0],P_{0}:=\{0\},P_{-}:=[0,\infty)\},

and dual complex
B^{\vee}\cong[\frac{-\delta}{2},\frac{\delta}{2}]
.

We first carry out the proof for maps whose target space is

X_{P_{+}}
, the case of
X_{P_{-}}
being similar. We recall from
(
7.30
)
there is a projection to the
J
-complex

\pi_{B_{J}}:X_{P_{+}}\to\operatorname{Cone}_{P_{+}^{\vee}}\simeq(-\infty,0].

We choose any unpartitioned squashing map (as in Definition
7.10
(
c
))

\operatorname{Cone}_{P_{+}^{\vee}}\simeq(-\infty,0]\xrightarrow{\aleph}[\tfrac{-\delta}{2},\tfrac{\delta}{2}]\simeq B^{\vee}.

Such a map is a translation on an interval

[\tau,\tau+\delta]\subset\mathbb{R}_{-}
and a locally constant map on the
complement.

Definition 7.30
.

(Crossing) A connected component
C\subset{\operatorname{Cyl}}
of

(\pi_{B_{J}}\circ u)^{-1}([\tau,\tau+\delta])
is called a

crossing
if
(\pi_{B_{J}}\circ u)(C)
intersects both boundary
components of
[\tau,\tau+\delta]
.

Step 1
: We will establish a lower bound on the squashed
area of any compact crossing
C
by applying the monotonicity result
Proposition
7.29
. First, we recall that since any
cylindrical almost complex structure
\mathfrak{J}
on
\mathfrak{X}
is the limit of
neck-stretched almost complex structures
(X^{\nu},J^{\nu})
,
\mathfrak{J}

corresponds to an
\omega_{X}
-tamed almost complex structure
J:=J^{1}
on

(X,\omega_{X})
, and further,
\psi_{\aleph,*}\mathfrak{J}=J
on subsets of
\mathfrak{X}

where
\aleph
is an isometry. We also note that
\psi_{\aleph}
is an
isometry on subsets of
\mathfrak{X}
if
\aleph
is an isometry on the
correponding region in the
J
-complex. Since
\aleph
is an
isometry on the image of
\pi_{B_{J}}\circ u|C
, it is equivalent to
work with

\psi_{\aleph}\circ u:C\to(X,\omega_{X})

instead of
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
. Choose

0<\delta_{1}<\min\{\frac{\delta}{2},r_{0}\}
where
r_{0}
is the constant
from the monotonicity result applied to
(X,\omega_{X})
. Let
z_{0}\in C

be such that
\pi_{B_{J}}(u(z_{0}))=\tau+\frac{\delta}{2}
. Applying the
monotonicity result to the ball

B_{\delta_{1}}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u(z_{0}))\subset X
, we conclude
there is a uniform constant such that for any compact crossing
C
,

\int_{C}u^{*}(\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{X})\geq c.

(7.37)

Step 2
: Next, we will show that the image
u({\operatorname{Cyl}})
is
either contained in a compact subset of
X_{P_{+}}
or after
truncating the domain cylinder by a finite amount, the image of
u

is contained in the
P_{0}
-cylindrical subset of
X_{P_{+}}
: After
passing to a truncation of the domain cylinder, we may assume that
the squashed area of the map is small enough to ensure that there
are no compact crossings. If there is a non-compact crossing

C\subset{\operatorname{Cyl}}
, the image of
u
is contained in a compact
set. Indeed, there exists
\ell_{0}\geq 0
such that
C
intersects
the image
u(\{\ell\}\times S^{1})
for all
\ell\geq\ell_{0}
, and
therefore the image
u({\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{0}))
is contained within a radius

2\pi\lVert du\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
of the compact subset

\{\tau\leq\pi_{B_{J}}\leq\tau+\delta\}\subset X_{P_{+}}
. Finally, if
u
does not have any crossings, the image
of
u
is either contained in the compact subset

\{\pi_{B_{J}}\geq\tau\}\subset X_{P_{+}}
; or it is contained in

\{\pi_{B_{J}}\leq\tau+\delta\}
which is in the
P_{0}
-cylindrical
subset of
X_{P_{+}}
.

Step 3
: In case the image of
u
is
contained in a compact subset of
X_{P_{+}}
, the result follows from
the removal of singularities result for compact symplectic
manifolds. Indeed, for any compact subset
K
of
X_{P_{+}}
, by Lemma

7.26
, there is a squashing area form
\omega_{\aleph}
that
is a symplectic form on
K
, and

\int_{\operatorname{Cyl}}u^{*}\omega_{\aleph}<E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u)
.

Step 4
: Next, we prove the result
in the case when the image of
u
is contained in the

P_{0}
-cylindrical end of
X_{P_{+}}
. The
P_{0}
-cylindrical end is a
semi-infinite cylinder
Z_{P_{0}}\times(-\infty,0]
. The

(\omega_{X_{P_{0}}}-\frac{\epsilon}{2}d\alpha_{P_{0}})
-area of the
projection
u_{P_{0}}:=\pi_{P_{0}}\circ u
is bounded by
E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u)

because if we define the squashing map
\aleph_{0}
so that

\aleph_{0}\equiv\tfrac{-\epsilon}{2}\quad\text{on}\quad(-\infty,0],

then

\int_{\operatorname{Cyl}}u_{P_{0}}^{*}(\omega_{X_{P_{0}}}-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}d\alpha_{P_{0}})=\int_{\operatorname{Cyl}}u^{*}\omega_{\aleph_{0}}\leq E_{\operatorname{Hof}}(u).

By the removal of singularities result for compact symplectic
manifolds applied to the projected map

u_{P_{0}}:{\operatorname{Cyl}}\to(X_{P_{0}},\omega_{X_{P_{0}}}-\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}d\alpha_{P_{0}}),

we conclude that

u_{P_{0}}
extends holomorphically to

u_{P_{0}}:B_{1}\to X_{P_{0}}.

Consider a holomorphic trivialization of the pullback bundle

u_{P_{0}}^{*}(Z_{P_{0}}\times\mathbb{R})\to B_{1}
. The projection of
u
to
the fiber, denoted by

u_{\operatorname{vert}}:B_{1}\backslash\{0\}\to S^{1}\times\mathbb{R},

(7.38)

is holomorphic, and the
\mathbb{R}
-coordinate has an upper bound.
Therefore,
u_{\operatorname{vert}}
extends over
0
to a holomorphic map in

\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Suppose
u_{\operatorname{vert}}
has a zero resp. pole of order

n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
at
0\in B_{1}
. Then the twisted map

\overline{u}(z):=z^{-n}u(z)
resp.
z^{n}u(z)
has the same projection to

X_{P_{0}}
as
u
, the vertical component
\overline{u}_{\operatorname{vert}}
has a
removable singularity, and therefore,
\overline{u}
has a removable
singularity. This proves (
7.2
) in Proposition

7.1
.

Step 5
: So far we have proved the result in the cases when
the target space is
X_{P_{\pm}}
. Next, consider the case of a map

u:{\operatorname{Cyl}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P_{0}}
. As in the previous paragraph, the
singularity at
\infty
can be removed for the projected map

\pi_{P_{0}}\circ u:{\operatorname{Cyl}}\to X_{P_{0}}
. To prove that the
singularity can be removed for the vertical component
u_{v}
(as in
(
7.38
), it is enough to show that the
\mathbb{R}
-component
has either an upper or a lower bound so that essential singularities
are ruled out. This bound is a consequence of a lower bound on the
squashed area
\psi^{*}\aleph
for crossings. The details are exactly
as in the case of
X_{P_{\pm}}
and are therefore omitted.
∎

Remark 7.31
.

(The integrality of edge directions)
As part of the above proof, we have shown that if the punctured end has a direction
\mu\in\mathfrak{t}_{P_{0}}
, then
\mu
is integral, and not just fractional. This fact relies on the torus actions being free in the neighborhood of cut loci (see Definition
1.2
and Figure
1.3
). Indeed, in (
7.38
) we use the fact that
Z_{P_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}\to X_{P_{0}}
is a
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
-bundle, and so the
S^{1}
factor in (
7.38
) is generated by an integral element in
\sqrt{-1}\mathfrak{t}_{P_{0}}
.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
in the case of a multiple
cut.

The proof is by induction on
\dim(P)
. By the induction
hypothesis, we assume that the result holds for maps in the cut space
X_{Q}
and the component
\mathfrak{X}_{Q}
of the broken manifold for any
Q\in\mathcal{P}
with
\dim(Q)<\dim(P)
.

We first consider the case when the target space is a cut space

X_{P}
, the case of a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is dealt with
subsequently. For notational simplicity, we assume that there is
only one zero-dimensional polytope
R\in\mathcal{P}
contained in
P
, and
so,
B^{\vee}=R^{\vee}
. We also denote
\mathfrak{t}_{R}=\mathfrak{t}
. The generalization
is easy and is described in Step 3
of the proof. We
also assume that
\operatorname{codim}(P)=0
so that
\mathfrak{t}_{P}=\{0\}
. Otherwise, all
instances of
\mathfrak{t}
are replaced by
\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{t}_{P}
.

Abbildung 7.14:
\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
is the
\omega
-complex of the cut space

X_{P}
. The faces of
\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
with dark grey labels are subcomplexes, and those with light grey labels are the
\omega
-complexes of other cut spaces.

The proof is similar to that of a single cut. The new feature is that
we now consider crossings defined with respect to various
one-dimensional subspaces of
\mathfrak{t}
, which are defined as follows.
Let
P_{1},\dots P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
be facets of
P
. That is, for any

\lambda\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
P_{\lambda}\subset P
and

\dim(P_{\lambda})=\dim(P)-1
. For any
\lambda
, let
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}
be
the the one-dimensional face of
P
that is transverse to
P_{\lambda}
,
that is,
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}:=\cap_{1\leq i\leq k,i\neq\lambda}P_{i}
. Let

\pi_{\lambda}:\mathfrak{t}\to\mathbb{R}

(7.39)

be a linear projection that maps the codimension one dual polytope

\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}
to a constant. In particular, since
we assumed
P^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{t}
is the origin, we have

\pi_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee})=0.

Denote by

\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}:=\pi_{\lambda}\circ\pi_{B_{J}}:X_{P}\to\mathbb{R}

the composition of
\pi_{B_{J}}:X_{P}\to\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}
with
\pi_{\lambda}
from (
7.39
). The projection
\pi_{\lambda}
has the useful property that the
P_{\lambda}
-cylindrical end in
X_{P}
is

U_{P_{\lambda}}(X_{P})=\{\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}>0\},

(7.40)

and the complement
X_{P}\backslash U_{P_{\lambda}}(X_{P})
is equal to

\{\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}=0\}
.

We choose a point
\tau\gg 0
in the interior of

\pi_{\lambda}(\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee})\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 7.32
.

(
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
-crossing) Given
\delta>0
, a connected
component
C\subset{\operatorname{Cyl}}
of

(\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}\circ u)^{-1}([\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta])
is
a
(\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee},\delta)
-crossing if
\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}(u(C))

intersects both boundary components of

[\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta]
. If
\delta
is clear from the
context, we refer to
C
as a
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
-crossing.

We interrupt the proof of Proposition
7.1
(multiple
cut case) to state and prove a technical Claim. The proof is
continued
after the proof of Claim
7.33
.
∎

The following Claim is the technical heart of the proof of the removal
of singularities result.

Claim 7.33
.

There are constants
\delta,c>0
and a squashing map
\aleph
such
that for any compact
(\delta,\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee})
-crossing

C\subset{\operatorname{Cyl}}
, there is a lower bound

\int_{C}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}\geq c.

Proof of Claim
7.33
.

We fix some constants in Step 1 of the proof. This is followed by an outline of the rest of the proof, with details given in Step 2 onwards.

Step 1
:
The first step is to fix the constant
\delta
and the squashing map

\aleph
. Cut up the
\omega
-polytope
\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}

along a level set of
\pi_{\lambda}
into two polytopes

\mathbb{B}_{P,\pm}^{\vee}
so that the facet

\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P_{\lambda}}\subset\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}
is contained in

\mathbb{B}_{P,-}^{\vee}
; see Figure
7.15
. Define

\delta:={\tfrac{1}{2}}\operatorname{length}(\pi_{\lambda}(\mathbb{B}_{P,+}^{\vee})).

We will define
\aleph:\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}

to be an unpartitioned undilated squashing map, and therefore

\aleph
will possess a right inverse that is a translation

\aleph_{\operatorname{inv}}:\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee}\to\overline{\aleph^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{P}^{\vee,\circ})}.

In fact,
\aleph
is fully determined by the map
\aleph_{\operatorname{inv}}
,
which we define by the condition

\aleph_{\operatorname{inv}}(\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,P}):=\{\pi_{\lambda}=\tau-\delta\}\cap\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}P_{\lambda}^{\vee}.

(7.41)

We point out that both sides of the equation (
7.41
) are
points. The squashing map
\aleph
defined in this manner has the
property that for any facet
\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
of

\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
that contains

\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}P_{\lambda}^{\vee}
(that is,
Q\in\mathcal{P}
,

Q\subseteq P_{\lambda}
and
\dim(Q)=1
),

\aleph^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{P,Q}^{\vee})\subset\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}.

(7.42)

Abbildung 7.15:
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
-crossing with
\lambda=1
.

Outline of the proof of the Claim
:
Compared to
the single cut, the most significant difference in the multiple cut
case is that the image of a crossing may not be contained in a
subset of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
where
\psi_{\aleph}
is an isometry. This
corresponds to the fact that
\aleph
is not an isometry on

\pi_{\lambda}^{-1}([\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta])
.
The way around this issue is that if the image
u(C)
is contained
in a subset of
X_{P}
where the map
\psi_{\aleph}
is a projection
whose fibers are
T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}
-orbits for some
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
for
which
Q_{0}\subseteq P
,
Q_{0}\nsubseteq P_{\lambda}
, Then we apply
monotonicity result not on
u
(as in the single cut proof) but on

u
composed with a quotient by the
T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}
-action. Observe
that the fibers of
\psi_{\aleph}
are
T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}
-orbits exactly if
the map
\aleph
is
\mathfrak{t}_{Q_{0}}
-squashing
, that is, it is a
projection map whose fibers are
\dim(\mathfrak{t}_{Q_{0}})
-dimensional and
parallel to
\mathfrak{t}_{Q_{0}}
. We note that
\aleph
is chosen so
that in the subset
\pi_{\lambda}^{-1}([\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta])
, the

\mathfrak{t}_{P_{\lambda}}
direction itself is not squashed (that is,

Q_{0}\nsubseteq P_{\lambda}
); this feature is crucial in getting the
lower bound on area. The details of the rest of the proof
of Claim
7.33

are as follows.

Step 2
:
Determining the polytope

Q_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
(from the proof outline) via a decomposition of the

J
-polytope.

The subset of the
J
-complex corresponding to the

\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
-crossing has a decomposition

\pi_{\lambda}^{-1}(\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta)\subset\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{P},Q\nsubseteq P_{\lambda},Q\subseteq P}S_{Q},

(7.43)

where
S_{Q}\subset\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
is the subset on which

\aleph
is
T_{Q}
-squashing
, that is,
\aleph
projects to the
direction perpendicular to
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}
. See Figure
7.16
. The
decomposition (
7.43
) exists because (
7.42
)
rules out any other kind of squashing in
\pi_{\lambda}^{-1}(\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta)
.

Abbildung 7.16:
The
\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{\vee}
-crossing decomposes into
S_{P}
and
S_{P_{2}}
.

The monotonicity result will be applied on small balls in the target
space whose radius
\delta_{1}
we determine next. Let
N:=\dim(P)
.
Choose a constant
0<\delta_{1}<\delta/(N+1)
which is small enough
that for any
x\in\pi_{\lambda}^{-1}([\tau-N\delta_{1},\tau+N\delta_{1}])\subset\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
and any polytope
Q\subseteq P
,

B_{\delta_{1}}(x)\cap\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}\neq\emptyset\Longrightarrow B_{\delta_{1}}(x)\subset\bigcup_{Q_{1}\in\mathcal{P},Q_{1}\supseteq Q}S_{Q_{1}}.

(7.44)

Consider a compact
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
-crossing
C\subset{\operatorname{Cyl}}
. We
call
(x\in X_{P},Q\in\mathcal{P})
an
admissible pair
if

—

x\in\pi_{B_{J}}(u(C))
and
\pi_{\lambda}(x)\in[\tau-n_{Q}\delta,\tau+n_{Q}\delta]
, where
n_{Q}:=\dim(Q)
; and

—

Q_{x}^{\vee}=\cap_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}Q^{\vee}
, where

\mathcal{Q}:=\{Q\in\mathcal{P}:Q\subseteq P,Q\nsubseteq P_{\lambda},B_{\delta_{1}}(x)\cap\operatorname{NCone}_{Q^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\neq\emptyset\}.

Let

(x_{0},Q_{0})

(7.45)

be an admissible pair such that there is no admissible pair

(x,Q_{x})
such that
Q_{0}\subset Q_{x}
. In other words, we choose an
admissible pair
(x_{0},Q_{0})
for which the torus
T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}
is
minimal. See Figure
7.17
for an example.

Step 3
:
Applying the monotonicity result and obtaining the lower bound on squashed area.

We will apply the monotonicity result (Proposition

7.29
) to the map
u
quotiented by
T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}
. Let

u_{Q_{0}}:C\dashrightarrow X_{Q_{0}},\quad u_{Q_{0}}:=u/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}

be a map defined on the subset of
C
that is mapped by
u
to the

Q_{0}
-cylindrical end of
X_{P}
. Fix any
z_{0}\in(\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}\circ u)^{-1}(x_{0})
, and let

C_{1}:=u_{Q_{0}}^{-1}(B_{\delta_{1}}(u_{Q_{0}}(z_{0}))).

We will now prove that

u_{Q_{0}}(\partial C_{1})\subset\partial B_{\delta_{1}}(u_{Q_{0}}(z_{0})),

(7.46)

which is a hypothesis of the monotonicity result. If
(
7.46
) did not hold, there would be a point

z_{1}\in\partial C_{1}
such that
u(z_{1})
does not map to the

Q_{0}
-cylindrical end of
X_{P}
(assuming
U_{Q}(X_{P})
is an open
subset of
X_{P}
), and
\pi_{B,J}(u(z_{1}))\in\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q_{1}^{\vee}
for some

Q_{1}\in\mathcal{P}
,
Q_{1}\supset Q
. Then, setting

x_{1}:=\pi_{B_{J}}(u(z_{1}))
,
(x_{1},Q_{x_{1}})
is an admissible pair for
some
Q_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}
with
Q_{x_{1}}\subseteq Q_{0}
, where we use
the observation that since
|\pi_{\lambda}(x_{0})-\tau|<n_{Q_{0}}\delta_{1}
,
we have
|\pi_{\lambda}(x_{1})-\tau|<(n_{Q_{0}}+1)\delta_{1}\leq n_{Q_{x_{1}}}\delta_{1}
. We have thus contradicted the minimality of
T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}

from (
7.45
), and shown that (
7.46
) indeed
holds.

Abbildung 7.17:
Assume the setting of Figure
7.16
. For the map
u
,
(x_{1},P)
,
(x_{2},P_{2})
are admissible pairs, with
(x_{1},P)
being the pair chosen in (
7.45
).

To finish the proof of the result, we relate the squashed areas of

u
and its projection
u_{Q_{0}}
, via the following commutative
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{Q_{0}}(X_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\omega}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{Q_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\omega}_{Q_{0}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\aleph}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\aleph,/\kern-1.50693pt/}}

The maps denoted by dashed arrows are defined on subsets of the
domain. However these subsets contain the images of

\psi_{\aleph}\circ u
and
u/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}
. The left downward arrow is
a map between almost complex manifolds. The right downward arrow is
a map between symplectic manifolds, and is defined by an orthogonal
projection map
\mathbb{B}_{P,+}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{P,Q_{0}}
. The map

\psi_{\aleph,/\kern-2.1097pt/}
is induced by a squashing

\aleph_{/\kern-2.1097pt/}:\operatorname{NCone}_{Q_{0}^{\vee}}R^{\vee}\to\mathbb{B}_{Q_{0}}^{\vee}
which
is defined on a subset of the domain as follows: The restriction

\aleph|\aleph^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{\vee}_{P,+}\cap\mathbb{B}_{Q_{0}}^{\vee})

orthogonally projects to
\mathbb{B}_{Q_{0}}^{\vee}
. Therefore, it descends
to the
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}
-quotient of the domain, and yields the map

\aleph_{/\kern-2.1097pt/}
. The map
\psi_{\aleph,/\kern-2.1097pt/}
is an isometry on the
image of
(u/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}})|C_{1}
. Applying the monotonicity theorem on
the map
\psi_{\aleph,/\kern-2.1097pt/}(u/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}})|C_{1}
, we conclude

\int_{C_{1}}(\psi_{\aleph,/\kern-2.1097pt/}(u/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}))^{*}\omega_{X_{Q_{0}}}>c_{Q_{0}}\delta_{1}^{2}.

Finally, there is a constant
c(\mathfrak{X})>0
such that

\int_{C_{1}}(\psi_{\aleph}\circ u)^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}\geq c\int_{C_{1}}(\psi_{\aleph,/\kern-2.1097pt/}(u/T_{Q_{0},\mathbb{C}}))^{*}\omega_{X_{Q_{0}}}>cc_{Q_{0}}\delta_{1}^{2}.

This leads to the lower bound on
\aleph
-squashed area of
crossings, and finishes the proof of Claim
7.33
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.1
for multiple cuts, continued.

We recall that
P_{1},\dots,P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}
are facets of
P
.

Step 1
:
There is a constant
\ell_{0}
such that for any
facet
P_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}
of
P
, one of the two possibilities
occurs:

—

Either the image
u({\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{0}))
is contained in the

P_{\lambda}
-cylindrical end
U_{P_{\lambda}}(X_{P})
, or

—

the projection
\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}(u({\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{0})))
has an
upper bound.

For a given
\lambda
, we first consider the case that
u
has a
non-compact crossing
C
. We may choose
\ell_{\lambda}
so that

\{s\}\times S^{1}
intersects
C
for all
s>\ell_{\lambda}
. Therefore,

\pi_{B_{J},P_{\lambda}}\circ u|{\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{\lambda})
lies in a

2\pi\lVert du\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
-radius of

\pi_{B_{J},P_{\lambda}}^{-1}([\tau-\delta_{\lambda},\tau+\delta_{\lambda})
, and
therefore by (
7.40
) the image of
u|{\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{\lambda})
lies
in the
P_{\lambda}
-cylindrical end of
X_{P}
. Next, consider the case
that
u
does not have any non-compact crossings. Claim

7.33
implies that there is a constant
\ell_{\lambda}\geq 0

such that
u|{\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{\lambda})
does not have any compact crossings.
Then,
\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}(u({\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{\lambda})))
either has a lower bound of

\tau_{\lambda}-\delta_{\lambda}
or an upper bound of

\tau_{\lambda}+\delta_{\lambda}
. In the former case,
u({\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{\lambda}))

lies in the
P_{\lambda}
-cylindrical end. Finally, by taking

\ell_{0}:=\min_{\lambda}\ell_{\lambda}
, the condition stated in the beginning
of the paragraph is satisfied.

Step 2:
We use induction to finish the proof of the result for a
map in a cut space
X_{P}
, and for which there is only one
zero-dimensional polytope
R\in\mathcal{P}
in
P
. First consider the case
that for all facets
P_{\lambda}
, the projection

\pi_{B_{J},\lambda}(u({\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{0})))
has an upper bound. Then, the image
of
u
is contained in a compact subset of
X_{P}
, and the proof of
removal of singularity follows exactly as in Step 3 of the proof in
the case of a single cut.
The
other possibility is that for some
\lambda
, the image of

u|{\operatorname{Cyl}}(\ell_{0})
is contained in the
P_{\lambda}
-cylindrical end. Then,
we may view
u
as a map in
\mathfrak{X}_{P_{\lambda}}
and apply the induction
hypothesis to the map
\pi_{P_{\lambda}}\circ u:{\operatorname{Cyl}}\to X_{P_{\lambda}}
.
For the proof of the induction hypothesis, it is enough to have a
bound on squashed area corresponding to squashing maps on

\operatorname{NCone}_{P_{\lambda}^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
which are restrictions of squashing
maps
\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
with
\operatorname{NCone}_{P_{\lambda}^{\vee}}R^{\vee}

being embedded in
\operatorname{NCone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
far enough from the faces
corresponding
Q^{\vee}\subset P_{\lambda}^{\vee}
; such squashed areas are
bounded by
E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)
.

Step 3:

If the target of
u
is a cut space
X_{P}
and there are multiple
zero-dimensional polytopes
R\in\mathcal{P}
that are vertices of
P
, the
only difference is in the definition of the projection map
\pi_{\lambda}

from (
7.39
). First, observe that in the case of a single
zero dimensional polytope
R
, the map
\pi_{\lambda}
is actually defined
on
R^{\vee}
and
\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}R^{\vee}
, both of which were viewed
as subsets of
\mathfrak{t}
. In the general case, we define

\pi_{\lambda,R}:\mathfrak{t}_{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for each
R
so that

\pi_{\lambda,R}(\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda,R})=0
(where

\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda,R}^{\vee}\subset R^{\vee}
is the face corresponding to

\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
) and
\pi_{\lambda,R}|P^{\vee}_{\lambda,R}
is the same for
all
R\in P_{\lambda}
. The notion of
\mathfrak{p}_{\lambda}^{\vee}
-crossing can now be
defined, and the rest of the proof carries over.

Step 4:
Next, we consider the case that the target space of
u

is a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. We may assume that the projection

u/T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
lies in a compact subset of
X_{P}
; otherwise by applying
the preceding discussion to
u/T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
, we may conclude that its
image lies on a cylindrical end of
X_{P}
and hence the induction
hypothesis is applicable. As in the previous paragraph, we conclude
that
u/T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
has a removable singularity, and we assume that

(u/T_{P,\mathbb{C}})(\infty)=x_{P}\in X_{P}
. Analogously to Step 4 of the proof
in the case of a single cut,
we
prove the result in
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
by considering a holomorphic
trivialization of the
T_{P,\mathbb{C}}
-bundle

(u/T_{P,\mathbb{C}})^{*}\mathfrak{X}_{P}\to B_{1}

and projecting
u
to the fiber direction in a neighborhood of the
puncture at
0\in B_{1}
. To finish the proof, it only remains to show
that for any one-dimensional face
Q^{\vee}
of
P^{\vee}
(where

Q\in\mathcal{P}
), the projection of

\pi_{P,J}(u({\operatorname{Cyl}}))\subset\operatorname{Cone}_{Q^{\vee}}P^{\vee}\simeq\operatorname{NCone}_{Q^{\vee}}P^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{t}_{Q}

to
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}^{\vee}\simeq\mathbb{R}
has a one-sided bound. This fact follows
from a lower bound on squashed area of
\mathfrak{t}_{Q}
-crossings, which is
proved in a similar manner to Claim
7.33
, and whose details
are left to the reader. This finishes the
proof of Proposition
7.1
.
∎

The following is a version of the removal of singularities result for
punctures on the boundary. This result does not involve any technical
difficulties arising from neck-stretching because the Lagrangian
submanifold does not intersect the neck regions.

Proposition 7.34
.

(Removal of singularities on the boundary)

Suppose

u:(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,1],\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{0,1\}\to(X_{P_{0}},L)

is a map that is holomorphic with respect to a domain-dependent
cylindrical almost complex structure taking values in
U_{\mathfrak{J}_{0}}

(from Lemma
7.21
), and

E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)<\infty,\quad\lVert{\operatorname{d}}u\rVert_{L^{\infty}({\operatorname{Cyl}})}<\infty.

Then,
u
extends to a holomorphic map

u:(B_{1}\cap\mathbb{H},B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R})\to(\overline{X}_{P_{0}},L).

Beweis.

By the uniform bound on the derivative, the image of
u
is
contained in a compact neighborhood
K\subset X_{P}
of the
Lagrangian
L
. By Lemma
7.26
, there is a squashed
area form
\omega_{\aleph}\in\Omega^{2}(X_{P})
that is symplectic on the
closure of
\operatorname{im}(u)
and that tames the almost complex
structure. Therefore, the statement of the Proposition follows from the removal of
singularity theorem for compact symplectic manifolds.

∎

7.5
Hofer energy for Gromov compactness of broken maps

The results stated so far in this Chapter will be used in the proof
that a sequence of breaking maps with bounded area converges to a
broken map. In this section, we discuss analogs of the results which
will be used to show that a sequence of broken maps with bounded area
converges to a broken map. In components
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
of the broken
manifold, we use
P
-Hofer energy of maps.

The first result, which is an analog of Remark
7.19
for
broken maps, is that for a component of a broken map in
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
, the

P
-Hofer energy is bounded by a constant that depends only on the
topology of the map. In particular, we obtain a bound on Hofer energy
for components of broken maps
u:C^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
whose projection
to
X_{P}
is in class
\beta\in\pi_{2}(\overline{X}_{P},L)
, and whose tropical
graph is
\mathcal{T}
. Note that both
\beta
and
\mathcal{T}
are part of the
combinatorial type (Definition
6.13
) of the map
u
,
and that
\mathcal{T}
determines the number of punctures in the domain of

u
, and the edge direction
\mathcal{T}(z_{i})\in\mathfrak{t}{Z}
of the map at each of the
punctures
z_{i}
.

Proposition 7.35
.

For any polytope
P\in\mathcal{P}
, a homotopy class
\beta\in\pi_{2}(\overline{X}_{P},L)
and a tropical graph
\mathcal{T}
underlying
a relative map, there is a constant
c
such that for a holomorphic map
u:C^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}

with tropical graph
\Gamma
, and whose projection
\pi_{P}\circ u
is of class
\beta
,

E_{P,\operatorname{Hof}}(u)\leq c.

Beweis.

We first prove the result assuming that the
X
-inner product
g
(see (
3.10
))
is rational, so that the compactification
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}

is an orbifold with symplectic form
\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}
. Furthermore,

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u)=\int_{C}u^{*}\psi_{\aleph}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}=:\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}(u),

where
\aleph
is any squashing form. The above quantity is independent of which
\aleph
is used, because for any two choices

\aleph_{1}
,
\aleph_{2}
, the maps
\psi_{\aleph_{1}}
and

\psi_{\aleph_{2}}
are isotopic.

Under the splitting of
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}_{P}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{P}^{\perp}
, let

\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\operatorname{vert}}(z_{i})\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
be the
\mathfrak{t}_{P}
-component of
\mathcal{T}(z_{i})\in\mathfrak{t}
.
Recall that the fiber of
\pi_{P}:\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P}
is

P^{\vee}=\{x\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}:\langle{\mu_{i},x}\rangle\leq c_{i},i=1,\dots,N\},

where for any
i=1,\dots,N
,
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mu_{i}\in\mathfrak{t}_{P,\mathbb{Z}}^{\vee}
is a
primitive outward pointing normal to a facet of
P^{\vee}
.
Suppose
the base symplectic form
\omega_{X_{P}}
on
\overline{X}_{P}
is given by reduction at the
origin
0\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
. Then,

\langle{u_{*}[C],\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}}\rangle=\langle{(\pi_{P}\circ u)_{*}[C],\omega_{X_{P}}}\rangle+2\pi{\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}{\textstyle\sum}_{j}c_{j}\cdot m(z_{i},D_{j})\\
\leq\langle{(\pi_{P}\circ u)_{*}[C],\omega_{X_{P}}}\rangle+\sum_{i}|\mathcal{T}_{g}^{{\operatorname{vert}}}(z_{i})|\\
\leq\langle{(\pi_{P}\circ u)_{*}[C],\omega_{X_{P}}}\rangle+c_{g}\sum_{i}|\mathcal{T}(z_{i})|

(7.47)

where
m(z_{i},D_{j})
is the intersection multiplicity at
z_{i}
with
the toric divisor
D_{j}=\{\langle{\mu_{j},x}\rangle=c_{j}\}
,

|\mathcal{T}^{{\operatorname{vert}}}(z_{i})|
is the sum of the components of
\mathcal{T}^{\operatorname{vert}}(z_{i})

for a fixed
\mathbb{Z}
-basis. Indeed, (
7.47
) is a consequence of the
cohomological relation

[\omega_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}]=[\pi_{P}^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}]+\sum_{j}c_{j}[D_{j}]^{\vee},

where
[D_{j}]^{\vee}\in H^{2}(\mathfrak{X}_{P},\mathbb{Z})
is the Poincaré dual of
[D_{j}]
.
Since the origin
0\in\mathfrak{t}_{P}
is in
the interior of
P^{\vee}
, the constant
c_{j}
is positive for all

j
. The statement of the Proposition follows in the case when the

X
-inner product is rational.

For a general
X
-inner product
g
, we consider a sequence

\{g_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
of rational
X
-inner products uniformly converging
to
g
. A squashing map with respect to the
X
-inner product
g

is the limit of a squashing maps
\aleph_{\nu}
with respect to the

X
-inner product
g_{\nu}
, and therefore,

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}},g}\leq\limsup_{\nu}E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}},g_{\nu}}
. For any
\nu
,
the constant
c_{g_{\nu}}
is uniformly bounded, since it is chosen so
that it satisfies

|\mathcal{T}_{g_{\nu}}^{{\operatorname{vert}}}(z_{i})|\leq c_{g_{\nu}}|\mathcal{T}(z_{i})|
, and
the statement of the Proposition follows.
∎

The following is an analog of Proposition
7.27

for the convergence of a sequence of broken map components in
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
.

Proposition 7.36
.

(Hofer energy and limits of maps, broken version)
Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be an open domain, and let
u_{\nu}:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}
be such that there is a
polytope
Q\subset P
and a
sequence of translations
t_{\nu}\in\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
such that

d(t_{\nu},\nu P_{0}^{\vee})\to\infty,\quad\forall P\supseteq P_{0}\supset Q,

(7.48)

and the sequence of translated maps
e^{-t_{\nu}}u:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}_{Q}

converges uniformly on compact subsets to a limit
u:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}_{Q}
. Then,

E_{Q,{\operatorname{Hof}}}^{*}(u)\leq\lim_{\nu}E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u_{\nu}).

The proof is the same as that of Proposition

7.27
.

Kapitel 8
Gromov compactness

We prove two convergence results for maps in broken manifolds in this
Chapter. The first result, Theorem
8.2
, concerns
the limit of holomorphic maps to the neck-stretched manifolds
X^{\nu}

in the limit
\nu\to\infty
. The second result, Theorem

8.3
, concerns the convergence behavior of broken
maps. Both theorems require only an area bound on the sequence of
holomorphic maps. In the breaking case, area of a map in
X^{\nu}

refers to
\omega_{X}
-area (
7.3
). In the case of broken maps
in Theorem
8.3
, area refers to the sum of the

\omega_{X_{P}}
-areas (
4.24
) of the projections of the map
components to cut spaces
X_{P}
.

We sketch the definition of Gromov convergence, with a more precise
and detailed definition postponed to Section
8.1
. As in
symplectic field theory, convergence in neck-stretched manifolds is
modulo target rescalings. Rescalings are analogous to

translations
defined in Section
3.5
. Given

\nu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the set of translations is parametrized by the dual
complex
\nu B^{\vee}
. A translation

t\in\nu P^{\vee}\subset B^{\vee}
is an embedding

{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}:X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}\subset\mathfrak{X}

of the
P
-cylindrical subset
X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}
of the neck-stretched
manifold
X^{\nu}
. Gromov convergence of a sequence of maps

u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to X^{\nu}
on neck-stretched manifolds to a broken map

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
encapsulates the following
conditions:

(a)

(Convergence of domains) The sequence of treed disks
C_{\nu}

converges to a treed disk
C
;

(b)

(Convergence of maps) for each component
C_{v}
of
C
,
there is a sequence of translations
t_{\nu}(v)\in\nu P(v)^{\vee}

such that the translated maps
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t_{\nu}}u_{\nu}
converges to a map

u_{v}
;

(c)

(Thin cylinder convergence) for a tropical node
w
in the
domain
C
of
u
corresponding to an edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
, on the midpoints

m_{\nu}:=(0,0)\in[-l_{\nu}/2,l_{\nu}/2]\times\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\subset C_{\nu}

of the sequence of annuli in
C_{\nu}
converging to the node
w
, the
translated evaluations
{\mathbb{e}}^{{\tfrac{1}{2}}(t_{\nu}(v_{+})+t_{\nu}(v_{-}))}u_{\nu}(m_{\nu})

converge to the tropical evaluation (see (
4.15
)) of
u
at
the nodal point
w
.

The translation sequences yield tropical positions of the vertices
in the
tropical graph of the limit map, justifying the representation in
Figure
1.9
. For any
\nu

\mathcal{T}(v):=\tfrac{t_{\nu}(v)}{\nu}\in P(v)^{\vee},\quad v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)

is a vertex position map for the combinatorial type
\Gamma
of the
limit map. For a Gromov-converging sequence of maps, area is
automatically preserved in the limit, since the number of domain
marked points is preserved in the limit, and this number is a constant
multiple of area (see (
5.2
)). We remark
that
(Thin cylinder convergence)
is not part
of the definition of convergence of stable maps in smooth manifolds,
since it can be deduced from convergence of maps. However, in case of
neck-stretching,
(Thin cylinder convergence)

does not follow from
(Convergence of
maps)
. Moreover
(Thin cylinder
convergence)
is used in the proof of surjectivity of gluing in
Chapter
9
.

In the statement of Gromov convergence for maps on neck-stretched
manifolds, the perturbations on neck-stretched manifolds are obtained
by gluing a perturbation datum on the broken manifold. A cylindrical
divisor
\mathfrak{D}
in a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
can be glued to give a family
of divisors
D^{\nu}
in neck-stretched manifolds
X^{\nu}
. There is a
natural correspondence of tamed cylindrical divisor-adapted almost
complex structures

\rho_{\nu}:\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{D})\to\mathcal{J}^{{\operatorname{cyl}}}(X^{\nu},D^{\nu}).

(8.1)

Definition 8.1
.

(Breaking perturbation
datum) Let
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
be a perturbation datum for the broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
adapted to a cylindrical divisor
\mathfrak{D}
. A

breaking perturbation datum
is a family of perturbation data

\{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in[\nu_{0},\infty]}
for the neck-stretched
manifolds
(X^{\nu},D^{\nu})
satisfying the following property: There
exists a constant
\nu(\Gamma)
so that if
\nu>\nu(\Gamma)
then

\mathfrak{p}_{\nu,\Gamma}:=(\rho_{\nu}\circ\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma},F_{\Gamma}),\quad\text{where }\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}=(\mathfrak{J}_{\Gamma},F_{\Gamma}).

The statement of the convergence theorem does not require
perturbations be regular, but it does require perturbations to be
adapted to a stabilizing pair
(D^{\nu},J^{\nu})
on
X^{\nu}
. Such a
pair is obtained by gluing a stabilizing pair
(\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{J})
on the
broken manifold (constructed in Section
5.4
). We
recall that by Definition
5.24
of a stabilizing pair

(D^{\nu},J^{\nu})
, any non-constant
J^{\nu}
-holomorphic sphere in

X^{\nu}
intersects
D^{\nu}
transversally
at at least 3 points. The
theorem is regarding a sequence of treed holomorphic disks in
neck-stretched manifolds
X^{\nu}
converging to a stable broken disk
in a broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
. We recall from Definition
4.14

that a
broken disk
is a broken map, one of whose components in
the piece
X_{P_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{X}
is a treed disk with the disk
boundary and treed segments mapping to the Lagrangian

L\subset X_{P_{0}}
.

Theorem 8.2
.

(Gromov convergence for
breaking maps) Suppose
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
is a stabilizing pair for
the broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
in the sense of Definition

5.24
, and
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
is a perturbation datum
on
\mathfrak{X}
adapted to
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
. Suppose
\{\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\nu}\}_{\nu}

is a sequence of breaking perturbation data (as in Definition

8.1
) on neck-stretched manifolds

\{X^{\nu}\}_{\nu}
. Let

u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to X^{\nu}

be a sequence of treed
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\nu}
-holomorphic disks with uniformly
bounded area
{\operatorname{Area}}(u_{\nu})
. There is a
subsequence of
\{u_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
that Gromov converges to a

\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
-holomorphic stable broken disk
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
modelled
on a tropical graph
\Gamma
.
The limit
u
is unique up to domain
reparametrizations and the action of the identity component of the
tropical symmetry group
T_{\operatorname{trop},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)
(see
(
4.35
)).

The second result of the Chapter concerns convergence of a sequence of
broken maps with uniformly bounded area. After passing to a
subsequence the maps
u_{\nu}
have the same type, say
\Gamma
with
tropical structure
\mathcal{T}
. The limit
u
typically has additional
domain components. The tropical structure
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
of the limit is
related to
\mathcal{T}
by a
tropical edge collapse morphism
, which is
a morphism of graphs
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\kappa}\mathcal{T}
that collapses a
subset of edges of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, and the directions of uncollapsed edges are the
same in
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. See Figure
8.1
for an
example.

Abbildung 8.1:
Tropical edge collapse morphism between tropical graphs.

Theorem 8.3
.

(Gromov convergence for broken maps) Suppose
(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
is a
stabilizing pair for the broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
, and
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}

is a perturbation datum on
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
adapted to

(\mathfrak{J}_{0},\mathfrak{D})
. Suppose
u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
is a sequence of
broken
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
-holomorphic disks with uniformly bounded
area.

After passing to a subsequence, the type of the broken disks
u_{\nu}

is
\nu
-independent, and is equal to, say,
\Gamma
. The sequence

u_{\nu}
Gromov converges to a broken
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
-holomorphic disk

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}_{\mathcal{P}}
of type
\Gamma^{\prime}
for which there is a tropical
edge-collapse morphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
. The limit
u
is unique
up to domain reparametrizations and the action of the identity
component of the tropical symmetry group
T_{\operatorname{trop},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma)

(see (
4.35
)).

If the edge-collapse
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
is non-trivial (in the
sense of Definition
8.31
) then

\dim{C}(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime}))>\dim{C}(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma))
.

Remark 8.4
.

In theorems
8.2
and
8.3
, the
limit broken map is uniquely determined if it is rigid. Indeed, for
a rigid tropical graph
\Gamma
the tropical symmetry group is
finite.

8.1
Gromov convergence

In this Section, we define the notion of convergence in the
compactness results, Theorem
8.2
and Theorem

8.3
. Much of the Section is devoted to fixing
identifications between domain curves that are close to each other in
the compactified moduli space of stable curves. In addition to fixing such
identifications in the complements of nodes, we also define a notion
of when a sequence of annuli in nearby curves converges to a
neighborhood of a node in a nodal curve; this notion is used in

(Thin cylinder convergence)
, which is part
of the statement of Gromov convergence. Finally, we define

translation sequences
which are sequences of rescalings of the
target; the convergence of maps is modulo these rescalings.

To fix identifications between domain
curves that are close to each other in the compactified moduli space
of stable curves, we fix a choice of an exponential map in
the neighborhood of nodes. Different choices lead to the same notion
of convergence.
To prove the surjectivity of the gluing operation in Chapter

9
, it is helpful to fix these identifications and use
the same identifications in the statement of the convergence result
and in the gluing construction.
We start by recalling a construction of deformations of a stable nodal curve. We describe the construction for rational
stable nodal curves (without boundary). The extensions to treed disks
are explained later in Remark
8.9
. Let
\Gamma

be the combinatorial type of a rational stable nodal curve with

d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})
markings. The moduli space of curves
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is a
submanifold of the compactified moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}
whose
tubular neighborhood can be described as follows. For a stable curve

S
in
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
, let
\tilde{S}
be the normalization of
S
at the nodal
points. For any edge
