\kappa:\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
. A sequence of broken
maps
u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}
of type
\Gamma

(Gromov)
converges
to a limit broken map
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
of type
\Gamma^{\prime}

if the following conditions are satisfied.

(a)

(Convergence of domains)

The sequence of treed disks
C_{\nu}
converge to the treed disk

C
and for any tropical node
w_{e}
,

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
that is collapsed by
\kappa
, the
arguments
\frac{\delta_{e}(C_{\nu})}{|\delta_{e}(C_{\nu})|}
of the gluing
parameters converge to a limit. Let

S_{\nu}(v)\subset(S_{\nu})_{\kappa(v)}\subset C_{\nu}
be the
subset corresponding to a vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, and let

i_{v,\nu}:=i_{S_{v},S_{\nu,\kappa(v)}}:S_{\nu}(v)\to S_{v},\quad S_{\nu}(v)\subset S_{\nu},

be embeddings from (
8.8
) whose images

i_{v,\nu}(S_{\nu}(v))
exhaust
S_{v}^{\circ}
as
\nu\to\infty
. Here

S_{v}^{\circ}:=S_{v}\backslash\{w_{e}:v\in e,\kern 5.0pte\in\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma^{\prime})\text{ is collapsed by $\kappa$}\}.

(b)

(Convergence of maps)

There is a
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence

(t_{\nu}(v))_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})}
going to infinity in the
sense of Definition
8.36
, such that for any vertex

v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, the sequence of maps

S_{v}^{\circ}\supset i_{v,\nu}(S_{\nu}(v))\xrightarrow{e^{-t_{\nu}(v)}(u_{\nu}\circ i_{v,\nu}^{-1})}\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}

converges in
C^{\infty}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}(S_{v}^{\circ})
to

u_{v}:S_{v}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
. The map

e^{-t_{\nu}(v)}:U_{P(v)}(\mathfrak{X}_{P(\kappa v)})\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
is
defined on the
P(v)
-cylindrical end

U_{P(v)}(\mathfrak{X}_{P(\kappa v)})\subset\mathfrak{X}_{P(\kappa v)}
, see
(
8.34
). For each boundary edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
, the maps
u_{\nu}|T_{e,\nu}
on the
treed segment converge to a (possibly broken) treed segment in

u
.

(c)

For a node
w
in
C
corresponding to
a tropical edge
e
collapsed by
\kappa
, that is,

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime})\backslash\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
,
let

z_{\pm}:(U_{w^{\pm}},w^{\pm})\to(\mathbb{C},0)

be matching coordinates (see Definition
4.14
following
(
4.22
)) on neighborhoods

U_{w^{\pm}}\subset S_{{v}_{\pm}}
of the nodal point which respect
the framing
{\operatorname{fr}}_{e}
. Let

A(l_{\nu}):=[-l_{\nu}/2,l_{\nu}/2]\times S^{1}\subset S_{\nu}

be a sequence of centered annuli converging to the node
w
, see
Definition
8.10
. Then the sequence

x_{\nu}:=e^{-{\tfrac{1}{2}}(t_{\nu}(v_{+})+t_{\nu}(v_{-}))}u_{\nu}(0,0)\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}

converges to a limit
x_{0}
, and the components
u_{v_{\pm}}
of the
broken map are asymptotically close to

z_{\pm}\mapsto z_{\pm}^{\mathcal{T}(e)}x_{0}

in the sense of Remark
4.26
.

8.5.3
From an area bound to a bound on types

The following result is the first ingredient in the proof of Theorem

8.3
, which shows that there is a finite number of
tropical graphs corresponding to broken maps that satisfy an area
bound.

Proposition 8.41
.

(Finite number of tropical
graphs underlying broken maps) For any
E>0
,

d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\geq 1
there are a finite number of tropical graphs

\Gamma
that occur as tropical graphs of broken maps

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
(see Definition
6.13
) of area at
most
E
and
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
boundary leaves.

Beweis.

Consider a broken map
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
of type
\Gamma
and area at
most
E_{0}
, whose edge directions are given by a map

\mathcal{T}:\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)\to\mathfrak{t}{Z}
. We first consider the case when the

X
-inner product (see (
3.10
)) is rational, so that the
compactification
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is an orbifold for any

P\in\mathcal{P}
. We discuss the generalization at the end of the proof.

Step 1
:
Uniform bound on the number of vertices
.

The number of interior markings in
\Gamma
is bounded by
kE_{0}
,
where
k
is the degree of the stabilizing divisor from
(
5.1
). Since
u
is adapted to the stabilizing divisor (as in Definition
6.9
(
d
)), all the surface
components
S_{v},v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
in its domain
C
are stable.
Given a uniform bound on the number of interior and boundary
markings on the type of domain curve, we obtain a uniform bound on
the number irreducible surface components.

Step 2
:
Uniform bound on the
sum of vertical components of
edge directions
.

Let
u_{v}:S_{v}\to\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}
be a component of the broken map
corresponding to a vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
, and
P:=P(v)
. By
the balancing property (
4.30
), the sum of the edge
directions projected to
\mathfrak{t}_{P}
is

\sum_{e\ni v}\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}}(\mathcal{T}(e))=c_{1}((\pi_{P}\circ u_{v})^{*}\overline{Z}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P})).

The right-hand side, which is the pairing of

(\pi_{P}\circ u_{v})_{*}[C]
and the Chern class
c_{1}(\overline{Z}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P})
, has an
E_{0}
-dependent bound.
Indeed, for any

\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a constant

k_{0}
such that for any domain-dependent almost
complex structure
J\in B_{\epsilon}(J_{\overline{X}_{P}})_{C^{0}}
and a

J
-holomorphic sphere
u:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\overline{X}_{P}
,

\int_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}u^{*}c_{1}(\overline{Z}_{P}\to\overline{X}_{P})\leq k_{0}\int_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}u^{*}\omega_{X_{P}}\leq k_{0}E_{0}.

This estimate is similar to the one in Lemma
5.5
and the
proof is the same – by choosing a two-form on
\overline{X}_{P}
representing
the Chern class and bounding it pointwise by
\omega_{X_{P}}
.

Step 3
:
Uniform bound on the horizontal components of edge directions
.

For a vertex
v
and an incident edge
e
, the horizontal component
of the direction
\mathcal{T}(e)
is the sum of intersection multiplicities at
the node
w_{e}
with horizontal relative divisors of
\overline{X}_{P}
:

\pi_{\mathfrak{t}_{P}}^{\perp}(\mathcal{T}(e))={\textstyle\sum}_{Q\subset P}m_{w_{e}}(u_{v,P},\overline{X}_{Q})\nu_{Q},\quad u_{v,P}:=\pi_{P}\circ u_{v}:S_{v}\to\overline{X}_{P},

where the sum ranges over facets
Q\in\mathcal{P}
of
P
, and
\nu_{Q}
is the normal vector of the facet
Q\subset P
. For
any relative divisor
\overline{X}_{Q}\subset\overline{X}_{P}
, the sum

\sum_{e\ni v}m_{w_{e}}(u_{v},\overline{X}_{Q})
is bounded by

c\omega_{X_{P}}(u_{v,P})
for a uniform constant
c(\overline{X}_{P},\overline{X}_{Q})
. The
proof is similar to the vertical case, by expressing the
intersection number with any divisor as an integral of a two-form.

Step 4
:
Finishing the proof
.

We will show that the tropical edge directions
\mathcal{T}(e)
of
\Gamma
are
uniformly bounded in
\mathfrak{t}
for the set of all broken maps with
area at most
E_{0}
. So far we have shown that (Step 3) the
horizontal components of the edge directions are uniformly bounded, and
the vector sum of the vertical direction components of the edges
incident on any vertex are uniformly bounded. From here, we
conclude that for a vertex
v
and an edge
e_{0}
incident on
v
:

\exists c(E):|\mathcal{T}(e_{0})|\leq\sum_{e\ni v,e\neq e_{0}}|\mathcal{T}(e)|+c(E).

(8.38)

Recall that
\Gamma
is a tree, any edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)
is oriented so that it points away from
the root vertex. The direction of any incoming edge can be bounded by
the direction of outgoing edges by (
8.38
). Applying
(
8.38
) iteratively, we conclude that for any
edge
e
in
\Gamma

|\mathcal{T}(e)|\leq c(E)|\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)|,

where the constant
c(E)
is the same as the one in
(
8.38
). The Proposition now follows from the bound on
the number of vertices in Step 1 in cases when the
X
-inner product is rational.

For a general
X
-inner product
g
, we consider a sequence

\{g_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
of rational
X
-inner products uniformly converging
to
g
. A squashing map with respect to the
X
-inner product
g

is the limit of a squashing maps
\aleph_{\nu}
with respect to the

X
-inner product
g_{\nu}
, and therefore,

E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}},g}\leq\limsup_{\nu}E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}},g_{\nu}}.

The Proposition then follows from the uniform bound
on
E_{P,{\operatorname{Hof}},g_{\nu}}
for all rational

g_{\nu}
proved in the preceding paragraphs.
∎

8.5.4
A translation sequence from an approximate translation sequence

Another ingredient in the proof of Gromov compactness for broken maps
is a result that produces a relative translation sequence, starting
from an
approximate relative translation sequence
via a sequence
of adjustments that is uniformly bounded. We first define the approximate sequences that we require.

Definition 8.42
.

(Approximate

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence) Suppose the tropical
graph
\Gamma
is obtained by collapsing edges in
\Gamma^{\prime}
and the
induced map on the vertex set is

\kappa:\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
. Then, an
approximate

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence
consists of a sequence

t_{\nu}(v)\in\operatorname{Cone}(\kappa,v),

for every
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
satisfying the (Polytope) and (Direction for collapsed edges)
conditions
in Definition
8.36
and the following weakened version
of the (Direction for uncollapsed edges) condition.

—

(Approximate direction for uncollapsed edges)

For an edge
e

of
\Gamma^{\prime}
that is not collapsed in
\Gamma
,

t_{\nu}(v_{+})-t_{\nu}(v_{-})\mod\mathcal{T}(e)

is a bounded sequence in
\mathfrak{t}/\mathbb{R}\mathcal{T}(e)
.

An approximate
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence can be adjusted
by a uniformly bounded amount to produce an actual

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence.

Lemma 8.43
.

(From an approximate to an exact
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation
sequence) Let
\kappa:\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
be
a tropical edge
collapse, and let
\{t_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
be an approximate

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence. There exists a

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence
\{\overline{t}_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
such
that

\sup_{\nu}|\overline{t}_{\nu}(v)-t_{\nu}(v)|<\infty

for all
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
.

Beweis.

The proof is by replicating the iteration in Step 2 of the proof of
Lemma
8.30
. At the start, we set

t^{0}_{\nu}:=t_{\nu}
. At the
(i+1)
-th step, we construct
t^{i+1}_{\nu}

as follows. As in the proof of Lemma
8.30
, there
exists a vertex
v_{0}\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
such that the sequence

|t^{i}_{\nu}(v)|
has the fastest growth rate. That is, for all

v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,
\lim_{\nu}|t^{i}_{\nu}(v)|/|t^{i}_{\nu}(v_{0})|
is
finite. Define

t^{i+1}_{\nu}(v):=t^{i}_{\nu}(v)-|t^{i}_{\nu}(v_{0})|\lim_{\nu}\frac{t^{i}_{\nu}(v)}{|t^{i}_{\nu}(v_{0})|}.

For the sequences
\{t^{i+1}_{\nu}(v)\}_{\nu}
,
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,
the quantity

\pi_{\mathcal{T}(e)}^{\perp}(t^{i+1}_{\nu}(v_{+})-t^{i+1}_{\nu}(v_{-})),\quad e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma^{\prime})

vanishes for collapsed edges, and is uniformly bounded for
uncollapsed edges. Furthermore, for any vertex
v
,

t^{i+1}_{\nu}(v)\in\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)}
. After, say,
k
steps, the
sequence
|t^{k}_{\nu}(v)|
is uniformly bounded for all vertices

v
. Then,
\overline{t}:=t-t^{k}
is an exact
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation
sequence.
∎

8.5.5
Proof of Gromov compactness for broken maps

The notion of horizontal convergence extends in a natural way to
broken manifolds, though it is easier to state in this case.

Definition 8.44
.

(Horizontal convergence in a broken manifold) Let
P\in\mathcal{P}

be a polytope. A sequence of points
x_{\nu}\in\mathfrak{X}_{P}

horizontally converges in
X_{Q}
for a polytope
Q\subseteq P
if the sequence

\pi_{P}(x_{\nu})\in X_{P}
converges to a point

x\in X_{Q}\subseteq\overline{X}_{P}
.

Remark 8.45
.

Analogs of Lemma
8.17
and
8.18
hold for
horizontal convergence in broken manifolds. An analog of the
breaking annulus lemma (Proposition
8.20
) also
holds for broken maps. For all these results, we make the following
substitutions in the original result to obtain the broken version
for maps in
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
: a translation sequence is replaced by a
relative translation sequence going to infinity, any instance of
the translation map
{\mathbb{e}}^{-t}
is replaced by
e^{-t}
where

t\in\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}B^{\vee}
(defined in (
8.34
)), and
Hofer energy is replaced by
P
-Hofer energy. As an example, we
state the analog of Lemma
8.17
, leaving the rest to the
reader.

Lemma 8.46
.

(Broken analog of Lemma
8.17
) Let
P\in\mathcal{P}
be a
polytope. Suppose
x_{\nu}\in\mathfrak{X}_{P}
is a sequence of points, and

t_{\nu}\in\operatorname{Cone}_{P^{\vee}}Q^{\vee}
is a sequence of translations for
a proper face
Q\subset P
, such that
e^{-t_{\nu}}x_{\nu}
converges
in
\mathfrak{X}_{Q}
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(a)

The sequence
x_{\nu}\in X_{P}
converges
horizontally in
X_{Q}
(in the sense of Definition

8.44
).

(b)

For any
P\supseteq P_{0}\supset Q
(that is,
Q
is a proper face of
P_{0}
),
d(t_{\nu},\nu P_{0}^{\vee})\to\infty
.

Proof of Theorem
8.3
.

After passing to a
subsequence, the tropical graph
\Gamma
underlying the maps
u_{\nu}

is
\nu
-independent. Indeed by Proposition
8.41
, there
is a finite number of tropical graphs that underlie broken maps with
area
<E
. By Proposition
7.35
, the Hofer energy for
a map
u:(C,\partial C)\to(\mathfrak{X},L)
can be read off from its area
and its intersection data with relative divisors, which is provided
by the directions
\mathcal{T}(e)
of the edges of the tropical graph

\Gamma
. Consequently, the Hofer energy
E_{{\operatorname{Hof}}}(u_{\nu})
of the
maps
u_{\nu}
is uniformly bounded.

We first find the limit map at each of the vertices of the tropical
graph
\Gamma
. For each vertex
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
, we assume
that the domain curve
C_{\nu,v}
for
u_{\nu,v}
has marked points
corresponding to lifts of nodes
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)
,
v\in e
,
in addition to the marked points corresponding to leaves

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\to}(\Gamma)
. The proof of breaking maps carries over
verbatim to the sequence of maps
u_{\nu,v}
. Indeed, all the
results used in the proof of convergence of breaking maps have
analogs for the case of broken maps; see Propositions

7.35
,
7.36
, Remark

8.45
and Lemma
8.46
. The conclusion
is that there is a limit map
u_{v}
modelled on a tropical graph

\Gamma_{v}
, and the convergence is via a translation sequence

t_{\nu}(v^{\prime})
going to infinity,
v^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{v}
. The convergence
includes the following statements:

P(v^{\prime})\subseteq P(v);\quad t_{\nu}(v^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Cone}_{P(v)^{\vee}}P(v^{\prime})^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)}\simeq\sqrt{-1}\mathfrak{t}_{P(v)},

where the inclusion is obtained by
fixing a point in
P(v)^{\vee}
as the origin of the cone; the
sequence of maps
e^{-t_{\nu}(v^{\prime})}u_{\nu,v}
lie in the

P(v^{\prime})
-cylindrical end of
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}
, and converge uniformly on
compact subsets to a limit
u_{v^{\prime}}
in
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v^{\prime})}
. For any
tropical edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
of
\Gamma
, the limit of the nodal
lifts
w_{\nu,\pm}^{e}\in S_{\nu,v_{\pm}}
lies on some a domain
component of
u_{v}
, corresponding to a vertex
v_{\pm,e}
of the
graph
\Gamma_{v_{\pm}}
. By connecting the graphs

\{\Gamma_{v}\}_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}
by edges
(v_{+,e},v_{-,e})

for all edges
e
of
\Gamma
, we obtain a pre-tropical graph

\Gamma^{\prime}
for which there is an edge collapse morphism

\kappa:\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
. We will subsequently show that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is a tropical graph and
\kappa
is a tropical edge
collapse morphism.

Next, we simultaneously prove that the limit map
u
satisfies
matching conditions on the edges of
\Gamma
, and that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is
a tropical graph. As a first step, we show that the translation
sequence
\{t_{\nu}(v):v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})\}
in the previous
paragraph is an approximate
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence
in the sense of Definition
8.42
. The convergence to
the maps
u_{v}
implies that for any
\nu
,
t_{\nu}
satisfies the
direction condition (
8.36
) for edges collapsed by

\kappa
, that is, for edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma^{\prime})\backslash\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)
. Therefore, it remains to
prove that the
(Approximate direction
for uncollapsed edges)
condition is satisfied. Consider an edge

e
of
\Gamma
, that is incident on
v_{+}
,
v_{-}\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,
and the nodal point corresponding to
e
is the pair
w^{\pm}_{e}
on

C_{v_{\pm}}
. The edge matching condition for broken maps
(
4.18
) implies

(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ u_{\nu})(w_{\nu,+}^{e})=(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ u_{\nu})(w_{\nu,-}^{e})\quad\text{in }\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}.

(8.39)

On a sequence of converging relative maps, the projected tropical evaluations of the
relative marked points converge, that is,

(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ(e^{-t_{\nu}(v)}u_{\nu}))(z_{i,\nu})\to(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ u)(z)\quad\text{in }\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}.

This convergence is a consequence of the convergence of the marked
points
z_{i,\nu}
on the domain curve and the convergence of maps

e^{-t_{\nu}(v)}u_{\nu}
. Therefore, for the edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
,

d((\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ(e^{-t_{\nu}(v_{+})}u_{\nu}))(w^{e}_{\nu,+}),(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ(e^{-t_{\nu}(v_{-})}u_{\nu}))(w^{e}_{\nu,-}))\to\\
d((\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ u)(w^{e}_{+}),(\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}\circ u)(w^{e}_{-})),

where
d
is the cylindrical metric distance in

\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}/T_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}
. Using (
8.39
), the sequence

\pi^{\perp}_{\mathcal{T}(e)}(t_{\nu}(v_{+})-t_{\nu}(v_{-}))\in\mathfrak{t}{C}/\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{T}(e),\mathbb{C}}

(8.40)

is bounded, implying that
\{t_{\nu}(v):v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})\}
is an
approximate
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence.
An approximate
(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence can be
adjusted by a uniformly bounded amount to produce an actual

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequence by Lemma

8.43
. After making such an adjustment the quantity in
(
8.40
) vanishes. Then by (
8.39
) the limit map

u
satisfies the edge matching condition (
4.18
)
for edges in
\Gamma
, and
u
is therefore a broken
map. Furthermore, we now have that
\{t_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
is a relative
translation sequence going to infinity (as in Definition

8.36
), and then, Lemma
8.37
implies that

\Gamma^{\prime}
is a tropical graph, and therefore, the limit map

u=(u_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})}
is a broken map.

The proof of uniqueness of the limit is similar to the case of
breaking maps: The domain curve is uniquely determined, and any two

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation sequences
t_{\nu}
,
t^{\prime}_{\nu}
differ by
a uniformly bounded amount :

\sup_{\nu}|t_{\nu}(v)-t_{\nu}^{\prime}(v)|<\infty
. After passing to a
subsequence, the limit

t_{\infty}(v):=\lim_{\nu}(t_{\nu}(v)-t_{\nu}^{\prime}(v))

exists. The tuple
(t_{\infty}(v))_{v}
is an unsigned version of a

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma)
-translation since

t_{\infty}(v)\in\operatorname{Cone}(\kappa,v)
and for any edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
,

t_{\infty}(v_{+})-t_{\infty}(v_{-})\in\mathbb{R}\mathcal{T}(e).

Therefore, we obtain a tropical symmetry transformation

e^{-t_{\infty}}\in T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
that relates the limit maps

\lim_{\nu}e^{-t_{\nu}}u_{\nu}
,
\lim_{\nu}e^{-t_{\nu}^{\prime}}u_{\nu}
. Consequently, the limit is unique up to the
action of the identity component
T_{{\operatorname{trop}},\mathcal{W}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
of the
tropical symmetry group.

It remains to prove the last statement of the Theorem. Suppose the
tropical edge collapse
\kappa:\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
is non-trivial.
Then, either a tropical edge
e_{0}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
is collapsed by

\kappa
, or there is a vertex
v_{0}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
for which

P(v_{0})\subsetneq P(\kappa(v_{0}))
. In both cases, for large enough

\nu
, the translation
(t_{\nu}(v))_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})}
generates
a subgroup

\{(\exp zt_{\nu}(v))_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma^{\prime})}:z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}\subset T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime})

(see (
4.36
))
that is not contained
in
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. Indeed, in the first case of a collapsed
tropical edge
e_{0}=(v_{+},v_{-})

t_{\nu}(v_{+})-t_{\nu}(v_{-})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mathcal{T}(e_{0})

and in the second case where
P(v_{0})\subsetneq P(\kappa(v_{0}))
we
have

t_{\nu}(v_{0})\notin P(\kappa(v_{0}))^{\vee}.

Therefore
\dim{C}(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime}))>\dim{C}(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma))
.
∎

Remark 8.47
.

If a sequence
\{u_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
of broken maps with
tropical graph
\Gamma
converges to a limit
u_{\infty}
whose
tropical graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
is different from
\Gamma
, then,

i^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\operatorname{red}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\underline{x})\leq i^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\operatorname{red}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})-2.

(8.41)

Indeed, (
8.41
) follows from the definition
of the reduced index
i^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\operatorname{red}}
in (
6.32
), Theorem

8.3
which implies

\dim(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime}))\geq\dim(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma))+2
, and the
index being preserved in the limit:

i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\underline{x})=i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})
from Proposition

6.21
.

8.6
Boundaries of rigid strata

In the moduli space of maps, a stratum is
rigid
if it does not
deform to another type; that is, it does not occur in the boundary of
a compactification of a different stratum. To count isolated maps
under generic perturbations one must restrict attention to rigid
strata. In this section, we formally define rigid strata of broken and
unbroken maps, and describe the codimension one boundary components of
rigid strata.

For unbroken maps, rigid types are those whose domain type is a
top-dimensional stratum in the moduli space of treed disks. We recall
that top-dimensional strata in the moduli space of treed disks
correspond to disk types which do not have any interior nodes, and at
boundary nodes, the length of the treed segment is finite,
see Figure
4.2
.
Rigidity also includes the condition that the
map has simple intersections with the stabilizing divisor, because
maps with non-simple intersections can be deformed to maps with
multiple simple intersections.

Definition 8.48
.

(Rigid types of unbroken
maps)
The combinatorial type
\Gamma
of a (unbroken) treed holomorphic
map is
rigid
if the only edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)

corresponding to nodes are boundary edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
of finite non-zero length

\ell(e)\in(0,\infty)
, and intersection multiplicity

m_{w_{e}}(u,D_{P})
of
u
of type
\Gamma
at
T_{e}\cap S
with the
stabilizing divisor
\mathfrak{D}=(D_{P},P\in\mathcal{P})
is
1
.

Broken maps necessarily have tropical nodes, therefore in the
definition of rigidity of broken maps, the
no interior nodes

condition of the unbroken case is replaced by the condition that the
tropical graph is rigid.

Definition 8.49
.

(Rigid types of broken maps)

The combinatorial type
\Gamma
of a
broken treed holomorphic map is
rigid
if the tropical graph

\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
is rigid, and the only internal (non-tropical) edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{int}}(\Gamma)
are boundary edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
of finite non-zero length

\ell(e)\in(0,\infty)
, and intersection multiplicity

m_{w_{e}}(u,D_{P})
of
u
of type
\Gamma
at
T_{e}\cap S
with the
stabilizing divisor
\mathfrak{D}=(D_{P},P\in\mathcal{P})
is
1
.

Remark 8.50
.

The rigidity for types of broken maps includes the additional
condition of the rigidity of the tropical graph because any
non-rigid tropical graph
\Gamma
is related to a rigid tropical
graph
\Gamma_{0}
by a tropical edge collapse morphism, and therefore
tropical symmetry orbits of broken maps modelled on
\Gamma
occur
in the compactification of the moduli space of maps modelled on

\Gamma_{0}
.

Next, we address the question of which strata of broken maps occur in
the compactification of a one-dimensional moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(\underline{x})
where
\Gamma
is a rigid type. The following
result proves that the compactification does not contain broken maps
of
crowded type
(Definition
6.26
). Roughly speaking,
being of crowded type means that there are no ghost components with
more than one interior marking. We recall that interior markings
correspond to intersections with the stabilizing divisors, and in the
context of broken maps, a ghost component is a domain component on
which the map is
horizontally constant
(Definition

4.15
(
a
)). Once this is established, we prove in
Proposition
8.52
that the only strata that occur in the
codimension one boundary are those that contain either a broken edge,
or an edge with length zero.

Proposition 8.51
.

(No crowded strata in the compactification)
Let
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
be a regular perturbation datum. Let
\Gamma
be a
type of an uncrowded rigid broken map and let

\underline{x}\in(\mathcal{I}(L))^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+1}
be a tuple of limit points of
boundary leaves such that the expected dimension of the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
is at most
1
. Then, for any curve

u
in the compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
,

—

there are no horizontally constant components that contain interior
markings, and

—

u
has simple intersections with the stabilizing
divisor.

Beweis.

First, we point out that a horizontally
constant component containing an interior marking can not be a disk
component. Indeed, the Lagrangian
L
is contained in a
top-dimensional cut space
X_{P_{0}}
and the torus
T_{P_{0},\mathbb{C}}
is
trivial. Therefore, a horizontally constant disk component

u_{v}:S_{v}\to X_{P_{0}}
is in fact constant and maps to a point in

L
. Since
L
is disjoint from the stabilizing divisor there are no
interior markings on
S_{v}
.

Next, we rule out horizontally constant spherical components that
contain markings. Suppose the map

u\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
is horizontally constant on a
component
S_{v}
, for a vertex
v
of
\Gamma
, and suppose
S_{v}
has
an interior marking
z_{e}
.
Since
\Gamma
is an uncrowded stratum,
S_{v}
has no other
interior marking besides
z_{e}
. The component has at most two nodes
– otherwise moving
the marking
z_{e}
on the component
S_{v}
gives a two-dimensional
family of regular maps adapted to the stabilizing divisor. By
rigidity of the type
\Gamma
, all edges
e
corresponding to
interior nodes
w_{e}
are tropical edges. Since
u|_{S_{v}}
is
horizontally constant, the balancing property (
4.29
)
implies that there are exactly two edges
e_{1},e_{2}\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma)

incident on
v
, and they have the same direction
\mathcal{T}(e_{1})=\mathcal{T}(e_{2})
.
This means the type
\Gamma
has a non-trivial tropical symmetry
group
T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
, contradicting the rigidity of
\Gamma
.
(See related Example
6.31
.)

Next, we consider a map in a boundary of the moduli space. Suppose

\{u_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
is a sequence in
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
that
converges to a limit
u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
of type
\Gamma^{\prime}
. First we prove
the proposition assuming that the limit
u
is uncrowded. Then
u

is a regular map adapted to the stabilizing divisor. Since the index of
u_{\nu}
is at most

1
, standard arguments (see the proof of Proposition

8.52
, for example) imply that the type
\Gamma
of

u_{\nu}
is obtained from the limit type
\Gamma^{\prime}
by the morphism
(Making an edge length finite/non-zero). In particular, the tropical
graph associated to
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the same as that of
\Gamma
. By
the argument in the previous paragraph, there are no markings in
horizontally constant components of the limit map
u
.

Next, consider the case that the limit
u
has a crowded
component. Starting from
u
, we obtain a map
u^{\prime}
of
uncrowded type as follows: Forgetting all but one interior marking
on each of the crowded components yields a map
u^{\prime}
adapted to the stabilizing divisor. If in
this process, a domain component
S_{v}\subset S
(belonging to a
crowded component of
u
) becomes unstable, it is collapsed. Indeed,
the vertex
v
has valence
1
or
2
, and in either case, there is
an obvious way to drop
v
from the graph. Denote by
\Gamma_{s}
the
resulting type of the map. In
\Gamma_{s}
, the remaining interior
marking, corresponding to the leaf
e
, is assigned a multiplicity
of
\mu_{\mathfrak{D}}(e)
plus the sum of multiplicities
\mu_{\mathfrak{D}}(e^{\prime})
of all the forgotten leaves
e^{\prime}\neq e
. The
limit
u^{\prime}
is
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma_{s}}
-holomorphic because of the (Locality
axiom) in Definition
6.3
. Indeed, forgetting
markings changes the type of the limit curve, but it does not affect
the perturbation datum
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
on the other curve components on
which the map is horizontally non-constant. Therefore,
u^{\prime}
is
regular. If no component is collapsed in
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma_{s}
,
then, the expected dimension of the type
\Gamma_{s}
is the same as
that of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In this case,
u
is an uncrowded map with a
marking in a horizontally constant component. This possibility has
been ruled out in the last paragraph. If a component is collapsed,
the expected dimension of
\Gamma_{s}
is at least two lower than that
of
\Gamma
, and therefore, the map
u
does not exist.

Finally, we show that for a map
u
in the compactification

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
, the intersections with the
stabilizing divisor are simple. We assume that
u
is the limit of a
sequence of maps
\{u_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
.
Since
\Gamma
is rigid, the maps
u_{\nu}
have simple intersections
with
\mathfrak{D}
and each intersection is an interior marking. The map

u
can have a non-simple intersection with
\mathfrak{D}
only if two
sequences interior markings
z_{e,\nu}
,
z_{e^{\prime},\nu}
on the maps

\{u_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
coincide in the limit, which means that
u
has a
ghost component containing the two markings. Since we have shown
that
u
is uncrowded, we have arrived at a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 8.52
.

(Boundary strata) Let
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
be a regular perturbation
datum. Suppose
\Gamma
is a rigid type for a broken map and

\underline{x}\in(\mathcal{I}(L))^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
is a tuple of limit points of
boundary leaves such that the expected dimension
i(\Gamma,\underline{x})

is
\leq 1
. The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})

admits a compactification
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
. The
boundary strata

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})\backslash\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})

correspond to types
\Gamma^{\prime}
with a single broken boundary trajectory

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}({\Gamma^{\prime}}),\ell(e)=\infty
or a single
boundary edge with length zero
\ell(e)=0
.

Beweis.

For a sequence of maps
u_{\nu}:C\to\mathfrak{X}
in the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
, a subsequence converges to a limit

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
by Theorem
8.3
. By Proposition

8.51
,
u
is uncrowded and therefore,
u
is regular
and adapted to the stabilizing divisor. Interior nodes
w_{e}
corresponding to non-tropical
edges
e\notin\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
are ruled out in
u
for
dimension reasons using the index relation (
6.31
). We
next claim that the tropical type of
u
is
\Gamma
. If not, it is
of type
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and there is a non-trivial tropical edge collapse
morphism
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
. The last statement in Theorem

8.3
implies that
\dim(T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime}))\geq 2
. By
Proposition
6.21
, the expected dimension of the moduli
space of broken maps is unaffected by collapsing tropical
edges. Therefore, the index of
u
is at most
1
. However, the
positive dimensionality of the tropical symmetry group

T_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
, and the fact that the action of the tropical
symmetry group does not have infinitesimal stabilizers implies that

i^{\operatorname{brok}}(u)\geq 2
, leading to a contradiction. We conclude that the
tropical type of
u
is the same as that of the sequence
u_{\nu}
.
The only other phenomenon which occurs in the limit of

\{u_{\nu}\}_{\nu}
is the formation of a boundary node
w\in C

corresponding to an edge
e
of length
\ell(e)
zero, or the length
of a boundary edge
\ell(e)
going to zero or infinity.
∎

Kapitel 9
Gluing

In this Chapter, we show that a rigid broken map can be glued at nodes
to produce a family of unbroken maps, one in each neck-stretched
manifold. A fixed perturbation datum
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
on a broken manifold

\mathfrak{X}
can be glued in a natural way to produce a perturbation datum

\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\nu}
for
X^{\nu}
which is equal to
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
away from the
neck regions. With respect to these perturbation data, we construct a
bijection between rigid broken map and rigid maps in neck-stretched
manifolds
X^{\nu}
with sufficiently large neck lengths.
We recall from (
6.11
) that
\mathcal{M}^{{\operatorname{brok}},<E}(\mathfrak{X},L)_{0}
is the union of zero-dimensional strata in the moduli space of broken maps whose energy is at most
E
.

Theorem 9.1
.

(Gluing) Suppose that

u^{0}:C\to\mathfrak{X}
is a regular broken disk of index zero. There
exists
\nu_{0}>0
such that if
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
then there exists a
family of unbroken disks
u_{\nu}:C^{\nu}\to X^{\nu}
of index zero,
with the property that
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}[u_{\nu}]=[u^{0}]
. For
any area bound
E>0
there exists
\nu_{0}
such that for

\nu\geq\nu_{0}
the correspondence
[u]\mapsto[u_{\nu}]
defines
an orientation-preserving bijection between the rigid moduli spaces

\mathcal{M}^{<E}(X^{\nu},L)_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{{\operatorname{brok}},<E}(\mathfrak{X},L)_{0}

for
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
.

Remark 9.2
.

The gluing operation is defined on broken maps, and not on tropical
symmetry orbits of broken maps. In fact if the tropical symmetry
group is non-trivial (it is necessarily finite for rigid broken
maps), gluing different elements in the tropical symmetry orbit
produces distinct sequences of unbroken maps. The convergence
result also distinguishes between rigid maps in the same tropical
symmetry orbit. Indeed, in Theorem
8.2
, if the
limit map is rigid then the limit is uniquely determined up to
domain reparametrization.

Similar to other gluing theorems in pseudoholomorphic curves, the
proof of Theorem
9.1
is an application of a quantitative
version of the implicit function theorem for Banach manifolds. The
steps are: construction of an approximation solution called the
pre-glued map; construction of an approximate inverse to the
linearized operator; quadratic estimates; application of the
contraction mapping principle, and surjectivity of the gluing
construction. Through the proof of the gluing theorem, the notation

c
denotes a
\nu
-independent constant whose value may be different
in every occurrence. The fact that gluing preserves the orientation of
rigid moduli spaces is discussed in Remark
9.6
.

9.1
The approximate solution

Given a broken map, we start by constructing a family of approximate solutions, called

pre-glued maps
, with one map in each neck-stretched manifold
X^{\nu}
. These maps are obtained by
gluing the broken map in the neck region using a cut-off function, and therefore, they are not
holomorphic in the neck regions.

The pre-glued family for a broken map is constructed using tropical
vertex positions of the broken map. We recall that a rigid broken map is modelled on a
rigid tropical graph
\Gamma
, whose vertex positions

\{\mathcal{T}(v):v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\}
are uniquely determined.
These positions determine the neck lengths for the
approximate solution as follows. For any edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})
of

\Gamma
, there exists
l_{e}>0
such that

\mathcal{T}(v_{+})-\mathcal{T}(v_{-})=l_{e}\mathcal{T}(e),

(9.1)

where
\mathcal{T}(e)\in\mathfrak{t}{Z}
is the direction of the edge.

The domain of any map in the glued family is a treed disk obtained by
replacing tropical nodes in
C
with necks and leaving the tree part
in
C
unchanged: For any
\nu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
the curve
C_{\nu}
has a neck
of length
\nu l_{e}
in place of a tropical node
w_{e}
corresponding to

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
in
C
. Denote by

\Gamma_{{\operatorname{glue}}}

(9.2)

the type of the glued curve. Recall that the lift of a tropical node

w_{e}
in
C
has matching coordinates (see (Matching at tropical
nodes) in Definition
4.14
) in the neighbourhoods
U_{e}^{+}
,

U_{e}^{-}
of the lifts
w^{+}_{e}
,
w^{-}_{e}
, which are denoted by

S_{v_{\pm}}\supset(U_{e}^{\pm},w_{e}^{\pm})\xrightarrow{z_{e}^{\pm}}(\mathbb{C},0).

(9.3)

The coordinates can be chosen to be compositions of the complex
exponential map (
8.2
) and linear functions from tangent
spaces to
\mathbb{C}
. The glued curve
C_{\nu}
is obtained from
C
by
deleting a small disk in
U_{e}^{\pm}
for every edge
e
in
\Gamma
,
and gluing the remainder of the neighbourhoods
U_{e}^{\pm}
using the
identification
z_{e}^{+}\sim e^{-\nu l_{e}}z_{e}^{-}
, and leaving the tree
part
T
unchanged. So the surface and tree parts of
C_{\nu}
are

C_{\nu}=S_{\nu}\cup T.

For future use in the proof we point out that the punctured
neighbourhoods
U_{e}^{\pm}\backslash\{w_{e}^{\pm}\}
have matching logarithmic
coordinates

(s_{e},t_{e}):U_{e}^{-}\backslash\{w^{-}_{e}\}\to[0,\infty)\times S^{1},\quad(s_{e},t_{e}):U_{e}^{+}\backslash\{w^{+}_{e}\}\to(-\infty,0]\times S^{1}

given by
(s_{e},t_{e}):=\pm\ln(z_{e}^{\pm})
.

Translated maps can be glued at the nodal points to yield a sequence
of approximate solutions for the holomorphic curve equation in
neck-stretched manifolds. We recall that for a rigid tropical graph,
the translation sequences are unique and are given by

t_{\nu}:\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)\to\nu B^{\vee},\quad v\mapsto\nu\mathcal{T}(v).

(9.4)

Translation sequences give identifications between broken manifolds
and neck-stretched manifolds. For any
\nu
,
v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
the
map

{\mathbb{e}}^{t_{\nu}(v)}:\mathfrak{X}_{P(v)}\to X^{\nu}_{{\tilde{P}}}\subset X^{\nu}

is defined in (
3.61
). The complement of the neck region
in
S^{\nu}
is a disjoint union

S^{\nu}\backslash\operatorname{Neck}(S^{\nu})=\cup_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}S^{\nu}(v).

On
S^{\nu}(v)
, the approximate solution is defined as the translation
of the map
u^{0}_{v}
, that is,

u^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\nu}:={\mathbb{e}}^{t_{\nu}(v)}u^{0}_{v}\quad\text{on $S^{\nu}(v)$}.

(9.5)

On the treed part
T\subset C^{\nu}
, we have

u^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\nu}:=u^{0}|_{T}\quad\text{on $T\subset C^{\nu}$}.

Next, we define
u^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\nu}
on the neck region
\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S^{\nu})

corresponding to a tropical edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. The matching condition on

u^{0}
at the tropical node
w_{e}
implies that there is a point

x_{e}\in\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
such that the map
u_{v_{\pm}}
is asymptotically
close to the trivial cylinder

u_{e}^{\operatorname{vert}}:\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times S^{1}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)},\quad(s,t)\mapsto e^{\mathcal{T}(e)(s+it)}x_{e}

(9.6)

near the nodal lift
w_{e}^{\pm}\in C_{v}^{\pm}
. That is, if we define

\zeta_{e}^{\pm}\in T_{u_{e}^{\operatorname{vert}}}\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
by the condition

u^{0}_{v_{\pm}}=\exp_{u_{e}^{\operatorname{vert}}}\zeta_{e}^{\pm},

(9.7)

then

\lVert D^{k}\zeta_{e}^{\pm}(s,t)\rVert\leq ce^{-|s|}

(9.8)

for any
k\geq 0
. Indeed, in an almost complex compactification of
the target space
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v_{\pm})}
of
u^{0}_{v_{\pm}}
as in
(
6.20
), the map extends smoothly over
\mp\infty
, from
where we obtain the decay (
9.8
) in cylindrical domain
coordinates. In (
9.7
) we note that the target space of
the map
u^{0}_{v_{\pm}}
resp.
\exp_{u_{e}^{\operatorname{vert}}}\zeta_{e}^{\pm}
is

\mathfrak{X}_{P(v_{\pm})}
resp.
\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
, but the relation
(
9.7
) is well-defined because near the node the image of

u_{v_{\pm}}
lies in the
P(e)
-cylindrical end of
\mathfrak{X}_{P(v_{\pm})}

which is canonically identified to
\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}
. Define a cylinder
in
\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(C_{\nu})
corresponding to
u_{e}^{\operatorname{vert}}

by

u_{e,\nu}^{{\operatorname{vert}}}:={\mathbb{e}}^{{\tfrac{1}{2}}(t_{\nu}(v_{+})+t_{\nu}(v_{-})}u_{e}^{\operatorname{vert}}:[\tfrac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}\to X^{\nu}.

The approximate solution on
\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(C_{\nu})
is the trivial cylinder

u_{e,\nu}^{{\operatorname{vert}}}
corrected by a section obtained by patching

\zeta_{e}^{+}
,
\zeta_{e}^{-}
defined as

u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}(s,t):=\exp_{u_{e,\nu}^{{\operatorname{vert}}}}(\zeta^{\nu}_{e}(s,t)):[\tfrac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}\to X^{\nu},\\
\quad\zeta^{\nu}_{e}(s,t):=\beta(-s)\zeta_{e}^{-}(s+\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},t)+\beta(s)\zeta_{e}^{+}(s-\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},t).

(9.9)

where

\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},[0,1]),\quad\begin{cases}\beta(s)=0&s\leq 0\\
\beta(s)=1&s\geq 1\end{cases}

(9.10)

is the cutoff function from (
6.16
). In other words, in the
cylinder in
C_{\nu}
corresponding to an edge
e
, one translates the
domain on both ends by an amount
\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2}
, and then
patches the map together using the cutoff function and geodesic
exponentiation. The definition (
9.5
) of
u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}

in the complement of the neck, and its definition (
9.9
)
on the neck together give a smooth map
u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}:C_{\nu}\to X^{\nu}
.
Indeed, using the relation
t_{\nu}(v_{+})-t_{\nu}(v_{-})=\nu l_{e}
, we
conclude that the expression in (
9.9
) is equal to

{\mathbb{e}}^{t_{\nu}(v_{\pm})}u^{0}_{v_{\pm}}
in the neighborhood of the boundary
component
\tfrac{\pm\nu l_{e}}{2}\times S^{1}
.

Remark 9.3
.

The number
\nu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is called the
map gluing parameter

to differentiate it from the parameter used for gluing nodes in a
curve. In standard gluing results, the map gluing parameter
\nu

is taken to be the neck length in the domain
C_{\nu}
of the glued
map
u^{\nu}
. In our setting, the map gluing parameter is the neck
length
\nu
in the target space
X^{\nu}
. The domain neck lengths,
approximately equal to
\nu l_{e}
, are allowed to vary in the Picard
iteration argument. Note that resolving nodes
w_{e}
corresponding
to tropical edges
e
does not increase the index of the map. As a
result gluing an index zero broken map
u
produces an isolated map

u^{\nu}
in each
X^{\nu}
.

9.2
Fredholm theory for glued maps

We define a map between suitable Banach spaces whose zeroes describe
pseudoholomorphic curves close to the approximate solution.
Pseudoholomorphic maps are zeroes of the section

\mathcal{F}_{\nu}:=(\overline{\partial},\operatorname{ev}):\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}\times\operatorname{Map}(C_{\nu},X^{\nu})\to\\
\Omega^{0,1}(S_{\nu},u^{*}TX)\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,u^{*}TL)\oplus X(\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}})

(9.11)

defined in (
6.37
). The components of the map consist of

\overline{\partial}
operators on surfaces, shifted gradient operators on treed
segments, and various evaluation maps. In this section we define
metrics/norms in each of the spaces. We recall the operator
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}

later in the section.

We describe a metric on the moduli space of treed disks. Recall that
the domain of
u
is
C
, which is a curve of type
\Gamma
with
surface part
(S,j_{S})
and tree part
T
, and recall from
(
9.2
) that the type
\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}
of the glued curve
is given by collapsing all the interior edges in
\Gamma
. In a
neighborhood
U_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
of

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
, there is a projection map

\pi_{\Gamma}:U_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}

such that any curve
C^{\prime}\in U_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}}
is obtained by gluing at
tropical nodes of the curve
\pi_{\Gamma}(C^{\prime})
using the coordinates
(
9.3
). We remark that the domain curves
C_{\nu}
of the
approximate solutions which were constructed by gluing the tropical
nodes in
C
lie in the neighborhood
U_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}}
, and

\pi_{\Gamma}(C_{\nu})=C
. The subset

U_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
close to the boundary stratum

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is equipped with a metric

g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}:T(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}\cap U_{\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}})^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{R}

(9.12)

that is cylindrical in the fibers of
\pi_{\Gamma}
. That is, each fiber
of
\pi_{\Gamma}
is isometric to a product of cylinders

\Pi_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1})
parametrized by
gluing parameters
(s_{e},t_{e})
.

In the neighborhood of each of the glued curves, we give a description
of the complex structures on the surface components. As a preliminary
step, we describe the complex structures on curves close to the domain

C
of
u
. Let the complex curve
(S,j_{S})
be the surface part of

C
. A trivialization

\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}|U_{\Gamma,C}\simeq S\times U_{\Gamma,C}

(9.13)

of the universal curve
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
in a neighborhood

U_{\Gamma,C}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
of
C
yields a family of complex
structures (as in (
4.8
))

U_{\Gamma,C}\to\mathcal{J}(S),\quad m\mapsto j_{\Gamma}(m).

We write
j_{\Gamma}
as a sum

j_{\Gamma}(m)=j_{S}+\Delta j_{\Gamma}(m)

and assume that the trivialization in (
9.13
) is chosen
so that
\Delta j_{\Gamma}(m)=0
in neighborhoods of special points.
Consider a glued treed disk
C_{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
whose
surface part
(S_{\nu},j_{S_{\nu}})\subset C_{\nu}
is obtained by gluing
the tropical nodes in
S\subset C
. In a neighborhood

U_{C_{\nu}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
, choose a trivialization of the
universal curve
\mathcal{S}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
so that the
induced family of complex structures

U_{C_{\nu}}\to\mathcal{J}(S_{\nu}),\quad m\mapsto j_{\nu}(m),

(9.14)

satisfies the following : The function
j_{\nu}
is a sum

j_{\nu}=j_{S_{\nu}}+\Delta j_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(m)+\Delta j_{\operatorname{neck}}^{\nu}(m)

where

—

j_{S_{\nu}}
is the complex structure on the glued curve
S_{\nu}

and is constant over
U_{C_{\nu}}
,

—

the function
m\mapsto\Delta j_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(m)
is supported in the
complement of the neck regions of
S_{\nu}
and is equal to

\Delta j_{\Gamma}(\pi_{\Gamma}(m))
,

—

and
\Delta j_{{\operatorname{neck}}}^{\nu}(m)
is supported in the neck regions of

S_{\nu}
, and the support is contained in a uniformly bounded
neighborhood the boundary of the neck, that is, there is a

\nu
-independent constant
L
such that

\operatorname{supp}(\Delta j^{\nu}_{\operatorname{neck}})\subset\bigcup_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}\{s:\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}-L\leq|s|\leq\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}\}\times S^{1}\subset A(l_{\nu})\subset S_{\nu}.

(9.15)

Furthermore, there is a
\nu
-independent constant
c
such that on
the neck region corresponding to any edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
and for any
\nu

\lVert\Delta j^{\nu}_{\operatorname{neck}}(m)\rVert_{C^{1}}\approx c|\delta_{e}(m)-\nu l_{e}|,

(9.16)

where
\delta_{e}(m)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1}
is the gluing parameter used at
the node
e
to smoothen the node
w_{e}
in the curve

\pi_{\Gamma}(m)
.

Indeed, (
9.16
) can be satisfied by making suitable choices,
as it amounts to choosing a diffeomorphism from a cylinder of length

\delta_{e}(m)
to a cylinder of length
\nu l_{e}
that is a translation
map outside a length
L
sub-cylinder, and whose derivative is bounded
as in (
9.16
). Such a choice of the trivialization of the
universal curve ensures that there is a
\nu
-independent constant
c

such that for any two curves represented by
m_{1}
,
m_{2}

|\Delta j^{\nu}(m_{2})-\Delta j^{\nu}(m_{1})|\leq cd_{g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}}(m_{1},m_{2}).

(9.17)

These uniform estimates are used in the proof of the quadratic
estimate in Section
9.5
.

The second space in the domain of (
9.11
) is a space of

W^{1,p}_{\operatorname{loc}}
maps

\operatorname{Map}(C_{\nu},X^{\nu})_{1,p}\subset\operatorname{Map}(S_{\nu},X^{\nu})_{1,p}\times\operatorname{Map}(T,X^{\nu})_{1,p}

defined by requiring that the maps from
S_{\nu}
and
T
agree on the
intersection
S_{\nu}\cap T
. We recall that
T
is the disjoint union
of treed segments
\cup_{e}T_{e}
corresponding to boundary edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
with positive lengths; and
T_{e}=[0,1]
if
the segment is finite, and
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}
or
\mathbb{R}
if

\ell(e)=\infty
. The tangent space of
\operatorname{Map}(C_{\nu},X^{\nu})_{1,p}
at a
map
u:C_{\nu}\to X^{\nu}
is the space of sections

\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},u^{*}TX)=\Omega^{0}(S_{\nu},(u|_{S})^{*}TX^{\nu})\oplus\Omega^{0}(T,(u|_{T})^{*}TL).

(9.18)

As in Abouzaid
[
1
, 5.38]
the first summand in
(
9.18
) is equipped with a weighted Sobolev norm based on
the decomposition of the section into a part constant on the neck and
the difference on the neck corresponding to each edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
described as follows. Denote by

(s_{e},t_{e})\in[-\nu l_{e}/2,\nu l_{e}/2]\times S^{1}

the coordinates on the neck region created by the gluing at the node
corresponding to the edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. Let

\lambda\in(0,1)

be a
Sobolev weight
. Define a
Sobolev weight function

\kappa_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to[0,\infty),\quad\kappa_{\nu}:={\textstyle\sum}_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}\beta(\nu l_{e}/2-|s_{e}|)(\nu l_{e}/2-|s_{e}|).

(9.19)

Here, the function
\beta(\nu l_{e}/2-|s_{e}|)
is extended by zero
outside the neck region corresponding to
e
. As
\nu\to\infty
,

\kappa_{\nu}
converges to the Sobolev weight function
\kappa
defined
on the punctured curve
C-\{w_{e}:e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)\}
in
(
6.17
). Given a section

\xi=(\xi_{S},\xi_{T})\in\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},u^{*}TX)

define

\|\xi\|_{1,p,\lambda}:=\|\xi_{S}\|_{1,p,\lambda}+\|\xi_{T}\|^{p}_{1,p}\\
\|\xi_{S}\|_{1,p,\lambda}:=\left({\textstyle\sum}_{e}\|\xi_{S,e}(0,0)\|^{p}+\int_{C_{\nu}}(\|\nabla\xi_{S}\|^{p}\right.\\
\left.+\|\xi_{S}-{\textstyle\sum}_{e}\beta(\nu l_{e}/2-|s_{e}|)\mathbb{T}^{u}_{s}\xi_{S,e}(0,0)\|^{p})\exp(\kappa_{\nu}\lambda p){\operatorname{d}}\operatorname{Vol}_{C_{\nu}}\right)^{1/p}

(9.20)

where
\xi_{S,e}
is the restriction of
\xi_{S}
to the neck region

[\frac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}
corresponding to
the edge
e
, and
\mathbb{T}^{u}_{s}
is parallel transport from
u(0,t)
to

u(s,t)
along
u(s^{\prime},t),s^{\prime}\in[0,s]
. Let

\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},u^{*}TX)_{1,p,\lambda}
be the Sobolev completion of

W^{1,p}_{{\operatorname{loc}}}
sections with finite norm (
9.20
); these are
sections whose difference from a covariant-constant section on the
neck has an exponential decay behavior governed by the Sobolev
constant
\lambda
.

The target space of (
9.11
) is a space of
(0,1)
-forms, which
we equip with a weighted
L^{p}
norm. For a
(0,1)
-form

\eta\in\Omega^{0,1}(S_{\nu},u^{*}TX^{\nu})
define

\|\eta\|_{0,p,\lambda}=\left(\int_{S_{\nu}}\|\eta\|^{p}\exp(\kappa_{\nu}\lambda p){\operatorname{d}}\operatorname{Vol}_{S_{\nu}}\right)^{1/p}.

Next, we describe a
\overline{\partial}
-operator pulled back by an exponential
map on which the implicit function theorem will be applied. Pointwise
geodesic exponentiation defines a map (using Sobolev multiplication
estimates)

\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}:\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{1,p,\lambda}\to\operatorname{Map}_{1,p}(C_{\nu},X^{\nu})

(9.21)

where
\operatorname{Map}_{1,p}(C_{\nu},X^{\nu})
denotes maps of class
W_{1,p}^{{\operatorname{loc}}}

from
C_{\nu}
to
X^{\nu}
.
We define

\overline{\partial}:U_{C_{\nu}}\times\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{1,p,\lambda}\to\Omega^{0,1}(S_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{0,p}\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TL),\\
(m,\xi)\mapsto\mathbb{T}_{u_{\nu}^{pre},\xi}^{-1}\left(\overline{\partial}_{j(m),J}(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}\xi)|_{S_{\nu}},\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{e}}\frac{d}{ds}+\operatorname{grad}_{F}\right)(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}\xi)|_{T}\right),

(9.22)

where

\mathbb{T}_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}},\xi}:\Omega^{0,1}(S_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{0,p,\lambda}\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TL)_{0,p}\\
\to\Omega^{0,1}(S_{\nu},(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\xi))^{*}TX^{\nu})_{0,p,\lambda}\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\xi))^{*}TL)_{0,p}

is the parallel transport defined using an almost-complex connection,
and we recall that
U_{C_{\nu}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
is a
neighborhood of
C_{\nu}
; and

\lambda_{e}:=\begin{cases}\ell_{e}(m),\quad e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}^{(0,\infty)}(\Gamma),\\
1,\quad e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}^{\infty}(\Gamma).\end{cases}

In (
9.22
), it is necessary to scale the derivative

\frac{d}{ds}
by
\lambda_{e}
because we took
T_{e}=[0,1]
for any finite treed
segment.

In order to construct local models for moduli of adapted tree disks,
we require that the treed disks
C_{\nu}
have a collection of interior
leaves
e_{1},\hbox to10.00002pt{.\hss.\hss.},e_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}
and

(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\xi))(e_{i})\in D,\quad i=1,\hbox to10.00002pt{.\hss.\hss.},n.

Additionally, we require matching conditions at boundary nodes and
lifts of
S_{\nu}\cap T_{\nu}
. Using notation from the proof of
transversality (Theorem
6.27
), these constraints
may be incorporated into
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
from (
9.11
) to produce a
map

\mathcal{F}_{\nu}:U_{C_{\nu}}\oplus\Omega^{0}(S_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{1,p,\lambda}\oplus\Omega^{0}(T,(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TL)_{1,p}\\
\to\Omega^{0,1}(C_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{0,p,\lambda}\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,(u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}})^{*}TL)_{0,p}\oplus TX(\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}})/\Delta(\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}).

whose zeroes correspond to
adapted
pseudoholomorphic maps near
the pre-glued map
u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}
.

9.3
Error estimate

We estimate the failure of the approximate solution to be an exact
solution in the Banach norms of the previous section.
To derive the
estimate, we split the treed disk
C_{\nu}
into neck regions
corresponding to tropical nodes in
C
, namely

\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu}):=\{(s_{e},t_{e})\in[-\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}\}\subset S_{\nu},\quad\forall e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma);

and its complement

C_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}:=C_{\nu}\backslash\cup_{e}\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu}),\quad\text{and}\quad C_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}=S_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}\cup T.

The one-form
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(0)
has contributions from the difference
between the perturbation data
P(C)
and
P(C_{\nu})
on the complement
of the neck regions, and from the cutoff function and the difference
between
J_{u}
and
J_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}
on the neck regions :

\|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(0)\|_{L^{p,\lambda}(S_{\nu})}=\lVert\overline{\partial}_{J_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}}u^{\nu}_{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(S_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}+\sum_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)}\lVert\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{e}}+(\operatorname{grad}_{F})_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\rVert_{L^{p}(T_{e})}\\
+{\textstyle\sum}_{e}\|\overline{\partial}\exp_{e^{(s+it)\mathcal{T}(e)}x_{e}}(\beta(-s_{e})\zeta_{e}^{-}(s_{e}+\nu l_{e}/2,t_{e})\\
+\beta(s_{e})\zeta_{e}^{+}(s_{e}-\nu l_{e}/2,t_{e}))\|_{L^{p,\lambda}(\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu})}.

(9.23)

The first two terms may not vanish because the perturbation is domain
dependent: in the complement of the neck regions, the map
u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}

is
P(C)
-holomorphic but not
P(C_{\nu})
-holomorphic. For any metric

d_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}
on the compactified moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}

of treed disks, the distance between the domain curves is bounded as

d_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}(C_{\nu},C)\leq c\max_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}\exp(-\nu l_{e}).

Therefore, the distance between the domain-dependent perturbations has
a similar bound. On the complement of the necks
u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}
is

J(C)
-holomorphic, so

\lVert\overline{\partial}_{J_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}}u^{\nu}_{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(S_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}\leq c\lVert J(C)-J(C_{\nu})\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\max_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}\exp(-\nu l_{e}).

(9.24)

The last term in the right-hand side of (
9.23
) is equal to

{\textstyle\sum}_{e}\|(D\exp_{e^{(s+it)\mathcal{T}(e)}x_{e}}({\operatorname{d}}\beta(-s_{e})\zeta_{e}^{-}(s_{e}+\nu l_{e}/2,t_{e})+{\operatorname{d}}\beta(s_{e})\zeta_{e}^{+}(s_{e}-\nu l_{e}/2,t_{e}))+\\
(\beta(-s_{e}){\operatorname{d}}\zeta_{e}^{-}(s_{e}+\nu l_{e}/2,t_{e})+\beta(s_{e}){\operatorname{d}}\zeta_{e}^{+}(s_{e}-\nu l_{e}/2,t_{e})))^{0,1}\|_{L^{p,\lambda}(\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu}))}.

On the neck regions, the almost complex structure is
domain-independent since it is equal to the background almost complex
structure (see (
6.4
)). Holomorphicity of
u
implies that
the terms are non-zero only in the support of
d\beta_{e}
which is
contained in the interval

[-1,1]\times S^{1}\subset[-\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}=\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu}).

Both
\zeta_{e}^{\pm}
and its derivative decay at the rate of
e^{-s_{e}}

on the cylindrical end
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{1}
in
S^{\circ}
, see
(
9.8
). As a result both the difference between
J_{u}

and
J_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}
, and the terms containing
d\beta
are bounded
by
ce^{-l_{e}\nu/2}
where
c
is a constant independent of

\nu
. The Sobolev weight function (
9.19
) has a multiplicative
factor of
e^{\lambda l_{e}\nu/2}
, and therefore,

\lVert\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(0)\rVert\leq c{\textstyle\sum}_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}e^{-(1-\lambda)l_{e}\nu/2},

(9.25)

with
c
a constant independent of
\nu
. (See Abouzaid

[
1
, 5.10]
).

9.4
Uniform right inverse

In this Section, we construct a uniformly bounded right inverse for the linearized
operator of the approximate solution. Using the right inverses of the
pieces of the broken map, we construct an approximate inverse. For
large neck lengths, the error of the approximate inverse is
shown to be small enough that an actual inverse can be constructed.

First, for any element in the target space of the linearized operator of the approximate solution,
we give a nearby element in the target space of the linearized operator of the broken map.
We denote the linearized operator of the approximate solution by

D_{u^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\nu}}:=D\mathcal{F}_{\nu}([C_{\nu}],0).

Given an element

\eta=(\eta_{S},\eta_{T})\in\Omega^{0,1}(S_{\nu},(u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}|S_{\nu})^{*}TX^{\nu})_{L^{p,\lambda}}\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,(u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}|T)^{*}TL)_{L^{p}}

in the target space of
D_{u^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\nu}}
one obtains an element in the
target space of the linearized operator
D_{u^{0}}
of the broken map

\tilde{\eta}=(\eta_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}\oplus\eta_{T},\quad\eta_{v}\in\bigoplus_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)}\Omega^{0,1}(S_{v}^{\circ},(u^{0}_{v})^{*}T\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{P(v)}^{\circ})

as follows. The element
\tilde{\eta}
is equal to
\eta
in the tree
components
T\subset C
and in the complement of the neck region on
the surface components
S_{v}\subset C
. On the neck region, for an
edge
e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
,
\tilde{\eta}
is defined
by restricting
\eta|\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu})
to half the neck and extending by
zero :

\eta_{v,+}:(-\infty,0]\times S^{1}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)},\quad(s,t)\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbb{T}^{+,\nu}_{e}\eta\left(s+\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2},t\right),\quad s\geq\frac{-\nu l_{e}}{2}\\
0,\quad s<\frac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},\end{cases}

and

\eta_{v,-}:[0,\infty)\times S^{1}\to\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)},\quad(s,t)\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbb{T}^{-,\nu}_{e}\eta\left(s-\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2},t\right),\quad s\leq\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2}\\
0,\quad s>\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2},\end{cases},

where

\mathbb{T}^{\pm,\nu}_{e}:\Gamma((u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}})^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)})\to\Gamma((u_{v}^{\pm})^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)})

(9.26)

is parallel transport along the path

\exp_{e^{(s+it)\mathcal{T}(e)}x_{e}}(\rho\zeta^{\nu}_{e}(s\pm\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},t)+(1-\rho)\zeta_{e}^{\pm}(s,t)),\quad\rho\in[0,1],

\zeta_{\nu}^{e}
is defined in (
9.9
) and
\zeta_{e}^{\pm}
is
defined in (
9.7
).

We apply the inverse of the linearization of the broken map to the element constructed in the
previous paragraph.
Since the broken map
u^{0}
is regular
and isolated, its linearized operator is bijective. We recall that
the linearized operator is a map of Banach spaces (see
(
6.41
))

D_{u^{0}}:T_{m}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\times\operatorname{Map}(C,\mathfrak{X})_{1,p,\lambda}\to\Omega^{0,1}(S,(u^{0}|S)^{*}T\mathfrak{X})\\
\oplus\Omega^{1}(T,(u^{0}|T)^{*}TL)\oplus\operatorname{ev}_{\Gamma}^{*}T\mathfrak{X}/T\Delta.

Bijectivity of
D_{u^{0}}
implies there is an inverse
(m,\xi)
for the
element
(\tilde{\eta},0\in\operatorname{ev}_{\Gamma}^{*}T\mathfrak{X}/T\Delta)
. We write

\xi=((\xi_{v})_{v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)},\xi_{T})
. The vanishing of the
last term in
D_{u^{0}}(m,\xi)
means that
\xi
satisfies matching
conditions at tropical and boundary nodes, and the interior markings

z_{i}
satisfy the divisor constraint :

\xi(z_{i})\in T_{u_{\pm}(z_{i})}\mathfrak{D}.

The matching at tropical nodes implies that for any tropical edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})
, the limit of
\xi_{v_{+}}
,
\xi_{v_{-}}
at the
cylindrical end
e
is equal :

\xi_{v_{+},e}=\xi_{v_{-},e}=:\xi_{e}\in T_{x_{e}}\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)}.

We now define the approximate inverse by patching the inverse of the
linearization of the broken map. We denote the approximate inverse of

D_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}
by
Q^{\nu}
. On the complement of the neck regions in

C_{\nu}
we define

Q^{\nu}(\eta):=\xi\quad\text{on }C_{\nu}\backslash\cup_{e}\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu});

and on the neck region corresponding to a tropical edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
we patch the solutions
\xi_{v_{\pm}}

together using a cutoff function:

\displaystyle Q^{\nu}\eta

\displaystyle:=\beta\left(-s+{\tfrac{1}{4}}\nu l_{e}\right)((\mathbb{T}^{-,\nu}_{e})^{-1}\xi_{v_{-}}(s+\nu l_{e}/2)-\mathbb{T}^{\nu}_{e}\xi_{e})

(9.27)

\displaystyle+\beta\left(s+{\tfrac{1}{4}}\nu l_{e}\right)((\mathbb{T}^{+,\nu}_{e})^{-1}\xi_{v_{+}}(s-\nu l_{e}/2))-\mathbb{T}^{\nu}_{e}\xi_{e})+\mathbb{T}^{\nu}_{e}\xi_{e}

\displaystyle\in\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},(u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu})^{*}TX)_{1,p,\lambda},

where
\mathbb{T}^{\pm,\nu}_{e}
is defined in (
9.26
) and
\mathbb{T}_{e}^{\nu}

is the parallel transport from
\Gamma((u^{\operatorname{vert}}_{\nu,e})^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)})

to
\Gamma((u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}})^{*}T\mathfrak{X}_{P(e)})
.

The approximate inverse
Q^{\nu}
is uniformly bounded for all
\nu
: It
follows easily from the construction of
Q^{\nu}
that the operations

\eta\mapsto\tilde{\eta}
,
\tilde{\eta}\mapsto\xi
are uniformly
bounded operators for all
\nu
, and that on the complement of the
neck regions there is a uniform bound on the norm of the operator

\xi\mapsto(Q_{\nu}\eta)|C_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}
. On the neck
\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu})

corresponding to a tropical edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
there is
a uniform bound

\lVert((Q^{\nu}\eta)-\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e})e^{\kappa_{\nu}\lambda}\rVert_{L^{p}(\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu}))}\leq c\lVert\xi\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}}.

However, we need a
\kappa_{\nu}
-weighted Sobolev norm bound on
((Q^{\nu}\eta)-\mathbb{T}_{e}(Q^{\nu}\eta)(0,0)_{e})
, which follows from the observation

|Q_{\nu}(0,0)_{e}-\xi_{e}|\leq\lvert\xi_{v_{+}}(\tfrac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},0)_{e}-\xi_{e}\rvert+\lvert\xi_{v_{-}}(\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},0)_{e}-\xi_{e}\rvert\leq ce^{-\lambda\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}},

which implies that

\lVert(\mathbb{T}_{e}Q^{\nu}(0,0)_{e}-\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e})e^{\kappa_{\nu}\lambda}\rVert\leq c\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}}.

We have thus shown that there is a
\nu
-independent constant
c
such that

\lVert Q^{\nu}\rVert\leq c.

Next, we give an error estimate for the approximate inverse. We need
to bound the quantity
D_{u_{{\operatorname{pre}}}^{\nu}}Q^{\nu}\eta-\eta
. On the
complement of the neck regions of
C_{\nu}
(including treed segments),
this quantity is bounded by the difference in the domain-dependent
perturbation on
(C,j)
and
(C_{\nu},j^{\nu})
. Similar to
(
9.24
) in the estimate of
\mathcal{F}^{\nu}(0)
, we have the bound

\lVert D_{u_{{\operatorname{pre}}}^{\nu}}Q^{\nu}\eta-\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(C_{\nu}^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}\leq c\max_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}\exp(-\nu l_{e}).

(9.28)

We bound
\lVert D_{u_{{\operatorname{pre}}}^{\nu}}Q^{\nu}\eta-\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}}
on

\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu})
corresponding to an edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
. We drop the parallel transport
notation in the analysis, noting that both the transport map and its
derivative contribute smooth multiplicative factors that decay as

ce^{\nu l_{e}/2-|s|}
on the neck. We recall that

\operatorname{Neck}_{e}(S_{\nu})\simeq\{(s,t)\in[\frac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}\}
, and analyze the half
\{s\geq 0\}
, the analysis on the other half being similar. On
\{s\geq 0\}
, we have

\eta=\eta_{v_{+}}=D_{u^{0}}\xi_{v_{+}},\quad Q^{\nu}\eta=\xi_{v_{+}}+\beta(-s+\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{4})(\xi_{v_{-}}-\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e}),

and therefore,

D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}}Q^{\nu}\eta-\eta=D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\beta(-s+\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{4})(\xi_{v_{-}}-\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e}))+(D_{u^{0}_{v_{+}}}-D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}})\xi_{v_{+}}.

(9.29)

We have

\lVert(D_{u^{0}_{v_{+}}}-D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}})\xi_{v_{+}}\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(S_{\nu})}\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\xi_{v_{+}}\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}(S_{v_{+}}^{\circ})}\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(C_{\nu})}

(9.30)

since

d_{X^{\nu}}(u^{0}_{v_{+}}(s-\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2},t),u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}(s,t))\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}.

Since
D_{u^{0}_{v_{-}}}\xi_{v_{-}}=0
on
\{s\geq 0\}
,
by a similar bound to (
9.30
) on

(D_{u^{0}_{v_{-}}}-D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}})\xi_{v_{-}}
, we have

\lVert D_{u^{\nu}_{\operatorname{pre}}}\xi_{v_{-}}\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(\{s\geq 0\})}\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\xi_{v_{-}}\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}(S_{v_{-}}^{\circ})}\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(C_{\nu})}.

(9.31)

From (
9.29
), (
9.30
), (
9.31
) we conclude

\lVert D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}}Q^{\nu}\eta-\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}}\leq\lVert\beta(D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e}))\rVert+\lVert d\beta(\xi_{v_{-}}-\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e})\rVert+ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\eta\rVert.

(9.32)

To bound the first term in the right-hand side of (
9.32
) we observe that
\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e}
is the parallel transport of a covariant constant section on the trivial cylinder
u_{e}^{\operatorname{ver}}
, and therefore,

\lVert D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e})\rVert=\lVert(D_{u^{\nu}_{{\operatorname{pre}}}}-D_{u_{e}^{\operatorname{ver}}})(\mathbb{T}_{e}\xi_{e})\rVert\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}|\xi_{e}|\leq ce^{-\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}}.

It remains to bound the second term which is supported in the unit
interval
[\frac{\nu l_{e}}{4},\frac{\nu l_{e}}{4}+1]\times S^{1}
in the
neck
[\frac{-\nu l_{e}}{2},\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times S^{1}
. In this
interval, the Sobolev weight in the curve
S_{v_{-}}
and the glued
curve
S_{\nu}
differ by
\frac{\nu l_{e}}{2}
:

\kappa_{\nu}(s,t)=\kappa_{S_{v_{-}}^{\circ}}(s+\nu l_{e}/2)-\nu l_{e}/2,\quad\mp s\geq\nu l_{e}/2.

Therefore there is a
\nu
-independent constant
c
such that

\lVert{\operatorname{d}}\beta(s-\nu l_{e}/4)\xi_{v_{-}}\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(S_{\nu})}\leq ce^{-\lambda\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\xi_{v_{-}}\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}(S_{v}^{\circ})}\leq ce^{-\lambda\nu l_{e}/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}(S_{\nu})}.

(9.33)

From (
9.28
), (
9.33
), one obtains an estimate as in Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono

[
36
, 7.1.32]
, Abouzaid
[
1
, Lemma 5.13]
: For some
constant
c>0
, for any
\nu

\|D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}}Q^{\nu}-\operatorname{Id}\|<c\min_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)}(\exp(-\lambda\nu l_{e}/2),\exp(-(1-\lambda)\nu l_{e}/2)).

(9.34)

It follows that for
\nu
sufficiently large an actual inverse may be
obtained from the Taylor series formula

D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}}^{-1}=Q^{\nu}(D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}}Q^{\nu})^{-1}=Q^{\nu}\sum_{k\geq 0}(I-Q^{\nu}D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}})^{k}.

The approximate inverse
Q^{\nu}
is uniformly bounded for all

\nu
. For large enough
\nu
, (
9.34
) implies that

\lVert D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}}Q^{\nu}-\operatorname{Id}\rVert\leq{\tfrac{1}{2}}
, and so,

\lVert D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}}^{-1}\rVert\leq 2\lVert Q^{\nu}\rVert\leq c.

(9.35)

9.5
Uniform quadratic estimate

We obtain a uniform quadratic estimate for the non-linear terms in the
map cutting out the moduli space locally. We will prove that there
exists a constant
c
such that for all
\nu

\lVert D_{(m_{1},\xi_{1})}\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(m_{2},\xi_{2})-D_{u^{{\operatorname{pre}}}_{\nu}}(m_{2},\xi_{2})\rVert\leq c\lVert(m_{1},\xi_{1})\rVert_{1,p,\lambda}\lVert(m_{2},\xi_{2})\rVert_{1,p,\lambda}.

(9.36)

We prove the quadratic estimate for the
\overline{\partial}
term on surface components. The other terms in the operator
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
are left to the reader as the proof is similar.

As a preliminary step we prove a quadratic estimate on a simpler operator.
Define

\mathcal{G}:W^{1,p,\lambda}(\Omega^{0}(S_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}})^{*}TX^{\nu}))\to L^{p,\lambda}(\Omega^{1}(S_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}})^{*}TX^{\nu})),\\
\xi\mapsto\mathbb{T}_{\xi}^{-1}d(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\xi).

Claim 9.4
.

There is a
\nu
-independent constant
c
such that

\lVert D_{\xi_{1}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})-D_{0}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})\rVert_{0,p,\lambda}\leq c\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert_{1,p,\lambda}\lVert\xi_{2}\rVert_{1,p,\lambda}

if
\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert_{1,p,\lambda}
is small enough.

Proof of Claim.

For
x\in X^{\nu}
,
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in T_{x}X^{\nu}
, define

\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{-\xi_{1}}^{x}:T_{x}X\to T_{x}X,\quad\xi_{2}\mapsto\mathbb{T}_{\xi_{1}}^{-1}\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}\exp_{x}(\xi_{1}+\tau\xi_{2})|_{\tau=0}.

It extends to a map on sections. For a map
u:S_{\nu}\to X^{\nu}
, and

\xi_{1}\in\Gamma(S_{\nu},u^{*}TX^{\nu})
define

\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{-\xi_{1}}^{u}:\Gamma(S_{\nu},u^{*}TX^{\nu})\to\Gamma(S_{\nu},u^{*}TX^{\nu}),\quad\xi_{2}\mapsto(z\mapsto\overline{\mathbb{T}}_{-\xi_{1}(z)}^{u(z)}(\xi_{2}(z))).

We have

\displaystyle D_{\xi_{1}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})-D_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})=\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}\mathbb{T}_{\xi_{1}+\tau\xi_{2}}^{-1}{\operatorname{d}}_{z}(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}(\xi_{1}+\tau\xi_{2}))-\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}\mathbb{T}_{\tau\xi_{2}}^{-1}{\operatorname{d}}_{z}(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}(\tau\xi_{2}))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{z}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}_{-\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}-\xi_{2}),

(9.37)

where in the second line
\nabla_{z}
is differentiation along the
domain curve
S_{\nu}
. In the second equality we switch the order of
differentiation between
\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}
and
{\operatorname{d}}_{z}
. For any

z\in S_{\nu}
we have a uniform pointwise estimate

\displaystyle\lvert D_{\xi_{1}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})-D_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\nabla_{z}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{u(z)}_{-\xi_{1}(z)}-\operatorname{Id})\rvert\cdot\lvert\xi_{2}(z)\rvert+\lvert\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}(z)}_{-\xi_{1}(z)}-\operatorname{Id}\rvert\cdot\lvert(\nabla_{z}\xi_{2})(z)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq c((\lvert du_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}(z)\rvert\cdot\lvert\xi_{1}(z)\rvert+\lvert\nabla_{z}\xi_{1}(z)\rvert)\cdot\lvert\xi_{2}(z)\rvert+

\displaystyle\quad\lvert\xi_{1}(z)\rvert\cdot\lvert\nabla_{z}\xi_{2}(z)\rvert)

where the constant
c
is
\nu
-independent. Indeed, such a uniform
constant exists because the complement of the neck regions is
compact and identical for all
\nu
; and the neck regions have a
cylindrical metric, and only the lengths of the cylinders vary with

\nu
. Then,

\lVert D_{\xi_{1}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})-D_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\mathcal{G}(\xi_{2})\rVert\leq c(\lVert{\operatorname{d}}u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\cdot\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\cdot\lVert\xi_{2}\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}}+\lVert\nabla_{z}\xi_{1}(z)\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}}\cdot\lVert\xi_{2}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\\
+\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\cdot\lVert\nabla_{z}\xi_{2}(z)\rVert_{L^{p,\lambda}})\leq c\lVert\xi_{1}\rVert_{1,p,\lambda}\lVert\xi_{2}\rVert_{1,p,\lambda},

where for the last inequality, we use the fact that

\lVert{\operatorname{d}}u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
is uniformly bounded for all
\nu
by
construction, and the following bound from Sobolev embedding : For any
section
\xi\in W^{1,p,\lambda}(S_{\nu})
there is a
\nu
-independent
constant
c
such that

\lVert\xi\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq c\lVert\xi\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}}
. This proves the
Claim.
∎

We obtain the quadratic estimate (
9.36
) for
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
by
adapting the proof of the above claim. We note that, compared to
\mathcal{G}
,
the operator
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
additionally consists of a projection to

(0,1)
-forms :

\mathcal{F}_{\nu}:(m,\xi)\mapsto\mathbb{T}_{\xi}^{-1}\pi^{0,1}_{j(m),J(\xi)}{\operatorname{d}}(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\xi),

where we abbreviate
J_{\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\xi}
as
J(\xi)
. The
analog of (
9.5
) is

D_{(m_{1},\xi_{1})}\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(\xi_{2})-D_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(\xi_{2})\\
=\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}\mathbb{T}_{\xi_{1}+\tau\xi_{2}}^{-1}\pi^{0,1}_{j_{\nu}(m_{1}+\tau m_{2}),J(\xi_{1}+\tau\xi_{2})}{\operatorname{d}}_{z}(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}(\xi_{1}+\tau\xi_{2}))|_{\tau=0}\\
-\tfrac{{\operatorname{d}}}{{\operatorname{d}}\tau}\mathbb{T}_{\tau\xi_{2}}^{-1}\pi^{0,1}_{j_{\nu}(\tau m_{2}),J(\tau\xi_{2})}{\operatorname{d}}_{z}(\exp_{u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}}(\tau\xi_{2}))|_{\tau=0}\\
=\pi^{0,1}_{j_{\nu}(m_{1}),J(\xi_{1})}\nabla_{z}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{u}_{-\xi_{1}}\xi_{2})+(\nabla_{z}(\pi^{0,1}_{j_{\nu}(m_{1}),J(\xi_{1})}))(\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{u}_{-\xi_{1}}\xi_{2})\\
-\pi^{0,1}_{j(C_{\nu}),J(0)}(\nabla_{z}\xi_{2})-(\nabla_{z}\pi^{0,1}_{j(C_{\nu}),J(0)})\xi_{2},

(9.38)

where
\tau m_{2}
resp.
m_{1}+\tau m_{2}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
is a path
in a neighborhood
U_{C_{\nu}}
of
C_{\nu}
in the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{glue}}
. The estimate (
9.36
) can be obtained from
the above expression in a similar way to the proof of Claim

9.4
. We point out that the metric
g_{C_{\nu}}
on
U_{C_{\nu}}

is cylindrical on the non-compact ends of
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{glue}}
, and with
respect to this metric

\lVert j_{\nu}(m_{1})-j_{\nu}(m_{2})\rVert_{C^{1}}\leq cd_{g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}}(m_{1},m_{2})
, see (
9.17
).

9.6
Picard iteration

We apply the implicit function theorem to obtain an exact solution for
a glued holomorphic map. We recall the following version
of the Picard Lemma
[
55
, Proposition A.3.4]
.

Lemma 9.5
.

(Picard Lemma)
Let
X
and
Y
be Banach spaces,
U\subset X
be an open set
containing
0
, and
f:U\to Y
be a smooth map. Suppose
df(0)
is
invertible with inverse
Q:Y\to X
. Suppose
c
and
\epsilon>0
are
constants such that
\lVert Q\rVert\leq c
,
B_{\epsilon}\subset U
, and

\lVert df(x)-df(0)\rVert\leq\tfrac{1}{2c}\quad\forall x\in B_{\epsilon}(0).

Suppose
f(0)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4c}
. Then, there is a unique point

x_{0}\in B_{\epsilon}
satisfying
f(x_{0})=0
.

Picard’s Lemma and the estimates (
9.25
), (
9.35
),
(
9.36
) imply the existence of a solution
(m(\nu),\xi(\nu))

to the equation

\mathcal{F}^{\nu}(m(\nu),\xi(\nu))=0

for each
\nu
. The map

u_{\nu}:=\exp_{u_{\nu}^{{\operatorname{pre}}}}(\xi(\nu))

is a
(j(m(\nu)),J^{\nu})
-holomorphic map to
X^{\nu}
. Additionally,
there is a
\nu
-independent constant
\epsilon>0
such that

(m(\nu),\xi(\nu))
is the unique zero of
\mathcal{F}^{\nu}
in an

\epsilon
-neighbourhood of
((C_{\nu},j^{\nu}),u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}})
with respect to
the
g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
-norm on
m(\nu)
and the weighted Sobolev norm

W^{1,p,\lambda}
on
\xi(\nu)
.

9.7
Surjectivity of gluing

We show that the gluing construction gives a bijection between rigid
maps in neck-stretched manifolds and rigid broken maps. Note that any
family
\{u^{\prime}_{\nu}:C_{\nu}^{\prime}\to X^{\nu}\}_{\nu}
converges to a broken map

u:C\to\mathfrak{X}
by Theorem
8.2
. To prove the
bijection we must show that any such family of maps is in the image of
the gluing construction. Since the implicit function theorem used to
construct the gluing gives a unique solution in a neighbourhood, it
suffices to show that the maps
u^{\prime}_{\nu}
are close, in the Sobolev norm
used for the gluing construction, to the approximate solution

u^{\operatorname{pre}}_{\nu}
defined by (
9.9
).

We first show that the domain curves of the converging sequence of
maps are close enough to the domains of the approximate solution with
respect to the cylindrical metric
g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
from
(
9.12
). In the definition of Gromov convergence, the
convergence of domains implies that
C_{\nu}^{\prime}\to C
in the compactified
moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
, which implies

\pi_{\Gamma}(C_{\nu}^{\prime})\to C\quad\text{in $\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}$.}

(9.39)

We additionally need to prove that the distance
