d_{g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}}(C_{\nu}^{\prime},C_{\nu})\to 0
where the metric

g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
is cylindrical in the non-compact ends of

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
.
By assumption the limit map
u
does not have any tropical
symmetry. Therefore, the translation sequence
t_{\nu}
is uniquely
determined by the tropical graph of
u
and coincides with the
translations used for gluing. By
(Thin
cylinder convergence)
, for any tropical edge

e=(v_{+},v_{-})\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\operatorname{trop}}(\Gamma)
, the gluing parameters

l_{\nu}^{\prime}(e)+i\theta_{\nu}^{\prime}(e)
of the curves
C_{\nu}^{\prime}
satisfy

\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\theta_{\nu}^{\prime}(e)=0,\quad\lim_{\nu\to\infty}(t_{\nu}(v_{+})-t_{\nu}(v_{-})-\mathcal{T}(e)l_{\nu}^{\prime}(e))=0

The gluing parameter of
C_{\nu}
at the edge
e
is
\nu l_{e}
, which satisfies
the relation

t_{\nu}(v_{+})-t_{\nu}(v_{-})=\mathcal{T}(e)\nu l_{e}.

Therefore,

l_{\nu}^{\prime}(e)-\nu l_{e}\to 0
, and
d_{g_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}}(C_{\nu},C_{\nu}^{\prime})\to 0
as

\nu\to\infty
. In addition there is a biholomorphism

\phi_{\nu}:(C_{\nu},j_{\nu}([C_{\nu}^{\prime}]))\to C_{\nu}^{\prime},

(9.40)

where
j_{\nu}
is defined in (
9.14
).

Next, we show that the maps in the converging sequence are close
enough to the approximate solutions. Via the identification
(
9.40
), we view
u_{\nu}^{\prime}
as a map on
C_{\nu}
.
We need to
bound the section
\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{0}(C_{\nu},(u_{\nu}^{pre})^{*}TX^{\nu})

defined by the equation
u_{\nu}^{\prime}=\exp_{u_{\nu}^{pre}}\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}
in the
weighted Sobolev norm (
9.20
). Consider the neck region in
C_{\nu}

corresponding to an edge
e
with coordinates

(s_{e},t_{e})\in[-\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2},\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}]\times\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

Denote the midpoint of the neck as

0_{e}:=\{(s_{e},t_{e})=(0,0)\}\in C_{\nu}.

In the neck region, the maps
u_{\nu}^{pre}
and
u_{\nu}^{\prime}
are equal to a
vertical cylinder perturbed by a quantity that decays exponentially in
the middle of the cylinder. The vertical cylinder is determined by

u_{\nu}^{pre}(0_{e})
resp.
u_{\nu}^{\prime}(0_{e})
. The sequence

u^{pre}_{\nu}(0_{e})
converges to
x_{e}
because of the asymptotic decay
of the sections
\zeta^{\pm}
. The sequence
u_{\nu}^{\prime}(0_{e})
converges to

x_{e}
by
(Thin cylinder convergence)
. Indeed, since the complex
structure
\Delta j^{\nu}(C_{\nu}^{\prime})
is standard on a truncation

[-\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}-L,\tfrac{\nu l_{e}}{2}-L]\times S^{1}
of the
neck (see (
9.15
)), and the mid point
of the cylinder is preserved by the biholomorphism
\phi
in
(
9.40
),
(Thin cylinder convergence)
is applicable with the coordinates

(s_{e},t_{e})
. On the neck region, the section
\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}
and its
derivatives decay exponentially :

|D^{k}\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}(s_{e},t_{e})|\leq ce^{-(\nu l_{e}/2-|s_{e}|)},\quad k\in\{0,1\}.

This inequality follows from the decay of the terms
\zeta_{\nu}^{\pm}
in the
definition of
u_{\nu}^{pre}
, and the breaking annulus lemma applied to

u_{\nu}^{\prime}
. Consequently
\lVert\xi_{\nu}^{\prime}\rVert_{W^{1,p,\lambda}}
can be made
small enough by taking a large
\nu
and shrinking the neck by a fixed
amount: that is, we decrease the cylinder length to
\nu l_{e}-C
where
C
is a constant independent of
\nu
. Next we consider the complement of the neck regions. Here,
the sequences
u_{\nu}^{\prime}
and
u_{\nu}^{pre}
uniformly converge to

u
. So, by taking
\nu
large enough the maps

u_{\nu}^{pre}
and
u_{\nu}^{\prime}
are
W^{1,p}
-close enough in the
complement of the neck regions.
This finishes the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Remark 9.6
.

(Gluing preserves orientations)

From the definition of orientation of moduli spaces in Remark

6.28
it is easily seen that gluing preserves
orientation in the special case that the Cauchy-Riemann operator on
each surface component of
the broken map
u^{0}
is complex
linear. It remains to consider the effect of the interpolation from the
linearized operator
D_{u^{0}}
resp.
D_{u_{\nu}}
to a complex linear
operator
\overline{\partial}_{u^{0}}
resp.
\overline{\partial}_{u_{\nu}}
. This interpolation
can be accomplished by an interpolation on the Cauchy-Riemann operator one ach component
u|_{S_{v}}
to a complex-linear operator; the interpolations can be then be glued to obtain a interpolation of
D_{u_{\nu}}
to a complex linear operator. It follows that the gluing sign is the same as in the complex-linear case, which is to say, positive.

9.8
Tubular neighbourhoods and true boundary

In Section
8.6
we proved a convergence result that the
boundaries of one-dimensional moduli spaces of rigid broken maps
consist of maps that either have a length zero boundary edge, or a
broken boundary edge. Theorem
9.7
is the converse
gluing result, and implies
that given a broken map
u
containing a
boundary edge that is broken or has zero length, the map
u
indeed
occurs as the codimension one boundary of the expected moduli spaces.
The convergence and gluing results, together, allow us to identify the
true boundary of one-dimensional moduli spaces. A stratum containing
a zero length edge is actually a
fake boundary stratum
as it is
the boundary of two different rigid strata with opposite induced
boundary orientations. Thus, the
true boundary strata
are those
that have a broken boundary edge, see Remark
9.8
.

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
be a coherent regular perturbation
datum for all types
\Gamma
. Suppose
\Gamma
is a type of broken
treed disks and
\underline{x}\in\mathcal{I}(L)^{n+1}
is a set of limits for
boundary leaves such that
i(\Gamma,\underline{x})=1
.

(a)

(Tubular neighbourhoods) If a type

\Gamma_{{\operatorname{glue}}}
of broken maps is obtained from a type
\Gamma

by collapsing an edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma^{\prime})
with

\ell(e)=0
, or by making an edge length finite/non-zero, then the
stratum
{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{x})
is a codimension one
boundary of in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}(L,\underline{x})
and has a
tubular neighborhood in it; and

(b)

(Orientations) the orientations are
compatible with the morphisms
(Cutting edges)
and
(
Collapsing an edge
/
Making an edge finite/non-zero
) in the
following sense:

(i)

Suppose
\Gamma
,
\Gamma_{0}
,
\Gamma_{c}

are types of broken maps related by the morphisms

\Gamma\xrightarrow{\text{Make $\ell(e)$ zero}}\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{\text{Collapse $e$}}\Gamma_{c},

and
i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})=i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma_{c},\underline{x})=1
,

i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma_{0},\underline{x})=0
. Then, the boundary orientation
induced by
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})
on

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})
is the opposite of the boundary
orientation induced by
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{c}}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})
on

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})
.

(ii)

Suppose
\Gamma_{f}
,
\Gamma
,

\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
are types of broken maps and

\underline{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{I}(L)^{d_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma_{1})+1}
,

\underline{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{I}(L)^{d_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma_{2})+1}
are labels such that
there are morphisms

(\Gamma_{f},\underline{x})\xleftarrow{\text{Make $\ell(e)$ finite}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})\xrightarrow{\text{Cut $e$}}(\Gamma_{1},\underline{x}_{1})\times(\Gamma_{2},\underline{x}_{2}),

and
i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma_{f},\underline{x})=1
,

i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})=i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma_{1},\underline{x}_{1})=i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma_{2},\underline{x}_{2})=0
. Then, there is an isomorphism

\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{x})\simeq\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma_{1}}(L,\underline{x}_{1})\times\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma_{1}}(L,\underline{x}_{2}),

and the boundary orientation on
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})

induced by
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{f}}^{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{x})
is related to the
product orientation by a sign
(-1)^{\circ}
that depends only on
the domain type
\Gamma
and the labels
\underline{x}
.

Outline of proof.

The tubular neighborhood is constructed by gluing
and there are different cases depending on the morphism:

Case 1
:
Collapsing a boundary edge
e
of zero length
.

The proof is by gluing a boundary node, which is non-tropical since
the Lagrangian is disjoint from relative divisors. The gluing proof
is on the same lines as the gluing of tropical nodes in Theorem

9.1
, so we point out the differences, referring the
reader to
[
36
, Chapter 7.1]
for the full proof.

—

Firstly, we choose strip-like coordinates on a neighborhood of
the boundary node
w_{e}
and we use weighted Sobolev spaces on
these strips, see Definition 7.1.3 in
[
36
]
. A difference
from our Fredholm set-up for broken maps in Section

6.3
is that we do not need cylindrical
coordinates in the target space since there are no relative
divisors.

—

The next difference is that the map gluing parameter
\nu

refers to the neck length parameter in the glued curve (see Remark

9.3
), and therefore in the domain of the operator

\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
in (
9.11
) the component
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}}
is
replaced by a subspace where the neck length corresponding to the
node
w_{e}
is fixed to be
\nu
. The proof of ‘surjectivity of
gluing’ also simplifies, since it is enough to show convergence of
the domain curves away from the neck as in (
9.39
) and
the arguments following (
9.39
) in that paragraph are
not necessary.

Cases 2 and 3 do not involve gluing of surface components.

Case 2
:
Making a boundary edge length
\ell(e)
non-zero.

The proof structure of Theorem
9.1
can be used in this
case also. However, the map gluing parameter
\nu
corresponds to
the edge length
\ell(e)
in the glued curve and goes to zero in the
limit. The domain curve for the glued map
u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}
is
allowed to vary among the set of curves of type
\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}
with

\ell(e)=\nu
. The approximate solution
u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}:C_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}

is defined to be equal to
u
, and the map
u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}
is defined
to be constant on the treed segment
T_{e}
, so the error

\lVert D\mathcal{F}_{\nu}(u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}})\rVert
is
\leq c\nu
. The rest of the steps
are analogous to the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Case 3
:
Making a boundary edge length
\ell(e)
finite.

Similar to Case 2, the map gluing parameter
\nu
is equal to the
length
\ell(e)
of the edge
e
, but unlike Case 2, it goes to
infinity in the limit. The domain curve for the glued map

u_{\nu}:C_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}
is allowed to vary among the set

\Gamma_{\operatorname{glue}}
curves with
\ell(e)=\nu
. The approximate solution

u_{\nu}^{\operatorname{pre}}:C_{\nu}\to\mathfrak{X}
is defined to be equal to
u
on all
components except the treed segment
T_{e}
, where it is defined by
pre-gluing the Morse trajectory (see (2.66) in Schwarz

[
73
]
) at the edge
e
in the map
u
. The norm of the error is bounded by
ce^{-\nu}
and is derived in a similar way to Section
9.3
. The rest of the steps are analogous to
the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

For the orientations result, part (
bi
) follows from
the definition of orientations in Remark
6.28
and the
sign computation for part (
bii
) is carried out in
Seidel’s book
[
76
, (12.25)]
.
∎

Boundary components
Fake boundary,
[formula]
True Boundary,
[formula]

Abbildung 9.1:
True and fake boundary strata of a one-dimensional
component of the moduli space of treed holomorphic disks. The
sphere components lie in different pieces of the tropical
manifold.

Remark 9.8
.

(True and fake boundary strata)

There are two types of strata that occur as the codimension one
boundary of a one-dimensional moduli space – one with a boundary
edge of length zero, and the second with a boundary edge containing
a breaking. The first is a
fake
boundary, and the second one
is a
true
boundary as we explain: If a type
\Gamma_{0}
of
broken maps has a boundary edge of length zero, we may make the edge
length
\ell(e)
non-zero or we may collapse the edge (by disk
gluing) to produce the following types

\Gamma\xrightarrow{\text{Make $\ell(e)$ zero}}\Gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{\text{Collapse $e$}}\Gamma_{c}.

Let
\underline{x}
be a tuple of input and output such that

i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})=0
. By Theorem
9.7
, the moduli
space
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma_{0}}(\underline{x})
is the boundary of the
one-dimensional moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma_{c}}(\underline{x})
and

\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma}(\underline{x})
with opposite orientations. So,

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{0}}^{\operatorname{brok}}(\underline{x})
does not represent a component in the
topological boundary of the compactified moduli space

\bigcup_{\Gamma:\Gamma\text{ is rigid, }i^{\operatorname{brok}}(\Gamma,\underline{x})=1}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{x}).

This is the fake boundary in Figure
9.1
. The only
(true) boundary components of one-dimensional strata thus consist of
maps with a single broken Morse trajectory; see Figure

9.1
.

Kapitel 10
Broken Fukaya algebras

In this Chapter we describe
A_{\infty}
algebra structures defined by
counting treed holomorphic disks on broken and unbroken manifolds, and
show that they are equivalent up to
A_{\infty}
homotopy.

10.1
A_{\infty}
algebras

The set of treed holomorphic disks has the structure of an
A_{\infty}
algebra.
A_{\infty}
algebras were introduced by Stasheff
[
80
]
in
order to capture algebraic structures on the space of cochains on loop
spaces. We follow the sign convention in Seidel
[
75
]
. A

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
A_{\infty}
algebra
consists of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded vector
space
A
together with for each
d\geq 0
a multilinear degree zero

composition map

m^{d}:\ A^{\otimes d}\to A[2-d]

satisfying the
A_{\infty}
associativity equations

[
75
, (2.1)]

0=\sum_{j,k\geq 0,j+k\leq d}(-1)^{j+\sum_{i=1}^{j}|a_{i}|}m^{d-k+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{j},\\
m^{k}(a_{j+1},\ldots,a_{j+k}),a_{j+k+1},\ldots,a_{d})

(10.1)

for any
d\geq 0
and any tuple of homogeneous elements

a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}
with degrees
|a_{1}|,\ldots,|a_{d}|\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The
notation
[2-d]
indicates a degree shift of
2-d
, so that the degree
of
m^{d}(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})
is

\sum_{i}|a_{i}|+2-d\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The signs appearing in
(
10.1
) are the
shifted Koszul signs
, that is, the
Koszul signs for the shifted grading in which the structure maps have
degree one as in Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
46
]
. The first of
these associativity relations is
m^{1}(m^{0}(1))=0
, and the second one
is

m^{2}(m^{0}(1),a)-(-1)^{|a|}m^{2}(a,m^{0}(1))+m^{1}(m^{1}(a))=0,\quad\forall a\in A

(10.2)

which may be intepreted as saying that
m^{1}
is a differential if

m^{0}(1)
vanishes. A
strict unit
for
A
is an element

1_{A}\in A
such that

m^{2}(1_{A},a)=a=(-1)^{|a|}m^{2}(a,1_{A}),\quad m^{d}(\ldots,1_{A},\ldots)=0,\forall d\neq 2.

(10.3)

A
strictly unital

A_{\infty}
algebra
is an
A_{\infty}
algebra that admits a strict unit.

The element
m^{0}(1)\in A
(where
1\in\mathbb{R}
is the unit) is called
the
curvature
of the algebra. The
A_{\infty}
algebra
A
is

flat
if the curvature vanishes. The
cohomology
of a flat

A_{\infty}
algebra
A
is defined by

H(m^{1})=\frac{\operatorname{ker}(m^{1})}{\operatorname{im}(m^{1})}.

The algebra structure on
H(m^{1})
is given by

[a_{1}a_{2}]=(-1)^{|a_{1}|}[m^{2}(a_{1},a_{2})].

(10.4)

The
A_{\infty}
algebras in our work are
curved
, that is
m^{0}(1)

typically does not vanish.

The coefficient ring of our

A_{\infty}
algebras are Novikov rings defined as follows.
Let
q
be a formal variable and
\Lambda
the
universal Novikov
field
of formal sums

\Lambda=\Set{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}c_{i}q^{\alpha_{i}}}{\quad c_{i}\in\mathbb{C},\ \alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\alpha_{i}\to\infty}.

The subring

\Lambda_{\geq 0}:=\Set{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}c_{i}q^{\alpha_{i}}\in\Lambda}{\alpha_{i}\geq 0}

with only the non-negative exponents is called the
Novikov ring
.

Denote by

\Lambda_{>0}
the subring with only positive exponents,
and by

\Lambda^{\times}=(\mathbb{C}-\{0\})+\Lambda_{>0}\subset\Lambda_{\geq 0}

the subgroup of formal power series with invertible leading
coefficient and non-negative exponents.
The Novikov ring has natural
adic topology, in which a sequence of elements converges iff it
converges in the quotient by any subring
q^{E}\Lambda_{\geq 0}
for any

E\in\mathbb{R}
.

Cohomology can be defined for a strictly
unital
A_{\infty}
algebra if the curvature is a multiple of the unit :

m^{0}(1)\in\Lambda_{\geq 0}1_{A}
, because in this case,
(m^{1})^{2}=0
by
(
10.2
).
More generally, the cohomology exists for any solution to the
projective Maurer-Cartan equation
[
36
]
. The
projective Maurer-Cartan equation for
b\in A
is

m^{0}(1)+m^{1}(b)+m^{2}(b,b)+\ldots\in\Lambda_{\geq 0}1_{A}.

(10.5)

A solution
b\in A
of odd degree to the equation (
10.5
) is
called a
weakly bounding cochain
and the set of all the odd
solutions is denoted
MC(A)
. Given a weakly bounding cochain

b\in MC(A)
, the

deformed composition map
is defined by

m^{n}_{b}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n+1}}m^{n+i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n+1}}(\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{i_{1}},a_{1},\\
\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{i_{2}},a_{2},b,\ldots,b,a_{n},\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{i_{n+1}})

(10.6)

over all possible combinations of insertions of the element

b\in A^{+}
between (and before and after) the elements

a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
. The maps
m^{n}_{b}
define an
A_{\infty}
structure on
A

if
b
has odd degree. By the weakly bounding cochain condition

m^{0}_{b}(1)\in\Lambda_{\geq 0}1_{A}
, and the
A_{\infty}
relations imply

(m^{1}_{b})^{2}(a)=m^{2}_{b}(m^{0}_{b}(1),a)-m^{2}_{b}(a,m^{0}_{b}(1))=0.

Consequently, the cohomology

H(m^{1}_{b})=\operatorname{ker}(m^{1}_{b})/\operatorname{im}(m^{1}_{b})

is well-defined. The function

W:MC(A)\to\Lambda_{\geq 0},\quad b\mapsto W(b),

(10.7)

where
m^{0}_{b}(1)=W(b)1_{A}
is called the
potential
of
the
A_{\infty}
algebra
A
.

An
A_{\infty}
algebra
A

with coefficients in
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
is
convergent
if

m^{0}(1)\in\Lambda_{>0}
. This property ensures that the expression

m^{0}_{b}
in the Maurer-Cartan equation (
10.5
) is well-defined
for any
b\in A
with positive
q
-valuation.

One also has homotopy notions of algebra morphisms. Let
A_{0},A_{1}
be
A_{\infty}
algebras. An
A_{\infty}
morphism

\mathcal{F}
from
A_{0}
to
A_{1}
consists of a sequence of linear
maps

\mathcal{F}^{d}:\ A_{0}^{\otimes d}\to A_{1}[1-d],\quad d\geq 0

such that the following holds:

\sum_{i+j\leq d}(-1)^{i+\sum_{j=1}^{i}|a_{j}|}\mathcal{F}^{d-j+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},m_{A_{0}}^{j}(a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{i+j}),a_{i+j+1},\ldots,a_{d})=\\
\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{m}=d}m_{A_{1}}^{m}(\mathcal{F}^{i_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\mathcal{F}^{i_{m}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{m-1}+1},\ldots,a_{d}))

(10.8)

where the first sum is over integers
i,j
with
i+j\leq d
, the
second is over partitions
d=i_{1}+\ldots+i_{m}
. The first
relation in the family of relations in (
10.8
) is

\mathcal{F}^{1}(m_{A_{0}}^{0})=m_{A_{1}}^{0}+m_{A_{1}}^{1}(\mathcal{F}^{0})+m_{A_{1}}^{2}(\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0})+\dots

An
A_{\infty}
morphism
\mathcal{F}
is

unital
if and only if

\mathcal{F}^{1}(1_{A_{0}})=1_{A_{1}},\quad\mathcal{F}^{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},1_{A_{0}},a_{i+2},\ldots,a_{k})=0

(10.9)

for every
k\geq 2
and every
0\leq i\leq k-1
, where
1_{A_{0}}

resp.
1_{A_{1}}
is the strict unit in
A_{0}
resp.
A_{1}
. The

composition
of
A_{\infty}
morphisms
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}
is defined by

(\mathcal{F}_{0}\circ\mathcal{F}_{1})^{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})=\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{m}=d}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m}(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}}),\\
\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{m}}(a_{d-i_{m}+1},\ldots,a_{d})),\quad d\geq 0.

(10.10)

There is also a natural notion
A_{\infty}
homotopy equivalence of
morphisms. Homotopies are defined as pre-natural transformations of

A_{\infty}
morphisms, which we define following Seidel
[
76
]
and
Charest-Woodward
[
17
]
: Given
A_{\infty}
algebras
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
,
the set of morphisms is an
A_{\infty}
category denoted by
\mathcal{Q}
whose
objects are
A_{\infty}
morphisms
\mathcal{F}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
, and whose morphisms
are
pre-natural transformations
. For
A_{\infty}
morphisms

\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
, a pre-natural transformation

\mathcal{T}=(\mathcal{T}^{d})_{d\geq 0}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
is a family of maps

\mathcal{T}^{d}:A_{0}^{\otimes d}\to A_{1}.

Pre-natural transformations are equipped with composition maps

m_{\mathcal{Q}}^{e}
for all
e\geq 1
defined as follows: For any
d\geq 0
,

(m^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{T})^{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})=\sum_{k,l}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}(-1)^{\dagger}m^{l}_{A_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}}),\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{2}}(a_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}+i_{2}}),\ldots,\\
\mathcal{T}^{i_{k}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k-1}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}}),\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{k+1}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}+1},\ldots,),\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{l}}(a_{d-i_{l}},\ldots,a_{d}))\\
-\sum_{i,e}(-1)^{i+\sum_{j=1}^{i}|a_{j}|+|\mathcal{T}|-1}\mathcal{T}^{d-e+1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},m^{e}_{A_{1}}(a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{i+e}),a_{i+e+1},\ldots,a_{d}),

(10.11)

where the sign
\dagger
is from
[
17
, p82]
. For
A_{\infty}
morphisms
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
, and pre-natural
transformations
\mathcal{T}_{0}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
,

\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
, the binary composition

m^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1})\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{2})
is defined as

(m^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2}))^{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})=\sum_{m,k,l}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}(-1)^{\ddagger}m^{l}_{A_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{k-1}}(\ldots),\\
\mathcal{T}_{1}^{i_{k}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k-1}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}}),\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{k+1}}(\ldots),\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{l-1}}(\ldots),\\
\mathcal{T}_{2}^{i_{l}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{l-1}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{l}}),\mathcal{F}_{2}^{i_{l+1}}(\ldots),\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{2}^{i_{l}}(a_{d-i_{l}},\ldots,a_{d})),

(10.12)

where the sign
{\ddagger}
is from
[
17
, p82]
. The higher order
composition maps
m^{e}_{\mathcal{Q}}
are defined analogously.

Finally, we define a homotopy between
A_{\infty}
morphisms. A pre-natural
transformation
\mathcal{T}
between
A_{\infty}
morphisms

\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
is a
homotopy
if

\mathcal{F}_{1}-\mathcal{F}_{0}=m^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{T}).

(10.13)

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{F}_{1}=(\mathcal{F}_{1,d})_{d\geq 0}
is only a collection of
maps from
A_{0}^{\otimes d}
to
A_{1}[1-d]
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}
satisfies the

A_{\infty}
morphism axiom then so does
\mathcal{F}_{1}
. Homotopy is an
equivalence relation in
\mathcal{Q}
, with transitivity seen as follows: If

\mathcal{T}_{0}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
and
\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
are
homotopies, then,

\mathcal{T}_{1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}_{0}+\mathcal{T}_{1}+m^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1})\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{2})

(10.14)

is a homotopy, see
[
76
, p16]
.
The
A_{\infty}
algebras
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
are
homotopy equivalent
if there are
A_{\infty}
morphisms
\mathcal{F}_{0}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1}:A_{0}\to A_{1}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{0}\circ\mathcal{F}_{1}:A_{1}\to A_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}\circ F_{0}:A_{0}\to A_{0}
are both homotopic to the identity
A_{\infty}
morphism.

Homotopies are preserved by compositions with
A_{\infty}
morphisms. Given
A_{\infty}
morphisms

A_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}_{0}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}_{3}}A_{3},

and a pre-natural transformation
\mathcal{T}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
, left and right compositions
as in
[
76
, p11]

give pre-natural transformations. That is,
L_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}\mathcal{T}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{3}\circ\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{3}\circ F_{2})

is defined as

(L_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}\mathcal{T})^{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})=\sum_{k,l}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}(-1)^{\dagger}\mathcal{F}_{3}^{l}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}}),\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{2}}(a_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}+i_{2}}),\ldots,\\
\mathcal{T}^{i_{k}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k-1}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}}),\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{k+1}}(a_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}+1},\ldots,),\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{1}^{i_{l}}(a_{d-i_{l}},\ldots,a_{d})),

(10.15)

where
\dagger
is as in (
10.11
),
and
R_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\mathcal{T}\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{F}_{1}\circ\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{2}\circ\mathcal{F}_{0})

is defined as

(R_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\mathcal{T})^{d}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})=\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{m}=d}\mathcal{T}^{m}(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{1}}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i_{1}}),\\
\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{0}^{i_{m}}(a_{d-i_{m}+1},\ldots,a_{d})).

(10.16)

Furthermore, a straightforward verification shows that if
\mathcal{T}
is a
homotopy from
\mathcal{F}_{1}
to
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, then,
L_{\mathcal{F}_{3}}\mathcal{T}
is a homotopy
from
\mathcal{F}_{3}\circ\mathcal{F}_{1}
to
\mathcal{F}_{3}\circ\mathcal{F}_{2}
, and
R_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\mathcal{T}
is a
homotopy from
\mathcal{F}_{1}\circ\mathcal{F}_{0}
to
\mathcal{F}_{2}\circ\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

10.2
Composition maps

In this section, we describe
A_{\infty}
algebras, called
Fukaya algebras

whose composition maps
are given by counts of treed holomorphic maps, both in an ordinary
symplectic manifold and in a broken manifold. The boundary of the
disks map to a Lagrangian, which in the broken case, is contained in
the complement of relative divisors.

Lagrangians will be equipped with additional data called
brane
structures
. We assume
Lagrangians are compact, connected and oriented. A brane structure
consists of :

—

a relative spin structure (see
[
36
, Chapter 44]
for the definition),

—

and a local system, which is an element

\operatorname{Hol}_{L}\in\mathcal{R}(L)=\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(L),\Lambda^{\times}).

Typically, a grading in the sense of Seidel
[
74
]
is included
in the definition of brane; however, since gradings are needed only
while working with multiple Lagrangians, we do not use them in this
book.

The Fukaya algebra of a Lagrangian brane involves counts of disks that
are holomorphic with respect to coherent perturbation data

\underline{\mathfrak{p}}:=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
for all types
\Gamma
of stable treed
disks. The data
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
consists of perturbations of a background
almost complex structure
J_{0}
on the manifold
X
and a Morse
function
F:L\to\mathbb{R}
on the Lagrangian. Theorem

6.27
constructs coherent perturbations for a
broken manifold
\mathfrak{X}
; for the corresponding result on a smooth
manifold, see
[
17
, Theorem 4.20]
. We first define
composition maps giving a (unbroken) Fukaya algebra without strict
units called the
geometric Fukaya algebra
; we later explain how
to upgrade the construction to include strict units.

Definition 10.1
.

(Geometric Fukaya algebra)

For a Lagrangian brane
L
and coherent perturbation datum

\underline{\mathfrak{p}}:=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
on the manifold
X
, the

geometric Fukaya algebra
is an
A_{\infty}
algebra consisting of
the space of
Floer cochains
over the Novikov ring
\Lambda_{\geq 0}

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}):=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}CF^{d}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}),\quad CF^{d}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}):=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{d}(L)}\Lambda_{\geq 0}\langle{x}\rangle

where
\mathcal{I}_{d}(L)
is the set of index
d
critical points of the
Morse function
F:L\to\mathbb{R}
, see Definition

4.5
; and equipped with

composition maps

m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}:(CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L))^{\otimes d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}\to CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L),\quad d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\geq 0

defined
on generators
x_{i}\in{\operatorname{crit}}(F)

by

m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})})=\sum_{\Gamma,x_{0},u\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(X,L,\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma},\underline{x})_{0}}w(u)x_{0}

(10.17)

where the sum is over all rigid types
\Gamma
of maps from Definition

8.48

with
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})

incoming boundary edges, and

w(u):=(-1)^{\heartsuit}(d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)!)^{-1}\operatorname{Hol}_{L}([\partial u])\epsilon(u)q^{A(u)}.

(10.18)

The factors in (
10.18
) are as
below:

(a)

\heartsuit={{\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}i|x_{i}|}
;

(b)

\operatorname{Hol}_{L}([\partial u])\in\Lambda^{\times}
is the evaluation of the local system

\operatorname{Hol}_{L}\in\mathcal{R}(L)
on the homotopy class of loops

[\partial u]\in\pi_{1}(L)
defined by going around the
boundary of each disk component in the treed disk once;

(c)

d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma)
the number of interior markings on the map
u
; and

(d)

\epsilon(u)\in\{\pm 1\}
is the orientation sign, as in Remark
6.28
.

This ends the Definition.

In the count of disks (
10.17
), we only consider rigid types, because types of maps that are not rigid occur in the boundary of a compactification of a larger dimensional stratum.

The
A_{\infty}
relation follows from the description of the boundary of
the moduli space. We recall from Section
9.8
, that the

true boundary
of one-dimensional moduli spaces of treed
holomorphic disks consists of maps with a broken edge.

Indeed, as a stratified space, the closure of the one-dimensional
moduli spaces have zero-dimensional strata which include both strata
where edge lengths have become zero and strata where edge lengths have
become infinity; however, only the latter types form topological
boundary strata, since the length-zero strata lie in the closures of
two one-dimension strata either by deforming to positive length or
performing the gluing construction for holomorphic disks.

Theorem 10.2
.

(
A_{\infty}
algebra for a Lagrangian in a symplectic manifold)
For a coherent
perturbation system
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
on the manifold

(X,\omega_{X})
, the maps
(m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})})_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\geq 0}
on

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)
satisfy the axioms of a convergent (possibly
curved)
A_{\infty}
algebra
CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)
.

Beweis.

We prove the
A_{\infty}
associativity relations by counting the ends of
one-dimensional moduli spaces of treed holomorphic disks. We perform
the count for a fixed number of interior and boundary markings, and
fixed limits on the input and output treed segments, and then sum
over all choices. Consider integers
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\geq 0

and a tuple
\underline{x}\in(\mathcal{I}(L))^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+1}
of inputs and
outputs. We denote the one-dimensional component of the moduli space
of treed disks of rigid type with
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
boundary inputs and

d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})
interior markings by

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}:=\cup_{\Gamma:i(\Gamma,\underline{x})=1}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{x}),

where
\Gamma
ranges over rigid types containing
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})

interior markings and
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
boundary inputs. By the unbroken
version of Proposition
8.52
and Remark

9.8
(obtained by
replacing broken maps with unbroken maps in the statement; this was
proved in
[
17
, Remark 4.22]
), the true boundary of

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}
consists of disks with a single
broken edge, that is, an
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}
with

\ell(e)=\infty
. The boundary points of one-dimensional moduli
spaces occur in pairs, and therefore,

\sum_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{x})}\epsilon_{\partial}(u)(d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})!)^{-1}=0,

(10.19)

where, in the summation,
\Gamma
ranges over all types in the true
boundary of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}
, and the sign

\epsilon_{\partial}(u)\in\{\pm 1\}
is given by the induced orientation
on the boundary of
\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}
. Consider
one such type
\Gamma
, and suppose
\Gamma_{+}
,
\Gamma_{-}
are the
treed disk types obtained by cutting the broken edge, each
containing
d_{\pm}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}):=d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma_{\pm})
interior markings.
For any critical point
x\in\mathcal{I}(L)
, denote by
(\underline{x},x)_{+}
,

(\underline{x},x)_{-}
the input-output labelling on
\Gamma_{+}
,
\Gamma_{-}

where both ends of the broken edge are labelled
x
. There is a
bijection

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{x})\simeq\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{I}(L)}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{+}}(L,(\underline{x},x)_{+}))_{0}\times\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{-}}(L,(\underline{x},x)_{-}))_{0}.

(10.20)

However, any map in the right-hand side corresponds to

\binom{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}{d_{+}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}
maps in the true boundary of

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}
as follows: There are

\binom{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}{d_{+}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})}
bijections

f:\{1,\dots,d_{+}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\}\sqcup\{1,\dots,d_{-}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\}\to\{1,\dots,d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\}

whose restrictions to
\{1,\dots,d_{+}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\}
,

\{1,\dots,d_{-}({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\}
are order-preserving. Given a pair in

(u_{+},u_{-})
in the right-hand side of (
10.20
), each
bijection
f
corresponds to a map
u
in the true boundary of

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}
, where the
i
-th interior
marking in
u_{\pm}
is labelled
f(i)
in
u
. Therefore, the
expression in (
10.19
) is equal to

0=\sum_{(\Gamma_{+},\Gamma_{-}),x}\left\{\left(\sum_{u_{+}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{+}}(L,(\underline{x},x)_{+})}\epsilon(u_{+})(d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma_{+})!)^{-1}\right)\cdot\right.\\
\left.\left(\sum_{u_{-}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{-}}(L,(\underline{x},x)_{-})}\epsilon(u_{-})(d_{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}(\Gamma_{-})!)^{-1}\right)\frac{\epsilon_{\partial}(u)}{\epsilon(u_{+})\epsilon(u_{-})}\right\},

(10.21)

where
x
ranges over critical points in
\mathcal{I}(L)
and

(\Gamma_{+},\Gamma_{-})
range over all pairs of types obtained by
cutting an edge in a treed disk type occurring in the boundary of

\mathcal{M}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(L,\underline{x})_{1}
. The sign contribution

\tfrac{\epsilon_{\partial}(u)}{\epsilon(u_{+})\epsilon(u_{-})}
is equal to the
difference in the orientation of the moduli space of treed disks
with a broken edge when viewed as a boundary of a larger stratum,
and when viewed as a product, and is equal to the shifted Koszul
sign in the
A_{\infty}
associativity relation (
10.1
) by

[
76
, (12.25)]
. Adding the equations (
10.21
)
corresponding to all
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})\geq 0
, we obtain the associativity
relation on the maps
(m^{d})_{d\geq 0}
on
CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)

corresponding to
d=d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
. The convergence
property follows from the fact that any treed disk with no incoming
edges must, by stability, have a disk component with interior
markings. Indeed, the disk components that are furthest from the
outgoing edge have a single adjoining boundary edge, and so must
intersect the stabilizing divisor. By (
5.2
), the area of
the holomorphic disk is proportional to the number of its
intersections with the stabilizing divisor, and as a result, the

q
-valuation of
m^{0}(1)
is positive.
∎

The broken Fukaya algebra is defined analogously by counts of broken
disks.

Definition 10.3
.

(Geometric broken Fukaya algebra)
Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a broken manifold, let
L\subset\mathfrak{X}
be a Lagrangian
brane that is contained in a single piece of
\mathfrak{X}
and does not
intersect relative divisors, and let
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}:=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}

be coherent perturbation data on
\mathfrak{X}
(constructed by Theorem

6.27
). The
geometric broken Fukaya algebra

is an
A_{\infty}
algebra consisting of the space of
Floer
cochains
over the Novikov ring
\Lambda_{\geq 0}

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}):=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}}CF^{d}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}),\quad CF^{d}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}):=\bigoplus_{x\in\mathcal{I}_{d}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})}\Lambda_{\geq 0}\langle{x}\rangle,

where
\mathcal{I}_{d}(L)
is as in Definition
10.1
; and
equipped with
composition maps

m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}_{\operatorname{brok}}:(CF^{\operatorname{geom}}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}))^{\otimes d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}\to CF^{\operatorname{geom}}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}),\quad d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\geq 0

defined on generators
x_{i}\in{\operatorname{crit}}(F)
by

m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}_{\operatorname{brok}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})})=\sum_{x_{0},\Gamma,u\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{brok}}_{\Gamma}(\mathfrak{X},L,D,\underline{x})_{0}}w(u)x_{0}.

(10.22)

Here,
x_{0}
ranges over critical points of the Morse function
F
,
the combinatorial type
\Gamma
of the broken map
u
ranges over
all rigid types (see Definition
8.49
) with

d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})
boundary inputs, and
w(u)
is as in
(
10.18
). The orientation sign
\epsilon(u)\in\{\pm 1\}
, which
is a factor in
w(u)
, is determined as in Remark

6.28
.

Theorem 10.4
.

(
A_{\infty}
algebra for a
Lagrangian in a broken manifold) For any coherent perturbation
system
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
on the broken manifold

\mathfrak{X}
, the maps
(m_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}^{\operatorname{brok}})_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\geq 0}
on

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L)
satisfy the axioms of a convergent
(possibly curved)
A_{\infty}
algebra
CF^{\operatorname{geom}}_{\operatorname{brok}}(L)
.

We sketch two proofs. The first proof is an almost verbatim repeat of
the proof of the unbroken
A_{\infty}
relation in Theorem

10.2
from the description of the true boundary of the
moduli space in Proposition
8.52
. The combinatorial
factors arising from the distribution of interior markings are
accounted exactly as in the unbroken case. Another proof uses the
fact that the structure maps for the broken Fukaya algebra are the
limits of those in the unbroken case, by Theorem

10.31
, and is completed after Corollary

10.32
.

10.3
Homotopy units

In the Fukaya algebra constructed in the previous section, a homotopy
unit construction can be applied to produce a strictly unital
A_{\infty}
algebra. Recall that the Morse function
F:L\to\mathbb{R}
used in the
construction of
CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)
is assumed to have a unique
maximum point denoted
x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}\in{\operatorname{crit}}(F)
. In an idealized
situation where domain-dependent perturbations are not required,

\langle{x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}}\rangle
is a strict unit for
CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)
. This
is because a boundary marked point mapping to the unstable locus of

x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}
is an empty constraint, and such a marking can be
forgotten without affecting the disk. In our setting, marked points
can not be forgotten because domain-dependent perturbations depend on
them. The homotopy unit construction is a way of enhancing the Fukaya
algebra so that the perturbation system admits forgetful maps, so that
the algebra admits a strict unit and the potential of
(
10.7
) is well-defined.

We outline the idea of the homotopy units construction: For the Fukaya
algebra to have a unit, we would like the perturbations on the Morse
function to be domain-independent on the treed segments asymptotic to
the maximum point
x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}\in{\operatorname{crit}}(F)
. However, we can not
impose such a condition on the perturbation, because the perturbation
depends only on the “domain” which does not include the information
about which critical point a Morse trajectory asymptotes to. One can
not include the critical point label into the domain data, because
Morse trajectories may break in the limit, and the label at the
breaking point is not known beforehand. Therefore, we add a new kind
of boundary marking to the domain, which is “forgettable”, and will
serve as a unit. The treed segment at a forgettable leaf is required
to asymptote to the maximum of the Morse function
F
, but is labelled

x^{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
(to distinguish it from
x^{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
). We also add
‘weighted boundary markings”, labelled
x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}
, to the domain
which give us a way of homotoping between treed segments with
domain-dependent perturbations asymptoting to
x^{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
and those
with domain-independent perturbations asymptoting to
x^{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
. The
data of the domain now includes the information about whether a leaf
is forgettable, weighted or unforgettable. This new kind of domain is
called a
weighted treed disk
and is defined below after the
statement of the main Theorem.

Theorem 10.5
.

(Homotopy unit construction)
Suppose
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
is a coherent perturbation datum for treed
holomorphic disks, and suppose

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L):=CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})
is the
A_{\infty}
algebra whose composition maps count
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
-holomorphic disks. Then
there exists a convergent strictly unital
A_{\infty}
structure on the
vector space

CF(L):=CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)\oplus\Lambda_{\geq 0}x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}[1]\oplus\Lambda_{\geq 0}x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}},

(10.23)

with gradings

|x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}|=0,\quad|x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}|=-1,

whose composition maps count weighted
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{wt}}
-holomorphic disks (see Definition
10.8
),
where
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{wt}}
is an extension of the perturbation datum

\mathfrak{p}
to weighted disks; and in the resulting
A_{\infty}
algebra,

(a)

x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
is a strict unit,

(b)

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)\subset CF(L)
is a

A_{\infty}
sub-algebra, and

(c)

m^{1}(x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}})=x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}-x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}\mod\Lambda_{>0}.

(10.24)

The theorem is proved later in the section after defining
weighted treed holomorphic disks. We remark that the expression
(
10.24
) for
m^{1}(x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}})
is similar to that in
Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono
[
36
, (3.3.5.2)]
.

The condition that
x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
is a strict unit determines all

A_{\infty}
structure maps involving occurrences of
x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
. In the
following geometric construction of a homotopy unit, the axioms are
designed keeping this fact in mind.

Definition 10.6
.

(a)

(Weightings) A
weighting
of a treed disk

C=S\cup T
of type
\Gamma
, with
S\neq\emptyset
, consists
of a partition of the boundary semi-infinite edges

\operatorname{Edge}^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}(\Gamma)\sqcup\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma)\sqcup\operatorname{Edge}^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\rightarrow}(\Gamma)

into
unforgettable
resp.
weighted
resp.

forgettable
, and a
weight
on semi-infinite edges

\rho:\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\rightarrow}(\Gamma)\to[0,\infty]

satisfying

\rho(e)\in\begin{cases}\{0\}&e\in\operatorname{Edge}^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}(\Gamma)\\
[0,\infty]&e\in\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma)\\
\{\infty\}&e\in\operatorname{Edge}^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}(\Gamma).\end{cases}

The weighting
\rho
satisfies the following axiom:

(Outgoing edges axiom)
A disk output

e_{0}\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}}(\Gamma)
can be weighted only if the
disk has exactly one weighted input

e_{1}\in\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma)
, all the other inputs

e_{i}\in\operatorname{Edge}(\Gamma),i\neq 1
are forgettable, and there are
no interior leaves,
\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
. In
this case, the output
e_{1}
has the same weight

\rho(e_{1})=\rho(e_{0})
as the weighted input
e_{0}
. A disk
output
e_{0}
can be forgettable only if all the inputs are
forgettable, and there are no interior leaves. In all the other
cases, the output of a disk is unforgettable.

In the exceptional case that the treed disk
C
is an infinite
tree segment and does not have surface components, the only
possible labels are

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}},\quad\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}},\quad\text{or}\quad{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}.

(10.25)

In the first two cases, the input has weight
\rho(e)
equal to

\infty
resp.
0
.

(b)

(Stability) A weighted treed disk
C=S\cup T
with

S\neq\emptyset
is
stable
if
C
is stable as a treed
disk. In case
S=\emptyset
and
C
is an infinite segment, then

C
is stable iff the labels are
\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
or

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
.

(c)

(Isomorphism) Two weighted treed disks
C
and
C^{\prime}
are
isomorphic if there is an isomorphism of treed disks

\phi:C\to C^{\prime}
, the edge labels are identical, and the following
is true.

(i)

If the output edge is not weighted, then the weights on the
inputs of
C_{1}
and
\phi(C_{1})
are equal;

(ii)

if the output edge
e_{0}
is weighted, then the weights on
the inputs are equal up to scalar multiplication, i.e.

\exists\lambda\in(0,\infty):\ \forall e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\circ,\rightarrow}(C)\backslash\{e_{0}\}\quad\rho(e)=\lambda\rho^{\prime}(\phi(e)).

(10.26)

Consequently, if the output edge is weighted, since there is
exactly one incoming edge by the

(Outgoing edges axiom)
, graphs with
different weights may be isomorphic.

(d)

(Combinatorial type)

The
type
of a weighted treed disk is
given by the type of the treed disk, and the labels

\{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}},\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf},{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}\}
at the inputs and outputs, and
whether the weight at any vertex is zero, infinite or neither. Thus the combinatorial type of a weighted treed disk includes a partition of weighted edges

\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma)=\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}_{0}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}_{(0,\infty)}(\Gamma)\cup\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}_{\infty}(\Gamma)

into edges of weight
0
, non-zero finite, and infinity.

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
of weighted treed disks can be identified
with

\begin{cases}\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times[0,\infty]^{|\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma)|},\quad\text{if the output label is not $\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}$}\\
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\quad\text{if the output edge is $\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}$},\end{cases}

where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the type of treed disk obtained by forgetting the
weighting. If the type
\Gamma
is
\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
resp.

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
is a point.

The (Cutting edges) morphism has some additional features for weighted
treed disks.

Definition 10.7
.

(Cutting edges in weighted treed disks) Given a type
\Gamma

of a weighted treed disk, we say that the weighted disk types

\Gamma_{+}
,
\Gamma_{-}
(here
\Gamma_{+}
contains the root of

\Gamma
) are produced by cutting
\Gamma
at an infinite edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
if
\Gamma_{+}
,
\Gamma_{-}
(with
weights forgotten) are the treed disk types produced by cutting the
edge
e
in
\Gamma
as in Definition
6.2
, and labels
and weights assigned as follows: Assuming that

e_{\pm}\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\rightarrow}(\Gamma_{\pm})
are the pair of leaves
created by the cutting, the label (
,

or
) and the weight at
e_{+}
are the same as that of

e_{-}
, and the label and weight at
e_{-}
is determined by the

(Outgoing edges axiom)
applied to

\Gamma_{-}
.

There is a new type of morphism for weighted disk types,
wherein we cut a weighted incoming edge of weight
0
or
\infty
.

(Cutting a weighted input
edge) Suppose
e\in\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma)
is an input, and

\rho(e)=0
resp.
\infty
. Cutting
e
produces two types :

\Gamma_{-}
is an infinite segment
\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
resp.

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
, and
\Gamma_{+}
is
\Gamma
with
e
as an
unforgettable resp. forgettable edge; see Figure
10.1
.
Additionally, if
\Gamma
has a weighted output
e_{0}
, we make the
output edge
e_{0}
in
\Gamma_{+}
unforgettable resp. forgettable.

Cut
[formula]
Cut
[formula]

Abbildung 10.1:
Cutting a weighted input edge.
The arrows on each input and output edge indicate whether
the weighting is infinite (white)
finite and non-zero (grey) or zero (black).

Perturbation data defined on the moduli space of weighted treed disks
are required to be coherent with respect to

(Collapsing edges)
,

(Making an edge length finite or non-zero)
,

(Making an edge weight finite or non-zero)
,

(Cutting edges)
,

(Cutting a weighted input edge)
,
and the

(Locality axiom)
.
The perturbation vanishes on infinite segments

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
and
\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
. Additionally, the
following coherence conditions are satisfied:

(a)

(Making an edge weight finite/non-zero)

If the weighted
treed disk type
\Gamma
is obtained from
\Gamma^{\prime}
by making the
weight of an edge
e\in\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
finite or
non-zero, then
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is the pullback of
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
by
the inclusion of the universal moduli space

\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}
.

(b)

(Forgetting edges) Suppose

e
is an input edge in a weighted treed type
\Gamma
that is
either forgettable or weighted with infinite weight, and
\Gamma^{\prime}

is the type obtained by forgetting
e
. Then
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
is the
pullback of
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Definition 10.8
.

(Weighted treed holomorphic disks)

Let
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
be a coherent perturbation datum for
weighted treed disks. A
weighted
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
-holomorphic disk

is a map
u:C\to X
that is
a
\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}
-holomorphic disk, and additionally satisfies the following:

(Label axiom) A treed input or output segment labelled

resp.
asymptotes to the maximum point

x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}\in{\operatorname{crit}}(F)
.

An weighted holomorphic disk
u:C\to X
is

stable
if for any component of the domain
C_{v}
, either the map

u_{v}
is non-constant, or the domain
C_{v}
is stable in the sense of
weighted treed disks. The new feature of stability for weighted
holomorphic disks is that a stable map
u
may be constant on an
infinite tree segment labelled
\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
or

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
. The
combinatorial type

of a
weighted treed holomorphic disk consists of the type of the domain
weighted treed disk, together with the combinatorial type data of the
treed holomorphic map, namely homology classes of the maps on surface
components, and intersection multiplicities with the stabilizing
divisor at the markings. The type
\Gamma
of a weighted treed
holomorphic disk is
rigid
if all the
edges
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{-}(\Gamma)
are boundary edges with finite non-zero
length, and in case of weighted inputs or output, the weight
\rho(e)

is finite and non-zero.

There is an additional notion of equivalence for holomorphic weighted
treed disks. Two weighted treed holomorphic disks
u
,
u^{\prime}
are equivalent,
if
u
has a weighted leaf
e
with weighting
\rho(e_{i})=\infty
resp.
0
,
and
u^{\prime}
is obtained from
u
by attaching to
e
a constant trajectory

u^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{R}\to L
with weighted incoming
e^{-}
and forgettable
resp. unforgettable outgoing edge
e^{+}
. See Figure
10.1
.
This ends the Definition.

The expanded set of labels on the ends of treed segments is denoted by

\hat{\mathcal{I}}(L):=\mathcal{I}(L)\cup\{x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}},x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}\},

(10.27)

where we recall from (
6.2
) that
\mathcal{I}(L)
is the set of critical points of the Morse function on the Lagrangian
L
.

Proposition 10.9
.

(Transversality for weighted treed holomorphic disks) Given a
regular coherent perturbation datum
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
for treed holomorphic
disks,
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
extends to a regular perturbation datum

\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{wt}}
on weighted treed holomorphic disks such that
the following holds: For an uncrowded type
\Gamma
of weighted
holomorphic maps, and a prescribed tuple of inputs

\underline{x}:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})})\in\hat{\mathcal{I}}(L)^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
and
an output
x_{0}\in\hat{\mathcal{I}}(L)
respecting the (Label axiom) and for
which
i(\Gamma,\underline{x})\leq 1
, the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})

of weighted
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
-holomorphic treed disks with limits
\underline{x}
is a
smooth manifold of expected dimension. The moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
is compact if
i(\Gamma,\underline{x})=0
,
and if
i(\Gamma,\underline{x})=1
,
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})
has a
compactification whose boundary consists of configurations with a
boundary node with length
0
or
\infty
, or a weighted edge with
weight
0
or
\infty
.

Proof of Proposition
10.9
.

We extend the perturbation datum
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=\{\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma}\}_{\Gamma}
on
domain curves that only contain unforgettable leaves to those that
have other kinds of leaves as well. First, consider domain types
whose output is unforgettable. Suppose that the domain type

\Gamma
has a single weighted/forgettable input leaf
e
, since the
other cases follow inductively.
Let
\Gamma_{0}
resp.
\Gamma_{\infty}
be the domain type obtained by
making the weight of
e
in
\Gamma
zero resp. infinity. By the

(Cutting a weighted edge)
morphism,
on the subset
\{\rho(e)=0\}
resp.
\{\rho(e)=\infty\}
of

\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
, the perturbation
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Gamma}
is given by

\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Gamma_{0}}
resp.
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
. The
perturbation
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Gamma_{0}}
is equal to

\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma_{0}}
. The perturbation
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Gamma_{\infty}}
is
defined via the
(Forgetting edges)

axiom, which means that the perturbation is independent of
forgettable treed segment on the domain curve. By standard
arguments, the perturbation
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}_{\Gamma}
can be extended over

\{\rho(e)\in(0,\infty)\}
while satisfying regularity.

For domain curves whose output is weighted or forgettable, we define
the perturbation to be domain-independent. Indeed, on such domains
maps are constant and lie in the maximum point in

{\operatorname{crit}}(F)\subset L
; and strata with only unforgettable leaves and
root do not occur in the compactification of strata where the output
is forgettable/weighted.

Next we prove the statement on compactification. We analyze the
cases where a sequence of weighted maps of type
\Gamma
that are
non-constant on either the surface or tree part converges to a limit
that has a breaking on a weighted/forgettable leaf
e
, and the map
is non-constant on both segments incident at the breaking. The other
cases are covered either by the compactification result for treed
holomorphic maps without weights; or, if the maps in the sequence
are constant, by straightforward formal arguments.

If
e
is weighted, for dimension reasons, the infinite segment

(-\infty,\infty)
in the broken segment maps to the maximum point
in
L
, and therefore, the map is constant on this segment. If
e

is forgettable, by the
(Forgetting
edges)
axiom, the only case to be considered is when

i(\Gamma,\underline{x})=1
and
\Gamma
is of the form shown in Figure

10.2
.

Abbildung 10.2:
A type of map. The black arrowheads mean that the input/output is unforgettable. The white attowhead means that the input/output is forgettable.

The map is constant on the surface, and the Morse index of
x_{0}
,

x_{1}\in{\operatorname{crit}}(F)
differ by
1
. We may assume the domain-dependent
Morse perturbation on the unforgettable edges is small enough that the
ends of the moduli space are given by the breaking of the
unforgettable input or output edge as in Figure
10.3

below.

\scalebox{.8}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{forget-eg2.pdf}}\put(-0.00181,0.01575){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$x_{1}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.41524,0.06842){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$x_{0}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{forget-eg2.pdf}}\put(0.19491,0.01863){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$x_{0}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{forget-eg2.pdf}}\put(0.65484,0.01085){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$x_{1}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.9362,0.07349){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$x_{0}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{forget-eg2.pdf}}\put(0.831,0.03855){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$x_{1}$\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}},

Abbildung 10.3:
Breaking an edge

This rules out the possibility of breaking on the forgettable edge.
∎

For a coherent perturbation system
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
for
weighted treed types define composition maps on the generators of the
Fukaya algebra

CF(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}}):=CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}})\oplus\Lambda_{\geq 0}\langle{x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}}\rangle\oplus\Lambda_{\geq 0}\langle{x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}}\rangle

as

m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})})=\sum_{x_{0},u\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}(L,\underline{\mathfrak{p}},\underline{x})_{0}}w(u)x_{0}

(10.28)

where

w(u)=(-1)^{\heartsuit}(d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})!)^{-1}\operatorname{Hol}_{L}([\partial u])q^{A(u)}\epsilon(u),\quad\heartsuit={\sum_{i=1}^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}i|x_{i}|}.

The sum ranges over all rigid types
\Gamma
of weighted treed
holomorphic disks as in Definition
10.8
. That is,

\Gamma
is a rigid type for broken maps (Definition

8.49
) after forgetting the weighting, and additionally,
the weight for any weighted input is finite non-zero in the type

\Gamma
. Rigidity includes the latter condition because a weighted
edge with weight
0
or
\infty
may be deformed to a weighted edge
with finite non-zero weight.

We now prove the main Theorem of this section.

Proof of Theorem
10.5
.

By Proposition

10.9
, the coherent perturbation data
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}
for
treed holomorphic disks extend to coherent data
\underline{\mathfrak{p}}^{\operatorname{wt}}

for weighted treed holomorphic disks. We first prove properties
(
b
)-(
c
), and then proceed to show the

A_{\infty}
associativity relations. The geometric part

CF^{\operatorname{geom}}(L)
is a sub-algebra because if the inputs to a
treed disk are unforgettable, the output is also unforgettable,
proving (
b
).

The element
x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
is a strict unit for the following
reasons. For
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})>2
, we have

m^{d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}(\dots,x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}},\dots)=0

because the input
x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
is an empty constraint, and can be
forgotten because the perturbation satisfies the

(Forgetting edges)
axiom. The term

m^{1}(x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}})
is also zero : any disk that is counted has
interior markings, and therefore, placing the marked point

x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
adds one to the dimension, and therefore the moduli
space is not zero-dimensional. Finally, by the same argument,

m^{2}(x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}},y)
and
m^{2}(y,x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}})
do not count any disk
with interior markings. The only contributions are from constant
disks. We conclude that both terms are equal to
\pm y
, for any
generator
y
, proving (
a
).

For (
c
), we recall from (
10.25
) that
the infinite tree segments with labels
\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
,

\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}\to{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
are rigid configurations with orientations
+1

and
-1
(see
[
17
, Remark 4.24]
). These contribute

x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}-x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}
to
m^{1}(x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}})
. The other terms in

m^{1}(x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}})
necessarily arise from configurations with at
least one non-constant disk component, by the definition of
stability, and so have positive
q
-valuation.

To show that the
A_{\infty}
associativity relationships are satisfied,
we consider one-dimensional moduli space of weighted maps of rigid
type. By Proposition
10.9
, the true boundary strata
contain one of the following configurations : a configuration with a
weight
0
or
\infty
at a weighted input which is equivalent to
the broken configuration in Figure
10.1
, a boundary node
with a broken segment, or a broken Morse trajectory. These
configurations exactly correspond to the terms in the
A_{\infty}
associativity relations, and so
CF(L)
is an
A_{\infty}
algebra.

In particular, the former boundary configuration contributes

m^{i}(\dots,x^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}},\dots)
,
m^{i}(\dots,x^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}},\dots)
to
terms of the form
m^{i}(\dots,m^{1}(x^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}},\dots))
in the
associativity relation. Any other kind of contribution to a term of
the form
m^{i}(\dots,m^{j}(\dots),\dots)
in the associativity relation,
comes from a non-constant disk breaking off, that is, the map is
constant on either the surface or the tree part.
∎

We recall from
[
17
]
a useful criterion for a Lagrangian
brane
L
to be weakly unobstructed.

Lemma 10.10
.

(A criterion for unobstructedness
[
17
, Lemma
4.43]
) Suppose for a Fukaya algebra
CF(L)
of a Lagrangian submanifold

L\subset X
,

m^{0}(1)=Wx^{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}
for some
W\in\Lambda_{\geq 0}
and every non-constant
disk has positive Maslov index. Then,
b:=Wx^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}\in MC(L)
and

m^{0}_{b}(1)=Wx^{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}
.

10.4
Quilted disks

Morphisms between Fukaya algebras are defined by counts of quilted
holomorphic disks. The domains of such disks are
quilted disks
,
which are ordinary disks with a
quilting circle
as defined in
Ma’u-Woodward
[
53
]
. In this
Section, we describe moduli spaces of treed nodal quilted disks.

Definition 10.11
.

(Quilted disks)

(a)

A
quilted disk

(S,Q,\underline{z})
is a complex disk

S\simeq\mathbb{D}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
with a collection
\underline{z}
of interior and
boundary markings, and a
quilting circle
, which is a circle

Q\subset S
tangent to the
0
-th boundary marking
z_{0}
. Two
quilted disks
(S_{0},Q_{0},\underline{z}^{0})
,
(S_{1},Q_{1},\underline{z}^{1})
are

isomorphic
if there is a biholomorphism which maps each marking
in
S_{0}
to the corresponding marking in
S_{1}
, and which maps the
quilting circle
Q_{0}\subset S_{0}
to the quilting circle

Q_{1}\subset S_{1}
.

(b)

(Treed quilted disk) A
treed quilted
disk
is a treed nodal disk
C
, a subset of whose components are
quilted components. The subset of quilted components satisfies the
following:

—

A path in
C
from any (non-root) boundary or interior marking

z_{e}
,
e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\to}\backslash\{0\}
to the root marking
z_{0}

intersects exactly one quilted disk.

—

(Equal lengths condition) For the disk
component
S_{v_{0}}
containing the outgoing leaf, and quilted
components
S_{v_{1}},S_{v_{2}}
, the sum of lengths
\ell(e)
of
treed segments
T_{e}
connecting
S_{v_{0}}
to

S_{v_{k}},k\in\{1,2\}
is independent of
k
.

(c)

(Combinatorial types) The combinatorial type
\Gamma
of a quilted
treed disk is the combinatorial type of the treed disk (without the
quilting datum) together with a subset of the boundary vertex set

\operatorname{Vert}^{q}(\Gamma)\subset\operatorname{Vert}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma),

corresponding to quilted disks, and are called
quilted
vertices
.

(d)

(Stability) A treed quilted disk
C=S\cup T
is stable if
the automorphism group of any surface component

S_{v},v\in\operatorname{Vert}(\Gamma)
is trivial, and there are no treed
segments both whose ends are infinite. This means a quilted disk is
stable if it has at least two special points.

Remark 10.12
.

(Interior markings on quilted disks)
An interior point in a quilted disk component
may lie inside, outside or on the quilting circle; the relative position of
interior points with respect to the quilting circle does not affect the
combinatorial type of the treed quilted
disk.
1
1

1

In
[
17
]
, the
compactification of the moduli space of quilted disks contains
configurations with “quilted spheres”. Quilted spheres arise in
the compactification only if we impose a requirement that markings
can not lie on dark components, that is, on unquilted components
lying below the quilting circle.

Remark 10.13
.

(Quilted disks via affine structures)
A quilted disk may alternately be defined as
a disk
S\simeq\mathbb{D}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
with markings
\underline{z}
, and a
biholomorphism

\phi:(S\backslash\{z_{0}\},\partial S)\xrightarrow{\phi}(\mathbb{H},\partial\mathbb{H}),

which we call an
affine structure
. Two affine structures

\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
are equivalent if there exists
\xi\in\mathbb{R}

such that
\phi_{1}(z)=\phi_{0}(z)+\xi
. Two quilted disks

(S_{0},\underline{z}^{0},\phi_{0})
,
(S_{1},\underline{z}^{1},\phi_{1})
are

isomorphic
if there exists
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
such that, defining

\tau(z):=z+\xi
, the biholomorphism

\phi_{1}^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\phi_{0}:S_{0}\to S_{1}
maps each
marking in
S_{0}
to the corresponding marking in
S_{1}
. A choice
of a quilting circle is equivalent to an affine structure, by
taking the quilting circle to be
Q=\{\operatorname{Im}(z)=1\}
.

The moduli space of stable quilted disks with interior and boundary
markings is homeomorphic to a compact cell complex. More precisely,
the moduli space is a compact topological space admitting a
Thom-Mather stratification into smooth submanifolds with toric
singularities. As the interior and boundary markings go to infinity,
the markings bubble off onto either quilted disks or unquilted disks
or spheres. The case of combined boundary and interior markings is a
straight-forward generalization of Ma’u-Woodward
[
53
]
; see
also Bottman-Oblomkov
[
10
]
where a generalization to
markings on arbitrary number of quilting circles is constructed. In the case with no
interior markings, the moduli spaces are cell complexes called the
“multiplihedra” introduced by Stasheff
[
80
]
. We denote by

{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}

the moduli space of stable marked quilted treed disks with
d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})

boundary leaves and
d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})
interior leaves.

Remark 10.14
.

(Shadings on a treed quilted disk) In Figures, we represent
quilted disks using shadings. The subset of a quilted disk lying
inside resp. outside the quilting circle is shaded dark
resp. light. An unquilted surface component
S_{v}
of a treed quilted
disk is shaded light resp. dark if the path from
v
to the root
vertex contains resp. does not contain a quilted vertex. See Figures

10.4
,
10.5
for examples.

Example 10.15
.

Figure
10.4
depicts the moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{0,2}

of treed quilted disks with two incoming leaves, one outgoing leaf,
and no interior marking; and Figure
10.5
) depicts the
moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}_{1,0}^{q}
of treed quilted disks with one
outgoing leaf and one interior marking. In both cases, there are 3
open strata, interspersed with four strata of codimension one.
Figure
10.4
shows
the type of each of these strata.
The hashes on the line segments
T_{e}
indicate breakings. We
justify the compactification of an open stratum in the moduli space
of quilted disks with one interior marking. Let
\Gamma
be a type
of quilted disk with a single interior marking, a boundary output
and no boundary inputs, and no boundary nodes (fourth figure from
the left in Figure
10.5
). The stratum

\mathcal{M}^{q}_{\Gamma}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{1,0}^{q}
is an open interval

(0,\infty)
parametrizing disks with markings

z^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}_{0,\nu}=\infty,\quad z^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}_{0,\nu}=i\nu,\quad\nu\in(0,\infty),

(10.29)

and quilting circle
\{\operatorname{Im}(z)=1\}
, where the disk is
identified to
\mathbb{H}\cup\{\infty\}
. In the limit
\nu\to\infty

resp.
\nu\to 0
, we obtain the strata to the left resp. right of

\Gamma
in Figure
10.5
. In particular, in the limit

\nu\to\infty
, the limit of the markings in (
10.29
)
gives the quilted disk in the limit, and the unquilted dark disk is
given by the reparametrization
z\mapsto\frac{z}{\nu}
, so that the
markings in the sequence are
z^{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}_{0,\nu}=\infty
,

z^{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}_{0,\nu}=i
, and the quilting circle is

\{\operatorname{Im}(z)=\frac{1}{\nu}\}
. This gives
rise to a limit disk which is fully in the dark region. This finishes the example.

Abbildung 10.4:
Moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{0,2}
of stable quilted treed disks.

Abbildung 10.5:
Moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{1,0}
of stable quilted treed disks.

As in the unquilted case, the top-dimensional cells in

{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
consist of strata
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}

which do not have any interior edges, that is,

\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
, and all boundary edges

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)
have finite non-zero length, and the
dimension of these strata is equal to

d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})+2d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})-1.

The true boundary of
{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
consists
of strata
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
where either

—

\Gamma
has a single broken edge

e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}}(\Gamma)
, or

—

\Gamma
has a collection of broken edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
,
such that the types
\Gamma_{0},\dots,\Gamma_{k}
obtained by
disconnecting
\Gamma
at the breakings at
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
consist
of an unquilted disk type
\Gamma_{0}
, all whose vertices have
negative distance from the quilting circle in
\Gamma
, and a collection

\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k}
of quilted disk types.

For example, the combinatorial types in Figure
10.6
occur
in the codimension one stratum of
\mathcal{M}^{q}_{0,4}
,

\mathcal{M}^{q}_{0,5}
.

The following is a useful quantity defined on treed quilted disks.

Definition 10.16
.

(Distance to the quilting circle) Let
C=S\cup T
be a treed quilted
disk of type
\Gamma
.
Given a point
x\in C

and a surface component
S_{v}\subset C
,
define the
distance
between
x
and
S_{v}
as

\overline{d}(x,S_{v}):=\ell(\gamma\cap T)=\sum_{e\in\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},-}(\Gamma)}\ell(\gamma\cap T_{e})\in[0,\infty],

(10.30)

where
\gamma
is any non self-intersecting path from
x
to any point in
S_{v}
.
For a point
z\in C
,
the
distance
to the quilting circle

is the signed distance to the closest quilted surface component, that is,

d(z):=\pm\overline{d}(z,S_{v})\in[-\infty,\infty],

(10.31)

where
S_{v}
,
v\in\operatorname{Vert}^{q}(\Gamma)
is the closest quilted
surface component to
z
with respect to
\overline{d}
in (
10.30
),
and the sign is
+
resp.
-
if
z
is above resp. Below the
quilted disk components (that is, further from resp. closer to the
root than the quilted disk components). Note that
d
is constant
on any surface component of
C
. Therefore, the distance

d(v)
is defined for any vertex
v
in the graph underlying
C
.
The distance function
d
is zero on a quilted disk component and
the spheres attached to it. To define perturbations,
d
is composed
with an increasing diffeomorphism

\delta:[-\infty,\infty]\to[0,1]

(10.32)

which is assumed to be fixed.

Remark 10.17
.

(Morphisms for quilted disk types)

Morphisms of graphs (Cutting an edge, collapsing edges, making edge
lengths finite or non-zero)
from Definition
6.2

induce morphisms of moduli spaces of
stable quilted treed disks as in the unquilted case. The new feature
is that (Cutting edges) is done
such that one of the pieces is quilted and the other unquilted. This
implies that output edges of quilted disks are cut simultaneously, and
therefore the output has a disconnected type.

For example, in Figure
10.6
, in the left picture, one can
cut the
e
at the breaking to obtain an unquilted disk with positive
distance from the quilting circle, and a quilted disk. In the picture to the
right, the edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
get cut simultaneously to yield an
unquilted disk with negative distance from the quilting circle and a disconnected
type consisting of two quilted disks.

Abbildung 10.6:
A quilted treed disk with edges of infinite length.

In a similar
vein, the morphisms

(Collapsing edges)
and

(Making an edge length finite or non-zero)

may involve several edges
instead of a single one. For example, in the quilted disk with three
boundary edges of length zero shown in Figure
10.7
,

Abbildung 10.7:
A quilted disk with length zero edges.

there is no
(Collapsing
edges)
or
(Making an edge length finite or non-zero)

morphism for
one of the edges alone; these operations can only be performed for all
three edges simultaneously in order to respect the

(Equal lengths condition)
.

For any combinatorial type
\Gamma
of quilted disk there is a

universal quilted treed disk

\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}

which is a cell complex whose fiber over
[C]
is isomorphic to
C
.
The universal disk splits into surface and tree parts

\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\Gamma}=\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{\Gamma}\cup\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\Gamma}\cup\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{{\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}},\Gamma}
, where the last two sets are the boundary and
interior parts of the tree respectively. This ends the Remark.

Weights can be added to the inputs and output of quilted treed disks
as in the case of treed disks. We suppose there is a partition of the
boundary markings

\operatorname{Edge}^{\includegraphics[width]{greyt.pdf}}(T)\sqcup\operatorname{Edge}^{{\includegraphics[width]{whitet.pdf}}}(T)\sqcup\operatorname{Edge}^{{\includegraphics[width]{blackt.pdf}}}(T)=\operatorname{Edge}_{{\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}},\rightarrow}(T)

into
weighted
resp.
forgettable
resp.
unforgettable

edges as in the unquilted case. The outgoing edge axiom is the same as in
the unquilted case. In the quilted case, the trees in Figure

10.8
are stable. Isomorphism of weighted quilted disks is the same
as the unquilted case, and therefore, the moduli space with a single
weighted leaf and no markings is a point.

Abbildung 10.8:
Unmarked stable treed quilted disks.

Orientations of the moduli space of quilted treed disks are defined as
follows. Each main stratum of
{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}

can be oriented using the isomorphism of the stratum made of quilted
treed disks having a single disk with
\mathbb{R}
times

{\mathcal{M}}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}
, the extra factor corresponding to
the quilting parameter. The boundary of the moduli space is naturally
isomorphic to a union of moduli spaces:

\partial\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}}),d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})}\cong\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\\
I_{1}\cup I_{2}=[d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})]\end{subarray}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{|I_{1}|,d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})-i+1}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{|I_{2}|,i}\right)\\
\cup\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\geq 1,I_{1}\cup\dots I_{r}=[d({\includegraphics[width]{black.pdf}})],\\
m_{1}+\dots+m_{r}=d({\includegraphics[width]{white.pdf}})\end{subarray}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{|I_{0}|,r}\times\prod_{j=1}^{r}\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{q}_{|I_{j}|,m_{j}}\right).

(10.33)

Here
